# Bezel: Design Rationale

**Companion to the Bezel Language Specification, v0.337 — October 2026**

The spec describes the language. This document explains the choices behind it — places where two reasonable designs were possible and one was picked, places where a familiar-looking rule has a less-stated rule behind it, and places where something widely done elsewhere was deliberately left out. The spec says what the language *is*; this document says *why*, and what the alternatives would have cost.

Read it when a rule surprises you. The recurring question this document exists to answer is "why doesn't Bezel just do Y?" — and the recorded form of the answer names the alternative, what it would buy, and why it was declined. Decisions are written down with their reasoning so they can be revisited deliberately rather than relitigated from memory. Each section stands alone.

This document is not exhaustive. Choices that simply follow settled convention (`:=` for assignment, word keywords for boolean operators, non-virtual by default, `case` for pattern matching) are not discussed; the precedent is the rationale.

<a id="sec-the-big-picture"></a>
## 1. The big picture

Bezel is a compiled language for services and tools. Two commitments define it. The first is the performance of a compiled language: native code, no garbage collector, and costs that follow from what the source says. The second is memory management that is predictable and checked: a resource-holding object has a single owner responsible for disposing it, ownership moves are visible in the source — `owned` marks the transfer, `using var` the owning binding — and the compiler's ownership tracking guarantees disposal exactly once. The mistakes manual management is famous for — leaks, double-frees, use-after-free — are mostly rejected at compile time; most of the remainder faults deterministically at run time in checked disposal mode; and the few admissions the model can back with neither are recorded individually in the spec rather than hidden ([§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows) of the spec). A small tier needs no ceremony at all — strings, boxed values, and function values are runtime-managed, and resource-free classes are compiler-freed at their owners' release points with no `using var` and no warning ([§4](#sec-memory-management), [§5.9](#sec-trivial-dispose-classes), [§5.28](#sec-silent-tracking)).

The ownership model bears some similarity to Rust's — single ownership, explicit transfers, borrowed references checked against their owners ([§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows) of the spec) — and the resemblance is deliberate: compile-time ownership is how Bezel stays memory-safe without a collector. Where Bezel differs is in how much its checker demands. There are no lifetime annotations to write, and no exclusive-writer rule: code that shares and mutates freely inside one execution domain compiles as written, because freedom from data races comes from the concurrency model — serial domains, with transfer or immutable sharing at their boundaries ([§12](#sec-async)) — rather than from aliasing restrictions. The result is more freedom in how code is written, without the common pitfalls of manual management. The price is stated as plainly as the bet: a small class of mistakes a stricter checker would reject at compile time surfaces in Bezel at run time — deterministically, as `DisposedAccessException`, under checked disposal mode; as undefined behavior in an unchecked release build (spec §28.2) — and in return the ownership rules stay few enough to learn in full and cheap enough to apply everywhere — simple enough to keep entirely in your head.

The weight of that bet lands in the memory model, and most of this document is the same kind of exchange, considered carefully and made again at smaller scale: what a stricter rule would buy, what it would cost, and why the balance landed where it did. Where Bezel looks familiar, it usually is; the chapters that follow are mostly about the places where it departs from familiar practice, and the reasons each departure seemed worth making.

<a id="sec-modules-and-files"></a>
## 2. Modules and files

<a id="sec-dotted-module-names-are-one-name"></a>
### 2.1 Dotted module names are one name

`module Foo.Bar.Baz;` declares one module. The dots are punctuation in the name. `Foo.Bar` and `Foo.Bar.Baz` are unrelated modules, with no implicit visibility between them.

The alternative — nested names that relate to each other, as C# namespaces do — is a reliable source of confusion, because the relationship is weaker than the syntax suggests. In C#, code inside `Foo.Bar.Baz` can name types from `Foo.Bar` without qualification, and that lexical convenience trains programmers to read the dots as a containment hierarchy — so they assume `internal` scopes to the namespace (it scopes to the whole assembly, ignoring namespaces entirely) and that `using Foo` imports `Foo.Bar`'s types (it does not). The hierarchy the dots suggest is not one any visibility or import rule actually follows. Bezel removes the suggestion: the dots are spelling, and the obvious reading — no relationship — is the correct one.

The cost: no sub-package privacy. Use `internal` and put related code in the same module.

**Member vs type at a chain head: position, not meaning (#2097/#2125).** When a bare name inside a type's body could denote either an accessible enclosing-type member or a same-named type, Bezel resolves by the name's *syntactic position*, not by trying each meaning to see which one the rest of the chain accepts. A chain head (`Color.Red`) prefers the type, so the `property Color: Color` self-naming idiom keeps working across modules; every other head — a value read, an assignment target, a bare callee, a `?.`/`!`-continued head — prefers the member. The declined alternative is C#'s Color-Color rule, where the head's meaning depends on whether the member reading can host the next segment — that is backtracking on semantic failure, which §2.7's "leftmost-first, type-wins, no backtracking" design refuses (adding an import can un-error a chain but must never silently re-mean one). The positional split buys the idiom without the backtracking; a warning covers the one residual case where a chain head resolves to a *different*-typed shadowing type, so the safety is delivered at zero cost to idiomatic code (spec §2.7).

<a id="sec-no-top-level-functions-or-fields"></a>
### 2.2 No top-level functions or fields

Every method, field, and constant lives inside a class, record, or enum. Classes already group code and own static state; a parallel module-level grouping mechanism doubles the surface area without solving anything. A `public static class` of static methods is the entry point for what other languages call free functions.

Extension blocks are the one exception. They live at module level because their identity is the type they extend, not a surrounding class.

<a id="sec-auto-imported-extension-modules"></a>
### 2.3 A type's standard surface follows the type (v0.278)

Spec §2.3 makes 26 standard-library type names visible in every file with no `import`, and §2.8.3 rule 2 lets a program *call* the public members of a value whose module it never imported. Against both, §26.5 rule 2 scoped extension-member candidacy to imported modules — so a program could hold an `IEnumerable<T>` and call its instance members with no import at all, while `xs.Where(p)` required `import System.Collections.Linq;`, a module naming neither the receiver's type nor the receiver's module. §2.3 now auto-imports the standard library's extension modules over always-available types, which today names that one module.

**What decided it was not the design argument but three facts about the rule's standing.** It had never been argued: the citation the standard library carried dated the *sentence* that stated it, not the rule, and the changelog entry it pointed at is a contract sweep containing nothing about extensions or imports. The compiler had never enforced it for the standard library — §5's operators are builtins matched by name, the import set is not consulted, and forty-three test files called `.Where(` with no import and passed the real compile-link-run path. And the specification's own examples did not follow it: six fenced examples called §5 operators with no import — in §14.5, §21.10, §27.4 and §27.6 — alongside two guides and a conformance case recorded as compiling.

So the question was never whether to relax a rule; it was which of two things the project already did should be written down. That reframing is what disqualified the option that looks conservative — keeping the rule and correcting the documents would have meant *enforcing* an unargued restriction at a cost of thirty-four tests, nine document sites and new compiler work, for a benefit no record states. Ratifying practice made nine sites correct without editing them.

**The gate is kept where it earns its keep.** An extension injects member names onto a type its author did not write, and §26.5 rule 4's ambiguity remedy — *remove or narrow a conflicting import* — is the language's only tool for choosing between two such blocks. That hazard is real for third-party modules and absent for the standard library, which is singular, compiler-known, and cannot collide with itself. Every comparable language draws the line in the same place after shipping the opposite first: C# made its `using System.Linq` implicit in the default template a decade on; Rust puts `Iterator` in the prelude while requiring `use` for other traits; Kotlin auto-imports its collections.

**The cost, which is not hypothetical and is not solved here.** §2.3 has no opt-out, and Bezel's extension blocks are nameless — so unlike C#, where a colliding extension can still be called through its static class, a user block declaring a member named `Where` over `IEnumerable<T>` has no qualified spelling and no import to remove. Renaming is the only remedy. This was accepted rather than answered, on three bounds: the escape did not work before either, since the gate was unenforced; the failure is an ambiguity diagnostic naming every origin, not a silent wrong answer; and the affected population was believed to be zero — which the landing gate falsified, finding the collision in §26.5.1's own worked example, where two blocks spelling `First()` met a §5 block that declares the same name. The example is renamed and §26.5.1 now states what rule 4's remedy list did not reach: against an auto-imported standard-library candidate, *narrow an import* is unavailable, leaving renaming. The cost is therefore instantiated, not hypothetical. Two fixes are recorded and deliberately not taken — a **provenance** tiebreak ranking a user block above a compiler-known one, which is not the auto-ranked *specificity* §17.10 deleted, or an import-suppression form on §2.3 — because neither is designable without knowing which of them a real collision wants.

**Extended to the collection module itself (v0.289, proposal 0122 L3).** The same three facts decided `System.Collections.Generic`. Unenforced: the compiler had never gated the module (its name resolver says so in a comment beside `KeyNotFoundException`). Unfollowed: 84 `.bzl` test files used a §4 collection type and 4 carried the import; 216 embedded programs and the specification's own fences — ten uses against two imports — did the same; no guide carried the line. Unargued: no decline record, and the two proposals that restated the rule did not argue for it. The row-by-row hoisting of §2.3 — `Array<T>`, `OwningArray<T>` and fourteen interfaces always available while their module was "not auto-imported" — had also produced a live contradiction (spec §14.2's sorting row against §2.3's own invariant). The cost is stated at full strength rather than as "one more module": twenty-nine names enter the bare-name space of every file — `Dictionary`, `Set`, `Stack`, `Queue`, `Span`, `BitReader`, `BitWriter`, `LinkedListNode`, `Counter`, `FrozenArray`, `KeyNotFoundException` and their kin, every one listed in §2.3's row — with no opt-out, so a future user module exporting one of them makes the bare name ambiguous wherever both are imported (§2.2's collision rule, which compares names together with their generic arity, so a non-generic `Counter` does not collide with `Counter<K>`; a current-module declaration of the same name and arity shadows it). That is the ground on which the decision could have gone the other way, and it was taken at that price. The extension-module invariant above is untouched and still names one module.

<a id="sec-syntax"></a>
## 3. Syntax

Most syntax follows Pascal/Modula-2 conventions; the precedent is the rationale. The choices that needed more than precedent are recorded below.

<a id="sec-always-semicolons"></a>
### 3.1 Always semicolons

Pascal famously had places where a semicolon was wrong — typically before `else` — and places where it was required, with the rule depending on what came next. The Ada designers fixed this by making semicolons unconditional: every statement ends with `;`, every block ends with `end;`, no exceptions.

Bezel follows the Ada rule. The rule is "always put a semicolon," which is impossible to get wrong by accident. The cost is occasional visual noise; the benefit is one fewer thing to remember.

**A third category, added with the contract clauses (v0.203/0055).** A `requires`/`ensures` clause carries **no** terminator — not because a semicolon would be wrong there in the Pascal sense, but because the clause's own parentheses are structural and already delimit it (spec §22.16), so there is nothing for a terminator to do. The rule above is therefore about **statements and blocks**, which is where it was always aimed: every statement ends with `;`, every block ends with `end;`. A self-delimiting *clause* is neither. The `invariant` member is the check on the reading — it is a **member**, so it keeps the member-terminating `;` exactly as the rule demands. *(Whether the clause region should also have carried a visible closing cue is the question proposal 0100 examined and closed at v0.260: the answer is a recommended blank line between the last clause and the first body statement — style, not grammar.)*

<a id="sec-the-discard-_"></a>
### 3.2 The discard `_`

`_` carries C#'s discard meanings — wildcard in patterns, discard for `out` arguments, and the expression-statement form `_ := expression;` (C# spells it `_ = expression;`), which evaluates `expression` and throws the result away. What Bezel changes is the weight the form carries, and — since v0.244 — its reach: the bare token is **reserved** (spec §1.3, §17.13), never a declarable or readable name in any position.

In C#, discarding a result is the default — an expression statement drops its value silently, so `_ = ...` is optional documentation of intent (most visibly for a deliberately-unawaited task). Bezel warns on every expression statement that discards a non-`()` result (spec §21.9), so the discard is the sanctioned opt-out: without it, a programmer who means to drop a value must either declare a throwaway local (which trips the unused-variable warning) or live with the discarded-result warning (which trains them to ignore warnings). The discard keeps the deliberate case syntactically distinct from the accidental one.

Ownership gives the same form a second job: applied to a named owned binding, `_ := x;` is a marker-free consume — the explicit "dispose this now" spelling, standing beside `throw x` and `owned` returns in the marker-free family ([§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model); spec §28.3). And one result type stays out of the discard's reach entirely: dropping a `Task` is a hard error, not a warning, because abandoning a running operation is a semantic decision — that case is routed to `Task.FireAndForget`, and the anonymous `using var _ := task;` binding, which nothing can await, is rejected with it (v0.288).

**Why `_` is reserved (v0.244, proposal 0083).** Through v0.243, `_` was a discard in three positions and an ordinary identifier everywhere else — approximately C#'s contextual rule, which C# keeps for backward compatibility Bezel does not have. The overlap produced corners no one designed, each individually ruled and jointly indefensible: a declared `var _` was readable but never re-assignable (the write-once corollary, forced by the discard statement's lexical shape); `_ := e;` and `var _ := e;` were different constructs with different disposal timing, distinguished by lexical shape alone; and `ref _` needed a dedicated owner ruling (v0.241, #2679 part B) just to decide *which* thing it was. Reserving the token deletes the corner class outright: there is one meaning everywhere, and every future position answers itself — a discard is valid exactly where receiving-and-ignoring is coherent, an error everywhere else. The corpus made the change free (zero reads of any `_` binding existed anywhere; the only rewrites were bind-and-ignore test sites with a mechanical spelling). Kept, honestly redefined: `using var _` as the anonymous owning binding — a real, corpus-proven idiom that binds nothing readable. Newly sanctioned: lambda-parameter and `for var _` true ignores (previously bindings *named* `_`, with `lambda(_, _)` a duplicate-name error). Rejected rather than made ignores: method/constructor parameters named `_` — parameter names are call-site-addressable API surface (spec §22.5) and carried by the artifact interface, so an unnameable slot would be a hole in that surface; lambdas have neither concern. One rule closes the family: a bare ignore takes its type from context, never an annotation. The v0.241 `ref _` ruling is completed, not reversed: it said `_` after `ref` is not a discard; reservation agrees and removes the identifier fallback too.

`_` is a sink, not a binding. It cannot be read.

<a id="sec-the-value-position-conditional"></a>
### 3.3 The value-position conditional is condition-first and paren-delimited (`(if c then a else b)`)

The expression form of selection is the parenthesized, condition-first atom — `(if c then a elif c2 then b else fallback)` — the same word order as the statement form, with the atom's parentheses as its delimiters and no `end` (spec §20, v0.208; proposal 0057). The form's history is a genuine reversal, recorded as one: through v0.207 the expression form was Python's postfix conditional (`value if condition else fallback`), a deliberate word-order split re-examined and confirmed in 2026-07 (issue #1373) on the strength of the value-first reading for the default-with-override idiom. The v0.208 re-weighing — by the same decider — overturned that preference: one selection word order across the language retires the asymmetry's erratum class (#748); first-class `elif` replaces the postfix form's right-nested chaining; the branches of a delimited atom derive full expressions, dissolving the v0.182 true-branch `throw` parse trap and the conditional's precedence entry outright; and the value-first idiom's strongest case keeps `??` for the nullable default, with the boolean-conditioned default now reading condition-first — a real, accepted residual cost. What made the reversal available rather than a re-ask was the delimiter design: the #1373-era condition-first candidate was `if c then a else b end`, declined for `end`-noise inside nested expressions; the paren-delimited atom adopts the statement's word order while deleting exactly that cost.

The atom's parentheses earn their keep three ways: `(` + `if` commits the parse at the second token; the delimiters make dangling-`else` and extent-by-precedence questions unaskable; and the form composes in any operand position like the `(case …)` atom. The recorded ergonomic cost: in call- and index-argument position the atom supplies its own pair inside the argument list's (`f((if c then a else b))`).

Alternatives declined: the postfix form (retired v0.208, above — reopening it requires new evidence against this section's reasons, per the standing discipline); `if c then a else b end` (the end-noise objection stands; the adopted form is its repair); C's `c ? a : b` (two more sigils in a language that spells `and`/`or`/`not` as words); the bare unparenthesized `if c then a else b` (statement-position prefix collision with the statement form, dangling-`else` under nesting).

<a id="sec-compound-assignment-spelling"></a>
### 3.4 Compound assignment keeps the C spellings

Assignment is `:=` and equality is `=` (the Pascal family), yet compound assignment uses the C-family spellings `+=`, `-=`, `*=`, `/=`, `&=`, `|=`, `^=`, `<<=`, `>>=`, `??=`. The Pascal-pure alternative (`+:=` and friends) has no precedent in any language programmers arrive from; omitting compound assignment entirely (the Ada position) buys no safety and gives up the evaluate-the-target-once guarantee the compound forms carry (spec §6.6) — the expanded `x := x + 1` spelling evaluates its target expression twice. Familiarity won over purity; the cost is aesthetic only. Nor is there ambiguity to pay: the compound forms exist only in statement position, where an equality expression cannot begin.

<a id="sec-arrow-body-family"></a>
### 3.5 The `=>` body family: one addition, three recorded divergences

v0.160 gives unit-returning methods the assignment arrow (`method SetX(v: int) => _x := v;`) that accessors always had — the two contexts are the same declaration shape, and the asymmetry had no recorded reason (review finding #1345). The **remaining** divergences are deliberate, with reasons:

- **Lambdas** do not take a bare-statement body: a statement's mandatory `;` collides with expression position (`Apply(lambda(x) => _total := x;, 5)` puts the arm form's `;,` seam inside an argument list; `var f := lambda(x) => _total := x;;` doubles the terminator). Multi-statement lambdas use `=> do … end`.
- **Operators** are expression-only: an operator must produce a value; a statement form could never typecheck.
- **Methods and constructors** have no `=> do … end` form: both already have the lighter bare-block body (`… end Name;`), so the arrow-block would be a second spelling of the same thing (declined for methods as far back as v0.84).

<a id="sec-destructor-takes-no-parens"></a>
### 3.6 `destructor` takes no parameter list

`constructor()` carries parens because parameters may follow — the parens are the parameter list, present even when empty for uniformity across constructors. A destructor can never take parameters (it is invoked by the compiler-generated `Dispose()`, §28.4), so a mandatory empty `()` would be pure ceremony attached to nothing. The asymmetry is intentional (confirmed 2026-07, review finding #1343): `destructor` is a keyword-introduced block like `try` or `extension`, not a callable signature. (The §1.6 closer taxonomy already treats `constructor`/`destructor`/`operator` as one keyword-labeled family.)

<a id="sec-case-scrutinee-of-delimiter"></a>
### 3.7 The `case` scrutinee is delimited by `of`

Adopted v0.166 (#1370). Through v0.165 nothing separated the scrutinee from the first arm's pattern, and since patterns may begin with `-` (a negative range bound), `..`, `..<`, or a relational token, the scrutinee had to parse at a restricted precedence level — multiplicative and postfix only, with a mandatory-parenthesization rule for anything lower (`case (x - 1)`). The restriction served no semantic purpose; it existed to substitute for the missing boundary, surprised users whose scrutinee was a perfectly ordinary arithmetic expression, and required a normative paragraph (and issues #1320 and #1233) to keep the rule honest.

`of` supplies the boundary explicitly: `case score of 90..100 => …`. The scrutinee becomes a full `Expression` with no restriction; the leading-pattern-token ambiguity vanishes by construction rather than by precedence surgery; and the spelling is the language's declared §3 Pascal heritage verbatim (`case x of` is Pascal's form). One extra keyword-token per case was weighed against the removed restriction, the removed parens rule, and the removed parser delicacy, and judged the better trade at every scale of program. Alternatives declined: a colon (`case x:`) reads as a type annotation; a mandatory newline resurrects layout-sensitivity, which #744 deliberately removed from this construct family.

<a id="sec-pattern-alternatives-pipe"></a>
### 3.8 Pattern alternatives are spelled `|`

Adopted v0.167 (#1371). Through v0.166 the comma did triple duty in pattern context — arm separator, alternative separator (`1, 2 => "low"`), and positional-slot separator — disambiguated only by scanning ahead for `=>`. Three consequences followed: alternatives had to be banned inside positional destructure slots (the #1300 fix), `catch` grew its own comma-based multi-type list that read differently from case alternatives (#1341), and every tool that parses patterns needed the lookahead.

`|` gives alternatives their own spelling: `1 | 2 => "low"`, `FormatException | OverflowException => …`, and — newly expressible — the per-slot form `TwoColor(Color.Red | Color.Blue, var x)`. The comma now means exactly one thing in pattern context; the `=>` lookahead is gone; case and catch share one alternatives syntax; and the spelling matches the Rust/Scala/Python-`match` precedent readers already know. The bitwise-or collision is dissolved structurally rather than by rule: the only expression positions inside patterns (relational operands, range bounds) parse below `|`'s precedence level, and the scrutinee is `of`-delimited (§3.7), so a pattern-level `|` is always the separator. Alternative members exclude types and bindings in case patterns (a type member stays a compile error; grouped type tests are separate arms) — the v0.144-era reasoning that constant-vs-type alternatives have different exhaustiveness and binding semantics is unchanged by the respelling. *(v0.183 widened the admitted members from constants-only to constants, ranges, and relationals: those are binding-free pure membership tests, which the recorded reasoning never targeted; the exclusion of types and bindings is the decision, and it stands.)*

<a id="sec-owning-prefix"></a>
### 3.9 The owning family is prefixed `Owning`, not `Owned`

Adopted v0.168 (#1372; the stronger remedy #1334 hedged toward). `OwnedArray<T>` grammatically read as the wrong axis: "an array that *is owned*" (the container axis), when the family actually denotes "an array that *owns its elements*" (the element axis). The misreading was not hypothetical — §28.3 and Appendix C.3 both carried defensive parentheticals explaining that a bare `OwnedArray<T>` return is a borrow, and `owned OwnedArray<T>` stacked one word across two meanings. The rename gives each axis its own word: **`owned` is the container qualifier; `Owning` is the element prefix.** `owned OwningArray<Dog>` reads correctly; a bare `OwningArray<Dog>` no longer *sounds* owned.

Folded in (per the v0.165 #1359 deferral): the owning **interfaces** gain the standard `I` prefix — `IOwningEnumerable`/`IOwningEnumerator` (and the async pair), `IOwningList`/`IOwningCollection` — since they were the stdlib's only unprefixed interfaces; `GetOwnedEnumerator()` follows as `GetOwningEnumerator()`. Method names that describe an owned **argument** — `AppendOwned`, `FromOwnedResult` — keep `Owned`: there the word correctly names the qualifier axis (the value being passed is owned), not the element axis. *(v0.250: with §22.6's ordinary-parameter overload ban lifted, argument-side borrowing/owning duals are same-name overload pairs and this suffix convention retires where a pair is declarable — §3.10's lift record, stdlib §1; `FromOwnedResult` stands, its would-be pair failing the declaration guard, and return-side `Owned` names were never this convention's subject.)* The inline-array slot marker is deliberately **not** touched (a separate decision, recorded here as deferred, not declined). Historical changelog entries ([`Bezel_Changelog.md`](Bezel_Changelog.md)) retain the names as they were at each revision.

<a id="sec-call-site-transfer-marker"></a>
### 3.10 Ownership transfer is marked at ordinary call sites

Adopted v0.169 (#1348 — an adversarially-verified review finding, not a rejected-then-revived design; no prior recorded decision had weighed a call-site marker). The gap: `ref` and `out` require call-site keywords, `move` spells a field take-out at the read site (§5.10), yet passing a named owned binding to an `owned` parameter — the mode that leaves the argument *moved-from* — was invisible: `M(value)`. The spec paid for the invisibility twice: §22.6's owned-overload ban and the stdlib's dual naming (`Append`/`AppendOwned`) both exist because the call site couldn't distinguish the forms.

The marker reused grammar the language already reserved: Appendix A.7's `ArgModifiers` carried an orphaned bare-`owned` alternative with no prose semantics — v0.169 defined it rather than inventing syntax. `M(owned x)` was required exactly when a **named owned binding** is consumed (an owned local or `owned` parameter); **fresh producers** (`new`, owned-returning calls, `move obj._f`) stay unmarked because no live binding is invalidated — the marker spells invalidation, not the parameter's mode. `owned` was preferred over a `move` spelling: it filled the reserved slot, mirrored `out owned var x`, and avoided relaxing MoveExpr's deliberate field-only operand restriction (§5.10). The §22.6 overload ban deliberately stood — its own carve-out criterion became satisfiable, but lifting it drags stdlib renames and resolution rules along; that was recorded as a follow-on, adopted separately or not at all.

**Reversed in token, kept in principle, at v0.246 (proposal 0090, [#3056](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3056); owner-ruled 2026-08-11).** The *marking* survives exactly as this section argued — a named-binding consume is spelled at the site — but the verb is now `move` at every named location: `M(move x)`, `var y := move x;`, `case move x of`, arm-result `move x`, alongside the pre-existing `move x.f`/`move coll[i]`; `owned` is purely declarative (parameters, fields, returns). The new evidence that licensed reopening a recorded decision is the reading-model cost the v0.169 record never weighed: one token carried a *state* meaning at declarations and an *action* meaning at use sites — `TakeOwnership(owned resource)` was a callee describing its parameter's obligation and, character for character, a caller performing an invalidation. The v0.169 grounds are each answered in the study (0090 §2–§3): the reserved `ArgModifiers` slot is deleted rather than filled — the marker is part of the argument expression; the coherent mirror of `out owned var x` is `owned p: T` (declaration ↔ declaration), not an action wearing the same token; and relaxing MoveExpr's operand restriction is the point, not a cost — §5.10's three reasons for an explicit take-out verb are reasons at every take-out site, none field-specific. The flip is spelling-only: every kernel rule reads unchanged with the marker re-pointed (spec §28.3, §28.8.1). **Follow-on reopened at v0.246, queued for owner ruling (question-only):** the §22.6 owned-overload ban and the stdlib's Try-pair dual naming — this record itself conceded the call-site marker satisfies §22.6's carve-out criterion, and the unified marker sharpens the premise (`M(move x)` vs `M(x)` is visibly distinct at every call site, with no declaration-token ambiguity left); whether to lift the ban is recorded as an open question in the v0.246 changelog, not adopted here.

**The follow-on resolved: the ordinary-parameter half of the ban is lifted at v0.250 (proposal 0091, [#3086](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3086); resolved 2026-08-12 under the owner's delegation — "Please work to resolve all questions the right way as you see fit. Once resolved, graduate both proposals into the docs" — all questions as recommended).** The lift is the ownership-mode applicability rule (spec §22.6, v0.250): a pair differing only by ordinary `owned` is declarable under a declaration guard, and at a pair position the argument's ownership classification completes *applicability* — an owned production reaches the `owned` member, a borrow the plain one, and the rest — `null` and the other context-typed productions, unclassified (K1.6) arguments, bottom-typed `throw` arguments — fall to the ordinary ambiguity rule with remedy-naming diagnostics. No ranking change: the marker participates in applicability, never in the better-than relation, so every selection is derivable from the call site's spelling — the v0.169 harm this record opens with (the caller needing the resolution outcome to write a correct transfer) inverts into the rule's soundness argument: the resolution outcome is now a function of what the caller wrote, K3.13's no-silent-consume rule and the §22.3 marker making the marked/unmarked split total before any candidate is consulted. The one genuinely new semantic decision is fresh-producer routing to the `owned` candidate — the alternative reading (a call-bounded borrow of the temporary) hands a storing callee a reference dying at the statement boundary, the very shape the §15.4 lint warns on.

The guard is an **erasure test**, not a constraint whitelist: a pair is declarable exactly when every differing position's declared type is a class, an interface, or a type parameter whose constraints exclude every substitution at which §28.1 erases the marker (and the move-only-value ones where it is admitted-but-redundant), decided by §14.2's conformance computation at the declaration. The formulation choice is deliberate — the test is semantic, decided by the same conformance computation sema already runs for constraint checking, so it can never disagree with the §28.1 erasure rule it exists to serve; a syntactic whitelist of qualifying bounds would be simpler to state but rejects qualifying declarations outside the list and adds a second, drift-prone definition of the erasing set. One arm is narrower than the computation alone would suggest, and deliberately: for an **interface bound**, qualification cannot be read off the conformers visible at the declaration — conformance to an ordinary user-declared interface is *open-world* (a record, enum, or choice in any downstream module may list it, §14.2 rules 2/4/7, under §2.8 separate compilation), so a declaration-site scan proves nothing about the substitutions a later module can add, and admitting such a pair would let it collapse retroactively with no error at either declaration. An interface bound therefore qualifies exactly when its conforming set excludes the erasing substitutions **as a matter of language rule** — today the `IDisposable` family, closed by §8.7 and inherited by every extending interface — not merely as a fact about the types currently visible. This geometry is also why `where T: class` fails the guard where `where T: IDisposable` passes — and why the one genuinely ban-*forced* stdlib pair, `FromResult`/`FromOwnedResult`, does **not** merge: the natural merged member needs `where T: class` (erasure collapse) or `where T: IDisposable` (a domain regression — `OwningTask<T>` admits every interface type where that constraint admits only the `IDisposable`-extending ones), and no §14.2 constraint spells the owning-generic domain (§14.4 records its own `where T: class` as "a slight imprecision" patched by compiler enforcement at the instantiation). The merge of that pair was investigated and withdrawn: the erasure geometry, not the ban's letter, keeps it apart, and it survives the ban's lifting — recorded honestly, with a liftable should §14.2's constraint vocabulary ever grow a form spelling that domain. Two further formulation rulings carry their reasons: the differ-only-by-`owned` predicate is measured on **substituted** signatures, after per-candidate inference — §22.6 already ranks substituted signatures, and the declared-signature alternative would give generic pairs observably different resolution than their substituted shapes; and a **no-classification** argument at a pair (a boxed value, a `string` under a reference conversion) is an ambiguity, not route-to-plain — uniformity with the null clause: route-to-plain is semantically safe (nothing to transfer) but would make one argument kind resolve silently where every other kind resolves by spelling, a divergence with no reader-visible reason.

The stdlib program landed as the merge, not admit-but-don't-rename: the convention-bred duals `WhenAllOwned`/`WhenEachOwned` — type-distinguished pairs that were declarable even under the ban, the ban's *culture* rather than its letter — become `WhenAll`/`WhenEach` overloads, the old names out of the stdlib in the same revision, no alias (the 0090 Q3 precedent; zero users price the rename at its lifetime minimum). Admit-but-don't-rename was declined as an end state because the tax the ban levied *was* the shipped vocabulary — the lift without the merge leaves the tax collected while removing the rule that justified it. Return-side `Owned` names (`Json.ParseOwned<T>`) stay: the return position carries no call-site marker and no overloading-by-return exists, so there the suffix is the §28.3 type-announcement discipline doing its job. The `out owned` and `scoped` halves of the ban **stand**, re-grounded (spec §22.6): the old "no resolution-by-call-site-form rule" sentence could not survive the lift — the lift is precisely such a rule for ordinary `owned` — so the `scoped` rejection now rests on the markerless function-typed operand kind (lifting only the marked class-typed face would split one modifier), and the `out owned` rejection keeps its recorded binding-form costs plus the corpus observation that every shipped `out` pair is operation-distinct (`TryGetValue` leaves where `TryPopValue` removes): merging them would key a mutation of the receiver to a binding form. Compiler catch-up is [#3086](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3086), sequenced after [#3056](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3056)'s marker respell — resolution keys on the very marker the corpus is still adopting.

**The redundant-marker asymmetry: examined and declined (v0.260, proposal 0102 §2, ruled under the owner's 2026-08-18 delegation).** The declaration side tolerates a redundant `owned` — spec §28.1 admits the marker on a move-only value type as admitted-but-redundant, and stdlib §6.8's `Domain.Spawn(...): owned Task` spells it on an announced return as documentation with emphasis — while the use side does not: `return move x;` under an `owned` return, `throw move x`, `_ := move x;`, and a marked store into an owning slot are all errors at their marker-free positions. The symmetry option — admit the use-site redundancy so that "every consume says `move`" — was put and is **declined**, on three grounds. First, it is an owner-ruled precedent of this same programme: proposal 0090's Q2 put exactly this choice and the owner ruled rejected-as-redundant at v0.246, the changelog recording the ruling's cost being paid in the same breath (three corpus lines of owned-slot stores, newly rejected, migration drop-the-marker); a ruling enforced at that price four revisions prior is not reopened by a symmetry argument carrying no new evidence. Second, the two sides are different things, and the line is nameable: declaration-side `owned` on a move-only type or a `Task`-family return is **type-level documentation** — written once, read by every signature reader, true of the slot — where use-site marker redundancy is **per-expression spelling variance** — written per call, introducing two spellings for one site, the both-ways surface v0.246's 633-line migration existed to eliminate; the v0.246 reading model is precisely that `owned` means *state* at declarations and `move` means *action* at sites. Third, the grep argument fails on its own terms: "every consume says `move`" is unachievable anyway — spec §28.3's marker-free positions consume without a marker by design — and admitting the marker at *some* of them makes the grep less reliable, not more. Not foreclosed: the reverse tightening (rejecting the redundant declaration-side `owned`) is a separate question with its own costs — §28.1's per-instantiation erasure makes the redundancy substitution-dependent, and stdlib §6.8's explicit spellings are load-bearing documentation — and is not raised here.

**The last use-site `owned` retired (v0.289, proposal 0122 L2).** `out owned var x` was the one use-site `owned` v0.246 left standing, on the ground that it was a declaration position. It was also the one place the language still demanded a marker for a fresh producer: the v0.270 adoption clause (spec §28.3, K1.7 + K3.11) lets `var x := Factory();` adopt owned from the callee's declared `owned` return with no use-site spelling, and an `out owned` result is the same situation — a fresh local, a callee-declared owned production. `out var x` now adopts what the resolved parameter announces, and the pre-declared `out x` form takes the same answer in an adoptable state. Alternatives weighed: keeping the spelling as a visual cue (declined — `var x := Factory()` lacks the cue already, and the rule contradicted the habit the code-generation reference teaches beside it); spelling it `out move var x` (declined — `move` marks the invalidation of a live binding, and nothing is invalidated). `out using var x` survives for §5.27's reason: the scope-bound binding is a declared constraint, not an inference. Cost accepted: a mechanical rewrite across the test corpus and a classification re-key in the compiler.

<a id="sec-uniform-end-closer"></a>
### 3.11 One uniform end-closer rule

Adopted v0.170 (#1374; one of #1343's recorded seams). The closer discipline was three rules: named declarations took an optional *name* label (`end Main;`), constructor/destructor/operator bodies an optional *keyword* label (`end constructor;`), and every statement-level block — plus extension and extern, the longest un-labelable bodies in the language — **forbade** any label, so the Ada-style keyword-labeled closers (`end if;`, `end for;` — the closer scheme of the very heritage §3 cites, though Ada itself spells every loop's closer `end loop;`) were an error. v0.170 collapses the three rules into one: `end` may repeat the construct's name (if it has one) or its introducing keyword (otherwise), checked when present. Bare `end;` stays legal everywhere, so the change is fully additive — zero migration — and long blocks gain the compiler-checked readability the declaration closers always had. The alternative of *requiring* labels on long blocks was declined: label ergonomics are a style choice, and the compiler-checked optional form gives tooling and reviewers the same benefit without ceremony.

<a id="sec-the-binding-atom-names-a-subexpression"></a>
### 3.12 The binding atom names a subexpression (`(var x := E; body)`)

Adopted v0.216 (proposal 0061 §1; owner-deferred 2026-07-21, activated on the owner's direction 2026-07-22). The atom exists for one job: naming a subresult where only an expression is admitted, so it need not be recomputed. The motivating position is the contract clause (spec §22.16) — clauses are expression-only and pure, so a clause using a subresult twice previously had to evaluate it twice: `ensures(var n := result.Count; n >= 0 and n <= _capacity)` names it once, harmlessly. Expression-position `case` arm bodies are the second beneficiary; helper extraction remains the doctrine for multi-*statement* logic.

The form passed the atom admission test proposal 0061 records — the test future paren-atom candidates must also meet: (1) it produces a value whose computation is ceremony-heavy in expression position today; (2) it rides existing kernel machinery — clause typing is spec §21.1's, ownership is K2/K3.4 verbatim plus one boundary clause (K8.5: an owned initializer is a *named unbound temporary* bounded by the atom — tighter than the enclosing full expression, escape rejected-never-extended per K8.4); (3) it makes no doctrine-declined path more ergonomic. The same review declined the try-expression and comprehensions under tests (3) and (1) — `DECLINED.md`, 2026-07-21.

Design choices worth recording: the spelling reuses `(` + `var` (a unique two-token commit, like `(if`/`(case`) and the statement declaration's own clause syntax — no new keyword, no new token; `;` separates because no expression contains a top-level `;`, where `in` collides with membership and `=>` with lambdas. The binding is **single-assignment by construction** — assignment is a statement and the atom admits none — and is additionally not an assignable location (the runtime-`const` rule, spec §21.1), which closes the `do`-bodied-lambda capture hole. The body is deliberately *not* a K3.4 marked-transfer position (an atom cannot launder its own binding into an owned result); lifting that, and admitting all-constant atoms into §17.14 constancy, are recorded liftable bounds. **It does not reopen §5.13's block-expression decline**: that decline is about statement *sequencing* in value position — effects, `do … end`'s single meaning, the trailing-expression/`yield`/`give` designs — while this atom admits no statements at all; a clause is a pure-shape binding, the body one expression. The distinction the two rules jointly draw: naming intermediate *values* in an expression is cheap and now admitted; interleaving *effects* into an expression remains declined.

<a id="sec-lambdas-are-keyword-led"></a>
### 3.13 Lambdas are keyword-led (`lambda(params): T => body`)

Adopted v0.217 (proposal 0064; owner-directed 2026-07-22). Through v0.216 a lambda was spelled `(params) => body` — the last major expression form with no committing first token: the reading was decided only at the `=>` after the matching `)`, which cost the parser its one unbounded lookahead, required a dedicated carve so a `when`-guard `(t > 0) => …` was not mis-scanned as a lambda (#1256), and gave the reader the same late signal in argument-position paren soup. The keyword fixes all three at once — `lambda` begins no other expression or statement, so the form commits at its first token, stronger than even the v0.208 paren atoms' second-token commits — and it opens the one slot the old spelling structurally could not host: a **return-type ascription** (`): T =>` after a bare parenthesized form collides with dictionary-literal key position), which resolves §27.3's divergent-return case at the site instead of at the enclosing variable. That capability is what distinguishes this landing from the spelling-only churn `DECLINED.md` D-0003 warns against: the revision retires the lookahead machinery *and* closes a recorded expressiveness gap, rather than re-spelling for taste.

The keyword is `lambda` because it is already the language's name for the construct — §27's title, the grammar's `LambdaExpr`, the diagnostics — and the term-of-art objection is answered by `async`/`await`/`yield`, which are equally terms of art and equally reserved. `function` was the runner-up (matches "function types" but is heaviest at the hottest sites and implies a free-function category the language lacks); `func`/`fn` would be the keyword set's first abbreviations; `method` is free but answers "method of what?" with "nothing". The word is unconditionally reserved, not contextual — reservation was free (the identifier appears nowhere in the corpus), and contextual status would reopen the `Identifier`-lookalike seams the keyword exists to close. The cutover is **hard** — the old spelling left the grammar in the same revision, the 0057-reversal precedent — because with no external users a deprecation window serves nobody and defers the entire parser payoff. The broader survey (eight syntax families, the verified colon-fatality of the literal Python form, the declined placeholder/brace/`end`-delimited shapes) is recorded in proposal 0064, whose §9 verification record backs every grammar claim here.

<a id="sec-named-destructure-patterns-equals-spelling"></a>
### 3.14 Named-destructure patterns are spelled with `=`

Adopted v0.224 (proposal 0065; owner-directed 2026-07-24). Spec §19.3 adds the named-destructure form `T(Prop = Pattern, …)` — named slots inside the existing destructure parentheses — completing the pattern grammar the way §3.8's per-slot alternatives completed it: same parentheses, no new token, one new list shape inside them. The motivating gap is real. Positional destructuring is deliberately confined to records and choice cases (§5.17 declines class *deconstruction* outright, because extraction and disposal are opposite operations on the same slots), yet a class's public readable properties *are* its encapsulation-respecting match surface, and reading them transfers nothing. Without the named form, matching on them forces bind-then-access with the property names stranded in a `when` guard, away from the pattern they interrogate; the named form puts the interrogation in the pattern.

**Why `=`, not `:=` or braces.** A pattern's data flow is the reverse of construction: a call site *puts a value in* by name with `:=` (`new Rect(Width := 3)`, §22.5), while a pattern *asks whether a value is present* — `Rect(Width = 3)` reads literally as "is `Width` 3?", which is exactly what `=` means (spec §8.4). The `:=`/`=` asymmetry between constructing and interrogating is the load-bearing distinction, not a break in symmetry, and the ML family (F# `{ X = x }`, OCaml record patterns) already spells record patterns with `=`. The collision analysis is clean: `=` is not a pattern relational operator, and the only expression positions embedded in a slot pattern parse below equality's precedence, so a slot's `=` can never be absorbed by a pattern-interior expression. The **brace form** `Rect { Width: var w }` (the original C#-style sketch) was declined under §5.16's recorded no-`{}` posture — `{}` is confined to string interpolation and never becomes a fourth bracket kind — and would also open a second destructure surface where the parentheses already suffice. The **`:=` spelling** was declined because assignment reads false in interrogation position.

**The v1 restrictions are the smallest sound cut.** A slot list is positional or named, never mixed (a structural rule, not a semantic check); bindings are borrows spelled `var w` (a naked identifier after `=` is a constant-or-type reference, never a silent binding — the one place the positional naked-binding shorthand would invert the reading); a constant slot is the `=` equality test; and exhaustiveness counts a named-destructure arm exactly where the corresponding positional or type-pattern arm would count, if and only if every slot is irrefutable — one level deep, no per-property value-space analysis. Each restriction is a compatible widening if evidence arrives (positional/named mixing, computed record properties, interface-typed heads, lambda-parameter named destructuring); the borrow-only, no-transfer-through-named-slots reading is the conservative one that keeps the feature free of any new ownership rule. The full fork record — the declined braces and `:=`, the naked-identifier decision, and the open questions left for later widening — is proposal 0065's.

*(v0.246 note — follow-on reopened, queued for owner ruling, question-only: the no-transfer-through-named-slots restriction. Proposal 0065's own record marks a transfer-through-named-slots form "liftable later" (its Q2), and the v0.246 marker unification (§3.10's reversal record; proposal 0090, whose inventory carries this as gap G5) changes the premise: named slots now have a natural transfer spelling — the unified `move` verb — where at v0.224 a marked named slot would have deepened the very `owned` conflation v0.246 removed. Whether to lift is recorded as an open question in the v0.246 changelog; nothing is designed or adopted here.)*

**The note resolved: the marked named slot lands at v0.250 (proposal 0092, [#3087](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3087); resolved 2026-08-12 under the owner's delegation — "Please work to resolve all questions the right way as you see fit. Once resolved, graduate both proposals into the docs" — all questions as recommended).** A named slot may carry the take-out verb on its **source** — `Fetched(move response = var r)` — admitted exactly in an owning match, on an `owned`-marked payload field (v1), with a binding RHS (spec §19.3/§35.5, v0.250). Unmarked named slots keep the borrow reading of this section verbatim; the marked payload transfers at arm selection, per slot, riding the positional form's machinery — no new kernel rule, no axiom touched: the delta is one admission (a second slot spelling routing a payload the construct already owns) plus its rejection set, and K3's consume enumeration gains no row.

The adoption weighed a close rival, recorded honestly. **Unmarked symmetry** — named slots transferring in owning matches exactly as positional slots do, no new syntax — was the strongest alternative, and the decline rests on the **per-slot peek alone**: unmarked symmetry deletes the named form's ability to borrow one payload while transferring another in the same list, the one capability no other form supplies; the marked spelling also keeps v0.246's one-verb explicitness discipline (`move` wherever a named location is emptied). The rival's flip of the landed named-borrow trace was acknowledged as definition-text-only — zero behavioral corpus, zero tests, zero guide examples at v0.249 — so the preference for the pure widening counted as *principle*, not as avoided breakage; the comparison was closer than the first draft presented it, and is recorded so. The **receiver-side spellings** were declined on operand identity: `Field = move x` and `Field = move var x` make `move`'s operand a fresh name — the one place the verb's operand would not be the location being emptied (and the first collides with 0065's naked-identifier doctrine) — and `Field = owned var x` reintroduces `owned` at a site performing an action, the exact conflation v0.246 removed. The marker sits on the source because a payload field is a field: `move response = var r` is the pattern-position analog of the field take-out `move x.f`.

The v1 confinement: `owned`-marked payloads only, RHS fixed to `var x`, with the liftables recorded — typed binders (`var x: T`), nested move-only-choice routing, and **move-only value payloads**, whose owning-match transfer semantics are undefined at v0.249 even for *positional* slots (§35.5 transfers `owned` payloads and copies value payloads; a move-only value payload can do neither) — a genuine spec gap, filed as its own defect issue [#3094](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3094) and named by the marked slot's diagnostic rather than resolved as a side effect of this lift. `move N = _` stays inadmissible, and harder than a style call: the `_` RHS is already excluded by the binding-RHS condition (`_` is no binding), and admitting it as an explicit dispose-here marker would create a **new consuming site** — a row K3's enumeration does not have and an extension of K4.2's closed site-announced kill set — kernel cost for an effect (dispose at construct completion) the plain discard already delivers; an explicit dispose-here spelling, if ever wanted, arrives as its own kernel-classified proposal. Multiple marked slots in one list compose per-slot: the admission conditions and transfer-at-selection are stated per slot, the positional form already transfers every obligation-bearing slot at once, and a single-slot cap would have needed a counting rule invented solely to reject a composable case. The marker on a value payload is an error (nothing to transfer — the §22.3 marker-on-borrow error shape), and the borrowing-match rejection's remedy names both routes (`case move x of`, or match the temporary). Implementation rides [#1409](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/1409) (the named-destructure implementation itself) behind [#3056](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3056); tracking [#3087](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3087).

**The gap resolved: move-only value payloads transfer like `owned` at v0.251 (spec defect [#3094](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3094); owner-directed 2026-08-12 — "propose the resolution and resolve 3094").** Two interpretations were on the table: **(1)** a move-only value payload *transfers* into its binding at arm selection in an owning match, exactly as an `owned` payload does, and *borrows* in a borrowing match under §19.3's move-only scrutinee rule applied at the slot; or **(2)** the payload borrows everywhere, extraction staying inexpressible. Interpretation 1 is adopted, completed on both match forms and through nested destructuring (spec §35.5/§19.3/§15.3, v0.251), on four grounds. *(a)* K3.12 already gives a move-only value unmarked transfer at every other flow position — assignment, parameter passing, return, owning stores; interpretation 2 would have made the owning match the one place a move-only value cannot go, in exactly the construct whose job is consumption. *(b)* The disposal half of the rule was already defined — §35.4's recursive active-case disposal covers a discarded or unlisted move-only slot with no new text — so interpretation 1 completes an existing rule rather than adding one. *(c)* No new kernel row: the transfer is routing within the one consume the owning match already performs (K8.3 for the temporary trigger, K3.4 for the marked scrutinee), precisely the account v0.250 gave the marked named slot; K3's enumeration gains nothing and no axiom moves. *(d)* **v0.250 carried a latent contradiction that only the admission dissolves**, recorded here honestly as a residue of that train: §28.1 admits the `owned` marker as admitted-but-redundant on a move-only value field, so a redundantly-marked field (`Fetched(owned data: [4 owned]Connection?)`) satisfied the marked named slot's condition (ii) — an `owned`-marked payload field — while the rejection taxonomy's move-only arm rejected the same slot: two rules claiming one declaration. Interpretation 2 would have needed a second carve to keep them apart; interpretation 1 makes the marked and unmarked field spellings behave identically, which is §28.1's stance verbatim. The ruling executes 0092 Q2's recorded liftable at exactly its recorded bar — its own defect issue, its own ruling, not a side effect of the v0.250 lift — and the rejection arm that named the liftable retires with it. The nested case follows the same geometry: a move-only *choice* payload (the one nested move-only shape with a pattern surface) routes recursively in an owning match — through the slots that transfer, a positional slot and any pattern nested within one; a nested pattern beneath an unmarked named slot routes nothing at any depth, and a marked named slot cannot carry one (condition (iii)) — inner bindings receiving their payloads at selection, the un-bound remainder at every level disposing at construct completion. §35.5's "nested destructuring of an owned payload does not arise" claim is corrected rather than narrowed: for a class-typed owned payload it arises in the *named* form (§19.3 has admitted named destructuring with a class head since v0.224; interface heads stay barred in v1) and routes nothing — each nested named slot a getter read, a borrow minting no obligation (§28.6), the payload never transferred, disposed at construct completion exactly as a discarded slot. (One clause of the v0.250 record above also reads narrower from this revision: "the marker on a value payload is an error" holds for *copyable* value payloads — a move-only value payload carries an obligation and is admitted under condition (ii).)

**The adjacent gate findings resolved: borrow-lifetime obligations re-key from the classification label to the **holds-borrows** property at v0.252 (renamed to that spelling at v0.257; the changelog carries the mapping) (spec defects [#3110](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3110)/[#3111](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3111); owner-directed 2026-08-12 — "resolve and push both 3110 and 3111 resolutions", one train).** The v0.251 gate's out-of-scope observation, filed as #3110: §35.4's classification cascade stops at the first matching label, so an owning choice carrying a plain class payload beside its owned one — `Loaded(owned response: Response, log: Logger)` — escaped every §9.7 borrow-lifetime rule, the borrow-lifetime paragraph being scoped to "borrow-bearing choices", the rule-2 label only. The resolution is a **scoping correction, not a missing rule**: §9.7's obligations were always about *carried borrows*, the choice text inherited them through a cascade that stops at the first matching label, and the re-key restores the rule to its own ground. The named condition — a choice **holds borrows** iff any case has a borrow-carrying payload field under exactly §9.7's definition, transitively — at v0.252 stated as rule 2's condition freed of its "otherwise"; §35.4 was restated the other way round at v0.257, defining the property first and letting rule 2 read it (the changelog carries the mapping) — is orthogonal to the classification, and every §9.7-derived constraint now keys on it (spec §35.4/§9.7/§30.1/§28.3, K5.5's contribution sentence, v0.252); the classification is untouched and keeps driving copy/move/dispose semantics — the property adds obligations, never subtracts semantics. **The declined alternative**: a fourth classification, "owning-and-borrow-bearing" — it doubles the label vocabulary for one orthogonal bit and forces every classification-keyed rule (boxing, sendability, equality, matching, the compiler-provided combinators' gates) to enumerate a fourth arm; holds-borrows spells the same fact as a second property read at exactly the sites that need it. **Two compositions are stated rather than left to inference.** Class-field placement narrows: §15.3's "a move-only value may be a field of a class" holds only for values holding no borrows, and the owning choice that holds borrows is the *only* move-only kind the condition excludes — an owning inline array's slots are `owned`, so it never holds borrows — since storing one would let the borrows outlive their sources. And §35.12's transfer-closure admission gains the borrow-free conjunct beside #3111's payload-kind-generic completion, landed as one coherent condition — made **total over payload kinds** by the same train's gate round, which caught the ruling's obligation-bearing quantification under-quantifying (a function-typed payload, never sendable, would have crossed unchecked inside an owning choice holding no borrows): no carried borrows *and* every payload field of every case transfer-compatible — an obligation-bearing payload recursively (an `owned` class payload by its class's sendability, an `owned` interface payload never, an owning-inline-array payload by its element class's — an interface element type never — a nested owning-choice payload recursively, the same reach as §35.4's recursive active-case disposal), every other payload by its own type's sendability, a function-typed payload disqualifying exactly as a function-typed field does — conformed identically at §30.14 rule 2's carrier, §35.12's home, and Guide 4 §5. #3111's other enumeration silence — §15.3's field-placement bullet never naming the choice-payload position that v0.251 gave full binding semantics — closes with one sentence and the §35.4/§35.1/§35.5 cites. Kernel accounting: no new K row, no axiom — K5.5's clause-5 tracing sentence reads the property, K2's source-liveness reasoning is unchanged, and the freeze and liveness machinery is §28.3's verbatim. **v0.327 addendum ([#5603](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5603)):** the property's domain is every value kind. On a record or an inline array it coincides with **borrow-bearing** (a record has no owning classification to part the two, §15.3 and §9.7's first paragraph; an owning inline array never holds borrows, §15.1.1), and on a choice it is what it was; so *a value type that holds borrows* is the one name for the union — borrow-bearing record, borrow-bearing inline array, choice that holds borrows — that the borrow-lifetime, async, static-field, consume-site and bare-type-parameter rules all reach. Proposal 0136 S2-16 had accepted **borrow-carrying value** for the union; that name is declined, since §9.7 uses *borrow-carrying* for the field-level predicate and §28.3 for closures, frames and class-typed values, four live sites the narrow sense would contradict. Leaving the union unnamed, the other option, kept nine phrasings of one condition in circulation. `holds-borrows` is a name the spec already applies to records and inline arrays at §22.12's verdict table, it is the name proposal 0095 §3.3 chose against the `borrow-<participle>` collision, and widening its domain changes no verdict: no rule keyed on the property being choice-only.

<a id="sec-the-unit-type"></a>
### 3.15 The unit type `()`, and where the no-value boundary sits (v0.283)

Bezel has no tuples, so `()` is not a degenerate tuple — it is a freestanding built-in whose one job is to make *returns nothing* a first-class result and instantiation like any other. That buys three things, each with a live client: a **single function-type grammar** — `(T) -> U` covers every result, so higher-order code never bifurcates (`method Retry<T>(op: () -> T): T` accepts a no-result operation at `T = ()` with no `Retry` twin, and the same holds for every memoize/backoff/`Locked.With`-shaped combinator to come; C#'s `Action`/`Func` split is the counterexample the design avoids — structural function types escape the *naming* half of that split, and only a unit type escapes the *member* half); a **no-payload instantiation where the wrapper is thin** — `EventSource<()>` (stdlib §6.5; adopted deliberately, proposal 0035); and a **typed statement calculus** — §21.9's unused-result warning keys on non-`()` results, §27.3 classifies procedure lambdas by the `()` result, and `catch Exception => ()` (§31.2.5) names suppression as a value.

**The language is deliberately hybrid about "no value", and the boundary is a rule, not an accident — but the rule is frequency, not rule-difference.** §30.5's Pattern A/B split keys on the result's *kind* (class vs. copyable), not on arity: a `Task<()>` would differ from `Task` by nothing, since `Task<T>` at a copyable `T` already shares `Task`'s row. What justifies the dedicated type is pervasiveness: the no-result task is the *common* case — every `async` procedure produces one — so a mandatory `<()>` there would be ambient ceremony at the language's highest-traffic seam, while the no-payload event is the *minority* case of its family, where one honest token per declaration is cheap. So: **a no-value variant earns a dedicated type where the no-value case is pervasive enough that the instantiation token would be ambient noise; where it is the minority case, it is an instantiation at `()`** — and a family's satellites follow its head (`TaskCompletionSource` is `Task`'s completion face, §30.11's "same shape minus the `result` parameter", so it sits with `Task` by family symmetry, not by an independent ruling).

**Unit's near-invisibility is the design working, not an argument for removal.** §22.1's procedure form (no `:` clause) means declarations never spell it; there are no unit locals, fields, or payloads in idiomatic code; a programmer meets `()` only where it carries information — a function type's result, a thin instantiation, a suppression arm. Removing the type would not remove those places, only re-spell them worse. The removal direction and the boundary moves are declined with reasons and bars (`DECLINED.md`); the keyword-led function-type respelling — under which unit would become unwritable in function types — is **deferred with its design worked** (proposal 0120 §5: the colon-form parse is settled viable; the costs are majority-pessimization, no parser win, and a whole-surface migration), reopenable on the conditions the row states.

<a id="sec-function-value-calls-are-positional"></a>
### 3.16 A call through a function value takes no named argument (v0.295)

Spec §27.1 rejects the named form at an invocation through a function-typed value ([#4085](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/4085)), rather than binding it by position, because a name accepted and then ignored could spell one binding and perform another. Two admissions were weighed and declined. **Binding *by* name:** a function type's parameter names are not part of its identity (the named and unnamed forms are the same type), so an ordinary assignment — exact-match, or the identity-preserving retyping of spec §27.1.1 — may drop or relabel them, and a name-keyed binding would mean one thing through one view of a value and another through the next. **An order-checked *label*** (a written name admitted only where it equals the declared name at that position): it would make documentation-only names a compatibility contract on every call site that spelled them. Both are recorded in `DECLINED.md`.

<a id="sec-memory-management"></a>
## 4. Memory management

Bezel's ownership model is the part most likely to surprise readers. The high-level shape:

- Class instances are heap-allocated and must be disposed exactly once.
- Records, enums, and primitives need no disposal.
- A small set of types — `string`, `Type`, boxed value types, function values — are **runtime-managed** and exempt from manual disposal.

For the runtime-managed set — `string`, boxed values, function values, and `Type`, and only them — reclamation is the runtime's business rather than the program's. For the first three the mechanism is not specified: reference counting works, region allocation works, a small collector over them works; implementations choose. `Type` is the fixed-mechanism member: the runtime interns one instance per type, holds it for the life of the process, and reclaims none (spec [§28.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-runtime-managed-types)). Every class instance, trivial-dispose included, is instead freed by compiler-inserted code at its statically-known owner's release point — no runtime lifetime mechanism exists for user-defined classes at all ([§5.9](#sec-trivial-dispose-classes), [§5.28](#sec-silent-tracking); spec §28).

<a id="sec-why-strings-are-runtime-managed"></a>
### 4.1 Why strings are runtime-managed

Marking `string` as owned would mean `using var` on the result of `int.ToString()`, every interpolation, every concatenation. The result would be unreadable.

The alternative — value-type strings with copy-on-write — silently allocates on every concatenation and complicates interop with languages where `string` is a reference type.

The chosen path is to put `string` (and a small set of other deeply-pervasive types: boxed values, function values, and the interned `Type` tokens) in a runtime-managed region and leave manual ownership clean for the 95% of types where it actually matters. Manual ownership would be unworkable for strings; runtime management is unworkable for resources. Each gets the right tool.

<a id="sec-why-function-typed-values-are-runtime-managed"></a>
### 4.2 Why function-typed values are runtime-managed

A non-capturing lambda is just a function pointer; a capturing lambda owns its captured environment; a method reference (`obj.Method`) carries the receiver. These are heterogeneous enough that requiring callers to track ownership of every function-typed value would force `using var` on every callback.

The simple rule — function-typed values are runtime-managed — makes them as easy to use as any other value while keeping the ownership story for resource-bearing types intact.

**The closure as a borrow-carrying value: one rule, no closed list (v0.298, [#4154](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/4154); ruled by the owner on design merits, 2026-09-10, proposal 0126).** Runtime management settles the function value's *own* lifetime; the borrows it captures were always tracked separately (spec [§28.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-runtime-managed-types)), and through v0.297 spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules) admitted a capture-bearing closure over a borrowed local under a closed list of three forms — immediate invocation, a `using var` local of function type, an argument to a function-typed parameter. The list was an accident of wording. The bullet as first written (2026-05-28) admitted closures "immediately invoked or whose lifetime is statically bounded by the borrow's source", naming the `using var` local and the extension-method parameter as *instances* of that criterion; v0.177's rewrite for the call-bounded default enumerated the instances and dropped the criterion, §28.14 (v0.175) and §28.9 (v0.178) restated the enumeration, and no changelog or rationale entry records a reason for excluding the plain local — the v0.177 entry explains only why a *received* callback is bounded by the call — and spec §28.10 had said all along that for a runtime-managed right-hand side of a `using var` "the only effect is the reassignability restriction", contradicting the §28.9 sentence in the same document. v0.298 restores the criterion in the kernel's vocabulary: a function value's **source set** is the union of its captures' sources (a receiver-binding method reference's receiver included; nested closures transitively — the same transitive contribution [§5.25](#sec-deferred-activation-capture) computes for frames), and every §28.3 borrow rule applies to the value by that set, the former three forms becoming consequences. Four grounds. *Uniformity*: §28.3's local-scope bullet already governs every other borrow-holding local by structural scope comparison, and §28.9 already framed the closure as a borrow-carrier; a second, syntactic list for one holder kind was a redundant special case. *Soundness unchanged*: the storage and return sinks — a field, a collection, an `out`/`ref` parameter, the method's return — stay closed to every function value with a non-empty source set, on K2.4's ground (a function type announces no source set, so no holder beyond the body could re-establish it and no caller bound it — the ground §28.14 already states for a received callback), which is what keeps §28.3's capture-set contribution ("borrow-free by construction"), [§5.25](#sec-deferred-activation-capture), §28.14's recorded asymmetry and the v0.177 record true verbatim; the only storage route for a capture-bearing closure remains §28.14's `scoped` admission, and what the rule opens is the binding, invocation, transitive-capture and argument positions the scope comparison already decides. *Ergonomics*: a local helper lambda over a borrow needs no `using var` ceremony, and a plain function-typed local may be reassigned, each assignment checked on its own. *The implementation had the general rule all along*: the compiler's only plain-local check is the block-depth comparison the local-scope bullet describes, and its pinned diagnostic ("closure capturing `rr` is assigned to `f`, which outlives it") is a scope comparison, not a form check — the closed list was the outlier, not the compiler. **Declined:** keeping the closed list, with `using var` as the sole capture-scoping marker on a function-typed local; and widening it by a fourth form only, which would have kept a list whose reason nobody recorded (`DECLINED.md`, *Closure-capture admission as a closed syntactic list*). The `using var` keeps its non-reassignability role on function-typed locals and nothing else; [§5.27](#sec-why-using-var-is-not-folded-into-var)'s reasons for the keyword are untouched — they are about owned and pinned bindings, which a function value is not.

**Function values in properties and indexers (v0.314, [#5303](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5303)).** The closure bullet bars a capture-bearing function value from fields, and an auto-property's assignment is a store to its backing field, so the property spelling is the field store under another name (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules), *property and indexer stores*). The rule reads every property or indexer assignment of a function-typed value as a store, whatever the accessor form, and gives a block-form setter's `value` the empty set. Three alternatives were declined. *Admitting property-backed storage under an ownership or release rule*, such as a `scoped` or `owned` property or a release obligation on an auto-property's backing field, would give properties a storage tier that fields lack. A property declares neither modifier, a plain field admits only a capture-free function value, and the one tier that stores a capture-bearing callback is the `scoped` field behind a `scoped` parameter, whose bounding-object admission needs a call site that a setter does not have. *Reading the assignment by accessor form*, with an auto-property's assignment as a field store and a block-form setter's `value` as a call-bounded parameter, fails wherever the property is reached through an interface or a `virtual` member. The site cannot see which form the dispatch reaches, and an auto-property implements an interface property (spec §11.2). *Keeping the accessor-form split and barring a function-typed auto-property from implementing or overriding an interface, `virtual` or `abstract` property* closes that seam at the declaration, but it leaves a block-form setter unable to store its `value` at all, so a settable function-typed property reached through an interface could hold nothing. The cost of the adopted rule is narrow: a caller can no longer hand a capture-bearing value to a block-form setter that only invokes or forwards it. The rule's other face is the read (v0.325, [#5602](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5602)): a function value read from a property or indexer carries the empty set, as one read from a field or returned by a method call does, because every value a getter can yield reached it through a store this rule admits only capture-free or through the getter's own return slot, a function-typed holder. Reading such a value as an unannounced source was declined: a callback stored through a property could then never be stored again, and an auto-property would read differently from the field its assignment is a store to.

**Function values in record components and primary-constructor captures (v0.316, [#5377](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5377), [#5378](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5378)).** A record's function-typed component is a field, so the closure bullet's rule for fields reaches it. The record's generated constructor has no body to check and the component is declared, so construction and `with` are judged at the site as a property assignment is (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules), *record components of function type*), and the generated parameter carries the empty set. *Classifying the record as borrow-bearing while it carries a capture-bearing function value* was declined: spec §9.7 classifies by declared field types, and a classification that follows the values a record holds is what spec §3.6 rule 1 forbids. A class's primary constructor differs in one respect: whether a parameter becomes a field depends on whether a body references it (spec §22.10).
