Its plain function-typed parameter is therefore call-bounded as every other plain function-typed parameter is, and the body reference that captures it is the rejected store. *Reading the construction site as the store, as for a record,* was declined: the caller's verdict would depend on whether some body of the class references the parameter, which the caller cannot see and which changes when a method is added or removed.

**Function values in choice payloads and inline-array slots (v0.317, [#5486](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5486)).** A choice's function-typed payload and a slot of an inline array of function type hold a function value exactly as a record component does, and spec §35.4 and §3.6 leave both holders classification-neutral, so the record ruling applies unchanged: the payload argument at a case construction and every store into such a slot, a local array's included (v0.320), are judged at the site, and only a capture-free value is admitted (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules), *choice payloads and inline-array slots of function type*). *Classifying the choice or array by what it holds* is declined on the record ground, spec §3.6 rule 1. A local inline array's slots are fields too (v0.320, *Function values in local collections* below).

**Function values at set-erasing positions (v0.320, [#5375](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5375); proposal [0133](proposals/0133-set-erasing-positions.md), owner-accepted 2026-10-08).** Two readings disagree about a function value at a `T` slot. A generic body is checked once under spec §28.1's class-or-interface assumption, where `_v := x` with `x: T` is a parameter-sourced borrow admitted under the field-store bullet's residual; the caller's closure bullet tracks the function value by its source set, which a plain `T` parameter cannot carry. A rule that names only sinks rejects on neither side, and `new Holder<() -> int>(f)` would store a closure over a scope-bound local in a field whose read "carries the empty set". `Object` erases the set the same way (spec §3.1: a function type reaches `Object` directly), and so does a function-typed parameter whose return slot is a bare `T`. The position that erases the set is the conversion to a type that is not a function type, so the rule names that conversion once, keyed on declared, pre-substitution types as every other classification in spec §28.3 is (the bare-type-parameter arm reading the instantiation's substitution: a function type, or — stated at v0.332, [#5708](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5708) — `Object`, the case the v0.320 letter had left to its own clause-5 reading) (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules), *set-erasing positions*): a capture-bearing value is rejected there, a capture-free one admitted. It is a derivation (K2.3 + K2.4), checked by the caller over the callee's declared signature, and the generic body is never re-checked. A `yield return` of a function value is judged as a `return` is, since an iterator's element type announces no set and a terminal accessor's result (row 5) would otherwise carry an erased one; and neither variance rule of spec §27.1.1 relates a function-typed slot to an `Object` slot, in either direction, so no retyping converts a function value's results or arguments to `Object` without a site. Two routes reach a `T` slot with no conversion at any caller, a method reference formed over the position and a `T` in a parameter slot of a parameter slot, and each is closed on an existing precedent: the method-reference formation bar of spec §28.14, and an instantiation exclusion shaped like the `Task`-family rule. The cost is a short list of non-storing calls that do not take a capture-bearing argument. `Contains`, `IndexOf` and `Remove` with such an argument would find nothing, since no collection can hold one once the routes are closed. `Console.Write`/`WriteLine(Object)`, `StringBuilder.Append(Object)` and `Equals(Object?)` reject it; `f = g` compares function values, and a description prints. `EventSource<T>.Raise` at a function-type payload rejects it; raise data and let handlers close over what they need. A selector `Pick<T>(a: T, b: T): T` rejects it, because its result would carry the erased set into a holder; the expression form `(if c then f else g)` keeps both sets, and a function-typed `Pick` returning a call-bounded parameter is the unbounded-tier limitation of spec §28.14 seen again. A received callback cannot be forwarded to a generic or `Object`-typed utility, since a plain function-typed parameter carries itself. Declined: *an instantiation exclusion for generic types with `T`-typed fields* (it rejects capture-free `Holder<() -> int>` uses, misses `Bag<T>` over `Array<T>`, and misses `Object`); *a declaration-site rejection of storing a `T` value in a generic body unless its constraints exclude function types* (the one constraint admitting every class and interface type, `where T: class`, admits function types, so every generic container would lose its own element type); *a K4.3 trust residual for function values* (it reopens proposal 0126 §2 on no new evidence and makes every function value read from storage carry an unannounced source); *a per-member exemption for `Raise`* (the first allowlist outside spec §28.14's register, which a user `IEvent<T>` could not share); *`ToString`-only stdlib overloads at function types* (they buy a rendering nobody uses at the price of a surface rule); *`scoped` on a bare-type-parameter parameter as the remedy* (its two tiers cannot share one field kind: a `scoped` field is never read out as a value, while a generic container's `T` field exists to be read); *row 5 announcing the union of its arguments' sets at a function-type substitution* (the same parameters may be stored, so the arguments must be capture-free and the announcement would announce the empty set); and *marking a lambda literal's erased parameter slots instead of excluding the nested-slot instantiation* (sound only with a new per-binding annotation that no forwarding position may drop). *A body-keyed exclusion from a per-body storing fact* is deferred with proposal 0132's fact, as its second dependent. *Exempting the return-slot arm where the member's own return is mint-announcing* (a lazy selector producing closures over a value-typed element, whose results live only in the frame) is sound and liftable, and deferred for one uniform rule.

**Function values in local collections (v0.320, [#5499](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5499), [#5486](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5486)).** Reading a purely local collection as a local binding for a call-bounded callback, while the closure bullet gives a function value read from any collection the empty set, would let `var xs: Array<() -> int> := []; xs.Add(f); _field := xs[0];` store a received callback in a field. A function value is therefore not stored in any collection while its set is non-empty, a local collection and a local inline array included, following the v0.316 local-record precedent (#5377). *Making a local collection or local inline array a carrier whose element reads carry its elements' union set* is declined: the collection is a value or a reference that copies into returns, fields, record components, plain arguments and `Object`, none of which announces a set, so each would have to become a sink and element reads a traced provenance; that is a design change with no measured want. Frames keep the exemption: a task or query minted by invoking a callback, or a frame carrying it, may sit in a purely local collection until it is consumed, which is the `Task` fan-out idiom (`pending.Add(cb()); await Task.WhenAll(pending);`). The cost is that a fixed table of received or capture-bearing callbacks is not kept in a collection even within one body; bind each to its own function-typed local.

**Function types over a type parameter: depth one, and the values a body hands out (v0.334, [proposal 0165](proposals/0165-function-types-over-a-type-parameter.md), [#5762](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5762), [#5763](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5763)).** Proposal 0133 closed a type parameter nested two function types deep with a per-instantiation exclusion stated over a parameter's declared type, and the `Task`-family exclusion read the same scope. The generics matrix found the same two hazards in a field, in a return slot of a function in a parameter slot, for the iterable family in the body-to-caller direction, and — at depth one — in a function value the body hands out at a `(T) -> …` return, field or `Object` position, which the caller invokes under the call-bounded default with a capture-bearing closure, and the mirror at a mint family, a `() -> T` return whose invocation the caller keys as a mint. v0.334 replaces the exclusion by a shape rule anyone can see — a type parameter occurs in a function type at depth one only, in a generic declaration's types and every type its bodies write, with zero measured sites — and closes the hand-outs by value: a function value a generic body forms or retypes with a type parameter in a parameter slot is bound, invoked, captured or passed, and never returned (from a lambda either), stored, converted to `Object` or to another generic's bare-`T` slot, or varied away as a type argument (one test program flips). Only such a value carries the hazard — every other value at a `(T) -> …` type was checked where it was formed, against the substituted type — so the declarations themselves stay free: a `(T) -> ()` callback field or return is admitted, and a declaration-site ban on them was dropped in review as redundant. A `() -> T` return, the thunk idiom with fifteen sites, stays and is excluded at the two mint families only. Declined: *a polarity rule over the member's whole declared type* (a per-position computation, and it leaves the untyped routes open, which are in the body and not the signature); *a per-instantiation exclusion for an exported `(T) -> …` slot* (the mirror of 0133 at one slot: a mechanism where the value rule serves, and the untyped routes stay open); *a declaration-site ban on a `T` parameter slot in an exported function type* (redundant with the value rule; it would reject callback fields and three test programs); *reading the hand-out as a source in the value's set* (a frame built over a selector with a `T` parameter would carry it, and no generic wrapper could return `xs.Select(lambda(t: T) => …)`); *keeping the nested-slot exclusion beside the depth bound* (redundant). 0133's declines (an exclusion for `T`-typed fields, a declaration-site store ban on bare-`T` values, a trust residual) stand.

**The keying follows the declaration through dispatch, containers and `scoped` fields; a body's bare-`T` lend stays out of `Object` (v0.335, [proposal 0172](proposals/0172-function-slot-keying-through-implementations.md), [#5836](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5836), [#5798](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5798)).** Proposal 0165 admitted a body-formed `(T) -> …` value into a function-typed parameter and into a `scoped` field, and carved a plain parameter's declared type out of *Exported mint slots*, on one assumption: whoever invokes the value reads its pre-substitution slot. The matrix re-run found three invokers that do not — a concrete implementation of a generic interface member (`Feeder: ISink<() -> int>` reads `f: (() -> int) -> ()` and feeds it a closure; `Drainer: ISink<IEnumerable<int>>` keys `f()` as a mint), a caller's own container slot (`sink: Array<(Box<T>) -> T>`, invoked as `fs[0](box)` at the caller's type) and a caller invoking a public `scoped` field — and the untyped hand-out 0165 §10 had filed: a capture-free lend at a bare-`T` return slot converted to `Object` and narrowed back to `(Box<Task>) -> Task`. v0.335 closes each at the declaration that already carries the keying: an implementation reads such a parameter at the declaring signature, as it already reads an inherited bare-`T` return; the exported-mint carve-out shrinks to a parameter's own function type (zero measured sites); a `scoped` field is invoked by its declaring type's bodies only (one test site, `lambda_test.cpp`'s `t._cb(10)`, respelled through a method); and a body-formed value with `T` at a return slot reaches no set-erasing position and no variance, as a `T`-parameter value already does not (zero sites). Declined: *pre-substitution keying at every invocation through a generic declaration's slot* (over-reaches plain `(T) -> …` holders, whose values were checked at the substituted type, and asks a caller to see a declaration behind a value it holds); *barring the body from passing its value to a member of a generic instantiated at its `T`* (rejects `_ev.Add(lambda(t: T) => …)`, the event idiom, and misses the mint twin); *a visibility rule for `scoped` fields over a type parameter* (`private` still admits a nested class holding a concrete instantiation, and repaired it is the who-invokes rule with a shape condition); *dropping the `scoped`-field exemption* (rejects the generic registrar idiom and closes nothing the invocation rule leaves open); *a caller-side lend reading of a function value narrowed from `Object` to a mint-announcing type* (keys an invocation on provenance the caller cannot see); *a per-substitution iterable exclusion at every parameter slot* (rejects `xs.Select(lambda(x) => x.Items)` at `U = IEnumerable<Item>`, which a parameter's own slot reads soundly).

**`scoped` is not admitted on `Object` (v0.321, [#5525](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5525)).** Spec §28.14 gives `scoped` two contracts keyed on the parameter's declared type. At a function type the callee may keep the callback in a `scoped` field, which is never read out, and the argument carries no call-site marker. At a class or interface type it may keep the borrow in an ordinary non-owned field, and the argument is marked `scoped x` and frozen. `Object` is the one class to which a function value also converts (spec §3.1), so a `scoped o: Object` parameter would take both kinds of argument under one declaration, and neither contract covers the other's: a callback would sit in a readable field from which a `case` recovers it, and nothing says whether it is marked. A position declared `Object` is set-erasing (*Function values at set-erasing positions* above), so the callback half could admit only capture-free arguments anyway. The declaration is therefore a compile error (spec §28.14, *Not at `Object`*). *Admitting it as a class-typed `scoped` parameter*, with the set-erasing rule rejecting a capture-bearing callback at each call, was declined: it keeps a declaration whose only distinct role at a function value is to be rejected, and the marker and storage questions for such an argument would still need answers. The remedy is the class or interface type the callee stores.

**The formation bar and `out` parameters; a function-typed `ref`/`out` location after the call (v0.327, [#5526](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5526); proposal [0140](proposals/0140-formation-bar-out-parameters.md), delegated ruling 2026-10-08).** The widened formation bar (above) exists because a call through a formed reference passes a capture-bearing argument to a plain function-typed slot, which the caller side admits, while the callee receives it at a position that announces no set. An `out` parameter takes no argument value: the call passes a location, the callee's slot is copied out on normal return (spec §22.3), and everything the callee can place in that slot arrived capture-free — through a set-erasing position, a holder, the return-slot arm or another `out` — so the formed type erases nothing the caller supplies. The bar therefore keys on the slots a caller's value reaches, plain and `ref`, and exempts `out`: `var tg := d.TryGetValue;` over `Dictionary<string, () -> int>` is admitted, as its direct call always was. The same ground decides the location's set after a call: a function-typed `ref` or `out` location is a holder the callee may fill only capture-free, so after the call an `out` location carries the empty set and a `ref` location what it carried before, instead of the union of the call's borrows a class-typed location takes. *Keeping the bar uniform over `out`* was declined: it protects nothing at that slot and rejects a capture-free read of a collection, with a lambda as the only remedy. *Dropping `out` locations from the set-erasing definition* was declined: an `out o: Object` location is a real erasure site in a checked callee body (`o := f`), and the definition keys on where a set is erased, not on which slots a caller's argument reaches, which is the bar's key. *Keeping the union at a function-typed location* was declined: it rejected `d.TryGetValue(k, out this._g)`, and a later field store of the `out` value, for a borrow the location can never hold.

**A class field declared at a bare type parameter is a set-erasing position (v0.328).** The set-erasing list named parameters, return slots, `ref`/`out` locations, record components, choice payloads and inline-array slots at a bare type parameter, and the holder list keys on a field's declared type being a function type, so a `public` field `V: T` of a `Box<T>` written through a `Box<() -> int>` receiver — `b.V := f` — sat in neither list while a read of it carried the empty set (proposal 0140's critique found it; filed from v0.327). The field joins the set-erasing list: the store is checked at the writer, the position announces no set, and the induction that keeps an `out` location capture-free already covers every store the generic body itself can make. The alternative — keying the holder definition on the field's substituted type, so that the field becomes a function-typed holder at that instantiation — was declined: the verdict is the same, the holder list is kept on declared types so that a checked body's stores classify without an instantiation, and the set-erasing list is where every substitution-made position lives, the field beside its component, payload and slot siblings.

**A box that carries borrows is the twin of a function value: borrow-erasing positions (v0.329, [#5645](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5645); proposal [0146](proposals/0146-borrow-erasing-positions.md)).** Spec §9.7 rejects the store of a borrow-bearing record into a class field statically, because a record-carried borrow — a span's buffer window — is read with no per-access disposed-flag check, so the trust regime that covers a class-typed borrow field has no backstop there. Spec §8.6 then admitted boxing such a value "for a short-lived interface call (e.g. passing to a method accepting `Object`)", and the callee received a parameter declared `Object`, which it may store under §28.3's field-store residual: `method K(x: Object) F := x;` called with a span kept a dangling view, and `b.Keep<Object>(span)` laundered the per-instantiation rejection of `b.Keep<Pair>(p)` the v0.328 ruling above states. The callee cannot tell the box from a class reference, and a callee-side rule keyed on the interface's implementers is not modular (the record may live outside the callee's closure); a K4.3 trust clause would reverse §9.7's own ground. So the rule is the one this section already records for closures: a value that holds borrows reaches no position declared `Object` or an interface type, nor a bare type parameter substituted with one — the caller's check over the declared signature, K2.3 + K2.4 — with two exceptions that are not erasures. A member-call receiver boxes for the call and the member reads the payload at its own type (spec §9.4), and `IReadOnlySpan<TElement>` is read as holding the span's borrows by every rule, so a callee's parameter of that type, and the `this` of an extension on it, is bounded as the span is (the compiler's #4751 field leg was already that rule for the store). An extension on `Object` or a user interface is not such a receiver: its body reads `this` at the head type and could pass it to a storing parameter, so that receiver is an erasing position. The cost is the set-erasing rule's: `Console.WriteLine(span)` is rejected and `span.ToString()` prints; two test files flip. Declined: the trust clause; a callee-side no-store rule for every `Object` or interface parameter, which retires the back-reference idiom; a register of non-storing standard-library `Object` positions, which would make the twins disagree at one position and be the first allowlist outside §28.14's register that user members could not share, the ground on which the v0.320 paragraph above declined one for `Raise`; `Object` locals with tracked sources. Deferred: user interfaces in `IReadOnlySpan<TElement>`'s role, for which no program exists. *(v0.332, [#5707](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5707): the ground moved. A span's reads pass through its backing object's indexer and meet the flag — [§5.15](#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans)'s record — so the rule stands on spec §9.7's posture that a value type that holds borrows is stored past its bound at no position, not on a missing backstop.)*

**An `as` at `Object` is a set-erasing position (v0.331, [#5675](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5675)).** The set-erasing list reached its positions "by implicit conversion", while its borrow-erasing twin in spec §9.7 named the `as` and the typed-pattern binding beside the implicit conversion. A function value has exactly one explicit conversion, to `Object` (spec §3.1), and `f as Object` yields a value whose static type no function-value rule reads: the holders key on the assigned value's static type, and §17.4's "a borrow with the same source as `x`" is written for classified references, which a function value is not (K1.6). So `_o := f as Object`, `objs.Add(f as Object)`, `s.Put(f as Object)` and a callee's `o := f as Object` into a `ref o: Object?` each carried a capture-bearing closure past the check, and the read-back `_o as () -> int` invoked it over dead captures — a span capture with no flag at all. The `as` is now a set-erasing position in its own right, the rejection at the conversion as for a box, and a function value narrowed back from `Object` carries the empty set, every function value at `Object` being capture-free by construction. Declined: tracking the set through the `Object`-typed result by flow analysis (the `Object` local row of v0.329, in function-value form), and rejecting the `as` for capture-free function values too (the set-erasing positions admit those everywhere, and `Equals(Object?)` and the description print rely on it).

**A retyping is a conversion: `IReadOnlySpan<TElement>` does not vary away from itself (v0.333, [#5726](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5726); proposal [0156](proposals/0156-span-interface-retyping-is-borrow-erasing.md)).** The v0.329 record above lets a value that holds borrows reach `Object` or an interface at two positions only, and `IReadOnlySpan<TElement>` is the one reference type read as holding them. It is still an interface, though, so the language's identity-preserving retypings reached it. Spec §27.1.1's covariant return slot turned a capture-free `(IReadOnlySpan<int>) -> IReadOnlySpan<int>` into a `(IReadOnlySpan<int>) -> Object`, whose result the field-store residual admitted into an `Object?` field. Spec §12.9's variance turned `IReadOnlyList<IReadOnlySpan<int>>` into `IReadOnlyList<Object>` with the same effect at its elements. The contravariant faces (an `(Object) -> ()` value at a span slot, a user `ISink<in T>`) and a method reference's formation adapter carried the span the other way, into a parameter declared `Object`. None converts a span value, so the borrow-erasing rule had no site. Each is now that conversion, rejected where the retyping is written: the span form of the function-typed exclusions §27.1.1 already makes for the set-erasing twin. It is a rejected conversion, not a missing type relation, so overload resolution and a generic body's per-instantiation reading treat it exactly as they treat the direct conversion, and a formation adapter needs no bar of its own, because a span's conversion to `Object` is never admitted, where a capture-free function value's is. Proposal 0153's flag already made the stale read fault in a checked build. The rule restores the static posture that a span is stored past its bound at no position, on which the instance-field ban stands. In a generic body a variant retyping over a type parameter is checked under the class-or-interface assumption and its conditions are read per instantiation, which also decides the value-type case §12.9 had left unstated. A run-time type test does not succeed through such a retyping either: a downcast from `Object` (`o as IReadOnlyList<Object>` over an `Object` holding a list of spans) would otherwise reach the same variance with no conversion written. Declined: the status quo, backstopped only at run time; the type-relation exclusion the issue recommended, which needs a formation clause and a generic reading of its own; tracking the span's sources through the retyped `Object`; banning `IReadOnlySpan<TElement>` as a type argument; and rejecting at the declaration every variance over a type parameter whose constraints do not establish a reference type.

**The span interface is closed, a record that holds borrows declares no `Equals(Object?)`, and an `as` at a type parameter over a span is a per-instantiation requirement (v0.334, proposal [0159](proposals/0159-holds-borrows-posture.md) option (b); [#5739](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5739), [#5740](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5740)).** The fourth matrix run found two routes by which a value that holds borrows reached a declared `Equals(Object?)` or an `Object` field with no conversion written in user code: the standard library's equality search, whose body contributes no requirement, hands a record declaring only `Equals(Object?)` — or a user class implementing `IReadOnlySpan<TElement>` — the boxed argument; and a generic body's `x as T` over a concrete span-interface value, read at `T = Object` as §12.7's ordinary run-time test. Both are closed by declaration rather than by a new hashable clause: the interface is closed to user implementations (zero exist; the deferred user borrow-view interface of the v0.329 record stays deferred, now honestly, since the stdlib interface is no longer a door to it), a record that holds borrows may not declare `Equals(Object?)` (zero exist; its `Equals(T)` is the only member the first kind reaches without boxing, the rule enums and choices already have), and the `as` or typed pattern at a type parameter is a requirement of the body's use of that parameter, read at each instantiation substituting `Object` or an interface other than the span interface — proposal 0156's per-instantiation posture, with a dispatched generic method deciding at its body. Declined with the proposal: retiring the storage bans on a value that holds borrows (its option (a), the retrospective review's R5) and the same retirement with a default-build warning (option (a')), each on the posture of the v0.332 record that such a value is stored past its bound at no position. The choice was soundness first: (a) would turn a compile-time rejection into a checked-mode fault, and in an unchecked build undefined behaviour, at every field, `async`, task-result and `Object` position such a value would newly reach — the posture a class borrow accepts for the back-reference idiom, here for programs the tree neither writes nor needs — while keeping the bans is never less sound than retiring them, a route the enumeration may still leave being, under the flag, at worst that posture at one position. The simplicity (a) offered is real and recorded; the reopening bar is in `DECLINED.md`.

**No reference type is read as holding borrows (v0.336; [proposal 0175](proposals/0175-retire-the-read-only-span-interface.md)).** The v0.329, v0.333 and v0.334 records above each carve `IReadOnlySpan<TElement>` out of a rule keyed on a value's kind; v0.336 retires the interface ([§5.15](#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans)'s record), and the carves go with it: a value that holds borrows converts to `Object` or an interface at one position, the receiver of a member call, and spec §9.7's borrow-erasing bullet, §12.9, §27.1.1, §27.6 and §12.7's narrowing paragraph lose their span clauses. The posture those records built — a value type that holds borrows is stored past its bound at no position — is unchanged, and is simpler to state with no reference type to except from it.

**One announcement-erasing-position definition; the value-copy register read off signatures; the report shrunk to its claim (v0.334; proposal [0169](proposals/0169-retrospective-consolidation.md)).** The fixes of v0.316–v0.333 closed, one position at a time, the places a capture-bearing function value could reach without its set: a field, a collection, a property, an `Object` parameter, a type-parameter substitution, an `as`, a nested parameter slot, a task result, a captured binding. Read together they are one sentence — a position that holds or converts a function value at a declared type announcing no source set (a function type, `Object`, or a type parameter substituted with either) is announcement-erasing and admits a capture-free value only, the set being compared only at a local, an invocation, a capture and a function-typed parameter — and spec §28.3 now states that sentence once, in the closure bullet, with *Function-typed holders* and *Set-erasing positions* as its two site lists; the three holder sub-bullets, which restated the sink rule per holder kind, became one bullet. No verdict moved. In the same pass the standard library's value-copy register (v0.333, #5717) became a signature-shape rule at spec §28.11 — an `owned` collection result, a borrowing-family or span receiver taking `IEnumerable<T>`, a read-only span or a `T` with no return, a written span argument — which reads the 27 marked declarations exactly as the register did, reads `Counter<K>.Increment`, which the register had missed, as the store it is, and names the four members a shape reads that copy nothing (`Set<T>.IntersectWith`/`ExceptWith` remove; `Span<T>.MoveFrom`/`MoveTo` move); the per-declaration markers went, because a census is a second statement of the rule and the register was the part #5717 had to amend. And spec §28.3's report paragraph dropped its per-family site enumeration for the clause list the enumeration restated: the claim's content is "every K4.3 clause", which K4's own sentence keeps enumerable, and the enumeration was the part 0157 had to amend. Declined: accepting the four over-reads as copies (a sound but needless rejection of `_set.ExceptWith(localArray)`); keeping the markers; retiring provenance clauses 1–6 and the captured-binding union by a no-binding or freeze rule (1,532 lambda-bound locals in the test tree; 0152's 32 reassigned function-typed locals).

**Capturing a `ref` or `out` parameter (v0.321, [#5534](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5534)).** A closure shares a captured variable with its scope (spec §27.5). For a `ref` parameter that variable is the caller's location, and for an `out` parameter the callee's slot for the call; neither can move into a heap cell without breaking the sharing, since a cell would hide the closure's writes from the caller and the callee's from the closure. The capture therefore holds the location, which exists for the call only, and the closure is bounded by the declaring body's scope as a closure over a captured owned local is: it may run within the call and may not leave it. This is spec §30.1's reason for barring `ref` and `out` from bodies whose frames outlive the call, applied to the closure and to any deferred-activation frame that carries it, the holders a synchronous body creates that could carry the location past the call. A task is the one such frame the body cannot consume, since its disposal does not stop it, so a task-returning call that would carry the closure is rejected there. *Capturing a copy of the value* was declined: the closure would read a stale value after the callee writes the parameter and its own writes would be lost, the one place where §27.5's sharing would silently not hold, and the copy is already one declaration away (`var snap := x;`). *Rejecting every capture* was declined as stricter than the hazard: a closure invoked or passed within the call is sound.

**Definite assignment across a closure (v0.322, [#5558](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5558)).** A closure body runs at its invocations, and once the closure is bound, passed or captured the enclosing body cannot see them, so definite assignment cannot follow them. The point the enclosing body does see is the lambda's formation, and the body is analysed from the state there (spec §27.5): a captured `out` parameter or non-defaultable local unassigned at formation is unassigned in the body, and a write in the body assigns it nowhere outside. The cost is a rejection where every invocation in fact follows the assignment; the remedy is to assign before forming the lambda. One shape is admitted by name: a lambda that is the whole right-hand side of an assignment to the captured variable reads that variable as assigned, because nothing can invoke the lambda before the store. Without it a recursive closure, `var fact: (int) -> int; fact := lambda(n: int) => … fact(n - 1) …;`, the only spelling of a self-calling closure over captured state (a local's own initializer is outside its scope, spec §21.1), would need a nullable variable and a `!` at every self-call. Declined: *checking reads at the invocations*, which for a closure passed to a callee would need a fact about another body that no signature announces; and *leaving closure bodies unanalysed*, which lets a closure read an `out` slot or a non-defaultable local before anything writes it, the unset read spec §7.9 exists to exclude; and *rejecting the recursive closure's self-reference* as well, as C#'s definite assignment does, for the cost above and no soundness gain.

**A captured binding is counted by every assignment to it (v0.332, [#5688](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5688); proposal [0152](proposals/0152-captured-binding-union-over-assignments.md)).** Captures share one heap cell (spec §27.5): a closure reads it at invocations the enclosing body cannot see, and a lambda body writes it at points the enclosing body's flow analysis has no edge for. So no assignment to a captured binding can be ordered against a closure's reads, and a closure's set read off the binding at its formation is wrong the moment the binding is re-pointed at a shorter-lived source — by a same-block reassignment the local-scope rule admits, by a store into a captured inline array's slot or a captured record's settable member, or by an assignment inside a lambda body, after which even the binding's own `return` reads a stale source. The escape exists for every value kind that has a null-source form or can be re-pointed. The rule is K2.2's union with the cell as the value: a captured binding has one source set, the union over every assignment to it in any body, and every reader reads that set — a per-body pre-scan, with no new mechanism. The cost is that an assignment counts against reads it cannot reach — one overwritten before the lambda forms, or one made after the closure's last invocation or before a read of the binding itself; zero corpus sites, and the remedy is a second binding. Declined: *a freeze on the captured binding after the closure forms*, which must reach inside every lambda body that captures it, keys a freeze on formation where K5 keys on liveness, and bars both re-pointing a captured binding at a longer-lived source and the in-body cache idiom (`if cache = null then cache := _store.Find(); end`), sound under the union; *rejecting only the in-lambda assignment*, with the union over the enclosing body — the cache idiom's cost again, and two rules where one serves; *a flow-sensitive union cut at the formation*, which gives one cell three position-dependent sets and buys a precision — a pre-formation assignment overwritten before the lambda forms, a read of the binding before a later assignment — that no corpus site uses; and *the status quo*.

**A boxed value at an interface-typed `scoped` parameter (v0.322, [#5559](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5559)).** A record, enum, primitive or copyable choice reaches an interface-typed parameter by boxing (spec §8.6), and the box is runtime-managed. A value carrying no borrow therefore leaves spec §28.14's bound nothing to protect: it is admitted under every bounding object, and nothing is frozen, the box holding a copy. A borrow-bearing value is rejected, because a `scoped` parameter exists for a store into a field of the bounding object, and spec §9.7 rejects a record-carried borrow in a class's instance field, for regime uniformity, a rule spec §8.6 applies to the boxed form. Declined: *admitting it with its carried sources tested and frozen as a closure's captures are*. That bound would be sound, since the declared-before test and the freeze make every source outlive the bounding object, but it is an exception to spec §9.7's instance-field rule keyed on a parameter modifier, and that rule's stated reason is its uniformity; lifting it is a proposal.

<a id="sec-why-classes-are-non-nullable-by-default"></a>
### 4.3 Why classes are non-nullable by default

C#'s opt-in nullable reference types proved that most reference fields are non-null in practice. Bezel goes one step further: non-nullability is the default, nullable types are opt-in via `?`. The cost is `?` on a small minority of types; the benefit is that `null` is noteworthy, not the default value of every reference.

<a id="sec-allocation-failure-fatal"></a>
### 4.4 Allocation failure is fatal

When the runtime cannot obtain memory — for a `new`, a growing collection, a string, a boxed value, or a closure/iterator/async frame — the program terminates: the runtime reports the failure and aborts the process (spec §31.4). Nothing is catchable, no unwinding runs, and the disposal-at-exit pass is skipped — allocation failure joins the abnormal terminations of spec §22.13, where disposal at exit is already excused. The report's form is implementation-defined; the termination is not.

C trained generations of programmers to treat an unchecked `malloc` as a bug, so refusing to surface the failure at all deserves a defense. Making allocation failure catchable — an `OutOfMemoryException` — would turn every `new`, every collection growth, and every closure, iterator, and async frame into a potential throw site: a permanent tax on the exception control-flow graph and on the ownership checker's discharge analysis, paid by every program on every path. What the tax would buy is a handler invoked at the precise moment allocation has stopped working — and OOM handlers almost universally allocate on their way to failing. Programs that genuinely must survive memory pressure do it before the fact — bounding inputs, pooling buffers — not by catching the failure after it happens. `StackOverflowException` had already established that resource exhaustion is uncatchable and fatal; allocation failure follows the precedent.

Declined: a named uncatchable `OutOfMemoryException`, purely for report symmetry with `StackOverflowException`. The report's wording is implementation territory, so the name is cosmetic — and revisitable without breaking anything.

<a id="sec-ownership"></a>
## 5. Ownership

<a id="sec-owned-not-borrows"></a>
### 5.1 `owned`, not borrows

Rust's borrow checker enforces single-writer-or-many-readers at compile time. Most working programmers don't need that guarantee; they need "free this once and don't use it after." Bezel targets the second and skips the first.

`owned` marks a field, parameter, or return as on the disposal hook for its containing object. The compiler enforces that owned values reach an owned location and don't escape to non-owned references. Use-after-dispose is not statically prevented; it's caught at runtime via the idempotent `Dispose()` rule.

Trade-off: programmers can write aliasing bugs that Rust would reject. They can also write a working web server in 200 lines without `Arc<Mutex<T>>`.

*(v0.180 narrowing — the consume-site freeze, recorded.)* Spec §28.3 (v0.180) makes it a compile error to **consume** an owned binding — move it into an `owned` argument (including a domain spawn or channel send), transfer it with the binding/assignment marker, store it into an owned slot, return it owned, or reassign it — while a live local borrow of that binding exists. This is a deliberate, scoped narrowing of this section's decline of compile-time borrow-liveness checking, **not** a borrow checker, and the recorded boundary is load-bearing: **explicit `Dispose()` while borrowed remains runtime-checked**, exactly as this section decided — disposal sets the per-instance flag, so checked mode deterministically catches every later aliased use, and the `Dispose()` call is itself the source-visible site announcement. What forced the static rule for the *silent* kills is the case with **no runtime backstop of any kind**: an object moved to another domain stays alive, no flag is ever set, and a surviving pre-transfer borrow is a live data race (probe-confirmed, 2026-07 review) — §30.14's isolation claim was false without the freeze. The freeze was applied uniformly at every consume site rather than at a compiler-known-transfer subset because transfer positions are not closed under composition (any `owned` argument may be forwarded to a spawn), and because the rule composes with §28.7's drop-on-overwrite — unborrowed reassignment stays legal (the reference compiler's former blanket rejection of owned-local reassignment, #1465, was narrowed to exactly the borrowed case). Cost accepted: the borrow-then-transfer-then-keep-using-the-borrow idiom is rejected (mechanical rewrite: re-borrow from the new owner). Alternatives declined: (a) freezing explicit `Dispose()` too — converts this section's recorded trade-off into a de-facto borrow checker for no soundness gain (the backstop is real); one deliberate exception is recorded: sources captured under §28.14's storage-bounded admission *are* frozen against disposal as well, because a stored callback's later invocation is invisible at the dispose site and the admission promises a fully static bound; a second exception recorded at v0.331 for a source a live span views (spec K5.9) was withdrawn at v0.332 with its ground — a span reads through its backing object's accessors, so there too the backstop is real ([§5.15](#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans)'s v0.332 record); (b) scoping the freeze to domain-crossing sites only — leaky (not compositionally closed), unpredictable at call sites, and it would leave the borrowed-reassignment silent kill entirely outside the rule.*

*(Supersession notes, v0.157.* Two of this section's original claims have been overtaken by later decisions and are preserved above only where still true. **Data races:** as first written, this section accepted that programmers could write data races; since v0.131–v0.133 the domain model closed that hole — continuations are serial per domain and no mutable state is reachable from two domains ([§12.5](#sec-execution-domains), [§12.6](#sec-sendability-spawning-channels)), so data races are no longer expressible in safe code and the remaining trade-off is aliasing/use-after-dispose, caught at runtime. **Discharge errors:** the original hard "must discharge every owned value" compile error became the affine auto-drop-with-warning model in v0.150–v0.152 — see [§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model).)*

*(v0.184 refinements, recorded.)* Three further boundary placements compose with the v0.180 narrowing. (1) **The join rule**: a read of an owned local after a *conditional* explicit `Dispose()` (or discard) is a compile error — owned ⊓ explicitly-disposed = maybe-consumed (spec §28.3). This is a deliberate step past this section's runtime-checked posture for site-announced kills: the per-path consumption tracking exists regardless (per-path scope-end disposal requires it), so the rejection is free precision, not a borrow checker; the posture continues to govern straight-line use after a site-announced kill, which stays a (now mandatory) warning plus the checked-mode backstop. (2) **The dispose-capability rule** (spec §28.6): `Dispose()` requires an owned receiver; disposing through a tracked borrow is a compile error — this is not borrow-liveness checking (it reads only the receiver's own ownership classification, already computed at every site) and it leaves this section's recorded trade-off intact: disposing an *owned* binding while borrows of it live remains legal and runtime-checked. The rule is exception-free since v0.213: the borrowing-collection transit regime (per-element disposal through element borrows, the manual form of the retired `DisposeElements()` escape hatch) was retired with axiom A8 when the 2026-07 corpus sweep found no load-bearing client — the owning family expresses the vouch structurally. (3) **Captured owned bindings** are borrow-state inside closures (no consume, no reassign, no dispose through the capture): the alternative admitted a silent drop-on-overwrite kill that the consume-site freeze — decidable precisely because it is local to one body — could never see.

*(v0.198: the model this section motivates is stated normatively as the ownership kernel — spec §28.3, K1–K9 — with this section's recorded decisions appearing in its axiom register; see [§5.26](#sec-the-ownership-kernel).)*

<a id="sec-dispose-is-not-on-object"></a>
### 5.2 `Dispose` is not on `Object`

Records, enums, and primitives don't need disposal. Putting `Dispose()` on `Object` would mean asking the user "should I dispose this `int`?" — a meaningless question. Disposal lives on classes, where the need actually exists.

Code that takes "any disposable thing" accepts `IDisposable`, not `Object`. This documents intent.

<a id="sec-collections-do-not-own-their-elements"></a>
### 5.3 Collections do not own their elements

The most-debated decision in this area. Three options were considered:

1. Collections always own their elements (Add takes ownership; Dispose disposes everything).
2. Borrowing collections (the original v0.52–v0.87 choice; remains the *default* in v0.97 alongside the explicit owning forms).
3. Collections optionally own via a generic flag.

Option 1 fails for collections of borrowed references, common when a class has a back-reference list. Option 3 doubles the API surface and forces the choice at type-construction time, which is the moment when the programmer least wants to think about it. Option 2 keeps collections universal; at the time it carried an explicit `DisposeElements()` escape hatch for holders that owned their elements — retired v0.213 with axiom A8, the owning family having taken that role structurally.

Asymmetric ownership in the values-owned direction is `OwningDictionary<K, V>`'s shape by construction; the own-keys direction has no recorded client.

v0.97's resolution: option 2 remains for the borrowing types (`Array<T>`, `Dictionary<K, V>`, etc.), and option 1 is provided as separately-named owning types (`OwningArray<T>`, `OwningDictionary<K, V>`, etc.). Users pick the form that matches their ownership intent at the declaration site. The earlier v0.88-v0.96 attempt at option 3 (an `<owned T>` generic-argument modifier with merged class declarations) was abandoned in v0.97 because the substitution machinery accumulated more complexity each revision; separate named types capture the semantic distinction directly without the substitution rules. The non-owning baseline retains its original justification — it is the right default for collections of borrows; a holding class that owns its elements commits the collection's type to ownership (the owning family), which is the honest spelling of what the retired escape hatch used to vouch.

*(The v0.184 per-element manual form and its `DisposeElements()` bulk sibling — the one exception spec §28.6 carried to dispose-requires-an-owned-receiver — were retired together at v0.213 (axiom A8's tombstone): the 2026-07 corpus sweep, #2075, found every use whole-collection-owned and migratable to the owning family; tracked borrows may not be disposed anywhere, with no residual carve.)*

<a id="sec-no-shared-ownership"></a>
### 5.4 No shared ownership

There is no `Rc<T>` or `Arc<T>`. Single ownership only.

The cases where shared ownership is genuinely needed are rare in Bezel's target use cases and are served by other patterns: handle-and-table indirection, channels, or the runtime-managed region for shared immutable data.

The handle-and-table pattern is itself a library type: `OwningSlotMap<T>` (stdlib [§4.21](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-owning-slot-map), v0.307) owns the table and mints `SlotHandle` values whose staleness after a removal is detected rather than silently reused — a stored key in place of a stored borrow, with no count and no trust clause.

If shared ownership turns out to be needed, it can be added as a library type without language changes. *(v0.134: it was — `Shared<T>`/`Weak<T>`, scoped, explicit, and domain-local; see [§5.21](#sec-shared-t) and language spec [§28.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-shared-ownership). The position of this section otherwise stands: single ownership remains the default and the only implicit regime.)*

<a id="sec-local-variable-ownership-is-inferred-not-annotated"></a>
### 5.5 Local-variable ownership is inferred, not annotated

Fields, parameters, and returns carry an explicit `owned`. Locals do not. The compiler infers each local's ownership state from how it was introduced — from the right-hand side's classification, for `using var` and plain `var` alike: `var x := new T()` is owned, `var x := someField` is borrowed, and a `using var` is owned over an owned producer but a scope-pinned borrow with no disposal obligation over a borrow ([§28.10](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-using-var-initialized-from-a-borrow)). The full rules are in [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows) of the spec.

The asymmetry is signal-to-noise. Annotations on field, parameter, and return positions communicate API intent across module boundaries — they need to be explicit. Annotations on every local would be redundant noise: the compiler can already see what the local was initialized from.

<a id="sec-the-lattice-direction-at-control-flow-joins"></a>
### 5.6 The lattice direction at control-flow joins

The [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows) lattice puts owned ⊑ borrowed: owned is the *stronger* state, borrowed the *weaker*. At a control-flow merge where two branches produced different states for the same variable, the meet would have to be the weaker state — but a meet between owned and borrowed at the same merge is rejected outright, because demoting owned to borrowed silently drops a disposal obligation.

The earlier reading (borrowed ⊑ owned, with the owned branch winning at the join) is wrong: there is no value to dispose on the borrowed branch, so the merged owned slot would carry an obligation it cannot discharge. The compiler instead requires programs to equalize the branches before the merge — either produce an owned value on both branches by allocation or `Clone`-style copy, or arrange for both branches to bind the variable to a borrow whose source outlives the merge. The latter does not mean "borrow the `using var`": an owned value discharged with `using var` in a branch must do its work and be released *within* that branch; the post-merge variable takes its value from a longer-lived source, because a borrow may never outlive the source it points into (the merge inherits, rather than escapes, that rule).

This is the standard lattice-meet rule in linear/affine type systems applied to Bezel's specific kinds. It is more restrictive than what some informal readings of v0.55 suggested, but the prior reading was unsound.

*(v0.198: this rule is kernel principle K7 — spec §28.3; see [§5.26](#sec-the-ownership-kernel).)*

<a id="sec-factory-method-ownership"></a>
### 5.7 Factory method ownership

v0.59 left a gap in the producers table for static factory methods. A method like `File.ReadAllLines(path)` returns a freshly-allocated `Array<string>` — semantically identical to a `new` expression — but the return type carried no `owned` annotation. Under the strict reading of [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows), the return was a "borrow," but from what source? A static method has no receiver.

The v0.60 rule was: when a method returns a class type without `owned` and the compiler cannot identify a borrow source, the return is treated as owned, with a warning recommending the explicit `owned` return type.

*(v0.201 — the heuristic is retired; proposal 0048.)* The warning was designed as a bridge, and both of its recorded motives discharged: the stdlib annotation pass completed (a 2026-07 sweep found **zero** static signatures relying on the treated-owned classification), and the module/artifact model (spec §2.8) delivers an unannotated third-party factory's diagnostic at the third party's own build rather than silently rewriting consumers' obligations. What remained was cost: a core ownership rule memorized rather than read, and the instance↔static refactor hazard — moving a method between kinds silently flipped every call site between borrow and owned. Spec §28.3 now **rejects at the declaration** a static method or operator whose class- or interface-typed return does not type-announce its classification (`owned`, a `Task`-family type, an iterable/enumerator interface, or a sourced bare type parameter); the v0.196 per-instantiation erasure was the last enabler, making `owned T` spellable over mixed constraint domains. The kernel's axiom A1 is tombstoned (the register's first planned decrease; budget → thirteen). Body-inference and call-site-error alternatives were declined with reasons in proposal 0048 §8; §5.5's boundary-explicitness doctrine is what the retirement completes.

*(v0.60–v0.200.)* The inferred rule was a safety net beside the preferred `owned` annotation, for unannotated third-party returns and for a standard library annotated in stages from v0.60; both motives are the ones discharged at v0.201 (above).

(v0.194) The premise — *cannot identify a borrow source* — was completed for generic signatures. A static method whose declared return type is a bare type parameter *does* have identifiable sources: its T-reaching plain arguments. Treating `Math.Max<T>(a, b)` as a fresh-owned factory at a class substitution had made the caller a second owner of one of its own still-owned arguments (two owners, one obligation — the exactly-once invariant broken through the argument binding), while the callee-side borrow-return prohibition made the selector body unwritable in-language. The bare-type-parameter rule (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-bare-type-parameter-returns)) classifies such calls as borrows carrying the union of the receiver's and the T-reaching arguments' source-liveness constraints, and reserves this section's owned-with-warning fallback for the genuinely sourceless shapes (`Create<T>(): T where T: new()`) — where fresh production is the only writable body and owned is the right answer (with one v0.194 tightening: a static-storage-sourced borrow return is rejected under such an empty-set signature — the caller side mints ownership there, so the borrow would acquire a second owner; a static property is the sound spelling). Callee-side, the bare-type-parameter return admits parameter- and receiver-sourced borrows, enforced per instantiation category (§3.6 rule 2's pattern): the announcement-by-declared-type doctrine of the iterator and `Task`-family conventions, and what makes the standard library's own `Min`/`Max`/`Clamp` writable at class substitutions. Fresh-producing generic signatures spell `owned T`, as `OwningSet<T>.Pop(query: T): owned T` does under `where T: class` (spelled `Take` through v0.258; renamed at v0.259, stdlib §4.10).

The v0.194 declines are recorded with the rule. *Post-substitution keying* — classifying each call by its substituted return type — reads plausibly at `Math.Max<Connection>` and is refuted one shelf over: `First<T>(): T` at `T = Task<int>` would substitute into the `Task`-family rule and mint a disposal obligation over a collection-owned element. The sound geometry, shared with the v0.190 ruling, is pre-substitution *keying* with per-instantiation *enforcement*: what rule governs a call is fixed by the declaration, while the checks fire only where a substitution gives them content. *Marking selector returns `owned`* fails twice over — as engineering it is marker spam across every generic selector in every library, and as semantics it is a false statement: a selector transfers nothing, and `owned` would mint exactly the second obligation the ruling exists to remove. What makes the borrow classification look inevitable in hindsight is the signature grammar it completes, one the standard library had been writing by instinct all along: bare `T` with T-reaching inputs is a **selector** and borrows (`Min`/`Max`/`Clamp`); `owned T` is a **producer** and transfers (`Pop`, `Deserialize`); bare `T` with no T-reaching inputs is a **factory** and is owned, the warning nudging toward the explicit spelling. A generic signature's ownership behavior is readable off its shape — the rule did not add a distinction, it recognized one the signatures already drew.

(v0.196) The spelling was then completed for *mixed* domains: `owned` on a bare-type-parameter slot became per-instantiation — the ordinary transfer at class and interface substitutions, **erased by the substitution** at value and runtime-managed ones — replacing v0.193's instantiation-site rejection, which had left fresh-producing generics over domains admitting value substitutions (the stdlib §2.19 numeric abstractions; `Create<T>(): T where T: new()`) with no spelling at all: the factory warning was pointing authors at a qualifier the checker then rejected at exactly the substitutions such domains exist for. Stdlib §2.19's producer members and spec §11.8's `Sum<T>` now carry the marker, which is what opens the class-conformer arithmetic surface (a heap-backed BigInteger is the recorded prospective occupant). A second, fresh-vs-selector marker was declined — it would duplicate `owned`'s class-substitution semantics keyword for keyword, differing only in value-substitution admissibility; restricting the numeric interfaces to value conformers was declined — spec §11.8's own canonical conformer is a class, and the restriction would only defer this exact design plus a compatibility event. The generic `owned` **field** rejection (spec [§28.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-owned-fields)) deliberately stands: a field marker commits the containing type's shape — destructor synthesis, derived properties — in a way a call-boundary marker does not.

(v0.243 — the concrete-selector cliff, recorded; proposal 0088 stage 1, #3054.) The rules above compose into a cliff this record had not confronted: the **concrete** selector signature is unwritable — `static method Pick(a: Res, b: Res): Res` is rejected at the declaration (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-announcement-table)'s announcement table, the plain-concrete row's static cell: an unannounced concrete static — no type announcement in its signature — is indistinguishable from a factory, so admitting it either mints a second owner or requires the retired v0.60 heuristic — the two-owners argument, preserved in full) — while the semantically identical generic twin `Pick<T>(a: T, b: T): T` compiles under the bare-type-parameter rule, whose bare-`T` return *is* the selector's type announcement. The sanctioned remedy is therefore to **generify**: a type parameter (with a constraint that keeps it meaningful) introduced for no generic reason, purchased to express a fact — "returns one of these arguments" — that the concrete signature grammar cannot announce. The stdlib's own selectors (`Math.Min`/`Max`/`Clamp`) are generic whether or not genericity means anything to them, and the declaration-site rejection's diagnostic is directed to name this remedy (compiler-lane, #3054). The announcement gap is a **recorded liftable, not taken**: proposal 0088 stage 2 designs the `sourced` return-position marker — the bare-type-parameter rule transcribed at a concrete type, with the unannounced signature staying rejected exactly as today — and it is gated, per the 0051 Part B evidence posture, on the first real concrete-selector want a stdlib wave or user corpus files. Auto-detection without announcement (classify any concrete static whose return type matches a plain parameter's type as a selector) stays declined on this record's own grounds: it reintroduces a heuristic where the design sentence is "read off the declared return type with no heuristic", it is shape-fragile in exactly the refactor-hazard way the v0.201 retirement closed, and it misclassifies real factories (`Clone(template: Res): Res` intends a fresh value).

<a id="sec-collection-literal-ownership"></a>
### 5.8 Collection literal ownership

A collection literal `[1, 2, 3]` targeting `Array<T>` (or another collection class) allocates a heap object. It is semantically a `new` expression with initialization — the fact that the syntax is a literal does not change the allocation behavior. v0.59 did not list collection literals in the producers table, leaving the ownership of `var xs := [1, 2, 3];` unspecified.

v0.60 adds collection literals to the producers table as owned values when targeting a heap collection class. The rationale is straightforward: the literal creates a fresh object, so the binding must own it. The same discharge rules as `new` apply. (Collection literals targeting inline arrays `[N]T` (v0.98+) produce value-typed inline arrays, with no heap ownership; they participate in the move-only category only when the inline-array slots are themselves `owned`.)

<a id="sec-trivial-dispose-classes"></a>
### 5.9 Trivial-dispose classes

Not every class holds resources. `Random` holds only an internal seed. `StringBuilder` holds a buffer that is plain memory. The `Encoding` singletons hold nothing at all. Under v0.60's strict rules, `new Random()` required `using var` or triggered a warning — adding ceremony for a no-op dispose.

The runtime-managed exemption ([§28.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-runtime-managed-types) of the spec) covers `string`, `Type`, boxed values, and function types — types the language itself creates implicitly and pervasively — but it is a fixed list, and joining it permanently is the wrong model for user classes: what a resource-free class needs is an exemption that is *computed from its declaration* and tracks the class as it changes.

That is the resolution: **trivial-dispose** is a derived type property (spec [§3.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-derived-type-properties), [§28.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-trivial-dispose-classes)). A class with no destructor, no `owned` fields of non-trivial-dispose type, no owning-inline-array fields, and a trivial-dispose base has a disposal that provably runs no user code and releases only memory, and the compiler drops the **ceremony** — never the tracking: a bare `var r := new Random()` compiles clean and the discard `_ := new StringBuilder();` is legal and purely cosmetic, while the instance keeps its statically-known owner and is freed by compiler-inserted code at that owner's release point (spec §28.9; no runtime lifetime mechanism is involved — [§5.28](#sec-silent-tracking) is the decision record). The two recorded steps that reached this endpoint were commitments about ceremony: v0.61 first softened the discharge diagnostic to a reduced-severity informational warning; v0.66 dropped even that — nothing is demanded *of the program*, so there is nothing to warn about. (v0.66 also specified an implementation-defined runtime reclaim mechanism for trivial instances; that half was retracted at v0.238 — the ceremony half is what survives, and [§5.28](#sec-silent-tracking) records why the mechanism went.)

The class-gains-a-destructor hazard is answered by recomputation, not by ceremony at every use site. Adding a destructor, an `owned` field of non-trivial-dispose type, or an owning-inline-array field ([§15.1.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-owning-inline-arrays) of the spec) flips the classification, and formerly-silent sites immediately draw the standard obligations — the must-use auto-drop warning, a discard that now actually runs a destructor. The flip changes ceremony only — both sides are tracked, and the free-or-dispose point is the owner's release point either way — but it is source-affecting by design, which is why spec [§28.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-trivial-dispose-classes) directs API authors to treat a public class's trivial-dispose status as part of its published contract. `using var` remains accepted (naming the owner explicitly, with a free that runs no user code) for code that wants to be robust against the flip in advance.

**§9.7's bullets in a generic body are read per instantiation, never at the declaration under the class-or-interface assumption (v0.328, [#5629](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5629)).** The keying/enforcement split above decides this too. A record over a type parameter is classified at each instantiation (spec [§9.7](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-records-and-disposal)'s declared-field-types paragraph, [§3.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-derived-type-properties) rule 2), so a body's store, copy, capture or return of a `Box<T>` or of a bare `T` gives a bullet nothing to read until a substitution makes the value hold borrows; the v0.326 ruling on an `owned T` or `where T: value` return ([#5616](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5616), [#5620](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5620)) was the first instance, and v0.328 states it once for every bullet and for a type formed over a parameter. The declaration reading — classifying `Box<T>` as borrow-bearing because `T` may be a class, and rejecting `Wrap<T>(a: T): Box<T> => new Box<T>(a)` outright — was declined: it rejects `Wrap<int>` and every generic pair-builder for a borrow that exists at no value substitution, the declaration-site over-rejection §26.5.1's `First` example already refutes. The cost lands on dispatched generic methods, whose bodies decide requirements at the body ([§14.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-dispatched-generic-methods), v0.327): an override storing its bare-`T` parameter into an `Object?` field is rejected at the body unless the declaring method is bounded `where T: class` (or `where T: sendable`, over values). That verdict already followed from v0.327's text — §14.2's body rule read with spec [§8.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-boxing)'s *Boxing of borrow-bearing records* — and proposal 0141's survey, which recorded the two banked bodies of that shape as unchanged, weighed only §8.6's move-only half; at a span substitution the store would keep a box whose buffer reads carry no flag test past the buffer's disposal. *(v0.332, [#5707](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5707): a span's buffer reads meet the flag — [§5.15](#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans)'s record — so the verdict stands on spec §9.7's posture that a value type that holds borrows is stored past its bound at no position, not on a missing backstop.)* Excluding value types that hold borrows at every call of a dispatched generic method, as 0141 excludes move-only types and inline arrays, would keep those bodies (zero measured call sites at such a type) but was declined in this train: it reverses 0141's recorded choice to leave borrow-bearing arguments admitted, and rejects the read-only bodies at an ordinary record argument that this reading leaves untouched. It needs its own proposal amending 0141; the reopening bar is recorded with the decline.

<a id="sec-the-move-expression-on-owned-fields"></a>
### 5.10 The `move` expression on owned fields

Through v0.86, ownership transfer in Bezel was *single-shot*: a value moved from a `using var` local to an `owned` parameter, from an `owned` parameter to an `owned` field, from an `owned` field to memory at destructor time. Each transition was implicit, named by the syntactic position. There was no transition for "take this owned field's value out and return it to a caller."

The reason was a deliberate conservatism. Field reads always produced borrows ([§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows) of the spec), because fields are *retained*: a class instance exists to be acted upon multiple times, and the natural operation on a field is "read while leaving the value in place." Owning vs. borrowing was a property of the read site's intent, but every field read promised the same thing — the field's value is still there afterward. A take-out operation on a field would have to invalidate the field's contents, and the spec had no machinery for tracking "this field is currently empty."

The cost of this conservatism was visible. The standard pattern "replace a resource and return the old one" (a transactional update on an owned field) had no clean form. The v0.85 spec showed a `Utils.Swap`-via-`ref` workaround that exchanged the field with a freshly-built local; v0.86 documented the gap explicitly when a related case (batch-aggregating `Task<owned T>` results) had no workaround at all. Each acknowledgement made the gap more visible.

v0.87 closes the field-take case with `move x.f`. The expression reads the field as an owned value and leaves the field in *moved-from* state. The compiler tracks the moved-from state across the rest of the method body and rejects any path that exits the method without first reassigning the field. The rule is the same definite-assignment analysis Bezel already performs for non-nullable locals, lifted to the field level.

The design choice is to make the move *explicit*. A bare `var x := _resource;` continues to produce a borrow as it always did; only the explicit `move _resource` form transfers ownership out. Three reasons for the explicitness:

1. **Visibility in code review.** A reader scanning a method body learns the local effect of each line. With explicit `move`, the take-out is spelled at the read site; the reader does not have to reason about subsequent lines to discover that the read was actually a take. This matches Bezel's existing preference for syntactic visibility — `using var` declares scope-bound disposal at the binding site, `owned` declares ownership transfer at the type position, `out` declares output at the call site. `move` is the analogous keyword for the take-from-field operation.

2. **Refactoring stability.** A version of the rule that *inferred* take-out from the pattern "read followed by reassignment" would be sensitive to insertions between the two lines. Adding a `Log(...)` call between read and reassignment, or extracting the reassignment into a helper method, would silently change whether the read was a borrow or a take. Programs that compiled would compile differently after small edits. Explicit `move` makes the take-out a user decision; inserting a log call between the move and the reassignment does not change the move's semantics.

3. **The exception-path obligation.** The field-restoration rule has to handle exception edges as well as normal control flow: if a throwable expression appears between the move and the reassignment, the throwing edge is an exit path on which the field has not been restored. The fix is the *build-before-take* discipline — construct the replacement value first, then take, then assign — which is the same discipline RAII code in C++ and transactional code in functional languages already commits to. Making the move explicit lets the spec write down this rule without forcing the user to discover it through experiment.

The `move` form was, at v0.87, permitted only on owned instance fields, not on locals (which already had the implicit move-on-read semantics), not on static fields (whose lifetime crosses methods, breaking the field-restoration rule's scoping), and not on collection elements (which were not owned slots in that version). The first two restrictions were by design; the third was open — a future revision that admits `Array<owned T>` ([§14.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-owned-as-a-generic-argument)) would naturally extend `move` to collection-indexed slots. *(v0.246 note: the locals restriction is superseded — the transfer marker unified on `move` (proposal 0090; §3.10's reversal record), so `move x` is now also the spelling for consuming a named binding at every marked position, spec §28.3/§28.8.1. The three reasons above — review visibility, refactoring stability, the exception-path obligation — argued for an explicit action verb at every take-out site; v0.246 takes them at full generality. The static-field rejection stands unchanged (K9.4).)*

The field-restoration rule's analysis reuses the existing definite-assignment machinery. The compiler's flow analysis already tracks "is this non-nullable local definitely assigned on every path that reads it." The same machinery applied to the inverse question — "is this owned field definitely reassigned on every path that exits after a move" — needs no new graph algorithms, only a new state on field nodes. This is why `move` was a feasible v0.87 addition: the cost on the implementation side is small and localized.

v0.87 originally shipped without addressing the collection-of-owned case — `Array<T>` did not own its elements, so `move arr[i]` had no slot to move from. v0.88 closes that gap by extending language spec [§14.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-owned-as-a-generic-argument)'s opt-in list to admit `Array<owned T>` and the other standard collection types; the next subsection covers the rationale.

**The throwable inventory's two boundary questions (v0.255, [#3240](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3240); resolved 2026-08-18 under the owner's delegation — "there are a few open spec questions from the last week. Please find them, resolve them cleanly, gate them and merge them to master. I trust your judgement." — the rulings posted on the issues, never per-question owner calls).** Implementing §28.8.1's exception-edge classifier ([#3080](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3080)) surfaced two self-tensions in the inventory reason 3 above motivates. First, `new T(...)`: the throwable list never named it, and the section's own `Wrong` example annotates only the non-throwing branch as unrestored — reading the post-move `_resource := new FileStream(_path, FileMode.Open);` as a valid restoration — yet a constructor body is ordinary code and may throw. Ruled **not analysis-throwable** (the issue's Reading A), now stated explicitly: §28.8.1 already separates raise-capable from analysis-throwable (`!` throwable only when the target is null; `as` expressly not), and `new` joins that stated family — a throwing constructor completes no object, so the restoration analysis loses nothing on that edge, and the hazard the throw does create, the partially-constructed object's owned state, is the constructor's own unwinding territory (spec §22.9; §28.3/§28.6), already handled there. Reading B would have outlawed the single most common restoration idiom — `var old := move _r; _r := new Res();` — everywhere, against the section's own worked example, for a hazard the section does not otherwise discuss; declined. The exclusion covers the construction only: a `new`'s argument expressions classify on their own. Second, the non-throwable list's compound-assignment carve-out, read literally, made `x /= 0` non-throwable (neither `x` nor `0` is itself throwable) while the throwable list named division two paragraphs later — the same arithmetic in both lists by spelling. Rather than the issue's suggested list patch ("or whose operator is `/=` or `mod=`"), the resolution is a principle: a compound assignment (and `++`/`--`) is throwable **exactly when its §6.6/§17.6 desugared form is** — alike spellings classify alike — which also catches what the list patch would have missed (`x += y` on an overflow-checked type desugars to checked arithmetic, already in the inventory) and leaves no second enumeration to drift. The shipped #3080 classifier already implements Reading A, so item one validates shipped behavior; the compound-arithmetic widening is compiler catch-up riding the #3080 family's surface, tracked on the issue.

**The window is straight-line, per-slot, and vacated (v0.334, [proposal 0163](proposals/0163-restoration-window-straight-line.md); [#5773](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5773), [#5774](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5774), [#5775](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5775), [#5776](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5776)).** The disposal matrix's first run found four shapes that put code *inside* a K9.2 window where it could reach the vacated location through an alias — a call under a restoring `finally` (v0.332's admission), an `await`, a `yield return`, a constructor body run by the restoring `new` — with nothing stated about what such code observes or where a value it stores there goes; a non-constant-index slot move "restored" by a store at any index; and an owned-field cycle formed by storing a moved slot's value into a field of the object it owns. Reason 3 above already argues the answer: the window exists to be short. v0.334 therefore narrows it rather than instrumenting it — between a `move` and its restoring store only the non-throwable forms and `new` are admitted, the `finally`-placed restoration (added at v0.332 for a read of a vacated move-only field, with no other user: zero guide, test-program or idiom sites) is withdrawn, with the `catch`-arm restoration the compiler admits by the same path reading (one test-program site, the #3241 pin), and `yield return` is named among the exits; a slot of a collection held by an owned local of the body keeps its unconstrained window, since every exit edge passes the collection's disposal; `move coll[e]` is admitted at a constant or unreassigned-local index only and restored at that index (the conservative whole-collection state, which the compiler never implemented, is deleted; its two measured clients bind their index in one line); and the one spelling the straight-line window still lets reach the location — a constructor body — meets §9.4's disposed state, now the posture of every vacated field or slot, under K4.1's checked-mode fault. The cycle is recorded beside `Shared<T>`'s, with Appendix C.3's acyclicity assumption made explicit. Declined: the run-time sentinel with the `finally` form kept (a fault where a compile error is available); banning `new` in the window (the v0.255 #3240 ruling); a null-guarded restoring store (throwable, against build-before-take); the conservative state kept with same-index restoration (a second tracking mode for one-line rewrites); a static rejection of the body-local cycle shape (a new analysis for zero sites). Recorded as a study: the replace primitive that would delete K9.2.
