For a **cycle** there is no such order: a mutual back-edge would require each node's source to outlive the other, which no construction sequence delivers, so the cycle-closing borrow cannot be shown safe and is rejected (`a.Next := a` is a borrow-into-field whose source is a scope-bound local — refused). The operational symptom is a test-honesty gap: the `serialized cyclic` positive round-trip tests cannot be written in ownership-valid Bezel and so bypass the ownership checker (#703) — a feature whose serialize direction has no value the language can construct. (The DAG tier is *not* in this position; its back-edges are constructable, so `serialized graph` is exercisable end-to-end. A stale sentence in §28.3 that read as a blanket "borrowing fields are not expressible" overstated the rule and was corrected separately, #717; the operative rule is the lifetime one.) With `Shared<T>`/`Weak<T>` the cyclic back-edge becomes a `Weak<T>` minted from a `Shared<T>` spine, and the cycle *is* constructable in honest Bezel (below); its round trip becomes expressible and passes through the checker like any other program. The defect dissolves not by relaxing a check but because the data finally has an ownership-valid producer.

**The construction, shown.** The reason the two-phase window becomes unnecessary is concrete, so it is worth exhibiting rather than asserting. A mutual cycle is built as a `Shared<T>` spine with `Weak<T>` back-edges wired after both nodes exist:

```bezel
using var a := new Shared<Node>(new Node(1));   // strong spine
using var b := new Shared<Node>(new Node(2));
a.Get().back := b.Downgrade();                  // Weak<Node>? back-edge, wired post-construction
b.Get().back := a.Downgrade();
```

Every step is ordinary ownership-valid code: `Downgrade()` yields an `owned Weak<Node>` from a live handle, stored into an `owned back: Weak<Node>?` field through the `Get()` borrow; the borrow checker accepts it (verified). The cost is real and stated: the back-edge field is **nullable** (`Weak<T>?`), assigned after construction rather than in the constructor. That is precisely *why* no two-phase window is needed — definite assignment ([§7.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-definite-assignment-for-non-nullable-fields)) is satisfied trivially by the null initializer, and the later wiring is ordinary mutation, not a synthesized binding of a non-nullable field in a provably-unobservable interval. The v0.123/v0.124 machinery existed to bind a *non-nullable* back-edge without an observable unset window; a nullable `Weak<T>?` removes the obligation at its root. This is a genuine semantic difference from the keyword model (whose back-edges were non-nullable) and the right trade: a cycle-closing reference that can dangle (its target released) is nullable in substance, and the type now says so.

**Why this rather than the alternatives.** #703 named three resolutions; recording why the others were declined is the project's discipline ([§5.21](#sec-shared-t)'s declined-alternatives register). *Relax the ownership checker* to track owned-subobject lifetimes (so a borrow to a transitive owner is accepted) was declined: it is an open-ended ownership-analysis enhancement of uncertain blast radius that special-cases serialization back-edges inside the checker, and — decisively — it does not actually reach the cyclic case, because even an accepting checker leaves a *mutual* cycle with no order in which a **non-nullable** back-edge can be initialized at construction; one would still need a nullable field or a post-construction window. `Weak<T>` already exists, is nullable (so the definite-assignment question simply does not arise), carries last-one-disposes semantics the serializer needs anyway, and expresses the cycle in a first-class type rather than a checker exception. *Document the limitation* (ship `serialized cyclic` as construct-from-external-bytes-only) was declined as strictly worse than giving the feature an ownership-valid form: a serialization feature whose serialize direction is unreachable is the test-honesty defect, not a resolution of it.

**The field types already carry the shape the keyword asserted.** Each field's serialized role is read from its type: an `owned` field is an owning edge written inline; a borrowing field — plain or `Weak<T>` — is a back-reference written as a `$ref`; a `Shared<T>` field is an identity participant, written once at its first occurrence and as a `$ref` at every alias. The shape the clause asserted follows from those types, and the safe claim — the only one the redundancy argument needs — is that **the types are at least as informative as the clause and never contradict the data**: a class with no back-references of any kind serializes as a tree; one with borrowing fields and/or `Shared<T>` may alias; one with `Weak<T>` (or `Shared<T>` reaching an ancestor, the documented Shared-cycle leak, [§28.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-shared-ownership)) may cycle. The clause therefore restated, as a header assertion, information the field types already carry — and could *diverge* from it (`serialized tree` on a class holding a `Shared<T>` field is a contradiction the compiler would have to police for no gain). The disposition is the one [§5.19](#sec-serialization-without-reflection) already took for the owning/borrow split it read straight from the annotations: delete the restatement and read the shape from the types. The clause is removed outright, not demoted to an optional checked assertion, because a checked assertion is still a second statement of the same fact, with the divergence-policing cost and none of the expressiveness.

**Retiring assert-and-enforce — and why it is not the regression it looks like.** v0.119 made *assert-and-enforce* load-bearing: tree-shape is not statically verifiable, so the programmer *asserts* `serialized tree` and the deserializer *dynamically enforces* it, raising on a repeated identity rather than silently duplicating (serde's documented footgun). Removing the keyword removes that assertion, and it deserves a direct answer. The answer is that the type-driven model does not need it, and the bad state it guarded against becomes *unrepresentable* rather than merely *detected* — the project's preferred disposition wherever it is reachable. Assert-and-enforce caught a *declared* shape that the data violates (`serialized tree` over a graph that aliases). With no declared shape there is nothing to violate: a `Shared<T>` field *is* an aliasing participant by its type, not by an assertion that might be wrong, so "declared tree, actually a graph" is not detected but cannot be written. The other thing the deserializer did — *identity preservation*, binding a `$ref` to the already-built object instead of silently duplicating — is **kept**, though its mechanism gains a step on the read side: the identity table now holds the first-reconstructed *handle* and resolves an aliasing `$ref` by `Clone()`ing it (incrementing the shared count) and a `Weak` `$ref` by `Downgrade()`, where the keyword model re-pointed a plain borrow. The wire form is unchanged; the deserializer's handle-aware resolution is the addition.

**No new wire, and the one soundness obligation.** No wire vocabulary is introduced: the `$id`/`$ref` identity envelope (v0.121, JSON form v0.139, stdlib [§2.15](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-concrete-serialization-formats)) carries everything, so an aliased/DAG payload round-trips identity-for-identity; there are no legacy *cyclic* payloads to preserve, because none had an ownership-valid producer. The must-not-miss point is the identity *key*. A `Shared<T>`'s identity is the **object-reference identity of the value `Get()` borrows** — the same object-identity basis the existing `$id` tag→object table already uses (`Object.ReferenceEquals`, language spec [§8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-object-class)) — taken for a non-null live handle; a null `Shared<T>?` takes the ordinary `T?` null path, not the identity table. Two independently `Clone()`d handles to one value are distinct owned objects but their `Get()` borrows resolve to the same underlying object, so they collapse to one `$id` and alias correctly; keying on the *handle* instead would serialize the body twice and destroy the aliasing with no error in an unchecked build. The key is sound because within a single serialize pass every keyed value is simultaneously live ([§5.21](#sec-shared-t)'s last-handle-disposes invariant, normatively [§28.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-shared-ownership)/Appendix C), so no address freed by a disposed value can be reused by another live one mid-pass.

**Disposal on deserialize.** Reconstructing a shared node returns `owned Shared<T>` — the caller's single vote, the handle at count one — and every alias in the graph is reconstructed by `Clone()`ing off the identity table, so the last handle disposes the value exactly once (the exactly-once invariant of [§28.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-shared-ownership)/Appendix C). A class that merely *holds* `Shared<T>`/`Weak<T>` fields, rather than *being* the shared node, returns `owned C`; the distinction is locally decidable from the entry type. Double-free is structurally impossible: the value is released only at the last handle, behind the disposed guard, and `Weak<T>` never owns.

**Weak-reachability is enforced where shape always was — at read.** A `Weak<T>` back-reference is meaningful only if its target is *strongly* reached elsewhere in the same serialized graph; a `Weak` whose owning `Shared` is not serialized would resolve to nothing. This is a per-*value* property the field types cannot decide — type identity is not value identity, and the strong owner typically lives in an enclosing object, not a preceding same-class field — so it is enforced the way `serialized tree`/`serialized graph` shape always was, **dynamically**: a `Weak` `$ref` to a target never strongly tagged raises `SerializationException` ([§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis)'s established read-side assert-and-enforce). A cheap static pre-check — a `Weak<T>` field is suspect unless some `Shared<T>` of the same `T` is serialized in the same class — is a lint that catches the trivial intra-class mistake early; it is necessary-not-sufficient and does not stand in for the dynamic guarantee. This keeps assert-and-enforce exactly where the keyword model kept it (the property the compiler cannot check is checked at read), now for the one obligation the types alone cannot carry.

**Polymorphic dispatch survives, rehomed — with one coupling rule retired.** The explicit closed registry (v0.122) — discriminator written first, dispatched through a closed registry on read, anything outside it raising — is orthogonal to graph shape and is **retained in mechanism**, including the security property that makes it closed-or-raise (the reflective `$type` remote-code-execution vector recorded in §5.19's v0.122 paragraph). What changes is more than its syntactic home: today the registry "may appear only together with a graph-shape assertion" and each registered type is eligible by "carrying a graph-shape clause" ([§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis)). With the clauses gone, that coupling is dissolved and the eligibility predicate is restated in type-driven terms (a registered type must be type-driven serialization-eligible — `ISerializable`/`IDeserializable` with serialization-eligible components); the registry itself moves to a class-body member declaration listing the discriminator-to-type map. The closed-registry security argument and the type-scoping are unchanged.

**The honest accounting of "complete."** v0.124's claim was true of the wire-and-synthesis layer and stays true of it: every documented graph shape encodes and decodes. What it could not deliver — and could not, lacking a shared-ownership primitive — was an ownership-valid *construction* of a cyclic input, so the cyclic feature was complete-but-unexercisable, which is the #703 gap rather than a wire defect. "Rendered unnecessary" therefore applies cleanly to the two-phase machinery: a cycle now closes through a `Weak<T>` → `$ref` on an already-tagged node, the ordinary DAG resolve path, with no forward reference and no mutable post-construction window to prove unobservable; the v0.120/v0.123 unobservability-under-constructor-side-effects obligation is not merely discharged but off the critical path. "Given an ownership-valid form" applies cleanly to construction. The completeness is not retracted; it is recorded as premise-bound, and a later, independently-motivated capability ([§5.21](#sec-shared-t)) superseded the machinery the earlier constraint forced.

**The staging.** The migration follows the same additive-then-resolving discipline as every prior serialization stage. `Shared<T>`/`Weak<T>` are first admitted to the recursive serialization-eligibility set (purely additive: a class with these fields enters the identity path; every existing `owned`+borrow class is unaffected). Then the graph-shape clauses are removed, the field types having superseded them, the test surface migrated to express shape through types, and the `serialized cyclic` positive tests — which never had an ownership-valid producer — retired rather than rewritten. The `$id`/`$ref` envelope is unchanged throughout, so the one behavioural difference a user can observe is the intended one: a `Shared<T>` value's disposal happens at the last handle's release, anywhere in the program, rather than at a single owner's destruction — a contract of the shared-ownership type ([§5.21](#sec-shared-t)) made visible through serialization, not a regression in it.

**Inheritance (v0.316).** A derived class's synthesized form carries its base chain's components, base-first, and its `Deserialize` constructs through an ordinary constructor call that binds the inherited components by name (spec §9.8). Three grounds. An instance's state includes its inherited fields, so a form without them does not round-trip the instance. The `polymorphic` registry already presupposes it: a concrete base registers itself beside derived types that inherit its storage, and each registered type is written in that type's own form (spec §9.8). And the ordinary constructor call the synthesis already makes constructs the base through the constructor's own chain, so there is no second construction path to specify. Each base that declares components must opt in itself, by declaring a serialization interface and leaving its members to synthesis: a base's fields, its private ones included, become wire names and constructor-parameter names only by its own author's opt-in, which already makes them that base's wire contract, so an edit to a base that never opted in cannot change a derived class's wire form; and a base whose author hand-wrote its form has chosen a wire contract that a field-by-field derived form would bypass. Either interface is the opt-in, because the derived class's `Deserialize` constructs through its own constructor and never calls the base's factory; so an abstract base with storage, which has no instance for a factory of its own to construct, opts in with `ISerializable` alone. The requirement is the narrow start: lifting it later admits more classes and breaks none. The derived class's synthesized members are its own conformance's, not declarations hiding the base's (spec §12.2), as the `polymorphic` registry's derived types' already are.

**Declined:** serializing only a class's own components, which leaves a base's state to whatever its constructor chooses and loses data; rejecting synthesis over any base with components, which strands the concrete-base `polymorphic` shape; rejecting only `Deserialize` there, which makes eligibility direction-dependent, as spec §9.8 declines for a record's collection components; synthesizing over a base that never opted in, or that hand-wrote its form, for the reasons above; requiring each base to declare every interface the derived class declares, which leaves an abstract base with storage no way to opt in to a derived class's `IDeserializable`; and nesting a base's components as a sub-object, which gives one instance two objects on the wire and a reserved field name per level, where the flat form needs none.

**A derived instance behind a base type (v0.317).** The synthesized `Serialize` of a class with no `polymorphic` registry of its own raises `SerializationException` when the run-time type is a derived class (spec §9.8). The class's form omits a derived class's components and reads back as the base, so writing it would drop data or change the type without a sign, which spec §9.8's *Field-name customization* rules out when it says adding a component never silently drops it. The check sits in the member itself, so one rule covers a base-typed component, a base-typed receiver, and a derived class that inherits its base's member without declaring the interface. It is the registry's closed-set rule with the class as its one member; a registry is how a hierarchy widens the set. The rule asks nothing static about derived classes: under separate compilation (spec §2.8) whether a class has subclasses is not known where a component is declared, and every class not `sealed` can have one. The disposition is the one this section takes for weak-reachability: a bad state is made unrepresentable where a static rule can reach it, and detected, never silent, where it is a per-value property the types cannot decide. Whether the value behind a base type is a derived instance is such a property, except for an `abstract` or `closed` class with no registry and a synthesized `Serialize`: every non-null value written through that member would raise and its `Deserialize` has nothing to construct, so such a class is rejected statically where an owning edge or a `Shared<T>` would call the member, and as an `IDeserializable` with no hand-written `Deserialize`. A back-edge never calls the member and stays admitted. **Declined:** a compile error at a derived class that declares components and inherits a synthesized `Serialize` without declaring `ISerializable`. A subclass that is never serialized would have to become serializable: a field of a non-serializable type would need `serialized skip`, which needs a defaultable type, so a non-nullable owned connection would have to become nullable to serve a serializer it never meets, the type deformed by how it would be read (§5.19). Nor would the rule close the base-typed receiver: a subclass that redeclares the interface, reached through its base, still runs the base's member. A warning at the same place was declined for the same reason: it would fire on subclasses that are never serialized, and the language has no spelling for "this class is not serialized". Also declined: rejecting a component whose static type is not `sealed`, which spec §9.8 rules out ("`sealed` is irrelevant to serialization") and which would reject the stdlib's own `Node` example; rejecting it when a derived class exists, which separate compilation cannot decide; and dispatching to the run-time type's own form without a discriminator, which writes a form the static type's `Deserialize` cannot read.

**The hand-written edges stay closed (v0.320).** Three owner decisions keep the current rules. A `serialized skip` or `serialized optional` component's type must be defaultable whatever conformances the class declares ([#5497](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5497)). Reading the requirement as `Deserialize`-only would make eligibility depend on direction, which spec §9.8 declines for a record's collection components and the v0.316 paragraph above declines for a base; a write-only class excludes a non-defaultable component by making it nullable, the cost the v0.317 paragraph states. A derived class does not hand-write `Serialize` or `Deserialize` over a base's synthesized member ([#5505](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5505)). The member would be a shadow selected by static type, which §8.1 declines, and exempting `base.Serialize(s)` from the run-time type check would relax the check that keeps a base-typed write from dropping a derived class's data. The design also left unstated which identity tag a `base.Serialize(s)` of `this` writes, and it has no read counterpart, since `base.` is illegal in the static `Deserialize` (spec §12.3). A hierarchy that needs a form per class hand-writes the base's `Serialize` as `virtual`, which a derived class overrides (spec §12.2). A derived class does not declare a recognized static `Deserialize` over a base's hand-written one ([#5506](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5506)): spec §12.2 gives a static member no `override` escape, so a hand-written hierarchy deserializes through one factory, which dispatches as it chooses. That decision is deferred rather than declined: promoting `IDeserializable.Deserialize` to a static interface member (#1507, proposal 0125 Part B) would make each class's factory a distinct conformance witness, and the question is revisited with it.

<a id="sec-the-affine-model"></a>
### 5.23 The affine model: auto-drop with a must-use warning

*(This section hoists the load-bearing reasoning of the v0.150–v0.152 revisions out of the changelog ([`Bezel_Changelog.md`](Bezel_Changelog.md)) — which is not part of the language definition — into the rationale body, per the 2026-07 documentation review. The changelog entries remain the detailed record.)*

Bezel's ownership model was originally strict-linear on the normal path: an owned local that reached scope end undischarged — neither moved, returned, stored, nor disposed — was a hard compile error. Three pressures broke that position:

1. **The language was already affine at the seams.** The exception path has always disposed live owned values during unwinding (nobody proposes a compile error for "an exception happened while you held a resource"), and v0.149 made *unbound owned temporaries* auto-dispose at end of statement. That left the absurdity that *naming* a value changed whether the program compiled: `new Foo().Use();` compiled while `var a := new Foo(); a.Use();` was an error — a refactoring hazard with no safety content.
2. **The error rejected sound programs.** Every program it rejected had a perfectly defined meaning (dispose at scope end) that the exception path already implemented.
3. **Precedent.** Rust — the reference point for compile-time ownership — is affine: values drop at scope end, and "you probably meant to use this" is a lint (`#[must_use]`), not a rejection. Strict linearity (Austral-style) buys explicitness at the price of ceremony on every early return and every conditionally-consumed value.

The resolution (v0.150 for locals, v0.152 for `owned` parameters) is uniform **affine auto-drop**: an owned value still held at a normal scope exit is disposed there, implicitly, by the same mechanism the exception edge already used — with a **must-use warning** on the implicit drop of a plain `var`. The two declaration forms keep distinct intent: `using var` means scope-pinned (never movable, no warning — you said what you meant); plain `var` means movable, warned if implicitly dropped. The discard `_ := expr;` ([§3.2](#sec-the-discard-_)) becomes the explicit "dispose this now" form. Exactly-once disposal on every path is preserved throughout — the move analysis marks transferred values moved-from, and scope-end disposal skips them.

What was deliberately *kept* strict: `Task`/`Task<T>` discard stays an error (abandoning a running operation is a semantic decision, routed to `Task.FireAndForget`) — and the anonymous `using var _ := task;` is the same decision one scope later, rejected with it (v0.288): nothing can await an unnamed binding, so the form is abandonment by construction, while the named `using var t` idiom stays, a name being exactly what a later `await` needs; moving out of a `using var` stays an error (scope-pinning is the form's entire meaning — the register's one recorded timing carve against the pin is axiom A12, Pattern A chaining, [§5.26](#sec-the-ownership-kernel)); and the §28.8.1 field-restoration rule is untouched (fields are object state, not scope state — auto-drop of a *field* would be a use-after-free generator, not a convenience).

The trade recorded honestly: the compile-time "you forgot to discharge" error is gone, replaced by a warning. Programs that leak by *intent-mismatch* (the author believed something consumed the value) now compile with a diagnostic instead of failing. Implementation experience since v0.150 supports the choice — the warning has proven sufficient signal in practice, and an entire class of false-positive discharge errors (branch-dependent consumption) disappeared with it.

*(v0.184.)* The three-pattern doctrine this section grounds is now stated once, normatively, in spec §28.3 "Choosing a disposal form": `using var` = declared intent + scope pin; plain `var` = movable, warned on the implicit drop; explicit `Dispose()` = an early/conditional release point (exception-safe by this section's own mechanism — the C# dispose-at-end anti-pattern deliberately does not transfer). The site-announced-kill family gained its third member — the discard of a named owned binding, `_ := x;` — under the same no-marker logic as `throw x` and `owned` returns; and the must-use warning was confirmed uniform across body kinds (frame-disposed `owned` parameters of iterators and async methods warn at the declaration, the adoption idiom being the warning-free spelling).

<a id="sec-the-full-expression-boundary"></a>
### 5.24 The full-expression boundary and the `case`-scrutinee extension (v0.193)

Once [§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model)'s model let unbound owned temporaries dispose implicitly "at the end of the full expression," that phrase carried load in a dozen rules — temporary-confined awaits, deferred-activation frame bounds, span admissions — without the spec ever saying what a full expression *is*. The 2026-07 round-2 sweep surfaced programs whose disposal points genuinely turned on the unstated boundary, and v0.193 defines it once (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-unbound-owned-temporaries), #1795): a full expression is a **maximal** expression — one that is not a subexpression of a larger expression — with the positions enumerated (initializers, assignment right-hand sides, expression statements, each evaluation of a condition or pattern guard, `for` sources, `return`/`throw`/`yield return` operands, and `case` scrutinees).

Maximality is the only defensible direction: any narrower boundary disposes a temporary while an enclosing expression may still hold a borrow into it, and every sub-maximal candidate we examined was either unsound or an arbitrary line no reader could predict. (It is also the boundary C++ draws for its temporaries — one of the few places that precedent is genuinely simple.) The refinement that earns its keep is **per-evaluation** boundaries for conditions and guards: a loop condition is a fresh full expression at each iteration, so a condition's temporaries die before the next test runs. Keyed on the lexical occurrence instead, a condition that constructs a probe object would accumulate one undisposed temporary per iteration — an unbounded implicit leak inside the very mechanism §5.23 adopted to make disposal automatic and predictable.

The one place the boundary is deliberately wider than the producing expression is the `case` scrutinee: a scrutinee temporary lives through the whole construct, in statement position exactly as in expression position. Pattern bindings are borrows whose source is the scrutinee, so disposing the temporary at arm *selection* would kill every binding under the selected arm's feet (`case pool.PopFirst() of var f: FastConnection => f.Warm(), … end;`). In expression position this already followed from maximality — the enclosing full expression contains the whole construct — so the ruling aligns statement position with what expression position already did, rather than inventing new machinery. The alternative was the [§9.7](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-records-and-disposal)-style rejection: forbid binding patterns over temporary scrutinees, as spans forbid temporary sources, and extend nothing. Declined, because the rejection could not be contained — to stay coherent it would have had to outlaw the expression-position form too, and that form was long-admitted and idiomatic. The stretch is recorded as the bounded thing it is: the boundary ends with the construct that syntactically contains the scrutinee, everywhere else §9.7's reject-not-extend posture stands, and Bezel still has no general lifetime-extension mechanism.

**v0.249 addendum: the second recorded stretch — the scoped await-bind (proposal 0082, [#3048](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3048)).** The paragraph above closed with one recorded stretch; the count is now two. Spec [§30.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-scoped-await-bind)'s scoped await-bind admits `var x := await FetchConfigAsync();` (the `const` spelling, and the completed-task `.Result` read, in lockstep) by desugaring the declaration into the hidden `using var` slot the programmer would otherwise have been told to write. The containment is the same recorded kind as the scrutinee's, and structurally tighter on every axis: **one construct** (a fresh `var`/`const` declaration whose initializer is exactly the await of a fresh unbound producer — a syntactic form plus one classification the compiler already computes at every await site), **one desugaring** (the slot *is* a `using var` at the same line; the rule is definable — and was prototyped — as macro-expansion into today's language, the #3048 stage-1 prototype measuring zero new analyses, zero freeze extension, zero checked-mode change), **one bound** (the innermost enclosing block: the same reader-computable bound the two-line form has, against the declined general mechanism's arbitrary consumer lifetimes). The honest asymmetry with the scrutinee precedent, restated rather than smoothed over: that stretch was *forced* — rejecting it would have outlawed a long-admitted form — while this one is *elective*, since the two-line form always exists; an elective stretch needed evidence, which is why it waited on the 0051 Part B gate (C1 measured at 7/11 strict Pattern A sites; C2 satisfied on the letter with the spirit-reading caveat recorded; C3 answered by prototype). The conceded cost stays recorded with the decision: the **invisible lifetime** — the line reads as "cfg is the config" while an unnamed slot holds the config until a scope end the line does not mention — which §30.5's teaching diagnostics and error-participation wording narrow but do not remove. The boundary ends with the innermost enclosing block of the one binding form; everywhere else reject-not-extend stands; Bezel still has no general lifetime-extension mechanism.

**v0.314 addendum: a callee that stores a temporary argument ([#5360](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5360)).** A fresh value passed to a plain parameter is a call-bounded borrow of an unbound temporary, and a callee may store that parameter in a field. The borrowed-field rule admits the store from any plain parameter, on the caller's recorded obligation that the source outlives the object (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules)). The call is admitted and the temporary is disposed at its full-expression boundary all the same; a later use through the stored reference is the checked-disposal fault. Two alternatives were declined. *Rejecting the call when the callee's body stores the parameter* would key a caller's verdict on information from inside another declaration's body. No per-body fact records that a parameter is stored (proposal 0105 §1), and the verdict of one call would change when its callee became virtual, moved behind an interface, or moved to another module, where no body is visible. It is also the general form of the extension spec [§15.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-collection-literals) declines for `Add`/`Insert`: "an opaque callee's signature type-announces no retention"; [§5.28](#sec-silent-tracking) already recorded that `coll.Add(new X())` and `sink.Consume(new X())` are indistinguishable plain-parameter calls. The language rejects a temporary where a declaration announces the retention: a returned frame, a frame taken by an `owned` parameter, a borrow-bearing record's or choice's field, a `scoped` parameter, a `ref` or `out` location. `scoped` is the spelling that turns a back-reference into a statically checked bound. *Keeping the temporary alive while a callee may retain it* is the general lifetime-extension mechanism the boundary excludes: the disposal point would depend on another body, no reader could see the extended lifetime, and the stored reference would still dangle once the extended lifetime ended. A scope-bound local passed to a storing plain parameter is the same rule at a wider boundary: admitted under the same obligation, disposed at its scope's end.

**v0.316 addendum: property assignments of borrow-carrying values ([#5387](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5387)).** An auto-property's assignment stores into its backing field (spec §23.1), and a block-form setter's `value` is a plain parameter (spec §23.3), so one assignment drew the field-store verdict under one accessor form and the plain-parameter verdict above under the other. Through an interface or a `virtual` member the assigning site cannot tell which applies. The verdict is read at the assignment, as a store into a non-`owned` field of the receiver (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules), *property stores of a borrow-carrying value*), as v0.314 ruled for function values ([§4.2](#sec-why-function-typed-values-are-runtime-managed)). A settable instance property is the declaration that announces the retention here: any implementation of it may be an auto-property, whose assignment is a store. This is also the reading spec axiom A4 already gives the write face: an assignment through `set(owned)` is a store into an owned field (K3.1) whatever the setter's body does, and an assignment through a plain setter is the store into a non-`owned` field. Two alternatives were declined. *Reading the assignment by the accessor form the site resolves* gives one object two verdicts: `obj.P := local` is rejected through the concrete type and admitted through an interface, and a fresh value admitted through an interface dangles in the backing field it reaches once its statement ends. *Reading every property assignment as a call of the setter* would turn the static rejections that reach an auto-property's assignment, a borrow of a scope-bound local in a field (spec §28.3) and a fresh value in a non-`owned` field (spec §28.6), into the plain-parameter residual for the commonest property form. The adopted rule's cost is narrow: a block-form setter that only reads its `value` can no longer receive a fresh value or a borrow of a scope-bound local, where a method with a plain parameter can, and a block-form setter of a borrow-bearing record type, such as a span, receives only a null-source value; the remedy is a method.

**v0.317 addendum: static property assignments ([#5487](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5487)).** Static properties are not dispatched, and spec §11.8 defers static interface properties, so a site always resolves the declaration it assigns through. The accessor-form split exists at statics all the same. Spec §22.12 rule 2 admits a mutable static auto-property of class type only at a synchronized-handle type or `Task?`, and there an assignment stores into a static that discharges a fresh value into process lifetime. A block-form setter at the same type takes `value` as a plain parameter, so a fresh value would be disposed at its full-expression boundary even when the setter stores it. The uniform reading carries over: a static property assignment is a store into a non-`owned` static declared as the property is (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-borrow-lifetime-rules), *property stores of a borrow-carrying value*). At an admitted type a fresh value is discharged and lives to process exit whatever the setter does; a setter that drops `value` therefore leaves it undisposed until exit, the same bounded cost spec §22.13 accepts for a displaced static. At any other type a settable static property is necessarily block-form, and a fresh value is the fresh-store error, as it is for an instance block-form setter. A `static readonly` auto-property is assignable only in its type's static initialization, and that assignment is the `static readonly` field store spec §22.13 already discharges. The ground is #5360's: rewriting an auto-property in block form must not change a client's verdict or the point at which its value is disposed. Two alternatives were declined. *Reading the assignment by accessor form* changes both when the form changes, and leaves a disposed referent in whatever static a block-form setter stores `value` into. *Rejecting every fresh value at a static property* would withdraw the store semantics spec §22.12 rule 2 gives an admitted static auto-property, the `Task?` cache among them, and the discharge spec §22.13 gives a `static readonly` one. The cost: a block-form static setter of a class type outside rule 2's forms receives no fresh value and no borrow of a scope-bound local; the remedy is a `set(owned)` setter or a static method.

**v0.316 addendum: no caller-side warning keyed on the callee's body ([#5374](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5374)).** Spec [§15.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-collection-literals) warns when a fresh temporary reaches `Add`, `Insert` or the indexer-set of a borrowing collection. Those positions are element stores the standard library defines, so they store on every call; the warning rests on that knowledge, not on the signature, which §15.4 itself says type-announces no retention. A user callee's plain parameter carries no such knowledge, so the same warning at `holder.Hold(new Item())` would need a per-parameter fact computed from the callee's body ("this parameter may reach a field or borrowing-collection store") and carried in its artifact interface. That is a wider sibling of the derived-retention fact proposal 0132 studied and deferred. 0132's fact counts only stores into `readonly` fields of `this`, a narrowing a temporary does not need, since its source dies at the full expression whatever the holder does next. 0132's objections apply to the wider fact unchanged. A body edit would change what another module's build prints, keyed on a fact no signature shows ([§9.7](#sec-pure-declared-not-derived)'s third reason; proposal 0048's refactoring seam). The warning would fire at a statically bound callee and stay silent at a `virtual`, interface or function-value call to the same body. The fact is also a may-fact, a store on some path, short of the certainty §15.4's warning rests on. And the spelling that turns the hazard into a checked bound exists: `scoped`, which rejects a fresh producer at the call, or `owned`, which takes the value. The temporary is the shape in which a caller-side warning would be most certain, since the source dies at the full expression's own boundary and no dominance analysis is needed. It is recorded as an instance for 0132's reopening, not as a separate mechanism. Until then the trust-admission report names the store at the callee (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-diagnostics)).

<a id="sec-deferred-activation-capture"></a>
### 5.25 Callbacks that outlive the call: frame residence and capture-carried sources (v0.196)

The capture rules of spec [§28.14](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-scoped-callback-parameters) classify a plain function-typed parameter as *call-bounded* — every captured source trivially outlives the call, because the callback is not retained past it. That premise is false for precisely the callees whose point is deferral: an iterator, async iterator, or lazy query operator captures its callback into a frame that runs after the call returns. v0.179 had noticed the async half and added the settlement obligation, but iterators had neither a callee-side rule nor a caller-side one — and on the caller side, the frame's carried-source enumeration (receiver plus plain reference-typed arguments) never included *capture sets*, so returning an iterator frame built over a capturing callback (`return Doubled(nums, lambda(x) => scale.Apply(x));` with `scale` a `using var`) passed the frame-return check by the letter, while the same frame with `scale` passed as a plain argument was rejected at the same return site: a statically certain dangle, guarded by nothing. The v0.196 ruling (#1790) closes both halves with the announcement-by-declared-type doctrine this chapter keeps returning to. Callee side, **frame residence is the admitted retention and the frame's life is the bound** — settlement for a task, completion-or-disposal for an iterator frame, the single consumption point for a lazy query — type-announced by the deferred-activation return type exactly as that type already announces the frame's carried object borrows (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-returning-a-deferred-activation-frame)). Caller side, a callback argument contributes its captured borrows' **sources** to the frame's carried-source set, so the ordinary borrow machinery — not a new check — rejects the dangling composition at the return site.

The contribution is computed from statically known provenance, transitively (a captured closure contributes *its* sources in turn), and the provenance table's edge rows are where the design earns its keep. A function value fetched from a plain field or a collection contributes nothing — *soundly*, because the closure-escape rules never admitted a borrow-capturing closure into such storage in the first place: plainly-stored function values are borrow-free by construction, and no whole-program analysis is needed. (The one storing location that does hold capture-bearing callbacks — a `scoped` field — contributes nothing only because the read-out is itself barred: passing a `scoped` field's value into a deferred-activation plain position is rejected as an escaping read-out, spec [§28.14](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-scoped-callback-parameters), v0.196 — the liftable widening, contributing the field's owner as the source, is recorded there.) And a plain function-typed *parameter* of the enclosing body contributes *itself*: the parameter becomes a carried source that every caller re-establishes against its own actual argument, which is what keeps the forwarding combinator — `Filtered(xs, f) => xs.Where(f)` — compositional. (Rejecting opaque parameter arguments instead was declined as outlawing half the motivation.) This is the same signature-level re-establishment as [§5.7](#sec-factory-method-ownership)'s source-set rule and §12.10's callee-side task rule; the three rulings share one skeleton.

**A captured value that holds borrows is counted by what it carries (v0.331, [#5677](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5677); proposal [0148](proposals/0148-captured-holds-borrows-value-contributes-its-sources.md)).** A span, a borrow-bearing record or inline array, or a choice holding a class payload lives in its capture cell like an `int`, and the cell is what the closure keeps alive — but the cell holds borrows, and those borrows' sources are what can die. The direct count was already in place: the same value passed as an argument "contributes the sources of the borrows it carries", and a live local of such a type freezes every held borrow's source at a consume site. Provenance clause 1 now counts the capture the same way, and the two exemptions ("a value-typed capture contributes nothing"; the §28.14 bound's "captured value-typed local or parameter") are narrowed to values that carry no borrow. One consequence is deliberate: a closure over such a value is now capture-bearing even when every carried borrow is a parameter's, so the sink rule keeps it out of returns and fields as it keeps a closure over a class-typed parameter (v0.307) — the caller of a method returning `() -> int` gets a function value carrying the empty set and could carry it past the array the captured span views. A method reference bound to such a value is counted the same way, its receiver being a by-copy capture. The alternatives were a flat ban on capturing such a value, which rejects the in-place invocation and the in-scope lazy query that §9.7's capture bullet and stdlib §4.16 admit, and a route list in §9.7's capture bullet (invocation results, frames, storage-bounded holders), which is the closure-specific list the closure bullet forbids and reopens at the next route. The set is the one mechanism; adding the item to it is the whole fix.

Two postures were declined by name. Spelling the retention `scoped` is the wrong tool on four counts: `scoped` means storage-bounded-by-an-*object*, with an admission and freeze built for open-ended object lifetimes, while a frame is a flow-tracked value the borrow machinery already bounds; v0.180 bars `scoped` on static methods without `owned` returns — a compile error for the dominant iterator shape; its admission forms exclude plain-parameter forwarding, half the motivation again; and it would force a breaking migration of every lazy standard-library operator. And leaving the retention *documented but unverified* — the pre-v0.179 async posture — was declined more firmly than anywhere else it has come up: v0.179 already rejected that posture for async, and the iterator case is worse, because the frame is long-lived *by design* (lazy evaluation is the feature), so the documented gap would be load-bearing for a statically certain soundness break rather than a hedge over a lifetime the checker cannot compare. What the declines protect: no standard-library signature changes (the lazy operators' plain signatures become the normative instance of the general rule, not a certified exception), async upgrades from trust to rejection — statically-certain dangles at return sites, and categorically, paralleling object-borrow escapes, at store and scope sites, and user code can finally write callback-taking iterators at all.

<a id="sec-the-ownership-kernel"></a>
### 5.26 The ownership kernel: reasons and the axiom budget (v0.198)

Spec §28.3 opens with the ownership kernel — nine principles, K1–K9, from which the chapter's rules derive — and closes its kernel subsection with a register of eleven recorded axioms and four tombstones (A1–A15; A1 retired v0.201, A2 retired v0.258, A8 retired v0.213, A13 retired v0.260): the rules the kernel cannot derive, each a design decision with a recorded reason. This section carries the reasons, and the discipline the register installs.

**Why a kernel.** The ownership chapters grew by accretion of rulings — each individually reasoned, collectively unpredictable: a reader could not derive the rule they hadn't read. Consolidating the load-bearing principles into one normative statement buys three things at once. *Predictability*: the freeze's membership, the admitted await positions, and the equalization patterns all compute from stated principles instead of being memorized as lists. *Auditability*: the "memory rules few enough to learn in full" bet (§1) finally has a count — nine principles plus the axiom register's rows are what "in full" contains (the count is the register's to state at any revision; the learnability claim is the *discipline* — visible append, tombstoned delete — not any fixed number). *Mechanizability*: a finite kernel is a modelable one, and a derivation-tagged chapter is one whose diagnostics can explain rejections by derivation. The kernel is deliberately prose, not formal notation: the spec's register is precise prose for the guides' audience, and the formal artifact has a better home in a mechanized model of the kernel, which can be exact without being normative.

**The budget discipline.** The axiom count is published as the language's honest complexity budget. A future ruling that touches §28/§30.5 states its classification — a derivation (naming the K's), an axiom extension (naming the axiom), or a new axiom (adding a register row with its recorded reason). This is a changelog-entry obligation on the spec-change checklist. Axiom ids are frozen: new axioms append; deletions leave a tombstone row pointing at the deleting revision's changelog entry, so "which axiom did this ruling extend?" stays answerable historically. Two candidate axioms were examined during consolidation and classified derived instead — the v0.196 property-getter-only static-storage admission (derives from K2.4 + the declaration-site static-return rule, v0.201 — at consolidation time, K2.4 + then-axiom A1: a storage-returning static *method* is signature-indistinguishable from a factory; the property face's read is the sound dual) and the maybe-consumed join (derives from K7 with the v0.184 free-precision argument) — the worked precedent that the register resists both inflation and laundering.

**Why K4.2's criterion is two bits, neither redundant (recorded here since v0.281; formerly a reading note beside the spec's kill table).** A site-announced kill must be *keyword-visible* **and** leave the *disposed-flag backstop*. Each conjunct is decisive against a real kill family, so neither can be dropped: an obligation-moving transfer (the consume table's family-1 rows — `move`, marker-free stores, `owned` returns, owning `yield return`) is keyword- or target-visible yet sets **no flag** — the value lives on under a new holder — so visibility alone would wrongly admit it as announced; drop-on-overwrite (K3.11) **sets the flag** for the displaced value yet announces nothing at the site — a bare `x := e;` — so the backstop alone would wrongly admit it. The two-bit form is the smallest criterion that excludes both.

**The axioms' reasons.** Where an axiom's full reasoning already lives in this document, the register row points here and this list cross-references it rather than duplicating:

- **A1 (tombstone, retired v0.201)** — [§5.7](#sec-factory-method-ownership): the v0.60 factory-default bridge, retired when its motives discharged; static class- or interface-typed returns now type-announce in the signature (spec §28.3). The register keeps the tombstone so the extension point stays answerable historically.
- **A2 (tombstone, retired v0.258)** — [§12.10](#sec-calls-mint-storage-lends): the task/iterable calls-mint split, retired when the fence-accretion ledger met the reversal's new-evidence bar — calls now mint for the iterable family too, the stored-collection view respelled to the property face exactly as v0.190 respelled the stored task; the register keeps the tombstone so the extension point stays answerable historically.
- **A3 (the two-container async design)** — [§12.1](#sec-pattern-a-vs-pattern-b-is-type-directed)–[§12.2](#sec-choosing-between-taskt-and-taskowned-t): fan-out versus acquisition is a surface design choice the producer makes in the signature; the kernel checks both flows, forces neither.
- **A4 (accessor faces are lend-only)** — signature predictability: an accessor read has no marker position, so admitting owned production through a getter would make ownership invisible at every property read; factories are methods, where the `owned` return announces the transfer (a marker-free position). (Recorded here; spec §23/§28.6 state the rule.)
- **A5 (`using var` is a scope pin)** — [§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model): the form's entire meaning is declared intent plus scope-pinning; movability was deliberately kept out at the affine transition so that the two declaration forms keep distinct intents.
- **A6 (`Task` discard rejection)** — [§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model) and [§3.2](#sec-the-discard-_): abandoning a running operation is a semantic decision, not a disposal; the routed spelling (`Task.FireAndForget`) keeps the decision visible. The anonymous `using var _` binding is inside the axiom's reach (v0.288): unawaitable, it is the discard one scope later.
- **A7 (no `??`-operand/operator-operand transfer; narrowed v0.209, wording corrected v0.246)** — the register's liftable bound, half-lifted as designed: proposal 0056 supplied the arm spelling (`owned x` in its era's token, `move x` since v0.246 — if-expression branches and expression-`case` arm results, a K3.4 derivation riding K7.4's existing per-path machinery, no new axiom), and the row narrowed to the positions still unspelled. The marker has parsed in the A7 positions since v0.209 (MarkedTransfer was a PrimaryAtom, pinned by `marked_transfer_positions_2080.bzl`), so the axiom's content has always been the recorded rejection rather than a spelling gap — the v0.246 rewording (proposal 0090 §5) corrects the row's earlier loose wording, it is not a consequence of the marker unification. The `??`-operand lift is gated on a genuinely new ruling 0056 records (the post-state of a marked nullable binding evaluated while null), sequenced after the flip so it would land in the unified spelling; operator operands remain unmotivated. No-silent-consume stays uniform: the unmarked read is a borrow arm.
- **A8 (tombstone, retired v0.213)** — [§5.3](#sec-collections-do-not-own-their-elements)/[§5.1](#sec-owned-not-borrows): the caller-vouched transit disposal, retired when the 2026-07 corpus sweep (#2075) found zero load-bearing clients — the values-owned shape is `OwningDictionary`'s by construction and the own-keys direction has none; the register keeps the tombstone so the extension point stays answerable.
- **A9 (the two collection families)** — [§5.3](#sec-collections-do-not-own-their-elements)/[§5.11](#sec-owned-element-collections): the v0.97 resolution after the `<owned T>` merged form (v0.88–v0.96) collapsed under its substitution machinery.
- **A10 (stdlib certification grants)** — a named allowlist of trust granted to specific stdlib types (`CancellationToken`'s source-outlives-settlement certification; the §30.11 completion-source callback admission, a named carve beside §28.14 rather than a derivation of it): by construction not derivable — the grant is the stdlib vouching for its own implementation — and acceptable only because the list is closed, named, and normatively stated at each type.
- **A11 (the §28.14 admission forms and bounds)** — decidability engineering (#1421): the chosen static story for "capture source outlives bounding object" among several sound alternatives; several bounds are recorded liftable, and lifting one swaps an axiom clause for a derivation without touching the principle (K2.3 + K5.7).
- **A12 (chaining's timing dispensation)** — a recorded timing carve against A5's pin for the one composition where the pinned slot already lives in the async frame: deferral to the outer task's teardown preserves exactly-once transitively and moves nothing.
- **A13 (tombstone, retired v0.260)** — the move-only reassignment carve, recorded as debt (tracked #1385, interacting with the then-open #747 design) precisely so it could not be mistaken for intended design — and retired exactly that way: proposal 0101's study tested the recorded ground ("a partial state the compiler cannot cheaply dispose") against the shipped machinery and found it discharged — the disposal pass #747 gated is normative (spec §15.1.1) and the reference compiler had already implemented the per-slot overwrite disposal against #1385's own sketch — so whole-value reassignment of an owning inline array now takes §28.7's drop-on-overwrite per live slot, no partial state is ever disposed (a moved-from slot contributes nothing; the kill discharges the displaced value's restoration obligations on K9.3's ground), and the exactly-once claim holds without qualification. The static form-(iv) residue rode §22.13's static-overwrite posture, a recorded rule, not an axiom, until v0.334 retired the form (proposal 0162). The register keeps the tombstone so the extension point stays answerable historically.
- **A14 (no `owned` generic arguments)** — [§5.3](#sec-collections-do-not-own-their-elements)/[§5.11](#sec-owned-element-collections): the substitution machinery was tried for nine revisions and abandoned; named types capture owning semantics without it.
- **A15 (the self-returning enumerator)** — [§12.10](#sec-calls-mint-storage-lends): the announcement table's callee-side set is closed and admits no borrow return, and `this` is a borrow, so §29.1's long-standing permission escapes the set rather than deriving from it — K6.3 demands a fresh or owned frame and a receiver return is neither. Recorded as an axiom rather than as a widening because the admission is keyed on three conjuncts (the class implements both enumeration heads; the member is the protocol's `GetEnumerator()`; the returned expression is literally `this`) and no caller-side classification changes; written generally it would be a design change. The half that makes it sound where the receiver is a borrow the caller does not own is the non-reclaiming disposal: the frame's disposal runs `Dispose()` and leaves the reclaim with the receiver's own owner, idempotence (spec §28.2) doing the rest. The *static* receiver-returning form remains deferred (#3255).

**What the kernel deliberately excludes.** Sendability and the domain memory model (§12.5–§12.7), nullability, and definite assignment are adjacent systems with their own rules; the kernel touches them only at named interface points (spawn/send as consume positions; the domain clause as K4.3's recorded limit). A second kernel for the domain model, if ever warranted, is its own proposal.

<a id="sec-why-using-var-is-not-folded-into-var"></a>
### 5.27 Why `using var` is not folded into plain `var` — or into `const` (v0.205)

The proposal recurs naturally, and this section is its standing answer. Local ownership is inferred, not annotated ([§5.5](#sec-local-variable-ownership-is-inferred-not-annotated)); `const` covers non-owned single assignment (v0.201, [§17.8](#sec-readonly-local-bindings-let-val-readonly-var)); and the affine auto-drop disposes a plain-`var` owned local at the same scope-end point as a `using var` on every non-moved path — the spec's own recap says the forms "never add *safety*; they add **declared intent** and **timing**" (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows), *Choosing a disposal form*). So the keyword can look like ceremony over inference: why not let `var` do everything? The question was examined against the full rule surface at v0.204 and declined, for four reasons that are one reason: **the keyword is not an inference annotation but a declared constraint on the binding's future, and a checker cannot infer the intent it exists to check.** Inference reads the initializer — the past. The keyword constrains what comes after — reassignment, movement, source liveness — which no use pattern present in the code can imply, because the constraint is deliberately stronger than any use.

*First, the pin has no substitute.* A5's guarantees — non-reassignable, never movable out — exist to make *maintenance drift* a compile error at the drift site: a lock guard threaded out of its critical section, a mid-scope re-acquisition, a borrow's source consumed under a view the author meant to hold whole-scope. The borrow-pin (spec [§28.10](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-using-var-initialized-from-a-borrow)) is the only spelling of "keep this source live to the end of this scope" for a source this body does not own — a borrow-returning call, a field, an indexer, a borrow-state binding; its nearest encoding without the keyword is a dummy use on the block's last line — an invariant written as dead code. (Over a named *owned* binding, whose only kills are this body's own consumes of it, the pin's only content would be a freeze against those consumes, and the unmarked spelling is rejected (v0.323): adoption, `using var y := move x;`, freezes more strongly, and `using var` on the source itself is A5. A freeze of an outer owned local confined to an inner block, after its view's last use, has no spelling; [proposal 0137 §5.1](proposals/0137-mark-the-using-var-pin.md) records that cost with the decision.) Movability was deliberately kept out at the affine transition so that the two declaration forms keep distinct, checkable intents ([§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model)); that decision is re-affirmed here, not merely inherited.

*Second, the lint and the keyword are one economy.* The must-use warning is the affine model's compensating diagnostic — the v0.150 trade replaced the hard discharge error with a warning precisely because a zero-ceremony declared-intent spelling exists to silence it ([§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model)). Without `using var`, the warning either fires on every idiomatic scope-held acquisition (`using var guard := mutex.Acquire();` is the standard library's own idiom, stdlib [§6.2](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-mutex-and-lock)), or is silenced by a trailing explicit `Dispose()` — ceremony that corrupts the doctrine that an explicit dispose signals an *early or conditional* release — or is retired, reversing the recorded trade and letting the intent-mismatch leak class go dark. The warning is high-signal because the silencer is zero-cost; the silencer is meaningful because the warning exists. The pair stands or falls together.

*Third, the keyword is a loudness channel for API evolution.* When a callee's return type flips between borrowed and `owned`, today's binding forms grade the blast radius at every call site: `const` sites reject (an owned production is inadmissible there, spec [§21.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-variable-declarations)), plain-`var` sites warn at the new implicit drop, and `using var` sites absorb the change correctly by uniform admissibility. Unification — with the lint necessarily detuned per the previous paragraph — reduces that matrix to silence: sound in every cell, affinity guarantees it, and invisible in exactly the dimension (who disposes, and when) this language undertakes to make visible.

*Fourth, "only `var`" is unreachable anyway.* Whether disposal *awaits* is deliberately not inferable: an implicit disposal point never awaits, so a class whose async cleanup must run needs an `await using var` binding (spec [§28.12](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-asynchronous-disposal)) — and `TaskScope` is consumable by `await using` only (stdlib [§6.10](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-taskscope)). The `out owned` recipient form `out using var` (spec [§22.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-inline-out-declarations)) survives for the same reason. The end state of the proposal is therefore not one binding form; it is the same number of forms with the common one missing.

**Nor does `const` absorb it.** The v0.201 settlement drew this seam deliberately ([§17.8](#sec-readonly-local-bindings-let-val-readonly-var)): `using var` is the owned, scope-pinned binding with declared disposal; `const` is the non-owned one whose bindings never change state — not by assignment, not by consumption, not by disposal — and the permissive owned-`const` was declined then as an undeclared-disposal duplicate of `using var`. The reasons hold from both directions. A `const` that accepted owned producers would hide a real scope-end event behind the one keyword whose entire claim is that nothing ever happens — `const guard := mutex.Acquire()` would release a lock at `end` — and the async surface makes the hiding structural: `await using var` marks a suspension point at scope exit, and an "await const" is incoherent. A `using var` doing `const`'s job forces pin semantics onto every fixed borrow — a `const` borrow's constraint on its source deliberately ends at its last use (spec [§21.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-variable-declarations)), so the source may legitimately be consumed or disposed afterward, a program the pin rejects — and reads absurdly over plain values, which is the pre-v0.201 state §17.8 records as tolerated-not-endorsed (#1360). A single merged fixed-binding form must choose always-pin (rejecting legal `const` programs) or never-pin (deleting the borrow-pin's only spelling, on which the [§29.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-desugaring-of-for) `for`-desugaring and deliberate iterable pinning stand); no intermediate point exists — a pin either is a compiler-enforced guarantee or it is not.

What the examination *conceded* is recorded with equal weight. The keyword adds no safety and no disposal timing on non-moved paths — the spec says so in terms. It does not tell the reader whether the binding is owned: its owned-versus-pin split is dispatched by the right-hand side (spec [§28.10](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-using-var-initialized-from-a-borrow)); at a bare named owned binding, and at an `if`, `case` or `??` arm-meet of such bindings alone, where both readings would be plausible, the form is rejected (v0.323, v0.327), so the split is decided by the right-hand side's visible shape: a producer adopts, a borrow pins. The residual overlap with `const` over inert right-hand sides is real, tolerated for generic and desugaring uniformity, and steered by diagnostics rather than semantics (the vacuous-pin lint, #2073). And the compiler's two-path local classifier is duplication the surface design does not require — the spec defines a `using var`'s state by the same right-hand-side algebra as `var`, so the implementation should compute it through one funnel with the pin and the lint boundary as layered flags (#2071, #2072). Formally, unification introduces no ownership ambiguity: inference is total either way, and always was. What it removes is the reader's instrument, not the checker's — the one binding form whose *scope-boundness* is declared at the declaration, locally visible, and compiler-enforced. The checker never needed `using var`. The reader is who it is for.

<a id="sec-silent-tracking"></a>
### 5.28 Silent tracking: trivial-dispose is a ceremony property, not a lifetime regime (v0.238)

Through v0.237, trivial-dispose bundled two separable exemptions. The **ceremony exemption** — no `using var` needed, no must-use warning, `_ := expr;` cosmetic — is what v0.61 and v0.66 argued for, what [§5.9](#sec-trivial-dispose-classes) records, and what every user-facing surface of the feature teaches. The **tracking exemption** — references to trivial instances could outlive any statically-known bound, with memory reclaimed by an unspecified runtime mechanism ("reference counting, region allocation, or another scheme") — existed only to make abandonment and two specific static-checking carve-outs sound (the spec's old §15.4 fresh-trivial-literal admission and the §22.5 trivial plain-parameter default), and it was the *only* reason any runtime lifetime machinery for user classes needed to exist. v0.238 keeps the first exemption verbatim and retracts the second (proposal 0077; owner-ruled 2026-08-04).

**Why the mechanism went.** The owner's objection was structural: a full reference-counting implementation — cycle policy, atomic counts at domain crossings, "not a GC in the sense Bezel avoids" hedging — is a hidden per-type runtime purchased for a feature whose entire recorded value is ceremony. The audit trail sharpened the case. The docs were internally split: §9.7 rejected `new Bag([10, 20, 30])` on the ground that the literal "has a lifetime that ends at the construction expression" — a statement only true under static tracking — while the mechanism sentences said the opposite; the ownership chapter's machinery (producers, borrow-escape, store rules, K8 boundaries, capture bounds) was already type-agnostic and tracking-shaped by its letter. And the implementation had never built the mechanism at all: sema classified every class-typed `new` as owned regardless of trivial-dispose and rejected every trivial borrow-escape probe, the runtime contained no count, registry, or arena for user class instances, and the abandonment surface was a patchwork of silent leaks plus one ICE (#2654, #2655, #2656, #2660 — the stage-2 conformance trackers). Retracting the tracking exemption aligned the spec with both its own dominant text and the implementation's architecture, and made the no-GC claim unconditional for every class a user can write.

**What silent tracking is.** Every trivial-dispose instance keeps the K1 classification it already carried, now load-bearing: exactly one statically-known owner at every point, with the compiler inserting a **free** — logical disposal (the §28.2 flag, so checked disposal mode backstops every dangle) plus reclamation, running no user code — at the owner's release point. The backstop clause is a mandatory companion, not an option: without it the retraction would have created the language's only un-backstopped dangling class. Cross-domain moves simply transfer the free obligation with the move (spec §30.14) — deleting what would have been the mechanism's hardest corner, cross-domain reclamation. The ceremony exemption survives verbatim as K1.6's rewritten content: the obligation's *user-visible* content is empty, and its discharge is never demanded of the program.

**The literal-tree ownership rule.** The tracking exemption's one real ergonomic pillar was the nested collection literal (`Dictionary<string, Array<int>>` built in one expression). The replacement (spec §15.4) makes the fresh nodes of a literal an **aggregate per maximal borrowing-family subtree**, owned by whatever owns the subtree's root — receiving binding, K8 temporary, owning slot, `owned` parameter/return/field, or static storage — and freed, all nodes as one obligation, at that owner's release point. Membership is fixed at construction; borrowed elements never join. Two of its recorded choices deserve their reasons here. *Per-aggregate conservative freeze* (the ruled Q3): a borrow of an interior node freezes the whole displaced aggregate at reassignment — cheap, coarse, and deliberately revisable, since per-node tracking would only admit more programs; it also keeps the implementation's sole-owner proofs simple, the sharpest risk the conformance audit named. *Maximal borrowing subtree, not outermost literal*: an owning collection inside the tree takes its fresh element nodes into its own cascade at construction, so no node ever has two owners.

**The one regression, and the lint (the ruled Q1).** `groups.Add("odds", [1, 3, 5]);` — incremental construction of nested trivial structures — was ownerless-and-safe only under the mechanism; under silent tracking the argument dies at its K8 boundary and the collection holds a flagged dangle. Rejection was declined because it cannot be scoped by signature — `coll.Add(new X())` and `sink.Consume(new X())` are indistinguishable plain-parameter calls, and the recorded reason the literal-only asymmetry exists ("an opaque callee's signature type-announces no retention") is type-agnostic. So the shape stays admitted, joining the existing recorded hazard for non-trivial arguments — but with a **targeted lint**, because under the retained no-warning rule the shape would otherwise compile with zero diagnostics while the compiler *knows* the argument dies at the boundary: a statically-certain dangle emitted silently is the one place the ceremony exemption's silence stops being honest. The lint is the exemption's counterpart, not its erosion — no `using var` is demanded, the program stays accepted. The documented remedy is the owning family (`OwningDictionary<string, Array<int>>`), whose costs are equally recorded: the owning binding is non-trivial (one `using var` per container), and `OwningDictionary` has no default entry iteration. Recorded follow-on, **taken at v0.243** (proposal 0081, #3047): the same lint shape now serves fresh **non-trivial** arguments in the same store positions — the `Task` family foremost (#1813's fresh-slot trap) and every other non-trivial-dispose class with it — spec §15.4's lint sentence carrying the full type set with the hazard stated per family.

**The doctrine's second and third instances (v0.243; proposal 0081, #3047).** The Q1 lint's design shape — a statically-certain hazard, an admission kept for recorded type-agnostic reasons, a normative warning naming the boundary event and the remedies, no new syntax — was applied twice more at v0.243. *The store lint's type-set extension* (the follow-on above, taken): for a task the identical store was strictly worse than the trivial case that already warned — the discard spelling of the same abandonment (`_ := call();`) is a hard error, and axiom A6 calls a task's loss "a decision, not a disposal" — yet it compiled with zero diagnostics; and a task-only extension was declined because it would have left the middle band (`conns.Add(new Connection("a"))` — non-trivial, non-task) as the *only* fresh store shape with no diagnostic, an inverted asymmetry stranger than the one being fixed. *The terminal-accessor lint* (spec §29.11; stdlib §5): a class-element terminal accessor (`First()` and family) over a pipeline whose enumerator owns the elements — a minting one at v0.243, and from v0.268 an adopt-then-yield one too (spec §29.11, #3285) — returns a borrow of an element the terminal's own internal enumerator already disposed — dead on arrival, Guide 5's trap #4 — and the checker already computes everything the predicate needs (§29.11's provenance trace; §29.2's per-yield-site ownership classification). Its v1 reach is deliberately **same-compilation-unit iterators only** — zero false positives, with a cross-module minting iterator escaping the lint and keeping the checked-mode backstop; the widening (carrying yield classification in the artifact interface, spec §2.8.2) is a recorded liftable, not taken. **No per-site suppression spelling exists for any of the three lints**, deliberately: the remedies — bind to a named holder; materialize or take the owning path — are one line each, already sanctioned, and convert the program from hazardous to sound, whereas a suppression spelling would be a new way to write the silent dangle the lints exist to remove; project-level lint tuning remains the only knob (spec §28.3's stated posture for the must-use warning).

**The doctrine's fourth and fifth instances (v0.307; proposal 0129).** Two collection shapes the guides taught as traps became diagnostics on the same terms. *The mutation-during-iteration warning* (spec §29.12): a structural call whose receiver is the very binding the enclosing `for` iterates, from which the loop's next advance is reachable, is certain to raise `InvalidOperationException` at that advance — the runtime rule is unchanged and the warning names it early; the predicate is *advance reachable from the call*, not dominance, because the common trap is the conditional `if p(x) then xs.Remove(x); end;` (certain on the branch that runs it) while the sanctioned `xs.Remove(x); break;` never advances and must stay silent. A modification through a borrow copy, a parameter, or a call is not traced — the same-binding predicate is local and exact, and following aliases would be the borrow-liveness checking §5.1 declines. *The element-borrow use-after-removal warning* (spec §28.11): an owning collection's `Remove`/`RemoveAt`/`Clear` dispose what they remove, and a borrow taken by the indexer, by default iteration, or by a §29.11-traced accessor and used after a *dominating* such call on the same binding is §28.2's dominating-kill shape with the kill on the collection — K4.2 + K7.4 with the source from K2.1, "free precision" in the v0.184 sense: the per-path state exists, the borrow's source is already resolved, and only the straight-line, same-index subset with no structural operation between the borrow and the kill is claimed; a conditional kill stays with the checked-mode backstop, as §28.2's conditional `Dispose()` does not here become an error for a borrow. An indexer-set at the same constant index is a certain dangle the warning does not yet name — declined for v1 so the two lints and §29.12 share one operation vocabulary — and a keyed `OwningDictionary` removal after a same-key borrow is the same shape over key equality; both are recorded liftable in the text. The predicate of each lint is stated over the loop's, or the body's, own callable body: a structural call inside a lambda the loop only defines is not a call the loop advances after, and is outside lint A by construction rather than by exemption. **No per-site suppression spelling exists for any of the five lints.**

**The definition amendment (the ruled Q2).** Requiring `owned` on fresh-tree fields (the uniform resolution of the fresh-store ambiguity) collided with the definition: an `owned` field flipped its holder non-trivial, so wrapper types — a user `Counter`-shape holding an `Array<int>` — would have lost the ceremony exemption for holding exactly what the free-cascade handles without user code. The definition therefore disregards `owned` fields of trivial-dispose type, recursively and per-instantiation: the synthesized cascade over such fields frees memory and runs no user code, so "disposal provably runs no user code and releases only memory" — the definition's amended prose — stays true in the sense that matters. The costs were ruled on with it: the definition is carried in artifact interfaces (spec §2.8.2), enters the delta classification (§2.8.6 — where verdict-*gained* is preserving, so the amendment's one-way effect is benign), amends §3.6's property-table row, and composes with sendability (§30.14 rule 2 already admits `owned` sendable-class fields, so a type can be simultaneously trivial-dispose and sendable — exactly the composition the moved-obligation clause carries). It also gives composite stdlib types their honest story: `Counter<K>` and the sorted forms hold backing *intrinsically* — a single-allocation layout fact — and the user-expressible spelling of the same shape is now `owned` trivial fields.

**Alternatives considered and declined.**

1. **Status quo plus the layered runtime** (escape analysis → arenas → domain-local RC with elision → atomic promotion at domain crossings; cycles leak with tooling). Workable and previously sketched — but every layer past the first two would have to be built from nothing (the implementation audit found zero), the cycle policy and the "not a GC" hedging never go away, and the owner's objection stands: a hidden per-type runtime purchased for a feature whose value is ceremony, which the retraction delivers without any of it.
2. **Restricting trivial-dispose to provably acyclic types** (so a simple mechanism without cycle detection would suffice). Declined for non-locality: acyclicity is a property of the global type-reference graph — "can anything I reach, reach me" — not of a type's own declaration. Enforcing it is either non-modular (a private field addition in one module flips classifications in another) or brutally conservative (every `Object`/interface field assumed cyclic, reclassifying the tier's most valuable inhabitants). It would also break §3.6's stated basis that a derived property is computed from the type's *structure* — its declaration and, for a generic, its type arguments: a global-graph property is computed from *other* declarations. (The Q2 amendment does not share this defect, though a fast reading might suggest it: the distinction is direction and boundedness.
