The trivial-dispose check follows *outgoing* owned-field edges — dependencies the declaration itself names, already carried in artifact interfaces (§2.8.2) and already traversed the same way by sendability's rule 2 — while acyclicity needs the *incoming* edge set, "who can reach me," which no artifact carries and no declaration bounds.)
3. **Uniform loud ceremony** (all classes tracked *and* warned). Declined by the recorded v0.61→v0.66 history: the warning had no content then and has none now. Silent tracking is precisely this option minus the noise.
4. **A declared opt-out marker** (or the empty-destructor idiom) letting performance-sensitive classes exit the mechanism. Solves nothing structural — the mechanism must still exist for everyone else — and adds an axis. Subsumed: under silent tracking there is nothing to opt out of.
5. **Uniform silence** (the mirror of alternative 3, added v0.239 when the owner posed it directly: if trivial-dispose only drops ceremony, why not drop the ceremony for destructor-bearing classes too — Rust-style implicit drop for everything?). Declined, and the retraction is what makes the reason precise. Nothing about ceremony is load-bearing for correctness — every class is tracked, auto-drop disposes correctly on every path, and the compiler could go silent for `Connection` tomorrow without a leak. What ceremony *is*, now that it is provably not a tracking tax, is the **announcement of observable disposal effects**: a trivial-dispose class sheds it because its disposal has no observable content, while a destructor-bearing class's disposal is an *effect* — a file closes, a lock releases, a subscription unhooks — and *when* it fires is part of the program's meaning. `using var` is the source-visible answer to "where does this effect fire"; the must-use warning is "you created an effect-bearing object and let its effect fire silently — did you mean that?" The ceremony criterion — disposal provably runs no user code — is therefore already maximal: extending silence past it means announcing effects nowhere. The line was never complexity: an arbitrarily large class whose disposal provably runs no user code — no destructor anywhere in its hierarchy, no owning-inline-array fields, its `owned` fields all of trivial-dispose type — is already ceremony-free, while a one-field class with a destructor keeps its announcement — and a wrapper whose only cleanup is cascading into an `owned Connection` keeps it too, because the connection's destructor runs *at the wrapper's drop point*. The external evidence points the same way: the silent-RAII languages retrofitted selective ceremony back in — Rust's `#[must_use]` and the clippy lints applied type-by-type exactly where a drop is suspicious, plus the known guard footguns where an invisible drop point releases a lock earlier than the author pictured (`let _ = guard`). Bezel's position is that arrangement with the annotation *derived from structure* rather than hand-applied: must-use on by default where disposal has content, off by rule where it provably has none. The per-site escape hatches already exist and cost a single spelling each — plain `var` where ownership moves on, `_ := x;` for "release now," explicit `.Dispose()` for early release — and each is deliberately an announcement itself.

**History.** v0.61 committed to ceremony relief only — fully compatible. v0.66 did commit to the mechanism, but as the honesty device that let "tighten the discard" avoid "embrace runtime-managed class instances — it's GC-shaped"; silent tracking *completes* that direction rather than reversing it, removing the last mechanism entirely. v0.184's decline of "backing-storage release as a real obligation — ceremony on every dropped `ToArray()` result" is not un-declined: ceremony stays waived, only the free point becomes static. v0.202's pure-throw carve-out survives with its justification re-derived (the dispose-after-arm is now the flag-setting free), and its recorded future widening — trivial-dispose construction in pure bodies — remains viable, a compiler-freed instance still being effect-free.

**An owning-choice field is owned-bearing (v0.334, [#5789](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5789)).** Spec §15.3 and §35.4 said the class's destructor disposes a move-only field, while §28.4's generated-`Dispose()` list, §28.9's trivial-dispose definition, §28.5's owned-bearing sentence and §22.13's warning named `owned` fields and owning-inline-array fields only — so a class whose one obligation was an owning-choice field was trivial-dispose by the letter, its compiler free "running no user code" over a payload with a destructor. The lists now name the fields of move-only value type, both kinds, concretely and at a bare-`T` field's move-only instantiations, and §28.9's `Box<T>` example says what its definition says. Forbidding the field instead would have stranded §22.12 4(g)'s own recommended spelling for a process-global owning choice and nine measured fields. The compiler already disposed such fields in the cascade; its trivial-dispose predicate lacked the arm, which is the tracked delta.

**Explicit disposal marks; the holder frees (v0.334, proposal 0162).** Silent tracking's sentence — every class instance is freed by compiler-inserted code at its statically-known holder's release point — was, through v0.333, true of trivial-dispose instances and of the implicit discharge paths only: an explicit `Dispose()` reclaimed "immediately after whichever logical-disposal path completes" (spec §28.4, §28.12), with one carve for a trivial-dispose field (§28.6). The disposal matrix's first run found the second model unsound in an unchecked build: the language itself issues a second `Dispose()` on an explicitly disposed instance — the cascade over an early-released field, drop-on-overwrite after a dispose-then-assign, the per-slot pass after a discard, the exit pass — and that call read a released block ([#5759](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5759)); and a direct `DisposeAsync()` had two reclamation points, the synchronous path's and the task's terminal state ([#5758](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5758)). v0.334 makes the one sentence the rule for every class: an explicit call performs logical disposal only, the holder's release point reclaims once, after logical disposal has completed, and the flag records when disposal begins, so a second call after that is a no-op, while checked mode faults from logical disposal's completion: the disposal's own accesses run on a live instance, and an alias's access inside a direct `DisposeAsync()`'s window reaches an intact block and intact owned fields. The cost is a block held past an early release until a point its holder reaches anyway; the remedy for a hot path is a smaller scope or an overwrite, both release points. Declined (proposal 0162 §9): a per-location released bit, making the repeated call undefined unchecked, vacating the slot at the call (the reference compiler's nulling, which puts a second run-time vacated state on non-nullable fields and a null fault where the chapter reads the flag), a joined second `DisposeAsync()` task, and faulting an alias's access inside the in-flight window while exempting the disposal's own (no flag tested at member entry can tell the two apart).

**The overwrite of an explicitly-disposed local is its release point (v0.335, [#5832](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5832)).** The v0.334 train landed the #5768 ruling (a moved-from, explicitly-disposed or maybe-consumed local is reassignable) beside the paragraph above, and the two disagreed about an explicitly-disposed left-hand side: §28.3 said it "displaces nothing", which left the logically disposed block with no release point once the explicit call no longer reclaimed, while §28.2 named the overwrite among the release points and §28.7's liveness test counted the value live. v0.335 reads the three together as proposal 0162's composition note intended: a moved-from left-hand side displaces nothing; an explicitly-disposed one displaces a logically disposed value, and the store is its release point — the no-op `Dispose()` on the set flag, then the free — on every path that reached the store disposed, a maybe-consumed binding taking that per path, and the three sentences that said the scope end "skips" an explicitly-disposed binding now say the skip is the disposal's, not the free's. No verdict moves and no run-time state is added (the flag is already set); what the text fixes is where the block is reclaimed. Declined: making the explicit call on a local its own release point, reclaiming at the call — it reopens #5759's alias hazard, a surviving borrow reading a freed flag in an unchecked build, which is the hazard the paragraph above closed.

<a id="sec-destructor-body-not-on-ctor-unwind"></a>
### 5.29 The destructor body does not run on constructor unwind (v0.256)

Resolved v0.256 (spec defect [#3245](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3245); ruled under the owner's 2026-08-18 delegation, per the review program's C10 ruling). Found independently by **both** owner-commissioned RAII comparative reviews (2026-08-18) as the top spec-clarity defect: spec §22.9's lead cited "the §28.4 pass" for constructor unwinding — and §28.4's pass's *step 1 is the destructor body* — while every §22.9 bullet enumerated only owned-field disposal plus the base chain. The two readings diverge observably (does a half-constructed `FileStream`'s destructor run `NativeClose` on a mid-construction throw?), the implementation had long taken one of them (owned fields + base chain; no derived destructor body — `src/codegen/LlvmBackend_Setup.cpp`, `LlvmBackend_Disposal.cpp`), and no text or test pinned it.

**Why the C++-matching reading is right, not merely implemented.** §7.9's never-observed-unset guarantee for non-nullable fields is issued only at constructor completion; a destructor body running mid-unwind would be exactly the forbidden observer — free to read fields the failed construction never assigned. The alternative (run the body, tolerate unset reads) would need either a null-watermark regime for destructor bodies — a second §22.7-style observation gate, purchased for an edge whose C++ precedent says "don't" — or per-field initialized-tracking at runtime, machinery the model deliberately lacks. The base chain is the principled boundary: the base subobject *completed* construction, so its `Dispose()` — destructor body included — runs soundly; completion is the event that licenses the body, on both sides of the line.

**The example consequence, and the discipline now taught.** §28.4's flagship `FileStream` acquires a raw `_handle: IntPtr` in its constructor body and releases it in its destructor — a shape that leaks the handle if any statement after the acquisition throws, since no owned field holds it and the destructor body will not run. The example itself was verified to conform (the acquisition is the constructor's final statement; the only later action is a `using var` disposal, a destructor §28.4's own rule expects not to throw), and the previously-unstated discipline that makes it conform is now normative teaching at §28.4: **one raw resource per owning wrapper**, acquired as the constructor's final act; anything richer composes wrappers as `owned` fields, which §22.9's unwinding covers on every edge — whereupon the composite needs no destructor at all (§28.5). The example was deliberately *not* rewritten into a wrapper-plus-factory shape: the single-class form **is** the wrapper the discipline prescribes, and stating why it is safe teaches more than restructuring it. The compiler-side regression test pinning the negative (derived destructor body does not run; base's does) rides #3245, which stays open as the tracker. This record sits here, beside the ownership chapter's disposal records, because no rationale section owned §22.8/§22.9's constructor history — §5.10's #3240 record (which named the partially-constructed hazard as "§22.9's territory") is the nearest neighbor, and this ruling completes exactly that territory.

<a id="sec-checked-disposal-default"></a>
### 5.30 Checked disposal is the default build (v0.307)

Spec §28.2 defines two conformance modes and says which is the language: "unchecked mode is the release-build relaxation of the checked semantics, not a different language." Every recorded trust admission in §28 names checked mode's `DisposedAccessException` as its backstop — kernel K4.1 calls it "the runtime backstop every trust admission names" — and §28.14, §29.11, §15.4 and §28.11 each say the checked backstop "remains the guard" where their static rules are silent. v0.307 makes checked mode the default build and unchecked mode the explicitly selected relaxation (proposal 0128; the prior default is the changelog's). Nothing else moves: the two-mode structure, the equivalence claim for programs that never touch a disposed instance, the cost statement ("unchecked builds pay no cost for the mode's existence"), and K4.1's posture are unchanged.

**The cost of the default, stated.** A checked build pays the flag test on member entry and **retains, per disposed instance, the state that makes the fault deterministic**: the disposed flag must stay readable through every alias for as long as any alias might be used, and without a liveness oracle that is the process's life. Spec §28.2's latitude ("quarantine, delayed free") permits deferring reclamation; it cannot permit reclaiming the flag while an alias could still reach it, because K4.1's "faults deterministically" would become "faults within a window" — a windowed quarantine that eventually reuses the block is the unchecked relaxation made probabilistic, and was declined. The reference compiler today retains the *whole* disposed block (its runtime calls this a deliberate leak; `--unchecked-disposal` frees it and elides the test), so under the default a program that disposes continuously grows for its lifetime. That is a known, disclosed cost of the default, not an accepted end state: reducing the retention to the flag's footprint — releasing the payload at disposal and never handing a disposed instance's address to a live one — keeps the fault deterministic and is recorded as a precondition of the reference compiler exercising the default (the tracking issue), a compiler-lane obligation and not a spec matter. Unchecked mode remains the release form for a program that cannot carry the retention at all.

Two things were decided with it. The **contracts mode keeps the opposite default** (spec §22.16): `ensures`/`invariant` non-evaluation is unobservable by construction, so the cheap build is the honest default there; a disposal relaxation turns a defined fault into undefined behavior, so the checked build is the honest default here. And the **reference compiler's library API keeps its zero-value** — `CodegenOptions.checkedDisposal` stays `false` while `bezelc`'s default flips — because the test suite's memory oracles need real frees: checked mode quarantines disposed blocks (spec §28.2's latitude), and a block never freed is one AddressSanitizer cannot see a use-after-free on, while the curated LeakSanitizer sites would report every quarantined block as a leak. The conformance default is the compiler's user-facing one; an embedder chooses explicitly. Alternatives declined: keeping unchecked as the default (the soundness story would describe a build the user did not get); deleting the relaxation (the cost statement is load-bearing); a compile-time warning on the unchecked switch (a deliberate build choice is not a hazard in a program — §5.28's doctrine lints programs).

<a id="sec-trust-admission-report"></a>
### 5.31 The trust-admission report: the K4.3 register is enumerable, and reportable on request (v0.307)

K4's third clause makes every admission the checker cannot compare statically or backstop by flag carry *"an individually recorded trust clause"*. The clauses are recorded in the text — field-held, collection-held and frame-held borrows, plus §28.14's two residuals — and at every one of them the compiler already distinguishes the admitted, trust-bearing outcome from the rejected one; it simply did not say so. Spec §28.3's trust-admission report mode (proposal 0131) makes it say so on request: a per-build mode — off by default, selected explicitly for a build — under which each such admission draws a warning naming the clause, the obligation and the static remedies — `scoped`, a `SlotHandle` key, `Shared<T>`, the owning family, awaiting to settlement — and under which a silent compilation unit provably carries no recorded trust clause.

Three placements were decided. **A warning, not a third diagnostic class**: Appendix D classifies outcomes as accept / warn / reject and nothing else, and a report that was neither would be invisible to every conformance instrument the project has. **In the specification, not only the toolchain**: the zero-report claim is a statement about the spec's own register, meaningful only if every conforming implementation reports the same set — and the K4 sentence that the set "is enumerable, and the report mode enumerates it" is a cheap ratchet on the clause's existing "no new admission without recording its clause". **Not always-on**: §5.28's doctrine reserves default-build warnings for statically-certain hazards, and a trust admission is not a hazard but the taught spelling of the back-reference (Guide 01 §5); a default-build warning on every `_owner := owner;` would erode the five lints' signal (§5.28). The mode is an audit: off by default, selected explicitly. Excluded from the report, each with its reason in §28.3: the site-announced kills (K4.2 — announced and flag-backstopped, a different posture), §22.12's statics obligations (already declaration-site warnings), §32.10's documented trust (keyword-visible at every `extern` call), and axiom A10's certification grants (an allowlist, not a recorded clause).

**The transfer entry (v0.333, [#5713](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5713); proposal [0157](proposals/0157-report-a-transfer-of-a-collection-held-source.md)).** A store into a method-local collection of an element sourced in the body is admitted by the scope comparison, which bounds the element against its source's scope end and nothing more. A transfer of that source while the collection still holds the element is admitted too, because a collection is none of the freeze's traced holders (proposal 0153 declined making it one), and that transfer is where spec §28.11's collection-held clause, with its domain obligation, comes into force. So the register names the transfer, not the store. Reporting every such store was declined: most never meet a transfer, so the report would list every local collection a program builds, and an audit whose lines mostly name no obligation stops being read. Narrowing the zero-report claim to except the shape was declined because the claim is what the mode is for. The kills that set the flag — the site-announced kills and a reassignment's drop-on-overwrite — stay outside the register: a later read through the element meets the flag (K4.1), and a disposed object crosses no domain. The entry does not ask whether the collection still holds the element at the transfer: retention is not body-local, since a callee handed the collection as a plain argument may `Remove` or `Clear` it, and a line under a collection that no longer holds the element is an over-report the audit can dismiss.

<a id="sec-nullability"></a>
## 6. Nullability

<a id="sec-field-narrowing-is-not-safe-across-threads"></a>
### 6.1 Field narrowing is not safe across statements

`if user <> null then user.Method() end;` works for locals because the compiler knows nothing else can reassign them. For fields, the same code is a compile error — not primarily because of threads (since v0.131 a domain's continuations are serial, [§12.5](#sec-execution-domains)), but because the field can be reassigned between the check and the use by paths the compiler cannot see: a method call whose body writes the field (directly or through an alias to the same object), a closure invoked in between, re-entrant code reached from the check's own expression, or — across an `await` — any other continuation of the same domain interleaving at the suspension point. Foreign threads introduced through the FFI remain a residual case ([§16](#sec-ffi), spec §30.12). An **uncaptured** local has none of these writers, so narrowing it is sound; a local mutably captured by a lambda has exactly one — the closure — which is why spec §7.4 (v0.182) invalidates a captured local's narrowing at any call, function-value invocation, or `await` inside the controlled block.

A `ref` parameter (v0.320, [#5495](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5495)) has every writer the field has, through the alias: the caller may pass a closure that nulls the referent beside the `ref` argument, name the same storage at a second `ref` argument, or pass the holder of a field it also passed as `ref`. The last two are plain stores inside the callee itself, so no invalidating form could be added short of invalidating at every store to anything that might alias, which is the mutable-field situation under another name. Spec §7.4 therefore never narrows a `ref` parameter and gives it the field remedy, the copy. Two alternatives were declined: invalidating a `ref` parameter's narrowing at every user-code transfer and every store that can reach heap storage or another `ref`/`out` parameter, which leaves narrowing only in a block with no call and no non-local store and must be re-derived with every new store form; and rejecting overlapping caller storage at two `ref`/`out` arguments of one call, which still needs the first alternative for the closure route and cannot see `G(ref h.F, h)`, one location under two spellings. Neither could be conditional on the caller's argument, because a body is checked once against its signature, which does not carry the argument's category. An `out` parameter is a callee-local slot until copy-out (spec §22.3, whose hidden-reference licence covers only storage that is the callee's for the call), which no caller alias names, so it narrows like a local; that is also what discharges spec §22.4's `when true` postcondition inside the declaring body, and treating `out` like `ref` for uniformity was declined for that reason.

The compiler does not narrow mutable fields. Since v0.214 the one field kind with *no* writers outside construction — `readonly` (single-assignment, §22.8; no `ref`/`out` escape, §22.3) — narrows, with the single-segment path and construction-window rules spec §7.4 states; the copy-to-local pattern remains for everything mutable:

```bezel
var u := _user;
if u <> null then u.DoStuff(); end;
```

Some languages (TypeScript) narrow property accesses and pay for it with subtle time-of-check/time-of-use bugs; Kotlin refuses to smart-cast mutable properties for exactly this reason. Bezel declines to narrow mutable fields and properties entirely.

<a id="sec-is-postfix-only"></a>
### 6.2 `!` is postfix only

`name!.Length` reads left-to-right; prefix `!name.Length` would have to be parenthesized. There is also a semantic argument: `!` asserts something about the value to its left, and putting the assertion to the left of the asserted value reads backwards.

There is no prefix `!` for boolean negation either; `not` is the boolean operator.

<a id="sec-interpolating-a-nullable-renders-null"></a>
### 6.3 Interpolating a nullable renders `null` (v0.184)

`$"{x}"` over a `T?` is the most common diagnostic line in the language; requiring narrowing or `??` there (the §4.6 strict-ordering template) would tax every log statement for zero safety — rendering is total and loses nothing. Between C#'s empty string and a visible token, the visible token wins: an empty hole *hides* the null that a log line exists to reveal. The format spec is skipped on null rather than dispatched (there is no value to format) but stays compile-checked against `T`, so a bad specifier target is caught regardless of the value's presence.

<a id="sec-operators-do-not-lift"></a>
### 6.4 Operators do not lift over nullable operands (v0.267)

Through v0.266 the built-in operators lifted over a nullable value type: arithmetic, bitwise and shift yielded `T?` (null if either operand was null), and the relational operators yielded a non-nullable `bool` — `false` if either operand was null. Spec §7.11 now rejects a nullable operand at all of them, with the compound-assignment (§6.6) and `++`/`--` (§17.6) forms and the relational and range **patterns** (§19.3, §16.6) rejected with them; equality (§8.4) and the marked propagating forms (`??`, `?.`, `?(args)`, `??=`) are retained unchanged. The whole ruling turns on one observation: **every construct in Bezel that propagates absence carries `?` in its own spelling** — `?.`, `?(`, `??`, `??=` — and lifted `+` and lifted `<` were the only two that did not. What is enforced is therefore not that propagation is wrong; it is that *unmarked* propagation is, and rejecting the two unmarked cases makes the language's own convention exceptionless.

**What follows.** No operator produces a value from an absent operand, and none consumes one silently: an absent value is confronted at the operand, in the source, once — the discipline spec §7.4 applies to member access, indexing and calls, with no exception to it. A nullable value therefore reaches a non-nullable position only through a narrowing check, `??`, or `!`, and the operations that do answer over an absent operand — equality (§8.4), the null and type tests (§12.7, §19.5), and interpolation (§18.5) — answer questions that have answers.

The relational arm is the one that had no defence at all. §7.11's *Safety* paragraph claimed that "lifting never silently produces a non-nullable value from nullable operands" while its own *Relational* paragraph, twenty lines above, produced exactly that; §18.5 held that in ordering "a null participant has no defensible result", and §4.6 acted on it for `string?`, while §7.11 asserted for value types that the defensible result is `false`. That collapse is not a smaller three-valued logic — it is a two-valued answer to a question with none, and it fails in both directions at once: on an absent `x`, `x < y`, `x = y` and `x > y` are all `false`, and `not (x < y)` is a definite `true`, so `requires(not (retries > Max))` **passes** on an absent `retries` while the positive spelling of the same contract faults. `while spent < budget do … end;` never executes when `spent` is absent, and a comparator built from `<` breaks the total order stdlib §4.4 requires, whose stated consequence is an unspecified permutation. This is worse than SQL, the usual cautionary tale for propagation: SQL's three-valued logic keeps `NOT (x < 5)` *unknown* and drops the row, which is precisely the protection Bezel lacked, having taken C#'s two-valued collapse without it.

The arithmetic arm follows from the same principle and from an asymmetry the language was already carrying: §7.11 rejected lifting for user-declared operators and named `!` and `??` as the remedies, so `total += price` silently poisoned an accumulator for `decimal?` and was a compile error for `Money?` — the same program shape, decided by whether the author reached for a primitive or a record, recorded nowhere. The external evidence points the same way and is unusually clean, because it turns on mechanism rather than heritage: the languages that enforce nullability by **flow analysis** reject lifted arithmetic and lifted ordering — TypeScript, which retrofitted its null-safety, rejects them too while permitting `==` — and the ones that lift represent absence as a runtime wrapper. C# lifted in 2005 because it had `Nullable<T>` and no flow analysis; Bezel has C#'s representation and none of C#'s excuse. Two data points carry most of the weight. Swift shipped lifted ordering and **removed it in a breaking release** (SE-0121, Swift 3), having concluded it was too easy to write bugs with — and Swift's semantics were strictly better than Bezel's, `.none < .some` being a genuine total order where Bezel collapsed to `false`, so the evidence applies a fortiori; SE-0121 names implicit `T → T?` promotion as the accelerant, which Bezel has (§5.1) — and which §7.11's former mixed-operand rule blessed at the operator until this revision. And C#'s designers declined to repeat lifting for C# 8's nullable reference types (csharplang #1865): it "doesn't really make good use of null tracking; just propagates nulls", and would "flag the problem 'at the end' rather than on the first undesired null" — the accumulator complaint, stated by the designers of the language Bezel inherited lifting from, as a reason *not* to lift.

**On v0.265's compound-assignment ruling (#2158).** That revision settled how `x op= e` types when the target is a nullable value type — on the underlying types, with §7.11's present-branch semantics for the operation and the write-back alike — and so made `b += 100` on a `byte?` well-typed, raising `OverflowException` when `b` was present and assigning `null` when it was not. It is not reversed on its own terms: it answered *how the compound form types when the operator lifts*, and this revision removes the premise. What it established survives — §6.6's nullable paragraph still rejects a nullable right operand against a non-nullable target and still rejects a nullable reference-typed target, now as one rule with the nullable-target case rather than as boundaries drawn around an admission. The asymmetry it left behind is the one this entry would otherwise have to defend: that write-back was loud for the loss of *range* and silent for the loss of the whole accumulated value. Migration cost is zero outside the compiler and its tests — no example in the specification, the standard library, or the six guides performs a lifted operation, and no conformance case does; what remains is compiler code, its diagnostic families, and a codegen path that is a net deletion.

**The narrowed target (v0.288, #3686).** The rejection reads the operand's *static* type, and §7.11 has always said what that means — the type after §7.4's flow narrowing, so a narrowed `n: int?` is a non-nullable operand of `n + 1`. The compound and `++`/`--` forms had no sentence of their own, and an implementation read them off the *declared* type — accepting `n := n + 1` and rejecting `n += 1` for the same narrowed `n`. §7.11 decides it: the compound forms inherit the base operator's rule ("rejected with them"), which is narrowing-keyed, so the declared-type reading would have made the compound forms the one place in the language where narrowing is invisible — for no semantic gain. (§6.6's narrow-integer write-back is the one deliberate place where `b += 10` and `b := b + 10` differ, and it is a typing rule, not a nullability one.) Keeping the compound forms declared-type-keyed was declined for the reason the compound-assignment-only rejection below was.

**Declined.** A **runtime null fault** at the operator: it would need no new exception type (`NullReferenceException` fits §31.4's admission principle), and is declined for coherence rather than cost — a faulting operator can never yield `null`, so its result type is `T` and the design is this one with the diagnosis moved from compile time to production, against Appendix C.1's "a nullable value may not be dereferenced without a null check or `!`", against §7.8's explicit-postfix-`!` design (it is an unspelled `a! + b!`), and against the pattern §5.4 and §6.5 both follow of taking static rejection wherever it is available and faulting only on the residual. **Rejecting only the compound-assignment forms**, the narrow option: it carves §6.6's desugaring equivalence for no semantic gain — the posture recorded at §11.6 — and its premise fails independently, since `total := total + p;` reproduces the accumulator verbatim and that option leaves it legal. **Marked lifted operators in the F# shape** (`?+?`, `?<?` — keep the semantics, spell the propagation): a real design that satisfies this entry's own marking principle, declined for surface cost — two operator families and their compound forms to serve a case better written with `??` or a narrowing check. *Reopening bar:* evidence from real Bezel programs that the `??`-and-narrow spelling is materially worse, not merely longer; it is the one recorded re-entry shape for operator-level propagation. **Retaining relational and range patterns over a nullable scrutinee** under a "never matches null" reading: declined because it would reproduce in pattern position the split verdict this revision removes, and because §16.6's claim that range patterns "agree exactly with `in`" is false while `in` requires a non-nullable left operand (§17.5) and the pattern silently declines to match. Constant patterns over a nullable scrutinee are unaffected, and the difference is the ruling's own line: equality over an absent value has an answer, ordering does not. **Lifting of user-defined operators**, which §7.11 had deferred: closed as a decline rather than carried, since with no built-in operator lifting a user-declared one has nothing to be consistent with.

**The null-conditional call `f?(args)` is kept (proposal 0136 §1.3).** It is the function-typed member of the marked propagating family this entry's principle rests on — `?.`, `?(args)`, `??`, `??=` — and it spells its propagation in its own token, as the others do; its evaluation is normative (spec §7.6, #3288). Its value form has no shorter replacement: `(if f <> null then f(x) else null)` names `f` twice and states the absent case by hand. Removing it is declined. *Reopening bar:* evidence from real Bezel programs that the form misleads, not that the `if` spelling exists.


<a id="sec-equality-and-hashing"></a>
## 7. Equality and hashing

<a id="sec-=-invokes-equals"></a>
### 7.1 `=` invokes `Equals`

A perennial C# bug is reference-equality on a class that should have had value equality. Bezel chooses the other way: `=` invokes `Equals`. For records, `Equals` is value equality by default. For classes, `Equals` is reference equality by default but overrideable.

The common case does the right thing. For genuine reference comparison, `Object.ReferenceEquals(a, b)` is available.

<a id="sec-=-cannot-be-overloaded-on-classes"></a>
### 7.2 `=` cannot be overloaded on classes

Operator overloading of `=` on classes invites bugs: the override defines value equality, but the rest of the language treats classes as reference types in many places (collections that hash on identity, `is` checks). Classes customize equality through `Equals`; records and enums may overload `=` and `<>` freely.

<a id="sec-overriding-equals-requires-overriding-gethashcode"></a>
### 7.3 Overriding `Equals` requires overriding `GetHashCode`

The "if you override one, override both" invariant is a source of bugs in other languages because it's a documentation rule, not a language rule. Bezel enforces it at compile time. The cost is a `GetHashCode` on types that may never be hashed; the benefit is that no Bezel type can produce silent dictionary-lookup bugs years later when someone refactors.

<a id="sec-float-equality-is-reflexive"></a>
### 7.4 Float equality is reflexive

`NaN.Equals(NaN)` is `true`. `(-0.0).Equals(0.0)` is `true`. The relational operators `<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=` keep IEEE semantics — any comparison involving `NaN` is false.

The asymmetry is intentional. Equality is what hash tables, sets, and `case` patterns use; an irreflexive `=` makes `Set<double>` misbehave when NaN is inserted. Relational operators retain IEEE semantics so ordering-based numeric algorithms work as published. The one published equality idiom this does not cover, the self-inequality NaN test `x != x`, does not port (`x <> x` is `false` for every `x`) and is written `Double.IsNaN(x)`. A warning on a float-typed self-comparison is deferred (changelog v0.308): the expression is certainly constant, but whether its author meant the IEEE test is not, and at a generic `T` the float-typed predicate is known only after substitution. Strict IEEE equality is available as `Double.IeeeEquals`; bit-pattern equality is `Double.BitwiseEquals`.

<a id="sec-equality-skip"></a>
### 7.5 `equality skip`: per-member exclusion from auto-equality (v0.244)

Body-record auto-equality is deliberately all-or-nothing at the *type* level (spec §9.2): the error is loud, never silent reference equality. But through v0.243, one cache field cost the whole package, and the road back was five member declarations (`operator =`, `operator <>`, `Equals(Object?)`, `Equals(T)`, `GetHashCode` — §9.2's remedy plus §25.2's co-declaration rule) all restating "structural equality over everything except this one field." The [§18](#sec-things-deferred) deferral ("Asymmetric record auto-equality customization") recorded the need before the fix's mechanism existed; `serialized skip` (v0.117, spec §9.8) then shipped exactly the right affordance — per-member exclusion that also removes its component from eligibility consideration — and applied it to only one of the two synthesis packages built on the same §9.2 membership rule. v0.244 mirrors it: **`equality skip`** (spec §9.2), one word for one concept across both packages.

The recorded choices: the eligibility **gate** is one-directional — *serialization's* gate (spec §9.8) reads both packages' skips (a member skipped by either clause is structurally absent for *that* check — the alternative, demanding both clauses on a cache field of a serializable record, was ceremony that reads as meaning something it does not), while the *equality* gate (§3.6's auto-hashable property) reads `equality skip` alone: the landing gate traced the symmetric form to silent reference equality in generated `=` via §9.8's own documented `serialized skip` remedy, the exact footgun §9.2's error exists to prevent, so the unmotivated direction was cut at landing (the v0.244 changelog's gate-round record). **Participation** never crosses (`equality skip` does not hide a member from the wire; `serialized skip` does not change what `=` compares). The `Equals`/`GetHashCode`-override disqualifier is unrescuable by design — the author who takes over equality takes over all of it (extended at v0.276 to a homogeneous `=`/`<>` overload, on the same ground: [§7.6](#sec-equality-operator-strips-auto-hashable)). All-members-skipped takes the zero-property semantics with no diagnostic (§9.2's own precedent). `ToString` keeps rendering skipped members (one clause, one effect; renderings are not contracts). Declined: a type-level exclusion list (`equality(Lo, Hi)` — the kept-synchronized-list failure mode, [§5.19](#sec-serialization-without-reflection)); auto-excluding private fields (visibility silently changing `=` is exactly what §9.2's error exists to prevent); other spellings (`transient`, `ignore` — vocabulary economy: the sibling clause spells exclusion `skip`); full custom-equality clauses (out of scope — the five-member route, 0012's `DateTimeOffset` precedent, remains the escape for *logic*; the clause covers only exclusion). The corpus honesty note stands from the proposal (0084): no accumulated workaround debt existed — the all-or-nothing rule *deterred* the cache-field shape rather than taxing it visibly; the evidence was the recorded deferral plus the mechanism's arrival, not corpus pressure.

<a id="sec-equality-operator-strips-auto-hashable"></a>
### 7.6 A homogeneous `=` overload strips auto-hashable (v0.276)

Spec §9.2's two lists under-determined one input for three years: the eligibility list admitted *"an overload of a non-equality operator"* and the disqualifier list named only the `Equals`/`GetHashCode` override, so an author who overloaded `=` itself fell between them. Read one way the record kept auto-hashable and received a synthesized `Equals`/`GetHashCode` computed by a rule its own `=` had just replaced; read the other, the overload disqualified it and §25.2's co-declaration rule supplied the pair. v0.276 rules the second, and writes it into both lists so neither can be read alone and be wrong.

The decisive argument is not textual but structural: it is the only reading under which §9.2 and §25.2 are **independent** guards against the same hazard. §25.2 exists to stop the three surfaces (`=`, `Equals`, hashing) diverging — *"`a = b` true while a `Set<T>` treats them as distinct"*. Under the rejected reading §25.2 is the *only* thing stopping it, so any exemption from §25.2 becomes a soundness hole — and that is observably how the `IntPtr` bug arose (v0.275's entry; spec §25.2's retirement parenthetical). Under the ruling the overload strips auto-hashable on its own, §9.2's use-site error blocks equality until the author supplies it, and an exemption is harmless. Three supports follow: §3.6 defines auto-hashable as the property of a type *for which* `=` is synthesized, which an overloading record is not; the word *non-equality* appears in two sections (§9.2, §35.7) and is drafting noise under any other reading, which §9.2's own `equality skip` discipline names as a defect; and the compiler has enforced it since v0.158.

**Heterogeneous overloads (`T = U`) do not disqualify**, aligning §9.2's trigger with §25.2's, which frees them from the co-declaration obligation for the same reason: such an overload defines a comparison the synthesis never provided and takes over nothing. That alignment is what makes §25.2's *"the **auto-generated or user-supplied** `Equals(Object?)` and `Equals(T)` methods continue to exist"* a live two-case split rather than a sentence with a dead arm.

The stated cost, not minimised: §9.2's recursive component rule admits *another record that is itself **auto-hashable***, so a record with an `IntPtr`, `UIntPtr`, or `Ptr<T>` component is not auto-hashable and cannot use `=` or key a hash container — the spec's own `record Sqlite3Db(Raw: IntPtr)` shape. The wart is not created by this ruling; it is created by the component rule saying *auto-hashable* where it arguably means *hashable*. Widening it was **declined here and routed**, because *hashable* would admit a user-equality record carrying a class-typed field as a component, smuggling reference equality inside a value record's `=` — what §35.7 calls *"virtually never the intended semantics"*. That is a design change owing its own proposal, not a rider on a ruling.

**The rule extends to value-like choices, and extending it forced a second edit nobody asked for.** Spec §35.7 has restated §9.2's operator clause for choices all along, so the ruling reaches them or leaves the two sections disagreeing. But §3.6's **hashable** row carried its *"nor declares user equality"* rescue for records only — so on the first draft a choice that did everything §25.2's newly-extended co-declaration rule demands was stripped of auto-hashable and then refused as a hash key, unrescuable and undiagnosed. The rescue arm is added rather than the extension dropped, because the alternative leaves a soundness hole open for one type kind on the grounds that nobody has hit it yet; the affected population is zero either way, and a rule with a hole is worse than a rule with a cost. That the gap existed at all is the ordinary consequence of a property defined in one section and consumed in four: §3.6 states what **hashable** is, and it had never had to consider a type that was outside auto-hashable *by choice* rather than by construction.

The `Equals`/`GetHashCode`-override disqualifier's ground ([§7.5](#sec-equality-skip)) — *the author who takes over equality takes over all of it* — is the same ground, and this is its extension to the surface the author actually writes.

<a id="sec-equality-without-a-definition"></a>
### 7.7 Equality a type does not define: box identity, and the per-instantiation check (v0.317)

A record or choice outside the auto-hashable set has no equality unless its author declares one, and spec §9.2 makes a direct use a compile error. Two routes reach such a value without its static type. The **boxed** route (an `Object`- or interface-typed receiver) cannot be rejected statically, so it gets a defined answer, member by member: the type's declared member where it has one, and otherwise the inherited identity members (spec §9.2 for records, v0.316; §35.7 for choices, v0.317). The answer is sound because a box has identity: identity-equal means the same box, so the same payload and the same hash, and §8.3's contract holds whatever the type declares.

The **type-parameter** route (`x.Equals(y)` on an unconstrained `T`) is decided differently, because it *can* be checked. Each use's kind (`Equals` against a `T`, `Equals` against anything else, `GetHashCode`) is fixed when the body is checked and read against each substitution, so it reaches that type's own member for the kind where it has one, and an instantiation at a type with none is rejected (spec §9.2, *Through a type parameter*; §3.6 rule 2). `Equals` against a non-`T` argument at a type whose only equality takes its own type compares a boxed argument's payload by that member and is false otherwise, the bridge an enum's box already performs; without it, `where T: hashable` would not cover that use at `IntPtr`, and an interior diagnostic would survive the constraint that §14.2 says replaces it. Two alternatives were declined. *Box identity* gives no sound answer here: a `T`-typed operand of a value substitution is a value, not a box, so `x.Equals(x)` would be false under an implementation that boxes per call and true under one that does not — an irreflexive equality ([§7.4](#sec-float-equality-is-reflexive)'s ground) whose answer turns on a realization strategy spec §11.8 leaves open. A *synthesized structural comparison* is declined on the ground v0.316 gave for the boxed path: it would answer on the generic path exactly the question §9.2 refuses to answer statically, and for a class-typed component its answer is reference identity, the silent fallthrough §9.2 calls error-prone.

The cost is stated: the instantiation's validity depends on what the generic body does with `T`, not only on its signature. That is the existing model for a property-dependent interior use (spec §14.2's derived-property paragraph speaks of *the per-instantiation re-check for those interior uses*; spec §7.10's copy-demand rejection of a move-only substitution is the same shape), and the remedy is the one that paragraph exists for: `where T: hashable` puts the requirement in the signature and moves the diagnostic to the caller's instantiation. A consequence is stated with it: adding such a use to a public generic's body can reject a client's instantiation with no declared contract changed, which spec §2.8.6 classifies as breaking by its requirement-summary rows, as it does for every interior-use rule; the constraint is how an author makes the requirement a declared one, visible in the signature. The bridge carries a cost of its own: at a record whose only equality is `Equals(T)`, such as `IntPtr`, `x.Equals(o)` through `T` compares a boxed argument's payload, while the boxed `o.Equals(x)` compares box identity (spec §9.2's boxed path), so the two routes can answer one pair differently. It is confined to that record shape, because a record's `Equals(T)` does not fill its box's `Object` slot (spec §25.2) where an enum's or a choice's `Equals` does (spec §35.7), and the record removes it by declaring `Equals(Object?)`. Answering the second kind by box identity instead would be symmetric, but `x.Equals(o)` with `o` a box of `x`'s own value would then be false, the irreflexivity on which box identity is declined above.

(v0.318, [#5492](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5492)) **Rendering through a type parameter is not checked per instantiation.** `ToString` is decided the other way from equality. A choice outside the auto-hashable set has no generated rendering, and a call at its own static type is a compile error (spec §8.5, §35.7); a `T`-typed operand instantiated at it — `$"{x}"` in a generic helper — renders the qualified type name, the answer its box gives, and so does such a choice as a component of a generated rendering (a positional record's field). Rejecting the instantiation was declined on two grounds. No constraint can state the requirement: `hashable` does not imply a rendering, since a value-like choice outside auto-hashable only through its own homogeneous `=` is hashable and has no generated `ToString`, so the rejection would be an interior diagnostic with no signature-side remedy, the shape spec §14.2's derived-property paragraph exists to replace; and it would fall on every generic logging or message helper that interpolates its argument. The qualified name is sound where box identity is not: it is a function of the type alone, so it does not turn on how an implementation realizes generics, and a borrowed owning value renders without being boxed. The cost is that `$"{x}"` prints a type name where the author may have wanted the value, and that a generic body and a concrete one disagree on whether the same rendering compiles; a choice whose rendering matters declares `ToString`.

(v0.319, [#5503](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5503)) **The same holds for a generic choice over a type parameter.** Inside a generic body, `r.ToString()` or `$"{r}"` for `r: Result<T, E>` names a choice type but not a classification: whether `Result<T, E>` is auto-hashable, and so has a generated `ToString`, is decided per instantiation (spec [§35.11](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-choice-generics)). Where it is not and none is declared, the rendering is the instantiated type's qualified name, and the instantiation is not rejected. The first ground above holds unchanged, because no admitted constraint establishes the classification: `where T: hashable` admits a class `T`, at which `Result<T, E>` is owning, and a record declaring its own equality, which is not an admissible component (spec §9.2). Rejecting would also make a more precise static type compile in fewer programs than a less precise one: a helper taking `x: T` renders a `Result<Widget, string>` value that a helper taking `r: Result<T, E>` would be refused. A choice type containing no type parameter (`Result<Widget, string>`) is statically known in a generic body as anywhere else and keeps the use-site error. `Result<T, E>` gains no declared `ToString`: one that rendered payloads at the owning and borrow-bearing instantiations is the case-shaped default v0.301 declined, on the ground that owned and borrowed class payloads are not the container's to format, and at the auto-hashable instantiations the generated rendering already exists. A user generic choice whose rendering matters declares `ToString`, which spec §35.2 admits at every classification.

<a id="sec-inheritance"></a>
## 8. Inheritance

<a id="sec-member-hiding-is-forbidden"></a>
### 8.1 Member hiding is forbidden

C# allows a derived member with the same name as a base member to *shadow* (not override) it, with dispatch depending on the static type of the receiver. Almost no one understands the semantics correctly, and the rare cases where it's the right tool are better solved by renaming.

Bezel rejects same-name members in derived classes outright. To override a virtual base member, write `override`. To add something new, choose a different name.

(v0.184) The blocked set is **visible** inherited members (§12.2): the recorded motivation above — shadowing and dispatch confusion — requires a member the derived author could see. A base class's `private` (and cross-module `internal`) names stay out of its compatibility surface; the residual cross-module collision creates two distinct members resolved by visibility and static type, recorded as the deliberate scope of the guarantee.

(v0.314, [#5370](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5370)) **A local does not redeclare a name of its own scope** (spec [§21.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-variable-declarations) *Local names*). The same reasoning reaches inside a body at its narrowest point. A second `var s` in one block, or a body-top local named like a parameter, leaves the earlier binding unreachable for the rest of the scope while it is still live: a local or parameter has no qualified spelling (`this.` reaches members, not locals), and the two bindings can carry different ownership obligations. Rust admits same-scope rebinding (`let x = x.trim();`); it is declined. In Bezel, rebinding is the place where an obligation moves, and a new name carries that better (`using var r := move reader;`, the spelling spec §29.8 uses). A `forbid → allow` widening stays compatible where the reverse would break.

(v0.316, [#5382](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5382)) **Nor does a declaration reuse a name of an enclosing scope** (spec [§21.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-variable-declarations) *Local names*). A nested-block local, a loop variable, an arm binding, a binding-atom clause, or a lambda's parameter, slot or local may not take the name of a local or parameter in scope at it. The ground is the same-scope rule's, one block in: the outer binding stays live under the inner one with no qualified spelling, so for the rest of the inner scope it is unreachable while it may still hold an obligation, and a reader has to find the nearest declaration to know which of two owned values `move x` consumes. The name rules inside a declaration already refuse a shadow: member hiding (above), loop labels (spec §21.5), a local type against a visible type (§21.10) and a method type parameter against an enclosing one (§14.1). The shadows the language admits are of module-level names, a declaration over an import or an always-available name (§2.2, §2.3) and an extension's type-parameter binding over a type, with a warning (§26.4), and each leaves the hidden name reachable by qualification, which a local or parameter never is. C# and Java refuse it within a body too. The test is what is in scope at the declaration, not C#'s whole-block reservation, so sibling blocks, successive loops and the arms of one `case` reuse a name freely, and the compiler's per-declaration keying of definite assignment and ownership state (#5314, #5335, #5267) stays load-bearing for them. A member's parameter may still take a primary-constructor parameter's name (spec §22.10): a parameter name is part of the member's signature, an address for a named argument, and forcing a rename there would let a class's capture leak into its members' interfaces, where a local's name is private to its body. Two alternatives are declined. *Admitting the shadow*, the inner binding hiding the outer for the rest of the inner scope (Rust, Swift, Kotlin): it is the obligation-hiding the same-scope rule refuses, moved one block in, and the compiler's checks met it as defects at least six times (#5314, #5334, #5335, #5267, #3755, #1566), each a check that consulted a name where it needed a binding. *Admitting it across a lambda boundary only* (C# 8 relaxed its rule for lambdas and local functions, so that their parameters and locals may hide an enclosing local or parameter; the C# design team's meeting notes of 2021-11-03 record regret that this admitted a name both captured and shadowed, too late to withdraw): a Bezel lambda captures by reference, and a captured owned binding is a borrow of it (spec §27.5, §28.3), so the hidden name would be a capture candidate inside exactly the construct whose captures the borrow rules reason about; #1566's, #3755's and #5267's lambda rows were this case. Either alternative remains a compatible widening on a recorded need; the ban could not be adopted later without breaking programs, which is C#'s position on its relaxation. The earliest record leans the other way: v0.17's changelog says shadowing "is allowed but discouraged". That entry predates the ownership model (compiler-checked ownership arrived in v0.22) and gave no ground, and the specification does not state it; Appendix B's licence to shadow a setter's implicit `value` is its one remnant, kept by this ruling and left to a separate question, which the #5484 paragraph below decides. Nor is this [§2.3](#sec-auto-imported-extension-modules)'s case for ratifying practice over an unargued restriction: the ban is argued above, and no specification or guide example shadows a nested name, so the only practice is the compiler's, which admitted the shadow while the question was recorded as open.

(v0.320, [#5484](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5484)) **A setter's implicit `value` is a parameter like any other** (spec [§21.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-variable-declarations) *Local names*, §23.3, Appendix B). Neither rule above exempts any other parameter, and the setter's is where the hazard they close is sharpest. Under `set(owned)` the implicit `value` holds the owned obligation the body exists to discharge, and a nested `var value` hides it for the rest of that block, from the reader and from any check that consults a name where it needs a binding: #5267's and #5252's setter rows were this case. Appendix B's licence rested on `value` not being a true keyword in that role. That fact settles only that the name is not reserved elsewhere. It gives no ground for the setter body to redeclare the parameter, and the licence was the last remnant of v0.17's "shadowing is allowed but discouraged", which the v0.316 paragraph found ungrounded. An author who wants the name for a local gives the local a fresh name; the setter's parameter is always `value` (spec §23.3). The rule reads the same for a property's setter and an indexer's, as Appendix B's sentence did. Two alternatives are declined. *Keeping the exemption*, at body top and nested, leaves one parameter outside both rules on no ground of its own, in the position where an owned obligation most often arrives. *Keeping it at body top only* is the same-scope rebinding the v0.314 paragraph declines, applied to one parameter. Either remains a compatible widening on a recorded need; the reverse could not be adopted later without breaking programs.

(v0.330, [#5664](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5664)) **`Object`'s virtual members block only their own signature** (spec [§12.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-hiding-is-not-allowed)). Every class inherits `Equals(Object?)`, `GetHashCode()` and `ToString()` from `Object` (spec §8.1), so read without a carve-out the rule above would forbid every class a non-`override` `Equals`, `GetHashCode` or `ToString` at any signature — and with it `IEquatable<T>`, `IEqualityComparer<T>` and `IFormattable`, which the stdlib (§2.9, §2.18, §4.8) and spec §18.5 admit on classes, and whose comparer constructors would otherwise have no admissible argument. The ground above — shadowing a member the author can see, with dispatch keyed on the static type — is met in full only by a declaration at `Object`'s own signature, which is the one `override` reaches; a declaration at another parameter list replaces nothing and is selected as every overload is, by its arguments (spec §22.6). One residual of the ground remains, and it is accepted: `p.Equals(q)` at a `Point` receiver may answer differently from the same call through an `Object` receiver. Every typed-equality interface carries that split, and a record whose only `Equals` is `Equals(T)` already has it on its boxed path (spec §9.2). Spec §8.3's hash rule reads every `a.Equals(b)`, so the author keeps `GetHashCode` in agreement with the typed overload, and overrides `Equals(Object?)` where the two answers must agree. So `Object`'s three virtual members block exactly their own signature, and the carve-out is theirs alone: a class's own `Equals(T)` blocks the name in its subclasses as any member does, which keeps the no-overload-across-the-boundary rule intact everywhere a user author could have chosen the base name. This composes with the value kinds' rule (§9.4 below; spec §9.2, §10.5, §35.2, v0.327): there too the `Object` slot is reached only by the one admitted signature — on an enum or a choice the own-typed `Equals`, on a class `override Equals(Object?)` — and a plain `Equals` at any other parameter type is an ordinary method, which on a class includes the own-typed one. Two alternatives are declined. *Reading the rule literally*, so that no class implements the three interfaces: it contradicts four normative statements and leaves stdlib surface unreachable. *Exempting any member that implements an interface member*, whatever base declares the name: it admits the static-type-selected shadow this section exists to refuse, through any interface a derived class lists, and is both wider (any base, through an interface) and narrower (an `Equals(a, b)` declared outside a conformance) than the admission the text needs.

(v0.332, [proposal 0155](proposals/0155-class-own-typed-equals.md), [#5668](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5668), [#5670](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5670)) **A class's own-typed `Equals` obliges `GetHashCode`; `=` stays the call.** The v0.330 admission left two consequences open. First, the compiler-enforced pairing (spec §8.3) read `override Equals` only, so a class with `Equals(other: Point)` and no `GetHashCode` override compiled, and a `Set<Point>` kept equal points apart. The pairing now reads a class's non-`override` own-typed `Equals` as it reads an override. That is v0.327's enum and choice decision carried to the one other kind that can declare such a member, and the divergence it closes is the one spec §25.2's co-declaration rule exists for. A warning was declined because the divergence is a wrong answer, not a style. The obligation stays the declaring class's own: an inherited hash written for another `Equals` does not answer for this one, and a base that seals its `GetHashCode` has fixed its subclasses' equality with it. Second, `=` is the call `a.Equals(b)`, so `p = d` at a subclass-typed `d` is §22.6's ambiguity, as `p.Equals(d)` is. A tie-break for the operator alone was declined: it is the ranking v0.330 declined, relocated from §22.6 to §8.4, and the evidence offered for it — a `Set<Point>` holding `d`, and `=` through a type parameter at `Point`, compare by `Equals(Point)` — is that decline's own remedy, a value bound at `Point`. It would also pick the base's non-virtual `Equals(Point)` over a subclass's `Equals(Object?)` override at two subclass operands without the author's choosing. Binding a fixed member would split `=` from the call where the call is unambiguous (`c = 5` against `c.Equals(5)` on `class C: IEquatable<int>`), and always binding the `Object` slot would split `=` from the hash containers, which spec §9.2 binds to the own-typed member. What the operator adds is the null-safe wrapper (spec §8.4), which reads a present operand at its stripped type, so `p = q` at `q: Point?` and `p = q!` agree.

(v0.333, [proposal 0158](proposals/0158-equals-ambiguous-type-not-hashable.md), [#5716](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5716)) **A type whose `Equals` is ambiguous at its own operands is not hashable** (spec §3.6, §9.2). Under the v0.330 admission, `x.Equals(y)` at two operands of a `Point3` deriving from a `Point` that declares `Equals(other: Point)` is §22.6's ambiguity, and spec §12.2 counts a use through a type parameter among such calls. Spec §3.6 still counted every class hashable, so a comparer-less `Set<Point3>` was admitted with no member to compare by. Such a type is now not hashable, and §9.2's type-parameter path rejects the instantiation, so `Set<Point3>`, an equality search over `Point3` elements and `=` through a type parameter at `Point3` agree with `d1 = d2`. Reaching the inherited own-typed member through the type parameter was declined. It is the ranking v0.330 declined and the operator tie-break above was declined for, relocated to §9.2. Where `Point3` overrides `Equals(Object?)` to compare more, it would compare a `Point3` collection by `Point`'s non-virtual `Equals(Point)` over that override, without the author's choosing. It would also admit through a type parameter what `d1 = d2` refuses. Reaching the nearest `Equals` up the chain would rank overloads by declaration depth, and the same values would compare differently in a `Set<Point>` and a `Set<Point3>`. Reaching the `Object` slot would keep apart, by identity, the points a `Set<Point>` merges. The cost falls on a collection keyed at the subclass, which is formed at `Point`, holding `Point3` values, or given a comparer in which the author binds the comparison. `Point3` cannot declare `Equals(Point3)` beside the inherited member, because §12.2 keeps the name blocked across the boundary, so choosing between the base's equality and the `Object` slot's stays with the author who forms the collection, where §22.6's ambiguity puts it.

**A function value's hash agrees with its equality; the function type stays not hashable (v0.335, [proposal 0173](proposals/0173-function-value-hash-agrees-with-equality.md), [#5792](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5792)).** Proposal 0161 (v0.334) removed the function *type* from the hashable set because the only hash a function value had was `Object`'s identity hash, which §27.7.1's equality (same method, same receiver) contradicts, and declined a hash definition as "a hash for a key nobody uses". The key is used: a capture-free function value is admitted at `Object` (§28.3), `Set<Object>` and `where T: hashable` at `Object` are legal, and §8.3 states its invariant as a *must* for every type — so `Set<Object>` held two evaluations of one static method as one or two elements by bucket placement, and a generic body relying on the invariant at `Object` was wrong. v0.335 defines the hash on the equality's own key (a lambda by closure identity; a method reference by method and class-typed-receiver identity; a value-shaped receiver's reference by its function object's identity), which restores §8.3's invariant everywhere and costs the runtime one kind-dispatched hash beside its kind-dispatched equality. The type stays not hashable: a set of lambdas would still hold every evaluation apart, the reason 0161 kept. Declined: *an identity `Equals` on the `Object` surface* (splits `=` from `Equals`, which §8.4 identifies); *recording the disagreement as a documented exception in §8.3* (leaves `where T: hashable` at `Object` promising what a legal key breaks).

(v0.334, [proposal 0160](proposals/0160-class-own-typed-equals-is-the-object-slot.md), [#5755](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5755), [#5731](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5731)) **A class's own-typed `Equals` fills the `Object` slot.** The v0.330 admission read `Equals(other: Point)` as an overload beside the inherited `Equals(Object?)`, and the two revisions above then spent a pairing clause, an operator sentence, a hashable clause, a `C?` carve, a register remedy and three equality-search amendments keeping two members from answering one question differently — at a subclass operand, through a type parameter, in a hash container and through an `Object` surface. The simpler rule is the one v0.327 gave enums and choices: the own-typed `Equals` *is* the slot, in a second spelling, one virtual member reached by every path. Nothing is ranked, because there is one candidate; `Set<Point3>` is hashable through the inherited slot and hash, as every class was before v0.333; and the subclass case improves, because the slot is virtual, so a `Point3` that overrides it is compared by its override wherever a `Point3` is compared, which the two-member text could not express (its own objection to reaching the base's non-virtual member). What the v0.330 decline refused — a tie-break keyed on one member name — is not reopened: no tie exists. Three alternatives are declined. *Keeping both members* (the status quo): it is the machinery above, ≈1,100 words for a case with zero measured sites. *Admitting both spellings on one class, the `Object?` one being the slot*: two members again, with the ambiguity the slot rule removes. *A non-virtual slot*, so that `p = q` at two `Point` operands is a direct call: it would compare a `Point3` by `Point`'s member against the subclass's override, the wrong answer 0158 refused, for a dispatch cost a `sealed` class already avoids. `string` keeps one spelling (stdlib §9.6).

<a id="sec-class-hierarchy-exhaustiveness-in-case"></a>
### 8.2 Class-hierarchy exhaustiveness in `case`

A sealed hierarchy can be matched exhaustively without a `_` arm: if the base class is `closed` (or abstract with only `sealed` subclasses, recursively forming a closed set — spec §19.6) and every concrete leaf type appears as a pattern, the match is provably total (root and leaves non-generic; the generic case is the record below).

This is the class-shaped form of sum types — useful where it applies, no language additions required. The value-shaped form is the `choice` kind (spec §35); [§9.6](#sec-choices-why-a-new-kind) records the reasons and the line between them — hierarchies for behavior-carrying, identity-bearing sums, choices for value-shaped outcomes.

**Generic hierarchies: the determination rule, retired (v0.245; proposal 0089, [#3055](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3055)).** From v0.196 through v0.244 the spec answered generic-leaf coverage with a *determination rule*: per leaf, compute the root instantiation by composing base-clause substitutions, solve for the leaf's parameters against the scrutinee type under exact invariant identity, filter by constraint satisfaction, and classify exempt/determined/undetermined — with three-valued rigid-parameter identity, the §7.10 `?`-pre-image split, and a census prohibition: roughly two pages of §19's hardest text and ~1,000 lines of sema (#1816, with its own post-landing defect history, #1862/#1874). v0.245 retires it by deletion: a `case` over a closed hierarchy with a generic root or leaf is not proven exhaustive by leaf coverage — the analysis is not attempted — and completable only by an irrefutable arm. The deciding evidence was the corpus: after two years of the rule's availability and one year of choices, no organic in-tree code — stdlib, guide, example, or non-§19.6 test — matched a generic sealed hierarchy exhaustively; the rule was exercised exclusively by its own spec-derived test family. Its recorded defense expired with it: v0.196 rejected the blanket never-exhaustive reading as "over-broad: it regresses the determined shape, the canonical sealed-match idiom" — but v0.200's choices re-homed the value-shaped sum ([§9.6](#sec-choices-why-a-new-kind)), the canonical `Result` is a choice (stdlib §2.20), and the determined shape's flagship inhabitant no longer exists as a class hierarchy anywhere in the tree. The blanket reading is therefore adopted **with its rejection's premise expired** — recorded here explicitly rather than in silent contradiction of the v0.196 reasoning. What is lost: `_`-free matching (and spec-§21.7 totality without an irrefutable arm) for generic, behavior-carrying, exhaustively-matched hierarchies — a residual class narrow enough that a one-line dead `_` arm is the recorded price, with virtual dispatch the spelling such hierarchies usually want anyway. Runtime semantics change nowhere: the deleted proof was sound, and deleting it changes no arm selection. **The recorded reopening shape is the existential pattern** — `var o: Ok` matching any instantiation of a generic leaf, which would make generic-leaf coverage *expressible* with no determination computation — pinned so a future re-ask starts there rather than at restoring determination; the full design remains in git history and the v0.196 changelog entry.

<a id="sec-records-and-classes"></a>
## 9. Records and classes

<a id="sec-why-records-cannot-have-destructors-or-owned-fields"></a>
### 9.1 Why records cannot have destructors or `owned` fields

A record is copied on assignment. If a record had a destructor, every copy would need to track whether it owned or borrowed the underlying resource — C++ value semantics, with their attendant complications.

The clean rule: records hold values and borrowed references. They never own resources. If you need ownership, use a class.

<a id="sec-why-records-are-sealed"></a>
### 9.2 Why records are sealed

Inheritance and value-equality conflict. The classic problem: does `Square(5, 5) = Rectangle(5, 5)`? Either answer is wrong in some context. Records side-step this by being sealed. They may implement interfaces, which gives polymorphism without the equality problem.

<a id="sec-auto-equality-on-body-only-records"></a>
### 9.3 Auto-equality on body-only records

A record whose body contains only auto-properties and methods (no explicit fields, no computed properties with side effects) gets auto-generated `=`, `<>`, and `GetHashCode` over those auto-properties. This is what makes `DateTime`, `TimeSpan`, `Guid`, and similar standard types work as hashable without per-type overloads.

The previous rule ("body-only records get no auto-equality") was simpler but forced every standard library record to write `=`/`<>` overloads explicitly, with no benefit. The refined rule keeps the simple cases simple.

**The use-site error is stricter than C#.** C#'s `record` types always auto-generate equality, but C# *classes* fall back to reference equality on `.Equals(object?)` silently. Bezel records do not have C#'s always-auto-generate guarantee (a body record opts out by including non-auto-property storage), and a silent reference-equality fallthrough on a value type would be an error-prone default: users expect content equality on records, and a body record's reference-equality `Equals` is virtually never the intended semantics. Spec §9.2's compile error at the call site puts the choice in the user's hands.

**Which properties "auto-properties" names for a compiler intrinsic (v0.255, [#3131](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3131); resolved 2026-08-18 under the owner's delegation — [§5.10](#sec-the-move-expression-on-owned-fields)'s v0.255 record quotes it).** §8.5's per-type sentence for `Range<T>` ("rendering its auto-properties") and §9.2's "always over storage, never over derived values" pulled apart at the one record whose declaration and storage diverge — the compiler intrinsic: stdlib §9.8 declares six properties in one uniform bodyless spelling, no expression-bodied form and no computed marker anywhere, while the intrinsic stores four slots (`start`, `end`, `step`, `flags`) and derives `IsOpenEnded` and `IsEmpty`. Resolved for the **declared surface** — all six render. §9.2's exclusion is phrased against *expression-bodied* properties, a syntactic category §9.8 never uses, so a reader of the declaration has no four-member referent available; the storage layout is an implementation fact the spec never promises, and a representation fact cannot shrink a normatively-declared surface. §9.2 now scopes its own rule (the storage-vs-derived line is about the declaration surface; for user records the two coincide), the derived pair renders recomputed by the same §16.3 rules member access lowers to — so the rendered component and `r.IsEmpty` cannot disagree, and an open-ended range renders `IsEmpty: False`, §16.3's never-empty rule — and equality is indifferent either way, the derived pair being a function of the stored four: the tension was rendering-only, which is why it sat unnoticed for three revisions. The implemented rendering ([#2661](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/2661)'s lowering) already conformed; the docs caught up, no compiler delta. The generic-substitution rendering residue recorded on the issue (`Range<char>` reached through an unresolved `T` rendering code points rather than characters) is a codegen bug under the each-component-renders-as-its-own-`ToString` rule, not a spec question — split to its own tracking issue, [#3242](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3242), at the v0.255 landing so it is not lost when #3131 closes.

**A record's own-typed `Equals` is its `Object` slot (v0.336, proposal [0176](proposals/0176-value-kind-own-typed-equals-is-the-object-slot.md); [#5793](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5793) reading 1, reversing the v0.335 decline).** After v0.334 a record was the one kind whose own-typed `Equals(T)` did not fill the `Object` slot: an enum's and a choice's did (v0.327), a class's did in a second spelling (v0.334, proposal 0160), and a record kept `Equals(Object?)` as the slot with `Equals(T)` beside it as §25.2's co-declaration member — two members answering one question, and with them one rule of their own (v0.334's, proposal 0159: a record that holds borrows may declare no `Equals(Object?)`, since the equality search would hand that member a `T`-typed argument boxed with no conversion written), a generated positional pair of two members, and a boxed `Money` declaring only `Equals(Money)` comparing by box identity. The v0.335 decline kept the two members apart so that a record that holds borrows could declare `Equals(T)` without reaching a box, and because no program compared such a record through `Object`. The first reason does not hold: an own-typed slot member boxes no argument, and the receiver box it needs is §8.6's admitted position, as for every enum, so such a record declares `Equals(T)` on the same terms either way; and with the `Object?` spelling rejected on every value kind the holds-borrows rule has nothing to guard (no record declares a member at `Object?`, so the first kind boxes nothing by construction — stronger than a declaration rule keyed on a classification a generic record decides per instantiation), and three kinds state one rule. The own-typed spelling obliges `GetHashCode` on a record as it does on an enum and a class, because §8.3's invariant must hold through a box; that is the one new rejection, at a handful of test records. Declined with the proposal: the status quo (two members and two rules for a case with zero measured sites); admitting both spellings on a record with the `Object?` one as the slot (#5793's reading 1 as worded: two members again, the shape proposal 0160 declined for a class); and exempting a record's plain own-typed `Equals` from the `GetHashCode` obligation (a slot `Equals` hashed by the box's identity breaks §8.3's invariant at every `Object` surface).

**Declared equality is user equality on every value kind (v0.337, proposal [0178](proposals/0178-declared-equality-is-user-equality.md); [#5915](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5915), [#5916](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5916), [#5917](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5917)).** Three seams were left when the own-typed `Equals` became the slot. First, §3.6's hashable row read *declares user equality* as the operator pair with `Equals(T)`/`GetHashCode` beside it, so a record declaring `Equals(other: Key)` and `GetHashCode` but no `=` — every member §9.2's three kinds reach, and the members a `Set<Key>` consults — was outside **hashable** by the letter while an enum declaring the same two members was hashable; the arm now reads the own-typed `Equals` with the `GetHashCode` §8.3 obliges beside it, with or without an overload, and the type is hashable on the members it declares.
