Second, §35.7 let a value-like choice's methods "neither qualify nor disqualify", so a choice declaring `Equals(other: Tag)` kept the generated structural `=` beside a replaced `Equals` — the three-surface divergence §9.2's second disqualifier exists to prevent on a record, with one `=` answering two ways at the declaration and through a type parameter; a declared `Equals` or `GetHashCode` now disqualifies a choice exactly as it disqualifies a record, the generated package going with it, `=`/`<>` rejected until the overloads are written, and the pair making the choice hashable where it is value-like. Third, no value kind can declare `Equals(other: Object?)`, so an interface member at that signature had no declared witness on a record, an enum or a choice; the member that fills the slot is the witness, on a class as on a value kind — on a value kind its own `Equals`/`GetHashCode`, declared or generated, and a kind with none does not conform, box identity not being reached through a type parameter (§9.2). Declined with the proposal: keeping the operator pair as the only user-equality arm (the pair alone supplies every member the row's consumers reach; zero sites need the operators for hashing); letting `=` on a value kind dispatch to a declared own-typed `Equals` as §8.4 does on a class (it reverses §9.2's recorded use-site error and §25.2's state-all-three discipline, and the pinned tests with it, for a convenience the overloads spell in two lines); keeping §35.7's status quo (a generated `=` beside a replaced `Equals` is the divergence itself); and refusing every value kind the interface (it would make `Object`'s surface the one surface a value kind cannot promise through an interface, with zero sites either way).

<a id="sec-enum-interface-implementation"></a>
### 9.4 Enum interface implementation

v0.60 prohibited enums from implementing interfaces, even though enums could already declare methods and properties with the same signatures. This meant a user enum with a `CompareTo` method could not declare `: IComparable<MyEnum>`, making it invisible to generic algorithms constrained on interfaces. The restriction was artificial — there is no semantic obstacle to an enum implementing an interface, since the interface methods are simply dispatched to the enum's declared methods.

v0.61 lifts the restriction. Enums may now declare interface conformance. The interfaces appear after the underlying type in the inheritance list: `enum Priority: int, IComparable<Priority>`. The enum must provide matching public members for every interface member, just as classes and records do.

**No intrinsic ordering (v0.314).** Spec §3.5 defines the ordering operators on the primitives other than `bool` and on `string`, and §25.2 resolves an operator on any other operand from declared overloads. Under that text an enum has no `<` unless it declares one, and §8.3's enum row is a row of the `Equals` table, not an ordering. Spec §10.5 states the answer, and the two intrinsic orderings that were available are declined. Four reasons.

1. **Intrinsic ordering and `IComparable<T>` travel together.** Every type with an intrinsic `<` also declares `IComparable<T>`: the numeric primitives and `char` through their stdlib declarations, and `String`. `bool` has neither, and spec §3.5 gives the missing `IComparable` as a reason. An enum conforms to `IComparable<E>` only by declaring it (§9.4 above), and no stdlib enum does. An intrinsic `<` alone would make the enum the one type that `a < b` orders while `Sort` and `OrderBy` reject it as unordered.
2. **There is no single order to pick.** C# orders an enum by underlying value. Java orders by declaration position, and so do Swift's synthesized `Comparable` and Rust's derived `PartialOrd`. The two differ whenever explicit values descend (`Small := 2, Large := 1`), and declaration order is not even defined under §10.2's aliasing, where two positions are one value. The enum's author knows which order is meant; the language would be guessing.
3. **A Bezel enum is a closed set of names, not an integer with names.** It converts to and from its integer only explicitly (spec §10, §5.4), its cast faults on an undeclared value (§9.12 below), and no flags idiom combines its values (§9.12, *The flags question*). An ordering by underlying value is the integer view, and the language keeps that view behind `int(e)`.
4. **The remedy is short and says what it means.** `int(a) < int(b)` costs one conversion at the use. A type that is ordered everywhere declares the four-operator group once, and its bodies are the order. `IComparable<E>` is the spelling generic code needs in any case.

**The cost.** C# habit writes `level >= Level.Warning`, which meets a compile error; the diagnostic should name the three remedies.

**Reopening bar:** a proposal that gives enums an ordering together with its `IComparable<E>` conformance (so operators and generics move together), names one of the two orders and says what it does under aliasing, and brings evidence that the explicit spellings cause defects that an intrinsic order would prevent.

**`Equals(Object?)` on an enum or a choice is rejected (v0.327, [#5598](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5598)).** Spec §10.5 admits `override` on an enum only for the three `Object`-virtual members, and names the `Equals` among them as the one taking the enum's own type: that member is what the compiler installs in the enum's box vtable, so it already fills the `Object` slot a record fills with `Equals(Object?)`. Proposal 0122 L11 (v0.288) left open whether `override` also reaches an `Equals(Object?)` declared beside it. It does not, and neither spelling declares one. Three readings were weighed: (A) the own-typed signature only, `Equals(Object?)` rejected in both spellings; (A′) `override Equals(Object?)` rejected, a plain one admitted as an ordinary method; (B) the record's admission carried over, so that `override Equals(o: Object?)` and the own-typed `Equals` are two overrides of two members. (B) is declined: boxed dispatch unboxes and calls the own-typed member, so the second declaration would be either a dead override (the shape the reference backend produced while sema admitted it: the `Object?`-signature member was never reached from the boxed path) or a second dispatch rule for one slot. (A′) is declined because the plain member is not inert: §8.1's `Equals(other: Object?)` already reaches every enum value, spec §8.3's *Through a type parameter* sends a `T`-typed `Equals` with a non-`T` argument to the type's declared `Equals(Object?)`, and a direct call with an `Object` argument would bind it, while a boxed receiver reaches the own-typed member. One equality would then answer differently by call path, the divergence §25.2's co-declaration rule exists to prevent. A record is different because its `Equals(Object?)` *is* its slot filler, and a class's `override Equals(Object?)` is its only one: under (A) each kind declares its slot's filler at one signature, in either spelling where the kind admits both, and the diagnostic names it. §35.2 carries the same answer to choices at every classification, since a generic choice's declaration is checked once (§35.11).

**Sibling constants in value initializers (v0.315, [#5380](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5380)).** Spec §10.2 lets an enum's value initializer name an earlier constant of the same enum, and the name denotes that constant's underlying value at the underlying type. The values the list computes are constants *of the underlying type* (§10.2), and the list is the one place those integers are written, so inside it the integer view is the one in play; the reading is a rule about the list, not a consequence of §10.2's identity of a value with its integer, which supplies no operator (spec §10.5, *Ordering*). C# reads the same construct the same way. The reading stops at the list: member bodies and every other use see enum values, so reason 3 above is untouched. Nor does it open a flags idiom (§9.12, *The flags question*): `ReadWrite := Read | Write` declares one more named constant, and enum operands still have no `|`. Three alternatives were declined.

1. **The sibling is an enum value, and the remedy is `int(A)`.** §17.14 admits no conversion in a constant expression, so this remedy needs the cast lift §17.14 records as liftable, opened for one position. It would also make `Current := Legacy` ill-typed, though that spelling states an alias exactly.
2. **No sibling references at all.** This rejects the alias spelling and offset layouts (`C := A + 100`) for no safety gain, because nothing outside the list observes the integer.
3. **Forward references with cycle detection.** Auto-increment continuation makes each value depend on its predecessor, so declaration order is already the evaluation order. An earlier-only rule needs no cycle check, and an author who wants a forward reference can reorder the list.

<a id="sec-sealed-abstract-classes"></a>
<a id="sec-closed-classes"></a>
### 9.5 `closed` classes (formerly `sealed abstract`)

The sum-type pattern — a closed set of alternatives matched exhaustively — is common in language design and appears frequently in Bezel's own `case` matching. v0.60 supported this via `abstract` base classes whose subclasses are all `sealed`, but the base class itself could not be both `abstract` and `sealed`, so external code could always subclass it (breaking exhaustiveness guarantees across module boundaries). v0.61 closed the gap with the modifier pair `sealed abstract`: cannot be instantiated, cannot be subclassed outside the declaring module — a compiler-enforced closed-hierarchy mechanism.

**v0.242 renames the spelling to the dedicated modifier `closed`** (spec §11.7; the 2026-08-11 elegance review). The pair had to be defended: `sealed` alone means *no subclasses at all* (spec §12.4), so `sealed abstract` read as a contradiction, and this section previously taught "read the pair, not the words in isolation" — the seal sitting at the module boundary only in the combination. A defense that concedes the words mislead is a teaching tax paid on every reading; `closed` is one word meaning exactly the §11.7 semantics, drawn from the spec's own vocabulary for the concept (§19.6's "closed hierarchy"/"closed set"), and spent while the language still has no external users — the rename only gets more expensive, and the first external user makes the pair permanent. The costs were weighed and accepted: one new reserved word (B.1), and a mechanical two-token respell across the documents and tests. `closed` names the root-marker idiom, not the whole concept — §19.6's closed-set rule continues to cover hierarchies closed without the marker (an `abstract` class whose subclasses are all `sealed`).

<a id="sec-choices-why-a-new-kind"></a>
### 9.6 Choices: why a new kind, and why not the alternatives (v0.200)

Spec §35 adds `choice` — closed nominal sums with payload-carrying cases — as its own type kind. The decision has two halves: why the kind earns its place, and why the three rival encodings were declined as the primary answer.

**Why a kind.** The language's stated domain (services, tools, parsers, protocol code) has "expected failure with information" as its bread-and-butter shape, and the guide's own advice ("return the outcome, don't throw") had no natural spelling: nullable returns cannot carry *why*; the Try pattern spends four interacting features (`out owned`, inline `out`, the `when true` postcondition, the stdlib shape conventions) to express "value or failure" and still carries the failure in out-parameters; `closed` hierarchies are the real sum workhorse but are heavyweight for a return type — heap allocation, reference semantics, type-test patterns instead of destructuring, and the v0.196 generic-leaves determination rule, a page of machinery that exists only because class sums encode the discriminant in the runtime *type* (retired v0.245 — [§8.2](#sec-class-hierarchy-exhaustiveness-in-case)). A choice's discriminant is a case *name*, so exhaustiveness is counted per name and the determination problem dissolves. Every hard ingredient existed by v0.197 — closed-set exhaustiveness, positional destructuring, derived type properties, the move-only category (whose extension §5.14 explicitly anticipated), the marker-free consumption forms (then *target-announced*), the full-expression `case`-scrutinee boundary — making the kind composition, not new theory.

**Why the ownership story is a classification.** The three-way payload-derived classification (value-like / borrow-bearing / owning, spec §35.4) reuses §9.7's derivation pattern exactly: aggregates carrying borrows need no new lifetime kind, and aggregates carrying obligations join §15.3's move-only category. The owning match's two announced triggers — fresh temporaries, and the explicit `case move x of` marker (`case owned x of` before the v0.246 respell) — follow the v0.180 marker principle rather than §30.5's marker-free await consume, because two of the three site-announced-kill ingredients are absent (a choice is legitimately multiply-inspected, and a moved value sets no disposed flag); the scrutinee marker carries meaning precisely because scrutinee position has an unmarked *borrow* reading to disambiguate. Guards observe borrows with transfer at arm selection — the only sound reading that keeps guards available. Owning choices deliberately escape §15.3's reassignment carve-out (#1385): a choice has no partial states, so automatic drop-on-overwrite is sound and taken.

**Declined: widening `enum`.** The enum contract is integer-backed at every joint — explicit values, duplicate-constant aliasing, integer casts, name-string serialization — and each would need an "unless it has payloads" fork: a kind split hiding inside one keyword. Swift's merged spelling works because it was built that way from day one. Two keywords, two clean contracts.

**Declined: class-backed sums (or sugar over them) as the primary answer.** Wrong cost model for outcome returns (allocation, reference semantics), no destructuring for classes by recorded decision (§5.17), and the determination-rule tax on generic coverage (the rule itself is retired v0.245, [§8.2](#sec-class-hierarchy-exhaustiveness-in-case); generic-leaf coverage now simply requires the irrefutable arm). `closed` hierarchies remain the right tool for open-ish, behavior-carrying, identity-bearing sums; the line is drawn at value-shaped outcomes.

**Declined: structural unions (`A | B`).** Anti-nominal: no case names to hang exhaustiveness on, unclear identity, an inference surface that pervades the type system, and a direct collision with the recorded nominal-products posture (D-0002; the records-vs-tuples argument). A payload's *meaning* is exactly what nominal cases record and structural unions erase.

**Equality follows the record precedent, not the inline-array one.** §15.3 gives owning inline arrays borrowing equality (per-slot reference equality for class elements); choices instead take §9.2's use-site error for the non-value-like classifications — reference equality smuggled inside a value type's `=` is virtually never the intended semantics, and the two recorded precedents genuinely conflicted; the conflict was surfaced and ruled, not papered over. *(v0.337, proposal [0178](proposals/0178-declared-equality-is-user-equality.md): a value-like choice's declared `Equals` or `GetHashCode` disqualifies the generated package exactly as a record's does, and the declared pair makes the choice hashable — [§9.3](#sec-auto-equality-on-body-only-records)'s v0.337 paragraph.)*

**`default` is declined for every choice.** A sum yields no distinguished zero case, and electing one would make meaning depend on declaration order; the enum precedent does not transfer because an enum value *is* its integer and zero exists regardless. Recorded liftable if evidence arrives. *(v0.257, ruled under the owner's 2026-08-18 delegation: the predicate "`default(T)` is defined" is registered as the spec §3.6 derived type property **defaultable**, defined at §17.11 — one name replacing the five scattered spellings the spec had accumulated for the predicate, this record's former wording included. The changelog's v0.257 entry carries the full retired-spelling table; no verdict moved.)*

<a id="sec-pure-declared-not-derived"></a>
### 9.7 `pure` is declared, not derived (v0.202)

Spec §22.15 adds the `pure` member modifier — effect-freedom as a declared, compiler-verified signature fact. The mechanism choice needed defending, because §3.6's closing paragraph pushes new classifications toward *derivation*. Three reasons it is declared:

- §3.6 governs structural classifications of *types*; purity is a behavioral contract of a *member* — the same grammatical species as `owned` on a return or `scoped` on a parameter, all declared, none derivable from structure.
- §12.6's principle (derived properties are observations, not obligations) inverts here: **inferred** purity would be an observation callers silently convert into an obligation — adding one log line to a leaf method would flip the inferred purity of every transitive caller, breaking every pure-context use with no declaration anywhere recording that a promise existed. Declared purity puts the promise, and the breakage, at an explicit keyword whose removal is a visible signature change.
- The artifact interface (§2.8.2) carries signatures, not bodies; an inferred fact would make module interfaces change when bodies change — the instability §2.8.2 exists to prevent.

The v1 boundary decisions, recorded: purity is **not determinism** (reads of mutable state are permitted; `det`/`total` are separate future promises — `pure` permanently means effect-free); class construction is excluded except the **throw carve-out** (a trivial-dispose class with a pure constructor as the immediate throw operand — without it, argument validation would be unwritable in the whole pure fragment and programmers would launder faults through provoked built-ins); function-typed values may be defined but not invoked (their types carry no effect information; the `(int) pure -> int` extension is the recorded v2, riding §27.1's modifier-identity precedent); override/implementation matching is strengthening-only (never-drop / may-strengthen / monotone — the exact-match alternative would sever every stdlib interface implementation from purity). The stdlib first wave annotates only non-virtual concrete members; annotating `IComparable<T>`/`IEquatable<T>`/`Object` roots is a recorded breaking-change event for a future major revision. The recorded widening (trivial-dispose construction admission, lifting `string.Split`-class exclusions) is proposal 0042 §8's variant B, staged behind one implementation cycle.

**The pure-body `for` widening (v0.244, proposal 0085).** Spec §22.15 rule 5 originally rejected `for` over `Array<T>`/`OwningArray<T>`/`string` sources in pure bodies — the desugaring constructs an enumerator — putting an index-loop tax on the most common loop shape in the language, taught as a trap (Guide 6 §6). v0.244 extends rule 5's own template: for concretely-declared sources of those types (class-typed `OwningBuffer<T>` included — the one §4.14 family member on the protocol path; excluding it would have been a one-type hole for no stated reason), §29.2's already-expected protocol elision becomes *the normative semantics inside a pure body* — exactly the move rule 5 had already made once for `Range<T>`'s counted loop ("elsewhere an admitted elision"). The condition is the **declared static type at the body's own check site**: generic type parameters stay rejected even when every instantiation is a concrete sequence type, because §22.15's checking discipline is single-pass over bodies by design ("checked once, at its declaration") — admitting loops per-instantiation would make body-statement admissibility substitution-dependent, a checker-architecture change for one loop form (the existing per-instantiation checks are signature-level facts, re-checked without re-entering the body). Interface-typed sources stay rejected on the elision's own condition ("see the type concretely" is false by construction). `IReadOnlySpan<TElement>` is deferred to its own stdlib-contract issue (its lowering is index-shaped but through interface-dispatched accessors not yet `pure`-declared); a pure-iterator design remains deliberately open, its demand now narrowed. Nothing outside pure bodies changes; the widening admits previously-rejected programs only.

<a id="sec-contracts-grammar-and-tiers"></a>
### 9.8 Contracts are grammar, and the tier is the contract's honesty (v0.203)

Spec §22.16 adds `requires`/`ensures`/`invariant` — SPARK-lineage behavioral contracts, not Dafny-lineage proof obligations. The decisions with recorded reasons:

**Grammar, not attributes or library calls.** Bezel has no attribute system and the deferral is reasoned (§18); C# Code Contracts — conditions as method calls, a bolt-on rewriter, no compiler ownership — is the named cautionary lineage, and §22.4 already chose the compiler-recognized-clause route for exactly this kind of declaration. A body statement (`Verify.That`) cannot appear in the signature or artifact interface, cannot bind overrides, cannot be consumed by callers, and has no `result`/`old` or every-return-edge evaluation point; 0031's `Verify.That` stays as the body-internal complement with its distinct exception.

**Clauses are pure borrows** — the 0042 dependency: because a clause has no effects, evaluating, eliding, and proving it are indistinguishable to every non-violating program, which is what makes the tier system (proven / checked / assumed) coherent and the checked→proven ratchet meaning-preserving. Eiffel's side-effecting assertions and its global suppression flag are the counter-examples; both are declined.

**One violation exception, always-on `requires`.** `ContractViolationException` for all three kinds — the `ArgumentException`-compatible alternative was presented and declined (no honest `ParamName` mapping for multi-term conditions; two types for one mechanism; recovery arms written for input validation should not silently absorb bug reports; the per-member migration cost is recorded and borne deliberately, which is why the stdlib ships no clause conversions yet). `requires` is never elided (the 0031 posture — it replaces an always-on idiom); `ensures`/`invariant` run under checked-contracts mode, the §28.2 two-mode shape with a crucially different relaxation: non-evaluation (defined behavior, purity making it unobservable), not undefined behavior.

**Behavioral subtyping without weakening syntax.** Overrides inherit clauses verbatim, may add `ensures`, may not declare `requires`; multi-root preconditions combine by disjunction (the Eiffel inherited-precondition rule, arrived at for the same reason); `require else`/`ensure then` refinement syntax is declined until demand materializes.

**Invariants at the public boundary with the `this`-reentrancy exemption** — the Eiffel lessons adopted (interior algorithms legitimately traverse broken states; checked/unchecked faces make the exemption statically decidable), the Eiffel machinery (suppression flags, refinement keywords) not. Getters exempt, with the residual recorded; construction checks at most-derived exit only, composing with §22.7's observation gate by construction.

**The prover floor is honest.** Exactly two things are proven in this revision: constant folds and §22.4's `when` clauses (the shipped flow prover, now *named* as the prover it is — §22.4 is retro-unified as a `static ensures`, surface-preserved, zero migration). Essentially every authored clause is checked; the extended fragment (linear integer arithmetic, null-test flow, closed-set case analysis) is fixed now as an Appendix D feature so `static`'s meaning never drifts — a demanded proof never silently becomes a runtime check.

**The `old(E)` capture taxonomy completed: move-only value operands are rejected at the clause (v0.253; spec defect [#3113](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3113); owner-directed 2026-08-12 — "Please also resolve and push 3113").** Filed by the v0.252 train lane and sharpened by that train's gate: §22.16's capture taxonomy had an arm for copyable operands (copy) and an arm for class- and interface-typed operands (frame-resident borrow), and none for a **move-only value** operand (§15.3 — an owning inline array, an owning-classified choice). The gate's sharpening is the defect's strongest statement: the gap was worse than undefined, because §22.16's own desugaring account — "a hidden entry binding equivalent to `var __oldN := E;`", every user-binding rule governing the hidden one — meets §15.3's local-to-local rule as a **move** (K3.12: every store of a move-only value into an owning location is a move), so a literal reading had `old(arr)` silently *consume* the operand at entry, leaving the parameter or field moved-from for the entire body — a contract position mutating the state it exists to observe. Three interpretations were weighed. **(1) Reject the operand** — adopted: a compile error at the contract clause, the diagnostic naming the copyable-projection remedy; the only arm that neither consumes nor lies. **(2) Capture by borrow** — the mechanism §19.3's move-only scrutinee rule and §28.9's move-only closure capture both use — declined on semantics, not mechanism: those adoptions are no precedent because scrutinee and capture positions observe *current* state by design, while `old`'s single purpose is the pre-state; a borrow observes post-state mutations, making `old(E)` silently not-old for exactly one operand kind — a contract clause that lies. The nearest counter-precedent is arm 2 itself, which *keeps* post-state observation — with a warning, not an error — for class-typed operands; the asymmetry is principled: there the captured value **is** the entry reference, exactly what `old` promises, and only reads *through* it are post-state (the shallow-`old` warning's territory), while for a move-only value the value is the mutable contents themselves, so a borrow yields no entry snapshot at all. **(3) Element-wise snapshot** — declined as disproportionate: deep-copy machinery in contract position, still undefined wherever an element is itself owned (the copy would mint a second owner), and it would import disposal-ownership questions — who disposes the snapshot's owned contents, and when — into contract evaluation, which has no machinery for them and deliberately none (clauses are pure borrows, the 0042 dependency above). The desugaring account is conformed rather than contradicted: the hidden binding is now stated to be a **copy** (or the reference arm's §28.3 unmarked borrow), never a consume — which is precisely why a move-only operand is rejected rather than desugared; the rejection arm preempts the silent move. The completion claim is made total by the same train's gate round: a **function-typed operand** — not a copyable value, record, enum, or `string`; not class- or interface-typed — landed in no arm, a pre-existing hole; arm 1 now names it, the capture a copy of the *reference* for the runtime-managed reference kinds (§28.9), no ownership machinery arising. Kernel cost: none — a use-site admissibility rule in contract position, grounded on K3.12's no-copy; no new K row, no axiom. The remedy is stated honest to the verified surface: an owning inline array has no helper route at all (a pure member declares no move-only value parameter, §22.15; extension blocks are closed against inline arrays and choices, §26.2), so its projections are element-derived — `old(pool[i] <> null)`, a `pure` member's value through a borrowed element — while an owning choice additionally has its own `pure` instance methods, `this` a borrow (§35.2) — a route real in the language yet empty for stdlib `Result<T, E>`, whose members are deliberately non-pure (the one-purity-posture ruling, stdlib §2.20), which is why the remedy names the universally available route beside it: a borrowing-match projection over the operand, `old((case c of …))`, consuming nothing (§35.5) and needing no member on the choice.

<a id="sec-interface-carries-contracts"></a>
### 9.9 The interface carries contracts, textually (v0.204)

Spec §2.8.2's contract records (v0.204) rest on three decisions worth recording.

**Clause identity is textual.** Semantic equivalence of clauses is undecidable (clauses are boolean expressions over arithmetic and calls; deciding `requires(a and b)` ≡ `requires(b and a)` in general is deciding formula equivalence), and any decidable approximation misclassifies in one direction or the other. Textual identity is the conservative choice with a stated bias: it never *under*-reports a contract change — soundness for any gate built on the classification — at the cost of over-reporting stylistic re-spellings (`1_000` → `1000`, added parentheses, re-qualified names) as clause replacements. The two admitted canonicalizations (comments and whitespace; parameter and type-parameter positions) are exactly the ones that keep the record stable under edits that are *invisible at every use site or already surfaced there as signature events*: a `ref`/`out` parameter rename is call-site-invisible (spec §22.5: those arguments are positional-only), and a plain or `owned` parameter rename is already a *signature* event (named arguments) and should surface once, as one — not twice with a spurious clause delta beside it. (The original wording grounded the `owned` case on call-site invisibility; v0.250 falsified that ground — an `owned` parameter is call-site-addressable by name, since v0.260 for move-rooted arguments too (spec §22.5's named-move lift; at v0.250–v0.259 the move-rooted spelling had stayed positional-only) — so the `owned` case now rides the plain-parameter surface-once ground, and the §2.8.2 position-canonicalization conclusion is unchanged; conformed v0.256, re-conformed v0.260. The surface-once ground is itself a recorded loose end rather than an anchor: spec §2.8.2's canonicalization means a parameter rename *changes no record* — that half is real — but spec §2.8.6's member matching does not key on parameter names and carries no rename row, so the signature event the ground appeals to is classified nowhere today: a plain or `owned` parameter rename, call-site-meaningful under named arguments, currently surfaces as no delta at all. The ground states where the event belongs — once, as a signature event — not where any current row puts it; a §2.8.6 rename row is the natural completion, and the position-canonicalization conclusion stands on the changes-no-record half alone.) Type-parameter names are likewise not part of signature identity (arity is). Semantic normalization — commutativity, constant folding, SMT-checked implication — was declined for the verdict itself: it would make the classification depend on prover strength, so two conforming tools could classify one delta differently, which for a CI gate is the worst available property. (Implication checking has a natural home as a future *advisory* layer, never as the verdict.) Redacting non-public state from carried clause text was also declined: a redacted record cannot be agreement-checked or diffed, which forfeits the staleness backstop exactly where stale consumption is least visible; the disclosure has the definition-site precedent and the same recompile-the-world cost profile.

**The recorded tier is demand-derived.** Spec §22.16 lets an implementation opportunistically discharge unqualified clauses and elide their checks, as-if. If the interface recorded those discharges, the recorded tier would be a function of the member's **body** — a body edit could flip proven→checked with no declaration change, silently invalidating the interface bytes (defeating early-cutoff builds keyed on interface identity) and, worse, silently invalidating any downstream proof that consumed the tier, with nothing in any declaration to point at. Deriving the recorded tier from the declaration alone — the `static` qualifier, plus §22.16's constant-fold floor, whose fold reads only the clause text and the `const` values it names — makes the tier a declaration fact: stable under body edits, guaranteed by compilation (a demanded proof that fails is a compile error), and therefore safe for downstream consumption. The recorded tier is simply the clause's §22.16 tier — one term, one value; what is never recorded is the per-site as-if elision of a checked clause, the only discharge that is not a declaration fact. The matching consumption rule in §2.8.2 is the same decision seen from the consumer: a proof no interface promises is not consumable.

**Identity excludes contracts; the verdict is layered.** Folding contract records into §2.8.3's type-identity key would make a clause edit mint a "new" type — every `is` test, `catch` clause, and pattern over it would bifurcate for values sharing one runtime representation — and would replace the *useful* answer to a contract mismatch (the staleness error, naming the stale artifact and the fix) with an unrelated-types error naming nothing. Identity is runtime oneness; contracts are obligations revised across revisions of one module while the type stays the same type. And a tier demotion gets a per-level verdict (spec §2.8.6) because both flat answers are wrong: flat-breaking cries wolf at every Core-level consumer (nothing they can consume changed — a tool that over-alarms teaches itself to be ignored), while flat-preserving mislabels a real break at the proof level. "Breaking where callers consumed the proof" is undecidable for a tool that cannot see callers; *level-gating* is its decidable refinement, and it keeps the ratchet culture healthy — promotion is everywhere-preserving, demotion is an honest, level-scoped event.

<a id="sec-method-local-type-declarations"></a>
### 9.10 Method-local record and enum declarations (v0.225)

Adopted v0.225 (proposal 0066; owner-directed 2026-07-24). Spec [§21.10](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-type-declarations) admits `record` and `enum` declarations as body-scoped statements. The feature is the **named remedy the anonymous-types decline already committed to** — this record graduates that remedy from the proposals' declined register (DECLINED.md D-0002) into a first-class rationale decision, so the accepted direction lives where accepted directions live.

**Why it is the remedy, and why nominal.** Anonymous types were declined on three structural grounds — they require structural typing inside a nominal system, they cannot cross method boundaries, and their killer habitat is reflection, which Bezel deliberately lacks (§18). The decline named its own answer: a method-local `record` puts the shape next to its single use, keeping the recorded records-over-tuples stance ("a name and a documented shape is a feature") while removing the distance cost — a projection's result type no longer has to live at module or class level, arbitrarily far from the one lambda that builds it. The type stays **nominal and named**: two same-shaped local records in different methods are distinct types, which is exactly the structural unification anonymous types would have imported. Nothing is inferred from a projection expression; the declaration is explicit, and cross-type mapping stays explicit (constructors, `with` for same-type variants) — automatic mapping remains reflection-shaped and declined (the D-0001 wiring posture).

**The scope cut is the whole design.** The local type is an *ordinary* record/enum — §9.2 auto-equality, §9.5 `with`, §9.8 serialization eligibility, the full §10 enum surface — with a single relaxation (where it may be declared) and four conservative rulings that keep that relaxation sound: strict body scope with no signature positions and no type shadowing; boxing-escape admitted only as far as §8.6 already goes (a non-borrow-bearing local record may escape boxed through an interface — the `private`-nested-type precedent — while a borrow-bearing one keeps §8.6's call-bounded-box rejection, the case that stops a `record View(Cell: Widget)` over a `using var` widget from dangling); no reference to an enclosing generic parameter in v1 (which would make identity and equality/serialization synthesis per-instantiation — a genuine design space disproportionate to concrete-field projections, and a compatible future widening where the reverse would break); and `enum` in, `choice`/`class`/`interface` out (the excluded kinds carry classification/coverage or ownership/disposal machinery that is real kernel-engaging surface for no recorded need). No new kernel rule: local records/enums are value types, and a borrow-bearing local record engages the existing §9.7/§8.6/§28.3 borrow machinery exactly as a module-level record does. The declined alternatives (enclosing-generic-parameter capture, local `choice`, type shadowing, local `class`, unqualified escape-via-boxing) and the liftables are proposal 0066's.

(v0.314) **A nested type does not capture its enclosing generic's type parameters** (spec [§11.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-nested-types)). The ground is the local-type restriction's, and the language already had it: [§2.8.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-transitive-closure-and-type-identity) rule 3 gives a nested type no identity component for its enclosing type's arguments, and A.5 has no spelling for `Outer<int>.Inner` in a type. A nested type that named `T` would be implicitly generic, with no name for any of its instantiations. C#'s nested types and Java's inner classes take the other road (`Outer<int>.Inner` and `Outer<string>.Inner` are distinct types there); here that is a design change, reaching identity, the type grammar, and every per-instantiation rule ([§14.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-generic-declarations)'s instantiation sites, static cells, equality and serialization synthesis), declined for no recorded need. A nested type that needs the parameter declares its own and is used as `Node<T>`, which says the same thing with a name for each instantiation. A `forbid → allow` widening stays compatible where the reverse would break.

(v0.316) **Outside the reach of [§2.7](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-name-resolution-order)'s enclosing-type lookup, a type nested in a generic type has no type-position spelling** (spec [§11.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-nested-types)). Two rules the language already holds settle it. Every reference fixes its arity by its own tokens ([§2.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-imports) *Collisions*, [§14.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-generic-declarations)), so the bare `Tree` in `Tree.Node<int>` names an arity-0 `Tree`, and A.5 admits type arguments after the last segment only. In `Tree`'s declaration, and in the bodies of types deriving from it, the type is `Node<int>`. Elsewhere chain heads reach its statics and inferred locals hold its values, but no annotation, `new` expression, `is` test or pattern, `catch` arm, `typeof` operand, explicit type argument or extension head names it, and an extension block over `Tree<T>` cannot name it in a member signature. The cost is real and bounded: a library that wants callers to name a helper type declares it top-level, as the standard library does with `LinkedListNode<T>` and `DictionaryEntry<K, V>`. Two alternatives were weighed. *Exempting a bare enclosing segment from arity selection* is declined. Its tokens would no longer fix its arity, against v0.303's raw-reference decline and v0.311's injected-name decline ([#5164](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5164)), and adding an import of a non-generic `Tree` that declares its own `Node<U>` would silently re-resolve `Tree.Node<int>`, against [§2.7](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-name-resolution-order)'s guarantee that an added import never changes a resolved chain. *Admitting type arguments on an enclosing segment* (`Tree<int>.Node<int>` in a type, the arguments fixing the arity and nothing else) is deferred. It composes with arity keying, since the tokens fix the arity, but it is a grammar change, and arguments outside identity invite the reading that `Tree<int>.Node<int>` and `Tree<string>.Node<int>` differ. It waits on a recorded need that a top-level declaration cannot meet. **Rendering.** `Type.FullName` shows an enclosing generic type as one `_` per type parameter (`"M.Tree<_>.Node<int>"`, stdlib [§2.13](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-the-type-class) rule 1). The nested type has no enclosing arguments to render. Dropping the segment's arity would give the nested types of the arity twins `Outer` and `Outer<T>`, two types under [§2.8.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-transitive-closure-and-type-identity) rule 3, one FullName, against rule 1's distinct-FullName guarantee; a counted marker (the CLR's ``Outer`1``) is the arity marker rule 3 declines. Both are declined. The `_` reads as "an argument not fixed here", and the form is a rendering, not a type expression.

(v0.316, [#5405](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5405)) **A local type does not capture the enclosing body's locals** (spec [§21.10](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-type-declarations)). Inside a local type's declaration the enclosing body's locals and parameters are not in scope, and a bare name spelled like one is an error naming the rule rather than a lookup that passes over it to a same-named member or module declaration, the posture of spec §2.7's *Through nesting*. A lambda captures because it is a value with a closure; a local type is a type, one per declaration, and an instance of it can outlive the body that declared it, boxed through an interface. Declined: capture, whether Java's local classes' copy of effectively-final locals into each instance or a reference into the declaring frame. The copy would give the record hidden state that §9.2's equality, §9.8's serialization and §9.7's borrow-bearing classification would each have to account for; the reference would point into a frame a boxed instance can outlive. An explicit field says the same thing with a name. Also declined: an exception for compile-time `const` locals. It would be safe, but it would make what is in scope depend on an initializer's form (spec §21.1 *Runtime `const` locals*), and a constant the type needs can be its static member. A `forbid → allow` widening stays compatible where the reverse would break.

<a id="sec-declared-result-target-typing"></a>
### 9.11 Declared-result target-typing: bare cases where the signature already says the type (v0.233)

Adopted v0.233 (proposal 0075 stage 1; owner-ruled 2026-07-29). Spec [§35.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-choice-construction) lets a bare case construction (`Ok(v)`, `Error(msg)`, a payload-free `NotModified`) target-type against the declared result type in declared-result positions. Three decisions behind it deserve a citable home.

**The mechanism is the declared-type-directed family, not inference.** The rule joins forms Bezel already has — `new()` in typed contexts ([§17.7](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-new-operator)), target-typed integer constants (§4.2), return-position collection literals — where a *written* declared type supplies information flowing inward. Nothing is inferred from the construction's arguments, so [§14.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-type-argument-inference)'s never-infer-type-arguments-from-constructor-arguments posture is untouched, and partial qualification (`Result.Ok(v)`) stays an error rather than becoming a third, half-inferred spelling. The evidence bar was the recorded one: the qualified spelling proved heavy in real code (nine full restatements of `Result<int, ConfigError>` in the first `Result`-dense program, proposal 0061 §3), engaging the liftable proposal 0041 recorded at the choice landing.

**Authoritative, not scope-first.** Inside a declared-choice-result position the case reading wins over any same-named local, parameter, member, or type, with a shadowing warning. The alternative — scope-first with case fallback — was declined as action at a distance: under it, adding a helper method (or local) anywhere in scope would silently re-mean a `return` statement far away, the exact hazard [§26.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-generic-extensions)'s type-parameter binding rule records as disqualifying. The authoritative rule keeps every `return` in a choice-returning body meaning the same thing regardless of what is in scope, and the warning surfaces the collision instead of hiding it.

**The narrow position family is the design.** Only positions with a *written* declared type participate — return operands, expression bodies, ascribed-lambda returns (v0.296 names these the declared-result group of the written-declared-type family, spec §17.7, and keeps the case reading to exactly that group; the `new()` shorthand's return position stays the `return` statement alone, its extension to the other two recorded liftable). Subexpression branch positions (`return (if c then Ok(v) else Error(e));`) are deferred, recorded liftable — extending declared-type flow into the `(if …)` atom is a [§20](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-if-expression) change with its own meet questions — and the `new()`-context family (typed locals, field initializers, default parameter values) is recorded but not taken: principled, but with no restatement pain in evidence. Each boundary is liftable by its own future ruling; none is an accident.

<a id="sec-checked-enum-conversions"></a>
### 9.12 Checked enum conversions: `E(n)` faults on undeclared values (v0.242)

Adopted v0.242 (proposal 0078; owner-ruled 2026-08-11, from the elegance review). Through v0.241, `E(n)` for an in-range `n` matching no declared constant succeeded and minted a "raw" enum value equal to no declared constant, "the call site responsible for validating." The behavior was inherited from C# and *documented* as a gap-fill at v0.72 — no rationale entry ever weighed it. This entry is the missing record, written for the decision that deletes it.

**Why checked.** Every other conversion in Bezel is checked or rejected: integer narrowing raises `OverflowException` (spec §5.2), constant narrowing out of range is a compile error (§5.1), float-to-integer checks — and even the enum cast's own *range* half was checked. Declaredness was the one carve-out, and the documents paid for it three times: spec §10.4's own `Name` example needed a paragraph defusing the raw value with a defensive `_ => "Unknown"` arm — advice directly contradicting Guide 3 §2's teaching that a `_` arm silences the compiler's help; §9.8's synthesized serialization rejects raw values on both directions, refuting the stated round-tripping use case; and §19.6's enum-exhaustiveness proof was unsound at runtime — coverage over the declared *values*, a value space the cast could escape. The checked rule (§5.4: constant operand → compile error; otherwise `OverflowException`) restores two invariants by deleting a carve-out: *conversions are checked* with no exception, and *enum exhaustiveness is real* — with the §17.11 rider (a `default(E)` on an enum with no zero-valued constant escalates from warning to error, closing the last producer of an unnameable value), every runtime value of an enum type is a declared value, end to end, which is what lets §21.7 grant statement-`case` totality to the enum constants-coverage proof.

**The boundary tier.** Wire parsers and FFI ingestion get the branch-shaped form as a compiler-provided per-enum static member, `E.FromUnderlyingOrNull(value: U): E?` (spec §10.4) — the stdlib §1 `*OrNull` tier's exact shape, a recorded deviation from the review's `TryFromUnderlying` sketch: a conversion is not acquisition-shaped, and an enum payload is a non-nullable value type, so `E?` carries the whole signal and the `Try` spelling stays reserved.

**The name tier (v0.293, proposal 0124).** The string→enum direction the gap map carried as row 32 ([#2244](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/2244)) lands as a sibling of `FromUnderlyingOrNull`, not as the `Enum.Parse<E>` the row sketched: `E.FromNameOrNull(name: string): E?` with its inverse `E.NameOf(value: E): string`, both compiler-provided per-enum statics (spec §10.4). Four choices, each with its reason. *Per-enum static, not generic:* §14.2 has no `enum` constraint, and `where E: value` admits records, choices and primitives, so a generic `Enum.Parse<E>` is either unspellable or under-constrained; a new constraint kind is a §14.2 design change with grammar and §3.6 knock-on, bought for one member family, while the per-enum static reuses the device the checked-cast revision shipped. What that gives up is reach from generic code — no constraint carries the enum domain, so a generic `Bind<T>(s): T?` has no spelling; accepted because no filed workload asks for one, and recorded as the reopening trigger. *Band 2 only:* an unrecognized name at a configuration or wire boundary is an expected absence carrying no information beyond its occurrence (stdlib §1's failure-signal rule); the name axis has no assert *tier* — `?? throw` is the idiom, and a compiler-provided `FromName` would have to pick one exception for every program where the call site knows which is right; the shape-(c) `TryParse` form has no honest false-path value, `default(E)` being ill-formed on an enum with no zero constant (§17.11). `FromUnderlyingOrNull` shipped on the same argument, and no demand for a throwing twin has been filed since. *The declared identifier, not the serialized name:* three surfaces must agree — `nameof(E.A)` (§17.9), `Type.EnumNames` (stdlib §2.13) and `E.NameOf(v)` — and they can only agree on the identifier table; §9.8's rename is by its own words the *encoded* form, which already round-trips through the serializers, and matching it here would let a JSON-motivated rename silently re-key a configuration parse. The cost accepted: a boundary that wants a renamed constant's wire spelling deserializes it rather than calling `FromNameOrNull`. Identifiers are unique within a declaration by construction, so the surface is well-defined without leaning on §9.8's uniqueness clause. *A static `NameOf`, not an instance `Name` and not `ToString`:* an instance `Name` would be the first compiler-provided instance member on enums and would collide with the user-declared `Name` property §10.4's own example carries; `ToString`'s qualified-name default (§8.5) is a separate rule with its own blast radius — every interpolation of every enum — and is left as it stands, with a one-line override as the opt-in. *The §18 boundary:* the table is a compile-time constant of one declaration — the list `nameof` already folds at compile time — consulted for a constant of a statically known type; nothing enumerates members, reaches a member by name, or constructs — the "closed, stateless, ownership-free" enum introspection §5.18 already classifies as the safest that exists.

**The flags question, investigated: no flags idiom exists.** §10 defines no flags mechanism, the stdlib contains zero flags-style enums (`FileAccess` spells `ReadWrite` as a distinct declared constant), and the corpus held exactly one flags-shaped artifact — a sema-only operator-overload test whose compositions all land on declared values. A flags *set* is honestly an integer with named `const` masks — §17.14's own example is precisely that shape. So the checked rule is total, with no unchecked side door; an unchecked entry point now, against zero demand, would be speculative surface. **Recorded liftable:** if a real flags workload arrives, the shape to lift is an explicitly-named `E.FromUnderlyingUnchecked(value: U): E` — loud, greppable, never the default spelling — or a first-class flags declaration form.

**The fault type is `OverflowException`.** §5.2 already uses it for every "value not representable in the destination type" failure; the checked rule reads as extending the destination's value set from *range of U* to *declared values of E*. A dedicated subclass was declined for economy (no plausible handler branches enum-cast-vs-overflow), `ArgumentException` outright (a cast site has no `ParamName` referent). The same ruling covers spec §6.5's shift-count fault and §16.4's computed-zero range step — one answer at all three sites, decided together.

**The enum→integer direction (v0.288, proposal 0122 L9).** The opposite cast had been shaped around one spelling's popularity: `int(p)` was defined on every enum by a two-step reading, `U(p)` by the underlying type, and `long(p)` on a `byte`-backed enum by nothing. §5.4 now defines `I(e)` for every integer target `I` as the same two steps — to the underlying type, then §5.2's `I(u)` — so the checked posture above is applied by the second step rather than restated per spelling, and the two cases the older text covered reproduce exactly.

**Declined:** status quo with a defending entry (the defense does not exist — the serialization use case is refuted by §9.8's own design); making `case` total over the underlying type (forces `_` arms everywhere — regresses to C#); validating at *use* instead of at the cast (moves the fault away from its cause and makes `=` partial); a `bool`+`out` Try shape (the bool adds ceremony over `E?` and no information).

<a id="sec-value-receivers-are-borrows-rationale"></a>
### 9.13 Value receivers are borrows: stores through `this` are rejected (v0.255)

**(Spec defect [#3124](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3124), the v0.254 residue; resolved 2026-08-18 under the owner's delegation — "there are a few open spec questions from the last week. Please find them, resolve them cleanly, gate them and merge them to master. I trust your judgement." — the ruling posted on the issue, not a per-question owner call.)** v0.254's boxed-write ruling ended with a promise (its changelog's closing paragraph; [§11.6](#sec-is-does-not-bind)'s record): how a value-typed receiver's `this` binds — alias of the receiver's storage, or something weaker — was stated nowhere, for records or for value types generally, and would get its own ruling rather than be settled as a side effect. This is that ruling; the promise is discharged.

**The gap's one live case** was the mutable body record: spec §9.4 admits settable properties, nothing forbade a record member body from assigning one, and the meaning of `r.Bump()` turned on the unstated binding — meaningful under an alias reading, silently useless under a copy reading. The other three value kinds were safe by construction (a choice has no assignable instance storage and §35.2 already said *borrow*; an enum's state is its underlying constant; inline arrays have no members). The v0.254 gate widened the stakes: a **boxed** receiver is reached not only by §12.7 narrowing but by plain interface dispatch — `var i: IBump := rec; i.Bump();` boxes at the assignment and invokes the member on the shared payload with no type test anywhere — so under an alias reading the write lands in the shared box through a completely unmarked call, reproducing exactly the aliased mutation v0.254 rejected for narrowed writes.

**Three interpretations were weighed.** (1) **`this` aliases the receiver's storage** — the C#-struct reading: `r.Bump()` mutates `r`; the boxed case then needs its own rejection, which has nothing to key on — the language has no mutates-receiver member classification, the interface-dispatch route has no narrowing site at all, and rejecting every member call on a boxed binding would break `o.ToString()` and §17.4's own inline-array remedy. (2) **`this` binds a borrow, stores through it rejected at the member body** — records become behaviorally immutable through members; settable properties stay assignable from outside through a named binding's lvalue chain. (3) **Reject self-mutating members at the declaration** — equivalent to (2) at the use site, stated as a declaration rule. **Adopted: (2) and (3) combined** — the binding is a borrow (the semantic statement), and the store rejection lands at the member body (the declaration side), so every call site is safe by construction and no caller ever observes a surprise.

**The four grounds.** *Uniformity*: every existing receiver and binding precedent reads value receivers as borrows — §35.2 for choices, §26.7 for extension receivers, §19.3 for move-only scrutinee bindings, §28.9 for move-only closure captures, §28.3's producers table for `this` itself — a reading that **widens** that row's recorded scope, acknowledged here: the pinned v0.254 entry read the row as classifying `this` for ownership only, while this ruling reads the unqualified row ("Borrowed (source: caller)") as stating both the ownership and the binding classification — no competing classification exists anywhere, and the widening is recorded rather than glossed; interpretation 1 would have made body records the one value kind with aliasing receivers, a special case with a blast radius. *It keeps closed what v0.254 closed*: under the borrow reading plus the settable-member conformance rejection, no member reachable **through a box** writes any receiver — a box's dispatch faces are `Object` and the conformed interfaces, `Object` declares no settable member, and no conformable interface may — where the alias reading re-opens the boxed write through two routes, one of them unmarked. (Not a vacuity: receiver-writing members exist on value kinds — the synthesized auto-property setter is one, invoked as an accessor (§6.6, §22.15, §22.16) — the closure is that no dispatch face a box has can reach one; the v0.255 gate round corrected an earlier vacuity claim here.) *It matches the record design's own update story*: records are value data whose update form is `with` (§9.5) — non-destructive, visible at the call site; what retires is only the hidden self-mutation a member body could smuggle, while external stores (`r.X := 5`) keep their announced, well-defined meaning. *§8.5's parenthetical conforms rather than contradicts*: the one nearby text, the boxed-`ToString` receiver's "call-bounded copy", is observably indistinguishable from a borrow of an immutable payload — values have no reference identity — so the uniform borrow spelling replaces the one-off copy claim at no observable cost.

**Scope facts the ruling's letter left implicit, stated at landing.** Constructor bodies are not receiver positions — a record constructor initializes the value under construction (§9.2's own canonical form assigns `Width := width;`); the rule would otherwise outlaw record construction itself. The store rejection keys on the store **landing in the receiver's own storage**, never on the chain's `this` root: a chain that hops through a class- or interface-typed member leaves the receiver (§9.7's borrow fields), so a store beyond the hop lands in the borrowed heap object and is legal — the mediating shape, which is what keeps stdlib §4.14/§4.15's span indexer setters (`Buffer[Start + i] := value`, `Source[Start + i] := value`) declarable; a chain-root key would have rejected the stdlib's own records (the v0.255 gate round's blocker). The rejection covers **author-written accessor bodies**, not only methods: a block-form setter storing its backing field through implicit `this` is the same receiver store by another spelling. The **synthesized** auto-property setter is a receiver-writing member body too — invoked as an accessor (§6.6's once-each accounting, §22.15's never-pure classification, §22.16's setter instrumentation), which is why the boxed case is *not* closed by any vacuity — but exempt: its store is the external assignment's own store, announced at the call site, and the rule governs author-written bodies. What closes the boxed case is the **settable-member conformance rejection** (spec §9.6, echoed §11.2/§35.8): a value kind cannot conform to an interface declaring a settable property or indexer member, so `i.X := 42` on an interface view of a boxed record has no settable member to name — no dispatch face a box has reaches any receiver-writing member. That rejection is **blanket** — a conformance is rejected for naming a settable member at all, not keyed on whether the accessor the value kind would supply stores the receiver — a deliberate simplification, not a decidability necessity: the keyed form is decidable at the same declaration site (the accessor body is present there). Its cost is named — the blanket form forecloses the sound mediating conformance shape, a record satisfying an interface's settable indexer member with a §4.14-style class-hop setter — and no stdlib interface needs that shape today (`IReadOnlySpan<T>`'s indexer is get-only by design; the settable-indexer interfaces `IList<T>`/`IOwningList<T>` have class implementers only). The recorded lift is exactly the keyed form — admit the conformance where every supplied accessor's stores land beyond the receiver — with no other rule moving. The corpus measurement is recorded honestly in the v0.255 changelog: the method-body shape is essentially unused, but a family of compiler tests exercises record block-form setters as accepted-and-lowered (the implementation had grown interpretation 1 for accessors); those are impl-ahead-of-spec artifacts of the unstated binding, newly rejected by this ruling — the conformance arm's own flip set measured separately (two further green tests, named in the changelog) — tracked with the sema catch-up on #3124.

**Kernel cost: none.** A receiver-binding classification plus a declaration-site admissibility rule; the borrow machinery reads it (§28.3's `this` row was already the borrow); no new K row, no axiom spent.

<a id="sec-constructor-only-records-rationale"></a>
### 9.14 Constructor-only records: one property, two consequences, and what it deliberately does not claim (v0.268)

**(Proposal 0107, accepted 2026-08-20; spec §9.9.)** §27.8 prescribes a single-field record for genuinely distinct types, and the language then falsified that record's one guarantee from two sides: §9.5 states that `with` *copies field values directly; it does not invoke the record's constructor*, and §17.11 made a record defaultable whenever its fields were, so `default(Port)` produced a `Port` that met no constructor. The half the language already had was constructor-exit checking — §22.16 admits `requires` and `ensures` on a record constructor and evaluates the `ensures` at body completion — so what was missing was never an instrumentation point. It was that such a record has *other producers*, and nothing in the language knew that some records should not.

**Why a derived type property and not a fold into `defaultable`.** The non-defaultability half folds cleanly: §17.11's computation could simply carry a declaration-keyed disqualifier, on the model of the *defaultable* row's own *an enum declaring a zero-valued constant*. The `with` half cannot, and cross-module carriage is why. §2.8.2 carries derived type properties *as computed verdicts*, but it carries contract records only for **every member the interface describes** — public and `protected` members. A record whose clause-bearing constructor is `private` or `internal` (A.3's `ConstructorDecl` requires a visibility and admits both) would therefore ship a readable *defaultable* verdict beside a `with` predicate the consumer cannot compute, forcing exactly what §2.8.2's own parenthetical says an interface must not force: *reject imported types at those rules or guess*. Restricting the trigger to public constructors is refused by §14.2's landed principle — keying satisfaction to accessibility *would let the same substitution satisfy the constraint in one module and fail it in another* — and a bespoke carriage channel for a structural classification is the parallel mechanism §3.6's closing rule forbids. The property exists for the `with` rejection; every other job it does could have been done without a name.

**Why the two predicates cannot be one.** Coarsening the `with` rejection to *every non-defaultable record* would have removed the property at a stroke, and it is refused by one landed example: `record Person(Name: string, Age: int)` is not defaultable — `string` is a non-nullable reference type — and is §9.5's own canonical `with` subject. Non-defaultability is a statement about a *zero value*; the `with` rejection is a statement about a *constructor gate*. They coincide on the new population and diverge on the oldest one in the document.

**Why the clause is the trigger.** The language has no other structural signal that an author means construction to be a checkpoint, and §3.6's closing rule forbids inventing one beside the mechanism. A purpose-built record modifier was the alternative, and it was declined: it is a grammar production and a contextual keyword for a fact §3.6 already computes; it sits at least in tension with rule 1's *computed, never declared*, where a contract clause does not, having independent runtime effect and being signature-side rather than a body (§22.16 places clauses *between its signature and its body*); and it would admit a record that states a validating constructor **without** the property — the silently-ineffective shape the positional rule exists to reject. The precedent that settles rule 1 is in the same table cell: *defaultable* already keys on *an enum declaring a zero-valued constant*, a member-level, author-flippable declaration fact. The cost is real and is accepted rather than hidden: a record that adopts a constructor clause for ordinary defensive reasons loses `default`, `with`, `where T: new()`, the `default(T)`-supplying serialization forms, and the one-argument `OwningBuffer<R>(length)`, with no in-constructor opt-out. The remedy is one line and it is landed — state the condition on a static factory method, which §22.16's scope already admits — and §9.9 names it in the normative text so the rule does not read as a tax on §22.16.

**Why `with` is rejected rather than instrumented.** With no standing predicate there is nothing to evaluate at the `with` point, and any check that could run there would be an `invariant`-shaped one evaluated in checked-contracts mode — absent from the default build, which is where the guarantee is wanted. Rejection also enforces advice §9.5 already gives (*the record should provide a named method for constrained updates*) instead of adding a rule beside it. It is unconditional rather than external-only: an inside-the-record `with` produces a value with no constructor behind it exactly as an outside one does, and the accessibility workaround §9.5 offers is not needed for a type that has the property.

**Why the positional door closes at the declaration.** `RecordDecl` admits no contract clause, and neither does `ClassDecl` on a class primary constructor, so barring the combination adds no asymmetry between the kinds. What it prevents is worse than an asymmetry: without it, an author writes a clause, the property flips for every client, and `new Port(-1)` still compiles through the auto-generated primary. The cost — §27.8's one-liner cannot be validated without moving to the five-line body form — is accepted because the rejection is diagnosed with two named remedies rather than silent, and the body form is §9.2's own shape. A `ContractClause` slot on `RecordDecl` would remove the cost and was declined: it is the design's only grammar change, it would give §22.16 a clause position whose meaning is a desugaring onto a constructor the author never wrote, and it would create the asymmetry with `ClassDecl` that the rule above avoids.

**What the property deliberately does not claim.** It is provenance, not validity: every value is produced by a declared constructor or is a copy of one, and nothing more. It does not say a predicate holds of a value at any instant, does not restrict writes by the declaring record's own code, and does not reach the FFI boundary, where §32.10's contract already assigns the obligation to the programmer and where §32.13's single-field typed handle is precisely the shape this feature serves. Rejecting constructor-only records at extern positions was declined: it is an enumerate-the-boundary-positions rule, and every other derived type property stops at the same line. Stating the claim narrowly is not modesty — it is what makes the design defensible, because the claim quantifies over a **closed, spec-enumerated set of producers** (§9.8 names the constructor-bypassing constructions itself, and every `default(T)` site is reachable from §17.11's catch-all) rather than over every way storage can come to hold a value.

**What is not here, and why.** No `invariant` member in record bodies. The instrumented design — an `invariant` clause plus a set of instrumentation points — was drafted, scoped into a revision, and withdrawn before landing, because its soundness rested on an instrumentation-completeness claim that three independent adversarial rounds each falsified: a private settable auto-property written from a static factory of the declaring record; a copy of `this` escaping from a constructor's first statement, §22.7's gate being vacuous for an all-primitive record; a write to another value's `readonly` storage from any constructor of the declaring record, that permission being positional rather than per-instance at the time (closed since — v0.270 made §22.8's assignable context per-instance, so this forge no longer lands; the withdrawal stands on the other four); an interface-typed field escaping borrow-bearing classification; and a clause over reachable static state going false with no store, no copy, and no instrumentation point anywhere. Closing that class needs a clause-admissibility rule strong enough to make the frame problem structurally impossible — a second effect lattice beside purity — together with a constructor-interval closure naming all three escape routes; neither exists, and the mechanism the class rule leans on (public-method entry/exit re-checking) is one the record form deliberately has none of. §22.16's out-of-scope deferral therefore stands unstruck, and the record of the decline, with its five forges and its reopening bar, is proposal 0107 §3. Reopening it takes that bar, not a fresh enthusiasm.

**Kernel cost: none.** A §3.6 classification consumed by §17.11, §9.5, §14.2, §7.9, and §9.8; records carry no owned state (§9.7), and clause evaluation is pure and borrow-only (§22.16). No K row, no axiom spent.

<a id="sec-value-receivers-fixed-at-the-call-rationale"></a>
### 9.15 Value receivers are fixed at the call; move-only receivers are borrowed in place (v0.321)

**(Proposal [0135](proposals/0135-value-receiver-by-copy-when-source-writable.md), [#5496](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5496); the direction accepted by the owner 2026-10-08, the move-only half decided under the owner's delegation.)** [§9.13](#sec-value-receivers-are-borrows-rationale) settled what `this` is in a value-kind member body — a borrow of the receiver value, admitting no store into the receiver's own storage. What the borrow is a borrow *of*, when the storage the receiver expression named is overwritten while the member runs, needed its own ruling. A closure the member invokes can reassign a mutably captured local (`p := other`), null a narrowed `n: Pt?`, or store to a field `h.P` through an alias of `h`. Read by address, the member then sees the replacement, or the payload bytes of a `null`; read by copy, it sees nothing new.

**Three options were weighed for copyable kinds.** (i) Pass the receiver by copy whenever its source may be written during the call — **adopted** (owner, 2026-10-08). (ii) Freeze the source for the call — declined: fields and slots are not frozen (spec K5.6: slot aliases are not body-local), so a freeze cannot reach `h.P.Twice(…)` with `alias.P := …` in the closure, and a value binding carries no obligation for a freeze to key on (K1.6). (iii) By address, with the overwrite visible — declined: every member body's correctness would depend on every caller's closures, and a borrow-bearing receiver would read a replacement whose borrows §9.7 never checked against this activation. The spec states the adopted option as an observable semantic, not a representation: the receiver value is fixed when the receiver expression is evaluated, a K8 full-expression temporary bounded by the call. Because §9.4 already rejects every store through `this` into the receiver, the fixed value is observably a copy only where the source is written during the call, so copying and passing in place are indistinguishable wherever the source is unwritten, and spec §9.4 states the one obligation: in place only to an activation that ends with the call, and only at storage no code reachable during the call can write or release — an unaliased local or parameter of the calling body, storage inside its value, or storage inside the enclosing activation's own fixed receiver. This is the posture spec §11.8 takes for dispatch, applied to receivers: a statement about semantics, with the implementation strategy free. A `readonly` field outside construction has no writer, but its holder can be destroyed during the call, after which in-place bytes would hold released runtime-managed references with no flag to test; a copy holds its own references. So the copyable in-place list stops at locals. A member whose activation outlives the call (`async`, iterator) holds the fixed value in its frame, as a frame holds a plain value argument.

**The move-only half.** The direction says *copy*, and an owning choice or owning inline array cannot be copied, so the direction is silent there. Two coherent designs were weighed. *R* rejects a bodied member call on a move-only receiver whose source is not provably unwritten. *F* — **adopted** — passes the storage in place at every source, `this` a borrow of that storage bounded by the call, and closes the one hazard an in-place receiver creates where it arises. The hazard is type confusion: a borrowing-match binding of a move-only payload addresses bytes inside the scrutinee's storage, and a whole-value overwrite with another case leaves the binding addressing that case's bytes, which no disposed flag reaches. The same binding is reachable with no member call — a non-destructive `case h.C of …` over a field is the default everywhere (spec §35.5) — so the closure belongs at the binding, and spec §19.3 states it once for `case this of` in a member body and `case h.C of` in a caller alike: such a binding may not be live at a write point unless the scrutinee's source is provably unwritten, and a use of it as a receiver or operand is live across the call. Five grounds decided it.

- *[§5.22](#sec-type-driven-serialization)'s preference, unrepresentable over detected.* Under F the confusing state cannot be constructed; R rejects one spelling of it and leaves the `case` twin admitted.
- *K4.3's "recorded trust, or nothing".* F adds no K4.3 clause. Each displaced payload object is flagged by the store that displaces it. A value emptied with no replacement (a null store, a holder kill) is left in a disposed state that checked mode tests on reads through `this` or a borrowing binding. A class-payload binding over an in-place receiver, live across a write point that moves the field to another domain, is a new **site** of §28.3 *Scope*'s existing slot-kill-by-move clause, which the trust-admission report enumerates.
- *K5.6's posture.* Borrows of a field's current value already live under the runtime-checked posture; F applies it to a move-only field with one static closure. R would make the owning choice the one field kind whose current value a member call cannot borrow.
- *The §7.4 analogy breaks at the remedy.* Narrowing's remedy copies. A move-only value's would move the field out and restore it around a call — a K9.2 window with a throwable, user-code-running expression inside it, exactly the shape build-before-take keeps programs out of — and a `readonly` field has no move-out at all.
- *§35.2 and §35.5 stay as they are.* `this` is a borrow, and a borrowing match binds a move-only payload by borrow; F adds one liveness condition to the second.

**Declined alongside.** R itself: once §19.3's rule closes the confusion inside the member body, R buys only a snapshot reading for a kind that has none, at the cost of the `_conn.IsOk()` idiom on a class's owning-`Result` field and a remedy that is a K9.2 window, absent altogether for a `readonly` field. A partial copy — copy the discriminant, borrow the payload — is a new representation with per-payload-kind rules for a hazard one sentence closes. A runtime tag re-check at each use through the binding is *detected* where F is *unrepresentable*, needs a new fault, and misses a replacement of the same case. Admitting a captured move-only local of an enclosing body as provably unwritten is sound (no closure can reassign it) but widens the list beyond an unaliased local for a shape no program needs; it is not adopted, and remains available. An `async` or iterator member cannot be called on a move-only receiver: its frame could hold the value only by consuming it, which a method invocation never does, and in place the storage would outlive nothing the frame can see (K6.4).

**Cost and generics.** The rule's cost lands on a borrowing `case` over `this`, a mutable field or a `ref` parameter that binds a *nested* move-only payload (an owning inline array, or an owning choice inside an owning choice) and uses it past a non-`pure` call. The ordinary owning `Result<owned Item, E>` has class payloads and is untouched. Reads before the call, `pure` calls, and field and auto-property reads through the binding stay free, and the owning match over the take (`case move h.C of …`) is the remedy where an arm must consume. For a payload typed by a type parameter the binding's kind is read at the substitution, and the rule is a requirement of the constructed type under spec §3.6 rule 2, as §7.10's copy demand is; spec §35.11 lists it, with the `async`/iterator rejection, among the classification-dependent rules enforced at instantiation.

**Kernel cost: none.** For copyable kinds, a derivation from K8.4 (the call-bounded temporary), K1.6 (a value carries no obligation, so no consume position is added) and K5.6 (non-local storage is classed writable and copied, not frozen). The binding rule is K5.6's posture plus a K5.5 liveness condition. Body-locality: local, with the type-parameter payload a §3.6 rule 2 carve-out.

**The owning inline array under a holder kill (v0.327, [#5554](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5554)).** Proposal 0135's second critique (N7) recorded a hazard the v0.321 text named only for owning choices in its examples: a whole-scrutinee borrowing binding over an owning-inline-array **field** (`case h.Slots of var a => …`) whose arm destroys the holder through an alias and then reads a slot. A class-typed slot's object is flag-backstopped (K4.1); a boxed value in an interface-typed slot is runtime-managed and carries no flag, so the read reaches a released box. The ruling takes the tier §9.4 already uses: the disposed state is the **value's** — the holder's per-slot pass (spec §28.4 step 2) leaves the array in it, and checked mode tests it on every read through a borrowing-match binding, slot reads, iteration and the compiler-provided operations included. That is the same backstop the move-only `this` has, extended to the second move-only kind, whose storage can be borrowed in place only through such a binding (an owning inline array has no bodied members). Two alternatives were declined.
