Extending §19.3's write-point rule to the whole-scrutinee binding over a field would turn a case K4.1's tier backstops into a static rejection; that rule exists for the type confusion a store under a payload binding causes, which an inline array's uniformly typed slots cannot. Rejecting a boxed value in an interface-typed owned slot would refuse the slot kind wholesale, since which value a slot holds is a per-slot runtime fact. Kernel: K4.1's flag tier at the sites v0.321 recorded, extended to the inline array's reads — no new axiom. The encoding is the implementation's (a sentinel no live slot holds, written by the pass; a side word), readable under checked mode's deferred reclamation. The adjacent case — a binding over the field surviving a whole-value `move h.Slots` whose taker is later disposed — is decided below (v0.328).

**A whole-value `move` of a move-only field leaves its storage in the disposed state (v0.328, proposal [0143](proposals/0143-move-only-field-move-disposed-state.md), filed from the v0.327 critique of [#5554](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5554)).** Between `var t := move h.Slots;` and the field's restoration store, a borrowing-match binding `a` taken over `h.Slots` before the move addresses storage whose value now lives in `t`; when `t` is disposed, a class-typed slot's object is flag-backstopped and a boxed one is not, and the read `a[0]` was neither rejected, nor backstopped, nor recorded trust: fields are not frozen (K5.6), §19.3's write-point rule covers payload bindings and not a whole-scrutinee binding over a field (the #5554 decline above), and K4.3's slot-kill-by-move clause is the *domain* clause, about another domain. Four answers were weighed. *(a)* The move writes §9.4's disposed state into the vacated field storage in checked mode, and the restoration store clears it — **adopted**: one store per whole-value move of such a field, the #5554 encoding reused, K4.1's flag tier at one more site and no new axiom. A read through the binding faults at once, even while the taker lives — stricter than a class field's borrow, whose object stays readable in its taker — which is the price of a value whose storage, not whose contents, carries the flag. *(b)* Extending §19.3's write-point rule to a whole-scrutinee binding over such a field — declined: the rule exists for the type confusion a payload store causes, which uniformly typed slots cannot; it cannot see a move made through an alias of the holder; and a field is never provably unwritten, so it would reject the binding across every non-pure call, the over-rejection the #5554 decline refused. *(c)* A recorded K4.3 trust clause naming the same-domain case — declined: K4's third clause admits trust only where no backstop is possible, and here one is, at the cost of a store. *(d)* Freezing the field against the direct spelling `move h.Slots` while the binding lives — declined: it would be K5.6's first exception, and it cannot see `move alias.Slots`. The owning choice takes the same rule at a field, its state being the one §35.9's null store already defines.

**The disposed state's fault is not a restoration edge (v0.332, [#5666](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5666)).** Proposal 0143's state makes a read through a binding over a vacated field fault in checked mode, and some such reads — an inline array's `Length`, a `case` over an owning-choice binding — are not throwable under spec §28.8.1's inventory, so no `finally` covers their edge and the field can leave the method in the state. Three answers were weighed. Counting every read that may address a vacated field as throwable — declined: the analysis would have to know which bindings address which fields, the alias spelling of the move (`move alias.Slots`) defeats that, and it would demand `finally` blocks in unchecked builds, where the read cannot fault. Narrowing the fault to the reads the inventory already lists — declined: the others would read the taker's storage unbackstopped, reopening the hazard 0143 closed. **Adopted:** the fault classifies no read, as K4.1's flag test on a disposed object's member access already classifies none, and the state it leaves holds no live value — no destructor or overwrite disposes through it, and every later read faults again. That is the posture an owned field disposed by an explicit `Dispose()` already has without a restoration obligation (spec §28.6), and it is not K9.2's hole, which is a slot whose bytes still name a value another owner disposes: the state marks the storage as holding nothing.

**The vacated field is gone (v0.336, [proposal 0174](proposals/0174-replacing-move.md); §5.10's v0.336 paragraph).** A whole-value `move` of a move-only instance field is now the replacing `move`, `var t := move h.Slots with fresh;`, which stores the replacement in the step that takes: the field's storage never holds nothing, so the state the two paragraphs above write into it at a `move` — and the fault, the inventory question and the two rule cases that exercised them — have no subject. A borrowing-match binding over the field reads the replacement, whole, which is the posture the whole-value overwrite already had here; the holder-destruction state of the v0.327 paragraph is unchanged.

<a id="sec-member-comparisons-with-csharp"></a>
### 9.16 Defaults, primary constructors and static initialization: the comparisons with C#

**Default-value expressions (spec §22.5).** C# requires default-value expressions to be compile-time constants (`int x = 42` is fine; `DateTime t = DateTime.Now` is a compile error). Bezel's call-site evaluation is strictly more expressive: anywhere C#'s rule allows a default, Bezel does too, and further cases (factory calls, `Now`-style defaults) are available without resorting to `null`-then-coalesce patterns. The C# choice was driven by ABI stability: the default value is encoded into the method's metadata at the *call site* (the compiler inlines the constant into each caller), so changing the default in the callee does not update existing callers without recompilation. Bezel makes no such metadata-encoding promise: defaults are part of the called method's signature, evaluated by the callee or by code generated at the call site against that signature, at the implementation's choice. Recompilation against a new version of a library may pick up a changed default; this is expected and matches the rest of Bezel's source-compatibility model.

**Primary-constructor capture is keyed on use (spec §22.10).** The capture-iff-used rule matches C# 12's primary-constructor design. Both languages treat primary constructors as ergonomic shorthand for short-lived data-carrier-style classes, where ABI stability is not a concern: code that writes a primary constructor opts into "the compiler chooses my field layout based on body content", and code that needs field-layout guarantees uses explicit declarations.

**Same-type static forward references are rejected (spec §22.13).** C# silently observes the field's default value during such a forward reference. That behaviour is error-prone: for a non-nullable reference-typed `static readonly` field no default value exists at all, and even where one does (value types, nullable references), the reading code rarely intends to observe it — the common cause is a declaration-order mistake. Rejecting at compile time forces a reorder or, for a genuinely cyclic case, a move into the static constructor, where ordering is explicit.

**A resource-bearing `static readonly` field without `owned` warns rather than errs (spec §22.13).** The singleton-with-resources pattern is widespread and idiomatic — a process-lifetime HTTP client, a database connection pool, a logger — and forcing every such case to declare `owned` or restructure costs more than it buys. Bezel's stance matches C# here: the lifetime contract is process-bound, and process termination handles the implicit release.

**A `static readonly` field is assigned only in static initialization (v0.327, proposal 0142, [#5577](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5577)).** Spec §22.8 names two assignment sites for a `static readonly` field: its own initializer and the declaring type's static constructor. An instance constructor of the declaring type is not one, although nothing in the per-instance `this`-rooted rule for instance fields excludes it by itself. Two rules fix the answer. §7.4 narrows `TypeName._s` on the premise that outside the field's construction window no path writes it; if `new C()` could write `C._s`, `if C._s <> null then new C(); C._s.M(); end` would narrow across a writer, and the only decidable repair would withdraw the static narrowing altogether, since any call can construct a `C` — a sound answer, but one that takes `TypeName._s` narrowing from every static field to keep a store whose purposes a `static constructor()` or a mutable static already serves. And §22.8's single-assignment unit for a static is one type initialization; a store from an instance constructor runs once per instance, outside that unit, so once-ness across instances would have no body-local checkable form (proposal 0105 §1). §23.1 already confines a `static readonly` auto-property to "the declaring type's own static initialization", so the field rule now says what the property rule says. Declined: a runtime first-construction-assigns once-ness (racy under §30.13's parallel domains, and it would move the field's value at an unbounded moment, against the narrowing premise). C# `static readonly`, Java `static final` and Swift `static let` all confine assignment to static initialization. A program that seeded a static from an instance constructor moves the store into `static constructor()`, or declares the static mutable under §22.12 rule 2 if it is per-instance bookkeeping.

**A lambda body is outside the `readonly` assignment window (v0.328).** Spec §22.8 admits a `readonly` field's assignment "in a constructor body"; read lexically, that admitted an assignment inside a lambda written there — a function value that can run after construction, or, invoked twice during it, assign twice — which contradicts §7.4's narrowing premise (no writer on any path outside the window) and the single-assignment rule's one-construction unit. The window is now stated lexical and exclusive of nested function bodies, for instance and static fields and for every window alike (constructor body, field initializer, static constructor, static initializer). The lexical-inclusion reading was declined, and so was admitting the assignment where the lambda is provably invoked only within the window: a function value's invocation sites are not a body-local fact, and the simplest rule is one site kind fewer. C# draws the same line (an assignment to a `readonly` field inside a lambda in a constructor is CS0191).

<a id="sec-generics"></a>
## 10. Generics

Two extreme implementations exist. Erasure (Java) is fast to compile but produces poor codegen and disallows reflection on type parameters. Monomorphization (C++ templates, Rust) produces excellent codegen but slow compile times and binary bloat.

Bezel's intended implementation specializes for value-type substitutions (where boxing would hurt) and shares for reference-type substitutions (where uniform layout is achievable). This gives most of the codegen benefit of monomorphization where it matters, and most of the binary-size benefit of erasure where it doesn't. That sentence records strategy *intent*, not a decline: a third point of the space — unboxed sharing at value substitutions through a per-instantiation witness descriptor — was examined and priced (proposals 0103/0104, 2026-08-19) and stands available under the spec's own latitude ([§11.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-static-interface-members)). The reason the intent stands is codegen quality at value substitutions — indirect dispatch, not boxing — the one cost sharing cannot shed. (v0.262)

`typeof(T)` and `value.GetType()` work because the runtime carries enough type information to resolve them, regardless of whether a particular generic was specialized or shared.

(v0.184) Type-argument inference (§14.5) follows the same temperament as the dispatch strategy above — the predictable subset, stated fully. Bindings must agree exactly: the compiler computes no common supertype (`Pick(1, 2L)` is an error, not `long`), the §27.3/§20 no-LUB posture applied to inference, so a call's meaning never shifts because a distant hierarchy edit changed a join. The call's target type participates only as a *completion* for parameters the arguments cannot see (`var a: Array<int> := Util.Empty();`) — unification against one declared type, no suprema, error when absent — a deliberate divergence from C# (which refuses return-target inference) in favor of the factory idiom the reference implementation has always supported. Explicit lists are all-or-nothing, constraints are checked after inference rather than feeding it (liftable), and an otherwise-tied non-generic overload beats a generic one — ranked *after* the defaulted-arity tie-break, so adding an optional-parameter overload still never re-routes an existing call (v0.181's guarantee, kept where C#'s internal order would break it). Each declined power — bound-set fixpoints, best-common-type, constraint-driven inference, partial lists — is liftable later without rejecting a program this rule set accepts. The v0.292 edge rulings (#1695) keep the temperament: a non-unique interface instantiation makes the candidate inapplicable rather than choosing; a collection-literal lambda body binds nothing and needs its return position closed (the literal has no standalone type — [§5.16](#sec-collection-literals-for-complex-collections)'s uniform target-derived rule; no second pass defers it); a nested lambda is an expression of its enclosing body and never re-enters phase 2; and a lambda literal at a non-call target position is admitted under the argument-position conversion rule (spec §27.3) — each the smallest reading that decides the case without a join or a guess. The v0.296 rulings (#4088, #4089, #4090) are cut the same way: a method-reference argument ranks at the callee as a lambda literal does — exact where the selected overload's signature equals the substituted slot, converting where formation bridges, and a candidate at which formation finds no unique overload drops out — so neither "always converting" nor "exact whenever formation succeeds" was taken; a parameter-count mismatch at a function-typed slot is an inference failure that drops the candidate, not an error at it; and an `async` lambda at a return slot with a free type parameter takes its wrapper from its own body, never from the slot's head — reading `Task<R>`'s head into the body would be a target-directed inference this rule set does not have.

(v0.196, #1803) The ownership marker meets this dispatch strategy at one seam: what does `owned` mean on a parameter or return slot whose declared type is a bare `T`, when one instantiation substitutes a class and the next substitutes `int`? Through v0.194 the revisions had backed into an impasse from both sides — the marker was checked per instantiation and rejected at plain value substitutions, while the factory warning of [§5.7](#sec-factory-method-ownership)'s v0.194 rule *recommended* the `owned T` spelling to every mixed-domain producer — so a generic that produces fresh values over a domain admitting both kinds (`Create<T>(): T where T: new()`; the arithmetic behind [§11.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-static-interface-members)'s `Sum<T>`) had no legal spelling of production at all. The v0.196 resolution is per-instantiation **erasure**: at class and interface substitutions the marker means transfer, exactly as on a concrete type; at value and runtime-managed substitutions the substitution erases it — the substituted signature *is* the unmarked one, down to function-type identity (`(owned T) -> ()` at `T = int` is `(int) -> ()`); a move-only value substitution instead keeps the marker admitted-but-redundant, as on the concrete type (spec [§28.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-where-the-owned-modifier-can-appear)). This is the same temperament as the specialize-or-share strategy above: the ownership layer is already vacuous at value substitutions everywhere else in the language, and erasure states that vacuity in the signature algebra rather than as a rejection — which is also why erasure beat an inert-but-present marker of identical semantics: erased signatures compose mechanically with everything keyed on substituted forms, so conformance matching needs no waiver clause and type identity cannot manufacture two `int`-function types differing by a meaningless marker.

Two alternatives were declined. *Restricting the domain* — confining the numeric static-interface surface to value and runtime-managed conformers — falsifies §11.8's own canonical example (`class Meters: INumber<Meters>`), guarantees an unwind the day the first genuinely heap-backed conformer arrives (`BigInteger` is the recorded future occupant), and repairs only the stdlib's seam while leaving every user mixed-domain generic holding the same unactionable warning. *A new keyword* — a fresh-production marker distinct from `owned` — would duplicate `owned`'s class-substitution semantics exactly, differ only in value-substitution admissibility, and fork every marker-keyed rule in the spec for the privilege; erasure extracts the same admissibility from the marker the language already has. (This is not a retreat from [§5.11](#sec-owning-collections)'s abandonment of `<owned T>`: that mechanism made ownership a property of *type arguments* and drowned in substitution rules; erasure touches only signature slots, where the marker already lived — and the one slot that commits a type's *shape*, the generic `owned` field, deliberately stays rejected.) The recorded cost is honest: class-substituted arithmetic mints a fresh value on every operation — in an accumulator loop, one mint and one disposal per iteration — inherent to value-semantics arithmetic without in-place operators, deterministic, and a performance note rather than a soundness one.

(v0.314, #5227, #5257, #5264) **A method's type parameter does not shadow an enclosing one.** C# admits `class G<T> { bool F<T>(T v) … }` with a warning (CS0693), the method's `T` hiding the class's for the whole method; Rust rejects it (E0403). Bezel rejects it at the method's type-parameter list ([§14.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-generic-declarations)), on the ground [§21.10](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-type-declarations) gives for a local type that would shadow a visible type name — a silent shadow is a trap, not a convenience — sharpened by one fact: a type parameter has no qualified spelling. A module type shadowed by a type parameter stays reachable by qualification (`System.Object`); a class `T` shadowed by a method `T` would be unreachable in that method while the fields and members it types stay in reach, so one body would hold two types spelled `T`, and [§14.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-constraints)'s `where U: T` bound on the enclosing parameter could not be written there at all. Nothing is lost. Renaming either parameter is local, and an override is not bound to its base's parameter names, since [§11.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-interface-declaration) and [§12.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-members-are-non-virtual-by-default) match method type parameters by position, which is what lets a generic subclass override a base method whose parameter happens to share its class parameter's name. A `forbid → allow` widening also stays compatible where the reverse would break.

(v0.314) **A method's own type-parameter count distinguishes overloads; its type-parameter names do not.** [§11.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-member-body)'s clash rule uses the signature identity [§11.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-interface-declaration) and [§12.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-members-are-non-virtual-by-default) match by: the same count, type parameters corresponding by position. The count is a distinguishing fact for the same reason [§2.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-imports) keys type names on generic arity: an explicit list's length selects among the counts, and without a list a candidate whose type parameter nothing binds fails inference and drops out ([§14.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-type-argument-inference)), so each member of a count-distinguished pair is reachable. The standard library already relies on it: `Task.FromException<T>` and `FromCancelled<T>` sit beside their non-generic forms with identical parameter lists ([§30.10](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-standard-library-task-helpers), stdlib [§6.7](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-task-static-helpers)). Declined: leaving the count out of the distinguishing facts. That would make those stdlib pairs ill-formed and force a rename for no soundness gain, since each member stays reachable. Also declined: making the permuted pair `M<T, U>(a: T, b: U)` / `M<T, U>(a: U, b: T)` clash. An explicit list naming two different types tells the two apart, and §11.3 rejects only what the type system cannot tell apart; a pair whose inferred calls are ambiguous is admitted here as `M(a: Animal)` beside `M(o: Object)` is. Constraints stay out of the distinguishing facts: §14.5 checks them after inference rather than using them to select, so two members differing only in `where` clauses accept the same calls.

(v0.316) **Constraints check the selected candidate; they do not select it.** [§14.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-type-argument-inference) checks a generic method's own `where` constraints on the candidate [§22.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-overloading) selects, so an unsatisfied constraint rejects the call rather than dropping the candidate. This is the inference temperament above applied to resolution: whether a `where` constraint holds never decides which overload a call reaches, so a conformance edit that changes only whether a constraint holds can turn a rejected call into an accepted one, or the reverse, but never re-routes a call. Declined: constraint-filtered applicability, C#'s rule since 7.3, under which `M(5)` beside `M(o: Object)` and `M<T>(x: T) where T: class` would box to the non-generic. Under it, a conformance edit that makes a candidate's constraints hold returns that candidate to the set, where it can beat the overload an existing call reached. The cost is the class/value split C# programmers reach for: `M<T>(x: T) where T: class` beside `M<T>(x: T?) where T: value` is declarable, but `M(5)` selects the first (exact at `T = int`) and is rejected, and `M(n)` for `n: int?` is ambiguous (both exact), so the split is spelled with distinct names. An extension block's `where` clause is a different fact and does filter ([§26.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-generic-extensions)): it decides which blocks apply to the receiver, not which overload of a member a call reaches. It stays liftable without rejecting any program this rule accepts: filtering only removes candidates, and a selected candidate whose constraints hold beats every candidate that filtering would leave beside it, so the only calls whose outcome changes are calls this rule rejects. The same fact decides [§22.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-overloading)'s declaration guard for a generic `owned`/plain pair whose members declare different constraints: each member's constraints must qualify on their own. A substitution one member's constraints exclude still reaches that member, so pooling the two sets would admit `M<T>(x: T) where T: SomeClass` beside an unconstrained `M<T>(owned x: T)`, and `M(5)`, a call the unconstrained member alone accepts, would collapse into an ambiguity. Declined: pooling the sets, for that reason; and requiring the two sets to be equal, which would reject `where T: SomeClass` beside `where T: IDisposable`, a pair at whose every collapsing substitution both members' constraints fail.

(v0.318, [#5493](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5493)) **An interior-use requirement belongs to the declaration whose parameter it uses.** When a member of a generic type uses the type's parameter in a way only some substitutions admit — `Equals` on a `T` (spec [§9.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-equality-through-a-type-parameter)), a copy of a `T` that may be move-only (§7.10) — the requirement is the constructed type's: `Bag<R>` is rejected wherever it is named, whether or not `Contains` is ever called (spec §3.6 rule 2). That is spec §14.1's definition read as written: an instantiation is of the declaration that declares the parameter, a generic type is instantiated by being named, and a rule that fires at the instantiation site fires at every such site. The alternative, checking a member's requirement only where the member is reached, would let `Bag<R>` stand while refusing `Bag<R>.Contains`. That is a member present at some instantiations only, conditioned on the enclosing type's arguments, which spec §14.2 rule 1 reserves to registered standard-library rows; it is the undeclared twin of the member-level `where` that Appendix C.1 rejects, and it would need its own account of members reached with no visible use (overrides and interface implementations, reached by dispatch; a destructor, reached by disposal). The cost is that a user collection with one equality-using member is unusable at a non-hashable element type, and that a body edit adding such a use rejects every client that names the type rather than only those that call the member (a breaking change under spec §2.8.6's requirement-summary rows, rationale [§7.7](#sec-equality-without-a-definition), at a wider reach). The remedy is the form spec §14.2 already names for a member present at some instantiations only: a constrained extension block, `extension Bag<T> where T: hashable`, whose member applies only where its clause holds and sees the type's public and same-module `internal` surface (spec §26.3), or a generic method over a parameter of its own. Neither form carries an override, an interface implementation, an operator or an indexer (spec §26.3), so a user `ICollection<T>` implementation whose `Contains` compares is usable only at the substitutions `where T: hashable` admits, where the standard library's `Array<T>` is usable at every element type. The rule reads user declarations only. Applied to the standard library's span records, which are realized in Bezel (stdlib §4.14, §4.15) and whose `IndexOf`/`Contains`/`SequenceEqual` compare by `Equals`, it would reject `ArraySpan<R>` and `ReadOnlyArraySpan<R>` at every non-hashable record `R`, contradicting the unconstrained signatures that name them (`Array<T>.AsSpan()`, every built-in's `FromCollectionLiteral(elements: ReadOnlyArraySpan<T>)`) and the spec's collection-literal lowering, which forms a `ReadOnlyArraySpan<U>` at any element type. A standard-library type is therefore rejected at an instantiation only by its declared constraints and the instantiation-site rules the spec states for it, such as spec §14.4's class-only restriction on the owning collections. The standard library's unconstrained equality-using members (`Array<T>.Contains`, the span `IndexOf` family) are [#5491](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5491)'s question, which the #5491 paragraph below decides; read with this ruling, that question's constrained-block option is the form a user library takes, and its use-site option is a §14.2 register row per member family.

(v0.319, [#5504](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5504)) **A requirement reaches the outer declaration through a substitution.** A user declaration that names `Bag<T>`, or calls `Eq<U>`, at its own type parameter forms that instantiation again, after substitution, at each of its own instantiations, and an instantiation-site requirement the parameter's constraints do not establish is decided there: `class Outer<T>` holding a `Bag<T>` is rejected at `Outer<R>` wherever `Bag<R>` would be (spec [§14.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-generic-declarations), §3.6 rule 2). Two alternatives were declined. Deciding the requirement at the inner site admits it there, because a type parameter has no structure for a property-dependent rule to read (spec §9.2's per-kind rule, so read, would reach `Object`'s member at a type-parameter substitution), and then one level of wrapping launders every such rule. That is not hypothetical: `OwningCache<T> where T: class` composed over `OwningArray<T>`, spec §14.4's own example, would admit `OwningCache<string>`, an owning collection of a runtime-managed element, which is a measured use-after-free ([#2713](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/2713)). Rejecting at the inner site instead, so that the wrapper must state the requirement as a constraint of its own, has no spelling for most of these rules: no admitted constraint states a copyable substitution (spec §7.10), an admissible `as` target (§17.4) or, short of `IDisposable`, the runtime-managed exclusion of §14.4 and §28.5. It would also reject wrappers at their admissible instantiations, against §3.6 rule 2's per-instantiation model. The carried requirement is a per-declaration summary composed by substitution from the declarations it names, not a re-check of any body, and spec [§14.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-recursive-instantiation)'s bounded instantiation closure keeps the formed instantiations finite. An instantiation reached by dispatch rather than formed by substitution, a generic method's override reached through its base's signature, is not decided here. The cost is the one stated above, at a wider reach: an edit that adds a requirement to a declaration's body or fields rejects clients of every declaration that wraps it, through any number of levels.

(v0.320, [#5510](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5510)) **A declared constraint at a type-parameter argument is decided where it is written.** `class Box<W>` holding a `C<W>`, under `class C<T> where T: new()`, is checked at that field by `W`'s own constraints: admitted when they guarantee `new()`, rejected otherwise, with `where W: new()` as the remedy (spec [§14.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-constraints)). The text already reads interface bounds this way (spec §14.2 conformance rule 6: a type parameter conforms when its constraints guarantee it) and the derived-property constraints (an unconstrained parameter never acquires the property); the ruling states the same rule for `new()`, `class`, `value`, `notnull` and the type bounds, whose substitution sets no type parameter belongs to, so that `Box<W> where W: new()` is not rejected merely because `W` is not a class. Declined: deferring the constraint to the wrapper's own instantiations, so that an unconstrained `Box<W>` stands and `Box<IFoo>` is rejected. A declared constraint is a signature fact. Deferred, it would make the wrapper's signature admit instantiations its fields refuse, which the paragraph above accepts for instantiation-site requirements only because no constraint states most of them. A declared constraint is a constraint already, so the wrapper can restate it (save the concrete-argument case below), and the body that names `C<W>` is checked under `W`'s constraints as every other use of `W` in it is. The paragraph above also declined requiring a wrapper to state a requirement as a constraint because that would refuse wrappers at their admissible instantiations, against spec §3.6 rule 2's per-instantiation model. That ground does not reach a declared constraint: rule 2's model evaluates properties and the requirements it carries, and it leaves a declared constraint at such an argument to spec §14.2, whose rule 6 and derived-property constraints already refuse an unconstrained wrapper at its admissible instantiations (`Box<W>` holding a `Counter<W>`, at `Box<int>`). The ruling extends that reading to the remaining forms and leaves the requirement decline, whose subject is a requirement no constraint states, as it stands. The costs: an inner bound of one parameter by another at a concrete argument no type bound can reach (`C<W, int>` under `where T: U`) has nothing to restate, so the naming is rejected for every `W`; and the standard library's `Counter<K>` and `Dictionary<K, V>`, recorded as deciding identically, decide at different sites for a type-parameter key: `Counter<V>` needs `where V: hashable` on the wrapper, while `Dictionary<V, int>`'s structural key check, a requirement, is carried to the wrapper's instantiations (stdlib [§4.20](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-counterk)).

(v0.320, [#5512](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5512)) **A change to the requirements a generic places on its arguments is a classified delta.** A use of a type parameter in a member body is a requirement checked wherever the constructed type is named, and it is carried through every declaration that wraps it (spec §3.6 rule 2), so an edit to a carried body or field can reject a client's instantiation with no declared contract changed. Spec [§2.8.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-interface-delta-classification) therefore classifies a public generic's **requirement summary** directly: a requirement gained is breaking and one lost is widening, the verdicts of a generic constraint added and removed, and only a carried change that leaves both the contract records and the summaries unchanged stays preserving. Declined: reading the summary as one of the carried derived-property facts of spec §2.8.2 clause 1, so that the existing verdict rows classify it. Those rows classify a property of a type, where a verdict gained admits the type in more contexts and is preserving; a requirement is a demand on a parameter's substitutions, where a requirement gained narrows admission, so the verdict-gained row would classify a breaking change as preserving, and spec §3.6's closed property table would gain an entry that is not a property.

(v0.320, [#5491](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5491)) **The standard library's equality search is refused at the call.** `Contains`, `Remove`, `IndexOf`, `LastIndexOf`, `LinkedList<T>.Find` and the span `IndexOf`/`Contains`/`SequenceEqual` compare their argument with stored elements by `Equals` on an unconstrained element type, and at a record or choice with no equality there is nothing for the comparison to reach. The answer is the one spec [§9.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-equality-through-a-type-parameter) gives a user generic body: the comparison is rejected where the element type has no member for §9.2's first kind, and a call in a generic body at a type parameter is that body's own comparison, checked at its instantiations as a written `Equals` is, so a user `Bag<T>` that delegates to `items.Contains(x)` is rejected at `R` exactly as one that writes `Equals` is. What differs is where the rejection sits. A standard-library type contributes no interior-use requirement (the v0.318 paragraph above), so `Array<R>` stays well-formed and only the call is refused; a member admitted at some instantiations only is a conditionally-admitted member, and the members take a spec §14.2 register row (stdlib §4). Two alternatives are declined (owner decision). *A `hashable` constraint* on the members, by a constrained block or a member-level `where`: a constrained extension is never consulted for a name the receiver declares (spec §26.5 rule 1) and cannot carry `ICollection<T>`'s `Contains`/`Remove` (spec §26.3), a member-level `where` on an enclosing parameter is the form Appendix C.1 rejects, and `hashable` asks for a `GetHashCode` a linear search never calls. *Comparing by identity* is the box identity §9.2 declines for a `T`-typed operand. The stated cost: a call through `ICollection<R>` is refused even where the implementing class is a user's whose `Contains` does not compare by `Equals`; such a class is called through its own type.

(v0.321, [#5527](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5527)) **A conditionally-admitted member in a generic body reads its condition as the operation written on the type parameter would.** Spec [§14.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-conditionally-admitted-members) rule 2 decides a register member at the call, after substitution, and a call in a generic body whose receiver is instantiated at the body's own type parameter has a type argument that is not yet substituted. The rule reads the condition as the section that defines it reads a type parameter. Conformance is decided by the parameter's constraints (conformance rule 6), so `Array<T>.Sort()` needs `where T: IComparable<T>`, exactly as a written `x.CompareTo(y)` does; a condition decided at each instantiation (the choice classification, the FFI-pointee set, §9.2's first kind) is an interior use, checked at the declaration's instantiations exactly as a written `Equals` on `T` is. Declined: one reading for every row. Rejecting every such call in the body would refuse `Result<T, E>`'s combinators in every generic body over a type-parameter payload, since no constraint names the classification (spec §26.2), and would make `items.Contains(x)` stricter than the `Equals` it performs. Carrying every such call to the instantiations would admit `Array<T>.Sort()` in a body whose signature leaves `T` unordered, which a written `CompareTo` is not, and would carry outward a condition the signature can state as a constraint. Either uniform reading makes a register member answer differently from the same operation written on `T`.

(v0.321, [#5528](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5528)) **An inline array's `Contains` is keyed on the comparison.** An inline array has equality only over a hashable element, because its `=` and `GetHashCode` reach the element's (spec §3.6's hashable row, clause (c)). `Contains` is a linear search: it calls the element's `Equals` and never a hash, as the standard library's equality search does, which spec §14.2's register keys on §9.2's first kind. Spec [§15.1.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-inline-array-members) keys `Contains` the same way, so `x in arr` over `[2]R` and `x in xs` over `Array<R>`, which spec §17.5 reads as one structural `Contains`, agree. Declined: keying `Contains` with the array's own equality members and recording the asymmetry. Those members compare arrays; `Contains` compares an argument with elements, and a record that declares an `Equals` without being hashable would be searchable in an `Array<R>` and not in a `[2]R`.

(v0.321, [#5529](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5529)) **A declared constraint whose fit turns on a type parameter is decided where it is written.** The v0.320 paragraph above decides a constraint at a bare type-parameter argument by the parameter's constraints. An argument that contains a parameter (`C<Pair<W>>`, `C<W?>`), and a concrete argument whose bound's argument is a parameter (`C<Widget, X>` under `where T: U`), are the same case: the naming declaration's signature fixes what its parameters admit, so whether the argument fits at every admitted substitution can be read at the naming. Declined: carrying such a constraint to the naming declaration's instantiations. The v0.320 grounds hold unchanged: the constraint is a signature fact, the naming body is checked under its parameters' constraints, and spec §3.6 rule 2 leaves a declared constraint at an argument that is or contains a type parameter to spec §14.2. Splitting the rule by the argument's shape would also decide `C<W>` at the naming and `C<W?>` at `Box<R>`. The remedy is usually a constraint on the parameter (a record holding a `W` is defaultable at every admitted `W` under `where W: value, new()`), but no constraint guarantees every fit, and where none does, the naming needs a different argument. The sharpest case is the mixed one: `C<Widget, X>` under `where T: U` is rejected wherever some substitution `X`'s constraints admit is not a type `Widget` is assignable to (for an unconstrained `X`, `int`), the mirror of the v0.320 cost `C<W, int>`.

(v0.321, [#5531](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5531)) **An extension head restates the extended type's constraints.** A generic extension head binds its parameters (spec [§26.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-generic-extensions)), and the block's body is checked under their constraints like any generic body. The extended type's declared constraints are decided at the head by the v0.320 rule above, so `extension OwningArray<T>` states `where T: class`. Declined: confining the block's parameters implicitly to the extended type's admitted arguments, with its constraints carried into the block. That reading is sound, since every receiver is an admitted instantiation, but it gives a parameter constraints no clause states: spec §14.2 says an unconstrained parameter never acquires a derived property, and spec §26.4 and the v0.314 record on repeated head identifiers read a head as binding names and stating no constraint. A constructed type nested in the head (`extension IEnumerable<Counter<K>>`) is already an instantiation site under spec §14.1, decided by the block's clause, and the top-level head reads the same way. Confinement without the carried constraints would leave the block unable to name its own receiver type in a signature (`other: OwningArray<T>`), since that naming is decided by `T`'s constraints. An override inherits its constraints because calls are checked against the overridden signature; an extension block has no such signature, and its head and clause are its own. The stated costs: one clause per block over a constrained type; and a head that places a parameter under a bound whose argument is concrete and not a class or interface (`extension C<X, int>` under `where T: U`) is rejected for every `X`, so such a block is written at concrete arguments.

(v0.322, [#5557](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5557), [#5560](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5560)) **The `ref`/`out` owned-state bound is read per instantiation, and a concrete move-only `ref` parameter is rejected where it is declared.** Spec §22.3 bars a location of move-only value type at a `ref` or `out` argument, because a write through the alias bypasses drop-on-overwrite. In a generic body a location typed by `T` is move-only at some substitutions only, and spec §3.6 rule 2 reads an interior use of a type parameter at the substitution, as spec §7.10's copy demand is read. The bound is such a use, so `Box<C>` is rejected wherever it is named for a move-only `C`, and the body is admitted at every other `T`. Declined: *checking the body under a move-only assumption whenever `T` admits one*. It would reject `Swap(ref a, ref b)` over `T`-typed locations at every `T`, in every generic container, for a hazard present at move-only substitutions only. Under the bound, a `ref` parameter declared at a concrete move-only type binds no argument: every local, parameter and field of that type is barred, and no property, indexer, collection element or inline-array slot has such a type. It is therefore rejected at its declaration, where the mistake is written; the remedy is a by-value parameter and return (`c := Step(c);`). Declined: *leaving the declaration legal and uncallable*, which reports the defect at each call, and for an interface member nobody calls, never. A `ref` parameter at a type parameter stays legal, since it binds at every other substitution.

(v0.322, [#5556](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5556)) **`new()` admits a nullable value type and no nullable reference type.** The constraint has two arms: a class with a public parameterless constructor, which `new T()` calls, and a defaultable value type, at which `new T()` is `default(T)` (spec [§14.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-constraints)). `int?` is a nullable value type, distinct from `int` (spec §14.2's `value` paragraph), and every nullable type is defaultable (spec §17.11), so it falls in the second arm and `new T()` there is `null`. The `value` constraint excludes it by an explicit sentence, which `new()` does not carry. `Widget?` falls in neither arm, since it is not a class type and not a value type. Two alternatives are declined. *Excluding every nullable type*, for uniformity with `notnull`, `class`, `value` and the type bounds: each of those states a non-null kind or contract, and a `null` satisfies none of them, while `new()` states only that `new T()` yields a value of `T`, which `null` is at `int?`. *Admitting `Widget?`*: `new T()` would have two readings with different ownership, a constructed owned `Widget` through the class arm, or the `null` that `default(T)` gives every other nullable substitution. The stated cost: a nullable choice or constructor-only record satisfies `new()` while its non-nullable form does not, because only the nullable form is defaultable; `new T()` is `null` there, as `default(T)` already is.

(v0.327, [#5509](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5509), proposal 0141) **A dispatched generic method's requirements are decided at its bodies, and its parameters admit no move-only or inline-array substitution.** The v0.319 paragraph above left one instantiation undecided: the one dispatch forms. A call `i.Find<R>(…)` on an interface instantiates `IFinder.Find<R>` and reaches `F.Find<R>`, a body the caller need not see and, across modules, need not have in its compilation closure (spec §2.8.3), so a requirement `F.Find`'s body carries on its own `T` — an equality use, an `as` target, a copy — can be decided at no call. Spec [§14.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-dispatched-generic-methods) decides it at the body: every body dispatch can reach is checked under its constraint set with no deferral, and the remedy is the one §14.2 already names, a constraint on the declaring interface or base method, which every implementation inherits. That alone would have closed generic interface methods over values to the most ordinary bodies, because no admitted constraint excludes a move-only or an inline-array substitution short of a class bound, and a body that reads its parameter twice, boxes it, binds it, or declares the `T?` local §7.10 advises (spec §15.1 admits no nullable inline array) defers exactly that. So the parameters of a dispatched generic method admit no move-only and no inline-array type argument, an instantiation-site rule of §14.4's kind checked at the call and carried through a type parameter in scope by §3.6 rule 2's chain; inside every reachable body the parameter is copyable and not an inline array. Three alternatives were declined. Carrying the body's requirement to the calls of the base or interface member, the reading §14.6's edge attribution suggests, is unenforceable: the implementing module is outside the caller's closure, and §14.2 already makes the dispatch surface's constraints the only ones a call is checked against; a link-time verdict over the program's instantiation set has no source site. A `copyable` admitted constraint, explicit at the declaring interface, would make every generic interface or virtual method whose implementations copy their parameter state it in a module the implementer may not own, for a program — a move-only value or an inline array through a dispatched generic method — that does not exist (zero sites in the test corpus and the docs; the standard library declares no dispatched generic method); it is the reopening form if one appears. Checking overrides at the body while leaving a `virtual` method's own body per instantiation at its direct calls would give one method two postures for no measured gain. Kernel: no contact. Body-locality: local at a declared body; fact-discharged at an inherited witness (the body's deferred set is its fact, consumed at the conforming class); the exclusion's chain is the #5504 carve-out.

(v0.328, [#5634](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5634)) **A method reference over a conditionally-admitted member is rejected where it is formed.** Spec [§14.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-conditionally-admitted-members) rule 2 decides a register member at the use site, and spec [§27.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-method-references) forms a reference over an instance member by capturing the receiver at formation. The formation is the last site that sees the receiver's type arguments: an invocation through the formed value reads a function type, so a condition left unread at the formation is never read, and `s()` formed over `Array<Widget>.Sort` at an unordered `Widget` would reach a sort with nothing to order by. So the formation is rejected as the call is, with the row's diagnostic and remedy, and the condition is read on the overload the target selects, as spec [§14.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-type-argument-inference) *Constraints* reads a generic method's own constraints on the candidate a call selects. Declined: reading a failing condition as the overload's absence from the reference's candidate set, so that `xs.Sort` at an unordered element would resolve to the comparer-taking overload. It is the register's form of the constraint-filtered applicability v0.316 declined for `where` clauses ([#5406](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5406)), with the same cost: whether a condition holds would decide which overload a reference reaches, so `Widget` gaining `IComparable<Widget>` would turn a `var s := xs.Sort;` that compiles into an ambiguity and re-route a method-reference argument between a callee's overloads; and in a generic body whose condition is an interior use read at the instantiations, one body's resolution would differ by instantiation. Proposal 0116's note that the compiler decides admissibility before it ranks predates that decline; §14.2's opening sentence now says that an inadmissible member is absent as a use, not as an overload.

(v0.328, [#5635](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5635)) **A dispatched generic method's requirement summary is its constraint set.** The v0.327 paragraph above decides every requirement a dispatched body places on its own type parameter at the body, and that is what keeps the method's interface delta (spec [§2.8.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-interface-delta-classification)) readable from its signature: a summary that an override's or implementation's body could change would make a client's compile depend on a body outside its compilation closure, the dependence the body-site rule exists to refuse. The exclusion is a rule of the kind, not a fact of a declaration: only a change of kind moves it, and §2.8.6 now rows that change — a generic method becoming dispatched is breaking for its callers at a move-only or inline-array argument, beside a row for the `abstract`/`virtual`/`sealed override` modifiers, which no row named before. The inherited witness is the one place a summary is read, and it is the base method's own, which spec §2.8.2 already carries; §2.8.6 says that a change to it reaches the conforming classes. Declined: a dedicated §2.8.6 row for the dispatched case — there is no delta for it to classify.

(v0.329, [#5648](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5648)) **The exclusion reaches the calls dispatch can route to a witness, not a base class's own generic method.** Spec [§14.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-dispatched-generic-methods) counts an inherited witness — a base class's ordinary generic method `B.M<T>` that a derived `class D: B, I` uses to satisfy `I.M<T>` — among the dispatched generic methods, and its exclusion is stated of "a call of one — through the interface, the base, or the implementing class". Read of the witness, "the base" could name `B`, and a module declaring `b.M<[2 owned]Node>(…)` on a `B` would be in error by a declaration in a module it cannot see (spec §2.8.3) — unenforceable, and needless: that call instantiates `B.M` directly and its body is checked per instantiation like any generic method's. So the exclusion reaches a call that names a dispatched declaration — the interface method, the `virtual` or `abstract` method, an override, a declared implementation — and a call on a receiver typed at the conforming class or a class deriving from it, where the witness is the method dispatch reaches; a call on a `B`-typed receiver stays ordinary. The complement is stated with it: the conforming class's check against `B.M`'s requirement summary (v0.328 above) discharges the entries keyed on a move-only or inline-array substitution, which the exclusion makes unreachable through `I` and `D`, and rejects the class for the rest. Declined: the literal reading. Measured reach: no inherited witness of a generic interface method exists in `tests/` or `docs/`.

(v0.334, [proposal 0166](proposals/0166-move-only-substitutions.md); [#5772](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5772), [#5769](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5769), [#5771](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5771), [#5770](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5770)) **A move-only substitution has no copy; the positions that copy reject it at the instantiation.** The generics matrix found four shapes at which a body checked once under the class-or-interface assumption meets an owning choice or an owning inline array with no rule stating what happens: a generic record's component (`record Box<T>(V: T)` at `Box<Slot>`, which a record's copy duplicates), a copy out of a binding held in the borrowed state (`var r := v as T; _f := r;`), the borrowing enumeration interfaces (`IEnumerator<Slot>.Current` is a property of move-only type, and a value-typed yield is "a copy"), and the event sources (a payload passed by value-move to each subscriber). One answer covers them, and it is the answer spec §15.1 already gives for an inline array's element: the written-type ban is read after substitution, at the instantiation site through any chain (§3.6 rule 2), and a standard-library type that reads its element by copy carries the exclusion as a stated instantiation-site rule, since its member bodies contribute no requirement. Declined: a frame-owned borrowing `Current` with frame disposal (a third yield regime for a type the owning forms already serve), a move-only record at a move-only substitution (§15.3 admits no user-defined move-only kind), reading the copy out of a borrow as a move (the owner still disposes), and leaving the event-source leg deferred — stdlib §6.5's recorded deferral asked for a rule that states the restriction, which this is; `IEvent<T>` stays unrestricted, a user raise being checked where it reads. Measured reach: no generic record, enumeration interface or event source is instantiated at a move-only type in `tests/` or `docs/` but one rejection test (`borrowing_match_move_only_binding_consume_4803`, `Iter(): IEnumerable<Outer>`), which E1 now rejects before its ownership needle is reached.

(v0.334, [#5751](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5751), [#5790](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5790), [#5778](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5778)) **Three readings fixed where the single check meets a substitution.** *An unconstrained `default(T)`* is the zero value of every substitution that has one and is rejected at the instantiation site at every substitution that has none, a non-nullable reference type included: §17.11's closing sentence read as written. The alternative, `null` at a reference substitution with the result typed `T?`, is sound only if that result may never reach a bare-`T` location, and so would reject at the declaration the generic zero-initialization the tests pin as legal (`F: T := default(T)`, `return default(T);` into a `T` result: thirteen programs); `null` at every non-defaultable substitution (B) fails at an inline array, which has no null form. Reading A flips four programs, each instantiating a `default(T)` body at a function type or `string`, and `default(T?)` spells the absent value. *A two-presentation type in a generic body* is read per instantiation against the presentation the substituted argument selects — the §3.6 rule 2 reading, under which `new OwningBuffer<T>(n)` at a non-defaultable value argument and a reliance on `IEnumerable<T?>` at a value argument reject the instantiation; checking once against the class presentation would fill `Pair` slots with null `Item`s, and admitting only the shared members would reject every `OwningBuffer<T>`-holding container at its declaration. *A nullable form* has its underlying type's borrow-bearing, holds-borrows, classification, trivial-dispose, element-category and sendable properties, stated once in §3.6: every lane of the matrix had assumed it, hashable's clause (c) already said it for hashability, and the literal reading — `Pair?` holds nothing — would let `Keep<Pair>` store a `T?` past its source. Defaultable and C-compatible keep their own nullable rows.

(v0.334, [proposal 0164](proposals/0164-every-naming-is-an-instantiation-site.md), [#5777](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5777), [#5784](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5784), [#5761](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5761)) **Every naming of a generic member is an instantiation site.** Spec §14.1 defined the instantiation site by enumeration, and three namings were off the list: a method reference formed over a generic method, whose own type parameters no rule bound; a generic type named in receiver position of a static member access, the spelling §22.12 prescribes; and a generic extension block's member, whose block-level parameter §3.6 rule 2 attributed to neither a generic type nor a generic method. Each left a substitution that no per-instantiation rule read. v0.334 puts all three on the list with one mechanism and no inference: a reference to a generic method names its type arguments (`xs.Select(Util.Id<int>)`), the formation being the instantiation (the §22.12 scan admits the closing `>` before a token that cannot begin an operand, so the form parses); a receiver-position name instantiates the type, as the compiler already had it; and each block member is read as a generic method declaring the block's parameters first, which is how §14.5 and §26.5 already bound them. Declined: *binding a referenced generic method's parameters by unification against the target* (a new inference phase for zero measured users, and a bidirectional one where the reference is an argument to a generic call); *rejecting every reference to a generic method*; *reading §14.1's list as exhaustive at the receiver position* (unsound); *a third clause in rule 2 for block parameters, or removing generic extension blocks* (the desugaring states the same verdicts; 138 heads use the feature).

<a id="sec-pattern-matching"></a>
## 11. Pattern matching

`case` is the multi-way conditional. Most of its design follows ML/Scala/F# precedent. Two Bezel-specific points:

<a id="sec-the-scrutinee-less-form"></a>
### 11.1 Condition dispatch is the `if`/`elif` chain; the `when` construct is retired (v0.289)

Through v0.288 the language carried a scrutinee-less sibling of `case` — the atom `(when c1 => a, c2 => b, _ => z)` and the statement `when … end;` — whose every arm was a boolean test, the first true arm winning. It entered at v0.144 as its own keyword, split *out of* `case` because the two readings had shared one keyword and been told apart by source layout; the record weighed it only against a scrutinee-less `case` ("forcing those into a scrutinee-having `case` would require gymnastics"), never against the `if`/`elif` chain, and proposal 0057 re-spelled it as a paren atom in the very train that made `elif` first-class without asking whether both ladders still earned their place.

Retired at v0.289 (proposal 0122 L1) because the two spelled one selection and `when` was the weaker spelling. `(when c1 => a, c2 => b, _ => z)` is `(if c1 then a elif c2 then b else z)` — same common-type fold, same textual-order evaluation, same ownership meet — and `when … end;` is `if … elif … else … end;`. What `when` added was cost: two grammar productions, a never-statically-exhaustive rule, a per-arm discard clause, a precedence note, a keyword-disambiguation sentence, and a third role for one token — and what it lacked was the totality grant §21.7 gives a trailing `if`/`elif`/`else` whose every branch exits. Alternatives weighed: keeping both (declined for exactly that asymmetry); retiring `if`/`elif` instead (declined — `if` is the statement form's word order, the two-way pick, and the form with the grant); a deprecation window (declined — no external users; the v0.217 hard-cutover precedent). The one terseness lost is the single-statement arm `c => stmt;`, which becomes a one-statement `elif` block. `when` stays reserved: it is the arm-guard keyword and the `when true`/`when false` clause, and a retired construct's keyword is kept out of the identifier namespace so the old spelling fails with a clear diagnostic. Reopening bar: a selection shape the `if`/`elif` chain cannot express, shown by a real program (spec §2.8.5's bar).

<a id="sec-naked-binding-inside-positional-patterns"></a>
### 11.2 Naked binding inside positional patterns

Inside `Point(x, y)`, `x` and `y` are bindings, not type tests. The explicit `Point(var x, var y)` is also accepted. Outside positional destructuring (at the top level of a pattern), an identifier is a type test, so a binding requires the `var` form.

The naked-identifier shorthand is allowed only where positional bindings are unambiguous, which is the common case and the natural reading.

<a id="sec-catch-is-case-for-exceptions"></a>
### 11.3 `catch` is `case` for exceptions

Bezel's `catch` is a multi-way pattern dispatcher: it takes a thrown exception and selects a handler based on type, optionally with a guard, optionally binding the value to a name. That is exactly what `case` does for ordinary values. v0.63 makes the syntax reflect this — `catch` arms now mirror `case` arms in every detail that matters.

The shape:

```bezel
try
  DoWork();
catch
  var ex: TimeoutException => RetryWithBackoff(ex),
  var ex: IOException | NetworkException => Retry(ex),
  InvalidOperationException => RecordInvalidState(),
  var ex: Exception => throw                 // re-raise unrecognized
end;
```

Arms are comma-separated. Bindings use `var` exactly as in `case`. Guards use `when` exactly as in `case`. Multi-type alternatives use `|` exactly as in `case` alternatives (v0.167, §3.8). Arm bodies are expressions or `do ... end` blocks, exactly as in `case`.

The previous syntax — repeated `catch ... then ... end` clauses — was inherited from C# without examination. C# inherited it from Java, which inherited it from C++. Each step preserved the surrounding shape because changing it would have broken existing code; Bezel has no such constraint and can pick the form that fits the rest of its design.

The same logic applied to C#'s `switch` statement and Bezel's `case`: C# has the heavy `case Type: ... break;` shape from C, while Bezel's `case` is arm-shaped from the start. The argument was structural — pattern dispatch reads more clearly as arms than as a sequence of labeled clauses. The argument applies to exception handlers too; v0.63 finally acts on it.

<a id="sec-why-the-change-was-deferred"></a>
#### Why the change was deferred

v0.62 explicitly deferred this change to give it dedicated attention. Three concerns held it back, all of which had answers but each of which deserved being thought through.

The first was the `=>` token's reading. In `case` expressions, `=> body` reads as "produces this value." In `catch`, the body is statement-position; nothing is produced. Resolved by noting that statement-position `case` already establishes that `=>` means "body for this match" without producing a value — the `case` and `catch` arms are both statement-position constructs in this respect.

The second was exhaustiveness. `case` warns when it can't prove exhaustiveness. `catch` should not — a `try` block usually catches a few specific exceptions and lets others propagate. Resolved by noting that exhaustiveness is a `case`-specific behavior, not a property of arm-shaped syntax. `catch` borrows the syntax without inheriting the exhaustiveness rule, just as statement-position `case` borrows the syntax without inheriting the value-producing rule.

The third was the cost on the dominant case. Most `catch` clauses today catch a single exception type and run a few statements. The new shape requires `do ... end` around any multi-statement body, where the old syntax accepted a bare statement sequence. This is real overhead for the most common form. The tradeoff: three extra tokens (`do`, `end`, comma) on the common case, in exchange for structural consistency with `case` and visibly cleaner multi-handler code. The empirical analysis was a near-tie on density; the structural argument tipped the decision.

<a id="sec-why-bindings-use-var"></a>
#### Why bindings use `var`

`for var i in coll`, `case ... var n: int => ...`, `out var name`. Every site in Bezel where a name is being introduced as a fresh local binding uses `var`. Catch bindings are no different — they introduce a name for the caught exception. Marking them `var` is consistency with the rest of the language, not new ceremony.

The cost is three letters per binding-form arm. The benefit is that a reader scanning code sees `var` and knows "binding being introduced," with no exception case to remember.

<a id="sec-multi-type-alternatives"></a>
#### Multi-type alternatives

The old syntax forced each exception type into its own `catch` clause. To handle two exception types identically, programmers either duplicated the body or used the `when ex is A or ex is B` workaround (documented in v0.62 [§31.2.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-arm-patterns)). The new syntax expresses multi-type catches directly:

```bezel
catch
  var ex: IOException | TimeoutException => Retry()
end;
```

This matches Java's `catch (A | B)` and Python's `except (A, B):` in expressive power; the `|` spelling matches both Java's `catch (A | B)` and Bezel's own `case` alternatives (v0.167, §3.8). Inside the arm body, `ex` has the static type of the nearest common base — usually `Exception`. For type-specific handling within a multi-type arm, use a nested `case ex` or `is` test, but the common case (treating multiple types uniformly) doesn't need that.

<a id="sec-bare-throw-as-an-expression"></a>
#### Bare `throw` as an expression

The transition to arm-form `catch` surfaced an asymmetry in v0.62: `throw expr` was an expression (with bottom type, valid in any expression position), but bare `throw;` was statement-only. In the old `catch ... then statements end` form, this didn't matter — both fit naturally in a statement sequence. In the new arm form, where bodies are `=> expression` or `=> do statements end`, the asymmetry would have forced bare re-raises into the heavier `do ... end` form even when the re-raise was the entire body:

```bezel
// Without the change:
catch
  var ex: TransientException => Retry(),
  var ex: Exception => do throw; end       // do/end just for one statement
end;
```

v0.63 resolves this by promoting bare `throw` to an expression form too. The operand becomes optional: `throw expr` raises the supplied exception, `throw` (no operand) re-raises the current exception. Both have bottom type. The bare form is restricted to catch arm bodies — the same restriction as the statement form `throw;`. The natural arm shape becomes:

```bezel
catch
  var ex: TransientException => Retry(),
  var ex: Exception => throw
end;
```

The statement form `throw;` continues to work and means the same thing as the expression form followed by `;`. Inside `do ... end` arm bodies that perform multiple steps before re-raising, the statement form is still natural:

```bezel
catch
  var ex: SqlException => do
    metric.RecordSqlError(ex);
    throw;                                  // statement form, in a sequence
  end
end;
```

Both forms coexist; the choice between them is whether the re-raise is the whole arm body (use the expression form) or one step of several (use the statement form). This is the same expression-vs-statement choice that programmers already make for method calls, `case` arms, and other expressions that can also stand alone as statements.

The change is purely additive — every program that compiled in v0.62 continues to compile in v0.63 — but it materially improves the arm-form's ergonomics. Without it, the most common idiom in catch arms that re-raise (handle some types specifically, re-raise everything else) would have been heavier than the form it replaced. With it, the idiom reads cleanly:

```bezel
catch
  var ex: TransientException => Retry(),
  Exception => throw
end;
```

<a id="sec-what-catch-and-case-still-dont-share"></a>
#### What `catch` and `case` still don't share

The unification is structural, not total. Exception dispatch and value dispatch differ in two ways the syntax doesn't try to hide.

The first is the source of the value being matched. `case` matches an explicit scrutinee. `catch` matches the implicit "currently-thrown exception" — there is no scrutinee to name, and the arms can only test types. A non-type `catch` arm pattern (a literal, a range, a record-destructuring) makes no sense and is a syntax error.

The second is the behavior on no match. `case` in expression position raises `MatchFailureException` on no match; `case` in statement position falls through silently. `catch` does neither — unmatched exceptions propagate to the enclosing handler. This is the right behavior for exception dispatch, and it is documented as a third position in [§19.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-exhaustiveness) (alongside expression-position and statement-position `case`).

These differences aren't gaps; they're the parts of exception handling that don't reduce to value pattern matching. The syntax unifies the parts that are the same and leaves the parts that differ to the semantics.

<a id="sec-one-rule-for-failure-signals"></a>
### 11.4 One rule for failure signals: why Bezel is not `Result`-first (v0.229)

The error-model study (proposal 0076, superseded at v0.229) asked the maximal question directly: is `Result<T, E>` a better general-purpose error mechanism than exceptions, and should Bezel migrate to it? The answer landed as doctrine — the failure-signal rule (stdlib §1) — rather than migration, and the two declines behind it deserve a citable home.

**Why information content is not the band test.** The intuitive line — exceptions for boring failures, `Result` where the failure carries information — was the guides' original teaching, and it was declined as the classification test. It breaks on the environmental fault: an I/O failure carries rich information (path, message, OS detail), yet its consumption site is almost always a boundary — a log, a retry loop, a protocol conversion — that reads the data once and lets it go, which is exactly what `catch`'s copy-out model (spec §31.1) serves. Classifying by information content would evacuate the environmental band into `choice` returns whose exhaustive matches every interior caller would then have to write, converting boundary handling into call-site ceremony. The test that survives is *where consumption happens* (call-site branching vs. boundary handling) *and whether the payload can be value-like* (spec §35.4) — never how much information the failure carries.

**Why maximal `Result` is recorded as not currently constructible.** A `Result`-first Bezel needs four things at once, and each is missing or expensive under landed rules: ownership-polymorphic combinators (`Map`/`AndThen` over owned payloads — the retired-and-deferred territory of spec §14.4), without which owning `Result` chains are match pyramids; a propagation operator, which sits behind proposal 0061 §2's evidence gate and a four-way collision on the `?` token; failure values that cross task seams, where an owned payload makes the instantiation owning and never directly sendable (spec §35.12); and a governed payload vocabulary, without which the error-type space fragments faster than the exception taxonomy it would replace — a choice is a closed sum, so every case addition breaks every exhaustive consumer (spec §35.6). None of this is a taste judgment, which is why the study's verdict is recorded with a reopening bar (proposal 0076 §6's four conditions) rather than as a preference: it is reopenable by evidence, not by re-arguing.

What Bezel adopts instead is the hybrid the rule encodes: `Result` and domain choices confined to the clusters where call-site branching on value-like payloads is the real consumption pattern (decode/deserialize, gathered validation), exceptions keeping the boundary-consumed residual, and one mechanism per fault condition — chosen per condition by the rule, not per language by fashion.

<a id="sec-enum-prefix-omission"></a>
### 11.5 Enum-prefix omission is a pattern-position affordance, deliberately not an expression-position one (v0.242)

Recorded v0.242 (proposal 0086 item 5; the asymmetry predates it). Inside a `case` over an enum (or choice) scrutinee, the prefix may be omitted (`case p of Low => …`, spec §10.6/§19.8) because pattern position has exactly one scrutinee, fixed by `of`, whose type is known before any pattern is read — resolution is scrutinee-directed by construction, and a bare constant there can mean nothing else (spec §19.8: "a case name resolves to a case pattern, never a type test"). Expression position has no such anchor: in `p = Low`, the two operands are peers — nothing marks `p` as the side that supplies `Low`'s type, and a rule that resolved `Low` against "the other operand's type" would make name resolution type-directed and bidirectional, which spec §2.7's position-keyed, no-backtracking design deliberately excludes (the recorded decline of C#'s dual-meaning rule). Swift's leading-dot syntax (`p == .low`) shows the feature is buildable — at the cost of a dedicated sigil form and inference-driven member lookup pervading expression syntax; Bezel declines that machinery for the saving of one qualifier. The asymmetry is therefore the composition of two settled designs, not an oversight: patterns are scrutinee-typed, expressions are position-resolved. Write `Priority.Low` in expressions; inside the type's own body, spec §2.7's enclosing-member lookup already shortens it to `Low`.

<a id="sec-is-does-not-bind"></a>
### 11.6 `is` does not bind — the three-construct decomposition, recorded (v0.242)

Recorded v0.242 (proposal 0086 item 7; the decline predates it — spec §12.7 states it flatly). Bezel splits what C#'s `is T name` bundles: `is`/`is not` are the *boolean* type tests (with flow narrowing on locals and `readonly` fields, spec §7.4/§12.7 — the tested variable itself narrows, so the common tested-local case needs no binding at all); `as` is the *nullable-binding* form; `case` typed patterns are the *multi-way binding* form. A fourth spelling, `is T name`, would buy one line at exactly the sites narrowing does not reach (mutable fields, call results) and would import the C# feature's recorded swamp: a pattern variable declared inside a boolean expression must be scoped by definite-assignment flow — bindings escape into `else` branches under negation (`if (!(x is T t)) return;` leaving `t` usable after), into the enclosing scope, and into `&&`/`||` arms — a scoping model so widely misread that it is a staple of C# style guides. Bezel's decomposition keeps every binding at a statement-shaped introduction (`var`, `case` arm) where scope is block-shaped and readable. The decline is of the *spelling*, not the capability: `var d := x as Dog; if d <> null then … end;` is the sanctioned two-step, one line longer, with no new scoping rules. If evidence accumulates that the two-step dominates real code at fields (post-§7.4-readonly-narrowing, the residual is mutable fields and expression results), the recorded lift is `is T name` with **true-branch-only** scope and no negation flow — the tight-scoping variant — which this entry pre-commits as the only acceptable shape.

**What a *write* through a value-narrowed binding at a boxed location denotes: rejected at compile time (v0.254; spec defect [#3117](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3117); owner-directed 2026-08-12 — "Please also resolve 3117").** §12.7 narrows the tested variable itself, which is the decomposition's whole point above — but at a variable whose *declared* storage is `Object` or an interface and whose tested type is a **value type**, the narrowed binding names a §8.6 **box**, and the spec said nothing about writing through it. The read direction had been settled a revision earlier (#3090, v0.252: the narrowed read produces the boxed value, which is what §17.4's inline-array remedy — `if o is [3]int then var a: [3]int := o; end;` — actually needs); the write direction was left to the implementation, which routed `lvalueAddr` to the box payload for reads and writes alike and so accepted `o[0] := 9` de facto. The measured behavior is the defect's sharpest statement: `var a: [3]int := [4, 5, 6]; var o: Object := a; if o is [3]int then o[0] := 9; …` prints `9` through `o` and `4` through `a`. Three interpretations were weighed. **(1) Legal, writes the box** — §8.6's wrapper "holds the value", boxing copied it, so the box is a distinct value with its own storage and mutating it is ordinary value-type mutation. Locally self-consistent (the write *is* readable back through the same binding, which is exactly what the `9` measures), and declined anyway, on the ground the local view hides: `Object` is a class and an interface is a reference type (§3.1, §8.1), so such a variable carries **the standard reference semantics** §3.2 defines, and `var o2: Object := o;` shares the box — the write is mutation of heap state observable through *every* other reference to it — aliased mutation of a value, the one thing value-copy semantics exists to exclude. Interpretation 1 is not a small liberty inside the value model; it is globally unsound against it. It also breaks the runtime-managed kind's own premise: boxed values are runtime-managed (§8.6/§28.9) and §9.7/§35.4 treat runtime-managed payloads as classification-neutral — no borrow tracking, no lifetime constraints — with immutable shared referents doing the work; a writable box payload would demand borrow and freeze machinery for the one kind defined by never needing any. **(2) A compile error** — adopted, on those two grounds, with the issue's own §23.5 analogy kept as the *secondary*, reader-facing one: a reader who writes `o[0] := 9` to update `a` updates neither `a` nor anything they can name, the same family as "would modify a discarded copy", and the diagnostic should echo that register. The adopted rule keys on **where the store lands**, not on the statement form, so it reaches every position that can put one in the box: the lvalue-chain statements, the same chain in a `ref`/`out` argument (§22.3's assignable-storage-location categories admit `ref o[0]`, and the callee's write lands in the payload — the `Swap` shape, observable through `o2`), and an inline-array argument at an `extern` parameter, which §32.5/§15.1.3 pass **by address** so that native writes land there too. By the same key it does *not* reach the **bare-binding** compound and `++`/`--` forms: §6.6 makes `o += 1` equivalent to `o := o + 1`, whose store targets the declared location and mints a fresh box — the legal reassignment below, spelled shorter. Rejecting sugar whose normative expansion is legal would carve §6.6's equivalence for no semantic gain.
