The analogy's imperfection is recorded rather than glossed: at a §23.5 indexer result the copy is discarded and the write is unobservable, while here the box persists and the write *is* observable through the writing binding — which is precisely why the aliasing ground, not the discarded-copy ground, is primary. **(3) Legal, reflected nowhere** (the pre-#3090 behavior, where the statement emitted nothing) was never live: a silently dropped write. What the rule does *not* touch is the whole-value assignment `o := b`, whose target is the declared `Object`/interface location and whose right-hand side is boxed under §5.1 — a **fresh** box, other references to the old one unaffected — which is why the diagnostic's copy-out/mutate/re-box remedy makes visible the box mint interpretation 1 hid, and which invalidates the narrowing exactly as §12.7's reassignment sentence has always said. Kernel cost: none — a use-site lvalue admissibility rule, grounded on the value-copy principle and §28.9's immutable-referent premise; no new K row, no axiom. **The scope check the ruling asked for, recorded with its residue:** no value-type *member* surface can mutate its receiver in the shapes that have one — a choice declares no instance storage and only expression-bodied read-only properties, with `this` a borrow (spec §35.2); an enum's instance state is its underlying constant (§10.4/§10.5); inline arrays declare no members and cannot be extended (§26.2) — so for those kinds the spec states nothing, as the ruling provided. The residue is the **mutable body record**: §9.4 admits a settable property and nothing forbids a record method from assigning one, so a call on a **boxed receiver** — `o.Bump()` on a narrowed one, or plain interface dispatch `i.Bump()` on an `i: IBump` holding a boxed record, which needs no narrowing at all — is a writable source shape whose meaning turns on whether a value-typed receiver's `this` aliases the receiver's storage or a copy of it. No section states that generally, for records or for value types: the nearest text is §8.5's parenthetical that a boxed receiver's rendering takes a **call-bounded copy**, said of the `ToString` path and generalized to no other member. Both readings are bad in the same way this entry rejects (an alias reproduces the aliased mutation; a copy reproduces interpretation 3's dropped write), but neither §12.7 nor this ruling can settle it without first ruling on value-receiver binding, and the language has no mutating-member classification a narrowing-site rejection could key on. It is left stated nowhere and flagged as spec defect [#3124](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3124), not resolved by side effect. *(v0.255: resolved — the value-receiver ruling, interpretations 2/3 combined; decision record [§9.13](#sec-value-receivers-are-borrows-rationale), spec §9.4.)*

<a id="sec-every-throwable-is-an-exception"></a>
### 11.7 Every throwable derives from `Exception` (v0.243)

Recorded v0.243 (proposal 0080, #3046 — the re-ruling of the #771 any-class ruling, discharging the owner-review flag spec §31.1 carried from v0.182 through v0.242). Through v0.242, `throw` admitted **any owned class instance**. The capability had no recorded motivating use and no reporting surface — `Message`, `StackTrace`, and `CauseChain` are `Exception`-only — and the v0.182 changelog itself recorded why the ruling was suspect: #771 predates the v0.162 completion of the exception model, whose reporting surface is written in `Exception` vocabulary throughout. The capability's entire cost was *rules*: a no-universal-catch-all caveat (spec §31.2.3), an `Object`-base carve in the multi-type binding rule (§31.2.1), an asterisk on the deliberate-suppression idiom (§31.2.5), and a hedged parenthetical in §31.1 itself — each a trap for the C#/Java reader who assumes a final `Exception` arm catches everything, with no compensating capability anywhere. The corpus made the case in its strongest form: of ~750 `throw new` sites across the docs and tests at drafting, not one used non-`Exception` throwability *for anything* — the only deliberate uses were the two test files pinning the permissive rule itself.

v0.243 requires the throw operand's static type to be `Exception` or a class deriving from it. All four rule artifacts delete; with single inheritance, the nearest common base of any two throwable classes is itself `Exception`-family, so the binding rule's `Object` case is unreachable rather than repealed-with-changed-behavior; and a catch arm — or a generic arm parameter's bound — naming a non-`Exception`-family class errors: **provably dead** for every such class but `Object`, universally matching for `Object` itself (the bare `Exception` arm is that spelling), the same reject-dead-shapes posture §31.2.1 already applied to non-class generic bounds. The requirement is a pure typing precondition at the throw site — one subtype check — with **zero kernel contact**: `throw x`'s K3.8/K4.2 site-announced-kill status, the post-throw moved-from machinery, the exception-ownership rules (transfer to the runtime, dispose-after-arm and its two owner-owned exemptions, the borrowed-field residual), and §22.15's pure-throw carve-out (whose conditions are purity and trivial-dispose, not derivation) all quantify over "the thrown owned class instance", never over its base class.

Alternatives declined: the **status quo** (the spec had carried the request for this re-ruling since v0.182); **requiring derivation as a warning** (a warned-but-admitted non-`Exception` throwable keeps every artifact alive, since the shape remains expressible — the payoff is the deletions, which only a hard rule buys); **structural admission** of any class exposing the reporting members (Bezel has no structural typing anywhere, and trace capture at throw is `Exception` machinery); and **restricting the arm side while keeping the throw side permissive** (incoherent — the throw side is the source of the artifacts; the arm side merely reflects it). Like [§11.3](#sec-catch-is-case-for-exceptions)'s unification, the ruling makes the newcomer's prior true: `catch var ex: Exception` is the catch-all — its one qualifier the uncatchable `StackOverflowException` (spec §31.4), a resource-exhaustion termination no arm has ever matched, not a typing carve.

<a id="sec-supersession-keeps-later-wins"></a>
### 11.8 Exception supersession keeps later-wins; the lost diagnosis is snapshot-recorded (v0.259)

Recorded v0.259 (proposal 0097, charter ruling C3). Bezel has a single in-flight exception slot and a **later-wins** rule: an exception raised in a non-normal position — a throwing `finally`, a throwing `when` guard, a later throwing disposer in a multi-value cleanup pass (spec §31.2/§31.2.1/§28.4) — replaces the one already propagating, and the replaced one is disposed. The cost was that the superseded exception's diagnosis vanished, and it is frequently the *interesting* one: a `finally` that throws while unwinding a real failure buries the real failure. The reviews' remedy was to flip the priority to **primary-wins** (the Java try-with-resources shape: the body's exception survives, the cleanup's is attached as suppressed). The ruling declines the flip and takes the thing the flip was actually *for* — the diagnostics: at every supersession site the runtime snapshots the superseded exception into the superseding one's `SupersededChain` (spec §31.4), on exactly the `ExceptionInfo` machinery `CauseChain` has used since v0.162, then disposes it exactly as before. Later-wins stands, the single slot stands, and the lost diagnosis is recovered.

**Why snapshot + later-wins beats primary-wins.** The case for primary-wins is entirely a diagnostics case, and the snapshot answers it — the buried failure's type, message, and stack trace survive into `SupersededChain`, printed by the uncaught-exception report and the FFI-boundary abort beside `CauseChain` (spec §32.8/§33.3). What primary-wins would *additionally* buy is that the surviving instance is the primary one, so an upstream `catch (IOException)` still matches the body's failure rather than the cleanup's. What it would cost: **a second live exception** — the suppressed instance must stay alive and reachable through the primary until handling completes, i.e. one exception owning another, which spec §31.4 declines in a sentence written about a different feature and applying verbatim here (*"a live-instance `InnerException` … would require exception instances to own exceptions, reopening the arm-lifetime rules"*) — primary-wins is that rejected design arriving through the supersession door; **a change to the in-flight-slot model**, whose single slot is what makes §31.2's dispose-exactly-once rule and its two named exemptions statable at all; and **a behavioral reversal for the repeated case** — at §28.4's cleanup pass, primary-wins means the *first* disposer's failure escapes and the rest are suppressed, defensible where "the body" is privileged, arbitrary for a scope-end pass over N locals where no disposal is the primary.

**The precedents, cited honestly.** Both mainstream precedents recover the losing exception; they disagree about which one wins, and the disagreement is not the part either is famous for. Java (`Throwable.addSuppressed`/`getSuppressed`, JLS 14.20.3) is primary-wins — but what Java *added* was the list, not the priority: before it, the cleanup exception simply replaced the body's, exactly as Bezel's later-wins does, and the complaint was the lost diagnosis. Python (PEP 3134, `__context__`) is later-wins with the earlier preserved — *"During handling of the above exception, another exception occurred"* — precisely this model. The diagnostics recovery is universal across both precedents; the priority direction is not, and Bezel already sat on Python's side for reasons that predate the proposal (the single slot). The difference from Python is that Bezel's carrier is a snapshot rather than a live instance — the same difference §31.4 already chose for causes, for the same reason.

**The reopening bar:** field evidence from external users that upstream `catch` arms are matching on cleanup failures often enough to matter — arm matching on the primary is the one gain the snapshot does not provide. The snapshot does not foreclose it: a future primary-wins would keep the same chain and only change which instance carries it. Zero new ownership rules were spent — the whole delta is a value-record copy into a field of an object the runtime already owns, at a point the runtime already controls.

<a id="sec-no-bit-syntax-patterns"></a>
### 11.9 No bit-syntax patterns: sub-byte matching stays out of pattern position (v0.263)

Recorded v0.263 from proposal 0106 §3.5. Erlang's bit syntax works in *pattern* position — a binary is destructured by a pattern naming bit-widths, binding fields and a tail in one form. Bezel declines the pattern-position half, and the decline is narrow: it is about pattern position, not about bit-granular reading.

The reasons are the pattern system's own. A bit-width pattern element binds a value whose *type* is a function of a width written inside the pattern, so pattern-position binding would acquire a computed-type form that spec §19.3's pattern kinds have nowhere to put. Exhaustiveness has no answer: the pattern kinds Bezel counts for coverage are case names, type tests, and the nullable split, all of which partition a finite declared space, while bit-width alternatives partition an integer's value space — the counting the retired generic-leaves determination rule shows the cost of getting wrong (§8.2). And the tail binding is the sharpest problem: a matched remainder wants to be a sub-view of the scrutinee, which in Bezel is a borrow-bearing span, so a pattern element would mint a borrow whose source is the scrutinee and whose lifetime pattern arms do not currently reason about — a borrow-producing binder, which no pattern kind is.

None of that reaches the *reader*, which needs none of pattern position: a bit-granular reader over a `ReadOnlySpan<byte>` is an ordinary borrow-bearing record whose lifetime the span rules already bound, and the GC-derived objection that forces Erlang's design (a matched tail must outlive the match, so sub-binaries are refcounted) does not transfer, because a Bezel span already *is* a lifetime-checked sub-view. That direction is a standard-library question and is filed as its own proposal (0109), where it must be settled on stdlib grounds; this entry exists so the pattern-position half is settled rather than left dangling behind it.

**Reopening bar:** a design that answers all three — a binding form whose type is not computed inside the pattern, an exhaustiveness account for width alternatives, and a borrow-producing binder with a stated lifetime rule — not a demonstration that bit matching reads nicely.

<a id="sec-exception-rendering-member"></a>
### 11.10 `Exception` declares the rendering its own text promised (v0.268)

Recorded v0.268 (proposal 0113, [#3259](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3259)). Since v0.162 spec §31.4 has said that an exception's diagnostic chain *"survives into logs and `ToString()`"*, and v0.259 added the same promise for `SupersededChain` with an output shape attached (*"renders it beside its causes"*). No member delivered either: `Exception` declared no `ToString`, so it carried spec §8.5's compiler-generated class default — the qualified type name alone. The two texts did not merely under-specify each other, they **conflicted**: an implementation that rendered the chains from `Exception.ToString()` violated §8.5, and one that obeyed §8.5 falsified §31.4. `Console.WriteLine(ex)` printed `System.ArgumentException` and nothing else.

**Declared rather than re-homed.** The smaller-looking fix — strike *"and `ToString()`"* from both sentences and point readers at the runtime reports — was declined on three grounds. It retracts a recorded decision (v0.259's output shape), which the change-process classifies as a design change in its own right rather than a wording fix. It leaves the footgun permanently: `Console.WriteLine(ex)` and `$"{ex}"` are what people write, and the language would have no member that renders an exception at all, so every log site hand-assembles four properties. And it is not actually smaller — it amends §31.1, the guides, and every prose site implying an exception renders itself, against one declared member plus its rendering text. Nothing structural was in the way: `Object.ToString` is already `public virtual` (spec §8.1), every class already carries the slot, no derived type's conformance moves, and trivial-dispose (spec §28.9) keys on destructors and `owned` fields, never on member count, so §22.15's pure-throw carve-out is untouched.

**The stack trace is out, deliberately.** Bezel follows Java's split rather than C#'s inclusion. Neither §31.4 sentence promises the trace — both promise the *chains*; `StackTrace` is *"formatted"* with no format stated anywhere and may be `""`; a one-line `Log(ex.ToString())` that expands to a multi-hundred-line dump stops being called, which would leave the promise unfulfilled a second way; and Bezel already has the structural equivalent of `printStackTrace` in triplicate — the uncaught report (§33.3), the FFI-boundary abort (§32.8), and the exit-time disposal report (§22.13) — all three of which do print it. `ToString` is the short half rather than a duplicate of the long one.

**Content normative, arrangement not — and the reports defined independently.** The posture matches what the language already does with exception text everywhere else: §33.3 and §32.8 fix *what* is printed and never *how*. Two arrangement facts are nonetheless fixed, because a reader can rely on them: the qualified type name is always present, so the rendering is never less informative than the default it replaces, and the two chains are distinguishable, since §31.4 gives their orderings different meanings. Stating that the three reports define their own content — rather than defining them as calls to `ToString()` — forecloses two live hazards rather than restating the obvious: double printing, which a shipped implementation was already positioned for (its uncaught report reads the type name through the vtable and `Message`/`CauseChain` by field offset), and a fatal allocation on the termination path, since `ToString` allocates and spec §17.7 makes a failed allocation terminate the process — on the very path whose purpose is to explain a failure. No second §17.7 carve-out was requested.

**Not `pure`, and that is the load-bearing half of the purity question.** Purity here is not about the string allocation (§22.15 admits runtime-managed allocation and string composition). It is about dispatch: §22.15's rules propagate a `pure` override down the whole chain, so marking this member would oblige *every* derived exception type's own `ToString` — user types included — to be declared `pure` and checked under the pure fragment, for a rendering whose whole job is to compose user-declared state. An absent contract is recoverable; a false one is not.

**The recorded cost, in full.** Every `Console.WriteLine(ex)`, `$"{ex}"`, and `Log(ex.ToString())` in every program changes output, and `base.ToString()` inside a user exception's own override now reaches this rendering rather than the type name. No program changes shape. A program parsing `ex.ToString()` for exactly a qualified type name should read `ex.GetType().FullName` (stdlib §2.13), which is stable and says what it means.

<a id="sec-patterns-read-intrinsic-ordering"></a>
### 11.11 Relational and range patterns read the language's own ordering (v0.314)

Recorded v0.314 ([#5269](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5269), [#5273](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5273)). Spec §19.3 compares a relational pattern's scrutinee "under the ordering of the scrutinee's type" and did not say where that ordering comes from. Three answers were open: the intrinsic ordering only (spec §3.5's primitives except `bool`, and `string`'s ordinal ordering, §4.6); the type's declared `operator <` group at relational patterns, with range patterns staying intrinsic; or declared operators at both. The first is taken. A range pattern additionally keeps spec §16.3's element set, the integer types and `char`.

**Why not the declared operators.** The reason is not that a pattern must never run user code. A named slot runs a class getter and a constant slot runs a record's own `=` (spec §19.3), so that line was never drawn. Three reasons belong to this feature. First, a type with an intrinsic ordering has exactly one, and the language fixes it. A declared group is four methods that spec §25.2 obliges only to exist together: not to agree with one another, and not to be an order. A type may also declare several heterogeneous groups (`operator <(a: Money, b: int)` beside `operator <(a: Money, b: Money)`), so "the ordering of the scrutinee's type" would name an overload resolution rather than an ordering. Second, range patterns cannot follow. A range pattern's test is `Range<T>` membership (spec §16.6), and widening `T` past §16.3's intrinsic set is a deferred design change whose open questions include these patterns (`docs/proposals/DECLINED.md`, *`Range<T>` element widening*). Reading declared operators at the relational form alone would give `>= lo` and `lo..`, one test by spec §16.2, opposite verdicts over every ordered user type. Third, a guard already writes the test with nothing lost: `var x when x > limit` resolves `>` as any expression does, at a position where a reader expects a call.

**Why the equality slot is not a precedent.** A constant pattern is spec §8.4's `=` test. A record that declares a homogeneous `=` must declare `Equals(T)` and `GetHashCode` beside it (spec §25.2's co-declaration rule), so the operator a pattern reaches is tied to the surface every other consumer of equality reads. No rule ties a declared `<` to `CompareTo`, or to anything else.

**The cost.** Over `float`, `double`, `decimal` and `string` a relational pattern is admitted and a range pattern is not, because the two read different rules: §3.5's ordering, and §16.3's range element set. Over `float`, `double` and `decimal` the split is the price of the deferred widening. `string` joins them because §16.3 excludes it too, and range patterns over it would leave spec §16.6's agreement with `in` without a `Range<string>` to agree with. No user type is added to the split. An `operator <` declared on a record, enum, choice or class does not reach pattern position, and the guard is the spelling.

**Reopening bar:** a program whose ordering test a guard cannot express, or a `Range<T>` widening that admits user element types, at which point both pattern forms would move together.

<a id="sec-async"></a>
## 12. Async

<a id="sec-pattern-a-vs-pattern-b-is-type-directed"></a>
### 12.1 Pattern A vs Pattern B is type-directed

The interaction between async and ownership is the hardest design problem in the language. A class-typed task result must end up somewhere with defined disposal — but that somewhere is sometimes "the task itself, with multiple readers borrowing through it" (a fan-out cache, a future shared between subscribers) and sometimes "a fresh single owner" (a connection from `ConnectAsync`, a token from `AcquireAsync`).

The producer's return type carries the choice:

- `Task<T>` for class T means fan-out. Every awaiter borrows; the task must occupy an owning location — `using var` is the canonical form; plain owned locals (v0.184), owned parameters, owned fields of `this`, owning-collection slots, admitted static task fields (v0.184), and (v0.182) an unbound temporary whose borrowed result stays within one full expression also qualify (spec [§30.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-result-lifetime-and-task-disposal)).
- `OwningTask<T>` for class T means single-consumer. One awaiter takes ownership; the task is consumed by `using var x := await t;`. A second await raises `InvalidOperationException`.

The rule reduces to "whatever the type says." Producers commit in their signature; consumers' obligations follow from that signature. There is no syntactic peek at the await context. For an `async` lambda the "type" is, in order, its ascription, a closed target slot, or — with neither — its own body (spec §30.6, v0.296): the third source is the one place the wrapper is inferred rather than committed in a signature, and it commits to the body's own ownership class — a fresh owned class result infers `OwningTask<C>`, which is why such a lambda is inapplicable at a `Task<R>` slot and applicable at an `OwningTask<C>` one, and why a `Task<R>` slot needs an ascription to take it.

The motivating case is `Semaphore.AcquireAsync(): OwningTask<SemaphoreToken>` — an operation whose entire purpose is to produce a fresh, single-consumer token. The consumer writes the canonical shape:

```bezel
using var token := await sem.AcquireAsync();
DoCriticalWork();
```

Producers who want both shapes declare two methods with distinct names — and the second is typically one line: a Pattern B await is an owned expression, so an async façade's `return await CoreOwnedAsync(…);` republishes the owned result through a Pattern A task's owned slot (the adapter shape worked in [§12.2](#sec-choosing-between-taskt-and-taskowned-t)).

<a id="sec-choosing-between-taskt-and-taskowned-t"></a>
### 12.2 Choosing between `Task<T>` and `OwningTask<T>`

*(Spelling note: revisions v0.78–v0.96 wrote Pattern B as `Task<owned T>`; v0.97 replaced the `<owned T>` generic-argument mechanism with the named `Owning*` types (spelled `Owned*` at v0.97; renamed at v0.168 — §3.9) — see [§5.11](#sec-owning-collections) and spec §14.4. §12.1–§12.2 were originally written in the old spelling and are stated here in the current one; the semantics are unchanged.)*

The choice between Pattern A and Pattern B is the producer's — it goes in the method signature and determines what every caller must do. The decision tree below is from the producer's perspective.

**Step 1: Is the result type a value type, `string`, or runtime-managed type?**

If yes, neither pattern applies. Return `Task<T>` and stop here. Value types are copied out; `string` and function values are runtime-managed. No ownership crosses the await boundary.

```bezel
public async method CountAsync(): Task<int>          // value type — no pattern
public async method FetchNameAsync(): Task<string>   // string — no pattern
```

**Step 2: The result is a class type. Will more than one consumer legitimately read the *same result instance*?**

This is the fan-out question, and it is architectural, not incidental: a config cache shared by every caller (spec [§22.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-static-initialization-order) pattern (b)'s static task caches), a lookup result several subscribers await, a task stored once and awaited from multiple places (spec §30.4). Fan-out is announced in a design — it is rarely discovered late.

If yes → **Pattern A: `Task<T>`** — and say why at the declaration:

```bezel
/// Pattern A (fan-out): one fetch, every caller borrows the same Config.
public async method FetchConfigAsync(): Task<Config>
```

The same answer covers **chained republication** — an async body that re-exposes a longer-lived owner's result by chaining (`return await repo.LoadAsync(id);` — the source task's ownership transfers into the outer task, spec [§30.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-result-lifetime-and-task-disposal)'s chained slots) or that shares a fresh value through the task's owned slot. Not "the task borrowing from the repository": a task cannot hold a borrow it did not produce — §30.5/§28.3's return rules forbid returning a bare borrow through a task (the original wording here predated the v0.67 owns-result ruling and was corrected in the 2026-07 review, #1332). Callers borrow *through the task*, whose teardown is the defined owner at every link.

**Step 3: Otherwise — the result is a fresh, single-consumer class value → Pattern B: `OwningTask<T>`.**

This is the default, and it covers more than classic resource acquisition. Connections, lock tokens, and file handles qualify, but so does ordinary fresh *data* the caller will hold: a parsed document, a materialized array, a response object. The consumer writes the form every caller writes unprompted:

```bezel
using var conn := await ConnectAsync(url);    // owned; disposed at scope end
var doc := await ParseAsync(input);           // owned local; affine discharge rules apply
```

**Summary**

| Question | Answer | Pattern | Return type |
|---|---|---|---|
| Result is value type / string / runtime-managed? | Yes | Neither | `Task<T>` |
| Several consumers read the same result instance (fan-out), or the body republishes a longer-lived owner's result (chaining)? | Yes | A — say why at the declaration | `Task<T>` |
| Fresh, single-consumer class result — including plain data the caller will hold? | Yes | **B (the default)** | `OwningTask<T>` |
| Still in doubt? | — | **B** | `OwningTask<T>` |

**When in doubt, choose Pattern B.** *(v0.204 — this reverses the guidance recorded here through v0.203; the changelog v0.204 entry carries the evidence for the reversal.)* Four reasons, each a recorded fact of the current model:

1. *The failure mode is caught.* Under the affine model ([§5.23](#sec-the-affine-model), v0.150), an owned result the caller never discharges is auto-dropped at scope end with the must-use warning — forgetting to dispose is not a leak, it is a diagnostic. The old argument for A-as-conservative ("no one can accidentally leak") was grounded in the pre-affine model; it is now equally true of B.
2. *A does not reduce the caller's obligations.* Since v0.190 ([§12.10](#sec-calls-mint-storage-lends)) the caller owns the *task* in either pattern. A moves the discharge obligation from the value the caller wants onto the wrapper around it; B puts it on the value itself and lets the await discharge the wrapper.
3. *The consumer form is the natural one.* B's canonical call site is the one-line `using var x := await …;` A's was the two-line bound-task form. *(v0.249: the consumer tax this reason conceded is repaid at the everyday site — the one-line `var`/`const` bind of a Pattern A await of a fresh producer now desugars to the hidden pinned-slot pair (spec [§30.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-scoped-await-bind), the scoped await-bind); naming the task, for multi-award or to hold it, remains the two-line form. The pattern choice stays the producer's — this reason no longer separates the defaults as sharply as it did at v0.204.)*
4. *B is recoverable; the recovery is one line.* A producer who chose B and later discovers a fan-out need does not narrow the existing signature (that would break owners); it publishes a sibling ([§12.1](#sec-pattern-a-vs-pattern-b-is-type-directed)'s "two methods with distinct names"), and the adapter is the shape §30.5 itself anticipates: a Pattern B await is an owned expression, and a Pattern A async body's `return` of an owned expression fills the task's owned slot.

```bezel
/// Pattern A façade over the Pattern B core, for subscribers.
public async method FetchSharedAsync(url: string): Task<Config>
  return await FetchOwnedAsync(url);   // B result (owned) fills the A task's owned slot
end FetchSharedAsync;
```

The reversibility asymmetry the old guidance rested on is real and stays recorded: **A→B remains the mechanical-migration direction** (widening `Task<T>` to `OwningTask<T>` breaks callers loudly and compilably — one-line binds keep compiling as owned binds; non-owned-consuming awaits and multi-await sites are rejected with the §30.5 diagnostics, and the fixes are local), and **B→A remains breaking** (callers wrote `using var x := await t;` and owned the result; under A that same line is rejected). What the flip re-weighs is which default you *start* from: the reversibility argument buys an option — start at A, widen to B once ownership need is proven — that the recorded surface has never needed (no stdlib API started at A and widened; every recorded doubt-case went straight to B), and it pays for that option with the two-line consumer tax on every call site in the meantime, while the reverse risk has the one-line sibling recovery above, which does not touch the existing signature at all. Optimizing the everyday path over an unexercised option is the trade; it is made with the evidence attached, so it can be revisited if the evidence ever inverts.

**The question that settles it** is no longer "would forgetting to dispose leak?" (the affine model answers that for both patterns) but: ***"can a second consumer legitimately need this same result instance?"*** If yes, Pattern A — the task is the shared owner. If no — or if you are unsure — Pattern B: hand the caller the value, and let the ownership machinery do what it exists to do.

<a id="sec-no-configureawait"></a>
### 12.3 No `ConfigureAwait`

C#'s `ConfigureAwait(false)` exists because the default is to capture the synchronization context, and library code needs to opt out. Bezel's runtime has no synchronization contexts in this version, so no opt-out is needed. If UI-thread affinity is added later, this will need revisiting.

<a id="sec-cooperative-cancellation"></a>
### 12.4 Cooperative cancellation

Forcible cancellation (Thread.Abort) is unsafe — it can interrupt code holding a lock, corrupt invariants, or skip cleanup. Bezel uses cooperative cancellation: a `CancellationToken` is passed in and the operation checks it at safe points. This is C#'s model (and Go's, via `context`); Rust async reaches the same cooperative end differently — a future is cancelled by dropping it, which takes effect at a suspension point.

The ground reaches **domains** unchanged (v0.263), and this is where an Erlang-style forcible teardown (`brutal_kill` on an execution domain) is declined. A domain is not a safer unit to interrupt than a thread; [§30.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-runtime-model)'s run-to-completion execution makes it less safe, because a forcible teardown lands wherever the domain's current continuation happens to be — inside a guard's critical section, between an owned value's production and its binding, with [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows)'s disposal obligations mid-discharge — and there is no unwind to run them. Ownership improves the normal and the exception paths, which is what it delivers; it leaves the asynchronous-interrupt path exactly as this entry describes it. The recovery need that motivates a kill primitive is real and is routed elsewhere: the ask-then-stop half to cross-domain cancellation through a shared source (stdlib §6.1, v0.263), the take-away half to process-level supervision ([§12.14](#sec-recovery-unit)).

<a id="sec-execution-domains"></a>
### 12.5 Execution domains: why the async model is serial per domain

Through v0.130 the spec said async continuations "may resume on any thread-pool thread" and that shared mutable state "requires explicit synchronization," while the rationale (§5.1) accepted that programmers can write data races. Stated together, those two positions made the ownership chapter unsound in any program that actually exercised the stated freedom: the borrow checker's lifetime reasoning assumes nothing else runs between two points of a continuation with no suspension between them, yet the runtime model permitted another continuation — on another thread — to dispose a borrow's source mid-use. The language was protecting against the *smaller* hazard while permitting the larger one: §6.1 refuses to narrow nullable fields because another thread could write null between check and use, but a concurrent thread was permitted to *dispose* the object a borrow points into. v0.131 resolves the contradiction in favor of the ownership model: a domain is a unit of serial, run-to-completion execution, and every program in this revision is a single-domain program. The wording change costs existing programs nothing — no construct could observe genuine continuation parallelism portably, since any program doing so had data races whose behavior was already undefined — and it converts the §28 soundness assumptions from accidents of the current implementation into normative guarantees.

Three alternatives were considered and **declined**. *Send/Sync-style thread-safety typing* (Rust's approach) statically tracks which types may cross threads; it is the right tool for a language committed to shared-memory parallelism, but it infects every generic signature, roughly doubles the constraint vocabulary, and buys safety for a parallelism model Bezel does not actually offer — the cost lands on every user, the benefit on few. *Documenting the status quo* (keep "any thread, synchronize it yourself") was declined because it leaves the flagship feature unsound and outsources the hardest correctness problem to convention — the precise failure C++ shows daily. *Locks as discipline* (rely on `Mutex` use) was declined because nothing checks it; a forgotten lock is exactly as silent as the race it permits. The serial-domain model, by contrast, requires no new constructs, no new annotations, and no migration.

The decided direction for *parallelism* — multiple domains communicating by ownership transfer over channels, with sendability derived from existing type properties (owned transfer for classes, the §9.7 borrow-bearing computation for value types) — is recorded in the project tracker (#600) and deferred to a later revision. The one capability genuinely given up in the interim is CPU-bound parallelism inside one process; I/O-bound concurrency, the dominant async workload, is interleaving at suspension points and is unaffected by serialization of continuations. `Mutex`, `Semaphore`, and `Atomic<T>` (§30.12) are retained: foreign threads can still enter through the FFI, and the types are forward-compatible with multi-domain programs. *(v0.133: the deferred direction was delivered — language spec [§30.14](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-multiple-domains)–[§30.15](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-channels); the reasoning is §12.6 below.)*

<a id="sec-sendability-spawning-channels"></a>
### 12.6 Sendability, spawning, and channels

v0.133 delivers the parallelism half of the domain model: `Domain.Spawn` creates domains, `Channel<T>` moves data between them, and the **sendable** derived type property ([§3.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-derived-type-properties) instance, defined in [§30.14](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-sendable-types)) is the entire safety story. The design's one invariant — *no mutable state is reachable from two domains* — is what makes the v0.131 single-domain guarantees survive parallelism unchanged: every crossing value is moved (the origin domain provably loses it, by the same machinery as `move` and owned parameters) or immutable (`string`, value copies), and the channel core is the single compiler-known synchronized exception. Data-race freedom is purchased with the ownership system the language already had, not with a new type system. The decisions, and what was declined:

**Sendable classes must be `sealed` (or `closed` over a fully-sendable closed hierarchy).** The subtyping hole forced this: sendability is checked on static types, but an owned field typed at a sendable open class could hold, at runtime, a subclass instance carrying a back-reference into the origin domain — a data race smuggled past the static check. Three closures of the hole were considered. *Sealing* (chosen): zero runtime cost, compile-time diagnosis, statically airtight — at the price of excluding open classes from crossing domains. *A runtime send-check* (walk the transferred ownership tree at the send site, verifying each concrete class is transfer-closed): sound and more permissive, **declined for now** because it hides a per-send cost proportional to the transferred graph and converts a compile-time error into a runtime one; it remains the natural widening if sealing proves too narrow, and admitting it later is non-breaking. *Subclass-must-be-sendable inheritance rules* (deriving from a sendable class may not break self-containment): declined because it inverts the dependency — a base author's coincidental sendability would constrain every subclass author, violating the principle that derived type properties are observations, not obligations.

**Spawn entries are capture-free; data enters through explicit arguments.** The alternative — *move-capturing lambdas* (a closure form that takes ownership of its captures) — was declined: §28.9's capture-is-borrow rule is a single uniform story for every closure in the language, and carving out a second capture mode for one call site would ripple into closure typing, disposal of captured-but-never-run closures, and the §28.3 escape analysis. An explicit `owned arg` parameter is semantically identical, visibly costed at the call site, and required no new machinery. Multiple values aggregate into a sendable class, an `OwningArray`, or flow through a channel.

**Faults cross as `DomainFaultedException`, not as the original exception object.** No exception class is sendable (spec §30.14 *Faults*: the built-in ones carry no sendability statement, and a user-declared one derives from a compiler-known base whose inherited state no section states transfer-compatible — the *v0.327 addendum* below; exception classes are typically open and carry arbitrary state), and special-casing "exception objects may cross anyway" would reopen the invariant exactly where programs are least disciplined. Copying the message and stack-trace text (strings — sendable) preserves the diagnostic value; programs needing structured fault data return it as a sendable result or send it over a channel. Declined: a sendability requirement on throwable types (intrusive, retroactive) and runtime graph-checking of exception objects (the runtime-check costs above, on the unhappy path).

**Channels are MPSC, with the `Channel<T>` object as the receiving end and `NewSender()` minting sender endpoints.** Declined alternatives: *a `Create` returning two endpoints via `out owned` parameters* — more ceremony at every creation site and a third type, for no expressiveness gain over minting senders on demand; *multi-consumer channels* (receiver cloning) — cloning a receiving end is shared ownership of the consume capability, #603's territory — deferred at v0.133 pending `Shared<T>` (since delivered, v0.134): the remaining blocker is §28.13's unresolved domain-locality for the synchronized set (see the §18 entry); *bounded channels* — back-pressure wants an asynchronous `Send` that awaits capacity, a useful but separable design, deferred. The unbounded MPSC form covers the dominant fan-in/pipeline shapes and every deferral is an additive extension.

**`CancellationToken` does not cross domains.** Not by a new rule — it falls out: the token is a borrow-bearing record, hence not sendable. The cross-domain cancellation pattern (a dedicated channel carrying a cancel message) is documented rather than mechanized for the token itself. *(v0.263: the primitive this sentence anticipated arrived, at the **source** rather than the token — `CancellationTokenSource` carries the synchronized fact, so `Shared<CancellationTokenSource>` is sendable and a cloned handle is the cross-domain cancellation currency; the token remains non-sendable exactly as above, and a published source is signal-only — it carries no registrations, because a registered callback is a function value whose captures belong to one domain. The channel-message pattern remains the answer where no source is published. Stdlib §6.1 carries the ruling.)*

**v0.327 addendum: the compiler-known classes are closed to inheritance** ([proposal 0138](proposals/0138-compiler-known-sendability.md); [#5613](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5613), [#5605](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5605)). The sealing rule above closes the subtyping hole for declared classes only. The stdlib states several classes sendable whose listings are not `sealed` (`Array<T>` and the other collections, `Mutex`, `Semaphore`, `Atomic<T>`, `Channel<T>`, `ChannelSender<T>`, `Counter<K>`, `OwningBuffer<T>`), spec §12.4 sealed only a class declared `sealed`, and stdlib §1 keeps the transfer-closed rules, rule 1 included, off compiler-known classes — so nothing barred `class Evil: Mutex` with a non-`owned` class field, and `Domain.Spawn` at static type `Mutex` could carry an origin-domain borrow. Three closures were weighed. *Closing the compiler-known classes to inheritance unless their text opens them* (taken, spec §12.4): one rule for every statement, present and future, at no cost to any program — no test, document or guide derives from a library class other than the opened ones (`Encoding`, `Stream`, `TextReader`/`TextWriter`, the exception family), which are the abstract library classes, whose abstract members exist to be supplied, and the exception family; a concrete class's inherited virtual members (`FileStream`'s `ReadAsync`/`WriteAsync`) or its `Object` overrides do not open it, and `sealed` on a listing becomes informative. *Sealing implicitly only the classes a sendability statement names* (declined): it closes each statement one at a time, leaves every other library class open for no reason anyone has named, and ties an inheritance verdict to a sendability paragraph a reader of the listing never sees. *The runtime send-check* stays deferred on the grounds above. The mirror question — a declared class deriving from an opened base, `sealed class E: Exception` — is decided by rule 2's compiler-known-base clause: the base's inherited state is read from its section, not from fields the stdlib does not show, and it is transfer-compatible only where the section says so; no section says so, so such a class is not sendable. Declined for now: stating `Exception`'s inherited state transfer-compatible (its surface is strings and arrays of a string record) so that a sealed user exception with sendable fields could cross. A caught exception is a borrow (spec §31.1), so the only owned exception that could cross is one constructed and never thrown, and faults already cross as `DomainFaultedException`; the statement waits for a program that needs it, and it is one sentence in §31.4 when it comes. Neither recorded decline in this area moves: *subclass-must-be-sendable* declined a base's sendability obligating its subclasses, and the clause only lets a subclass's verdict read its base's state, as rule 2 already does for declared bases.

<a id="sec-memory-model-edges"></a>
### 12.7 The memory model is five edges plus atomics

v0.184 wrote down the happens-before edges the runtime was always going to
need: mutex/semaphore release→next-acquire, channel accept→deliver,
spawn→entry and settlement→join, task settlement→awaiter (or blocking wait
at the process edge), and static publication — the store into an admitted
mutable static, and static-init completion, publishing the referent's memory
to observing domains. That fifth edge is the one §22.12's untorn-pointer
wording silently presumed, and the one that makes §22.13's cross-domain task
cache — and §30.5's borrowed-source chain through it — sound on weakly
ordered hardware rather than by x86 accident. Two object-specific guarantees
were already stated in place and are cross-referenced rather than restated:
CancellationToken's cross-thread flag visibility (§30.8) and Shared<T>'s
drop→last-drop-destruction ordering (§28.13). The striking property is how
little there is to say, and that is by construction, not omission: the
sendable discipline means no mutable state is ever reachable from two
domains, so a Bezel program cannot *write* the racy access whose semantics
memory models spend most of their pages on. The only deliberate hole is
memory shared with foreign threads outside the §30.12 primitives, which
belongs to the platform. Sequential consistency for atomics (v0.140) was
reconfirmed — no acquire/release variants until profiles demand them.

The same revision fixed the fate of a faulted channel send (the item is
disposed; a channel never retains what it did not accept — the TrySend
give-back RFC remains the path to a lossless failure mode), made the
TCS/OwningTCS source the owner of its task (every §30.11 example already
assumed it; the v0.75 changelog entry ([`Bezel_Changelog.md`](Bezel_Changelog.md)) records the alternative's typecheck failure;
disposal accounting splits by source kind so plain-TCS multi-await fan-out
survives untouched), and closed the one silent hole in structured
concurrency's quiescence guarantee: a TaskScope live across a yield in an
abandonable async iterator would have been physically reclaimed with
children running — now a compile error keyed on the same class-level fact as
§28.12's no-destructor warning, with the degraded-destructor fallback
recorded but not taken.

**Warned value statics (v0.221, #1393).** Rule 2's v0.163 text made every non-synchronized mutable static an error; the shipped compiler never enforced it for value types, and the corpus that grew in the gap — the destructor-counter idiom, ~800 declarations in the compiler's own suite — is single-domain and correct. The ruling recorded at #1393 admits primitive-, enum-, and heap-free-inline-array-typed mutable statics with rule 4's warning rather than an error, because the category is the violation-tolerant one: no reference content means ignoring the warning risks stale (or, for the multi-word forms, torn) values, never memory unsafety — a strictly milder violation class than rule 4's own. The same ruling named the owning-inline-array static field as rule 2's form (iv) — the admission §22.13/§11.7 had made in v0.181 (the static slot-pool the exit-disposal pass exists to dispose, necessarily a mutable field since restoration is per-slot assignment), which rule 2's "exactly two forms" sentence had silently contradicted — warned like rule 4 because its owned instances are cross-domain reachable *and* slot-mutable, its violation class being full UB. `string` and record statics were declined, not deferred: a mutable string static's overwrite forces a choice between a reclamation race (UAF through the runtime's own refcounting when the obligation is violated) and an unbounded never-release leak, the same payload-scaled cost declined for the v0.186 suppression-list types; and torn record reads fabricate invariant-violating values — a hazard heap-free inline arrays, bare element sequences with no §22.16 invariant surface, do not share, which is the recorded line between the two heap-free categories. Both remain errors with the stdlib's synchronized spellings (`AtomicInteger<T>`, `Mutex`, `Locked<T>`) as the remedies, liftable only by recorded ruling. Forms (iii)/(iv) deliberately get no memory-model publication edge: the obligation, not synchronization, is what makes them sound. (Form (iv) was retired at v0.334; [§12.15](#sec-statics-verdict-grounds).)

<a id="sec-locked-rwlocked"></a>
### 12.8 `Locked<T>`/`RwLocked<T>`: the lock owns the data (v0.186)

Real services share **structured state** — a session map, a cache, a registry — and before v0.186 the options were the
actor idiom (principled, heavy for a cache) or a `static readonly` collection "guarded" by a *separate* static
`Mutex`, which §22.12 rule 4 rightly warns about: the mutex is not tied to the data, and convention is what §30.14
exists to replace with checking. The shape adopted (from Rust's `Mutex<T>`/`RwLock<T>`, not .NET/Java standalone
monitors): the lock **owns** its interior, access is only through a scoped guard, and the guard's `Value` is a borrow
whose source is the guard — so the borrow-escape rules the language already has enforce the critical section, with no
new lifetime machinery. One type lifts any wholly-owned interior; the alternative — enumerating `ConcurrentDictionary`,
`ConcurrentSet`, … — grows the stdlib N types for N shapes and still leaves user classes unshareable.

The interior admission **reuses the sendable derived property** (§30.14) rather than defining a near-twin: a
`Locked<T>` is by design reachable from every domain (statics; `Shared<Locked<T>>`), so a borrow inside `T` would let
a guard-holder chase a reference into another domain's heap outside any lock. "T is a sendable class type" is exactly
the wholly-owned-tree condition, already implemented, already diagnosed.

**The read-guard ruling (recorded at graduation).** `RwLocked<T>`'s concurrent read guards across domains are the
type's point — and Bezel has no read-only borrow, so a "reader" mutating through `Value` would be a silent
cross-domain race. The graduation ruling splits the hazard the way v0.184 split the static-event rule: **stores are
checked where provenance is visible** — through a `ReadGuard`-sourced borrow, every assignment, `move`, and
`ref`/`out` pass is a compile error (expression-site, borrow-provenance-keyed, decidable, and **body-local**, because
borrow provenance does not cross call boundaries) — and the rest is a **recorded programmer obligation**: mutating
method calls through the read borrow (no const-method vocabulary exists, and one was declined — it is §12.5's
pervasive-constraint road), and callees reached with the read borrow as a plain argument (borrowed-parameter stores
are a core idiom no caller-side rule can reject). Declined: deferring `RwLocked` (cuts accepted scope and the
read-parallel complement of §12.9); pure residual trust (the flagship path would rest wholly on convention);
per-interior read facades (N types for N shapes again). The lift path, if evidence forces it, is a read-only
view/borrow form designed once with the frozen-view family (§12.9).

**Guard-kill and lock-kill strengthening (recorded at graduation).** §28.3's site-announced-kill carve-out is justified by
the killed object's own disposed-flag backstop; killing a guard sets no flag on the *interior* its surviving borrows
reference — while releasing the lock out from under them — so the carve-out's justification fails and the graduation
strengthens the freeze over guards (the §28.14 admission-freeze precedent): guard kills under live interior borrows,
and body-local lock kills under live guards, are compile errors. Lock destruction that no body-local rule can see —
the cross-domain last-`Shared`-handle drop while another domain holds a guard — takes the §28.2 posture, stated
normatively (checked-mode deterministic fault, unchecked UB) because the casualty here is user data mid-critical-
section, not a runtime lock word.

**Static membership, recast on v0.184's ground (recorded deviation from 0003's letter).** 0003 was written before
v0.184's store semantics for admitted mutable statics; its "join the rule-2 set" spelling would have made a *mutable*
static lock sound but overwrite-leaking — a displaced lock leaks its whole interior until exit, a payload-scaled cost
§22.12's `Task?`-cache acceptance does not cover — and racing first-use stores could transiently fork a registry into
two lock instances (last store wins): memory-sound, silently wrong. No use case demands lock-slot reassignment (the
interior's mutability lives behind the guards), so the locks join rule 4's new v0.186 **suppression list** — making
the `static owned readonly` registry, 0003's actual deliverable, warning-free and exit-disposed — and stay out of
form-(i). The same recast as `FrozenArray<T>`'s; liftable by separate ruling if a real mutable-slot use case appears.
(Presentation since v0.245: these memberships are carried by the types' stdlib §1 fact grants — **synchronized** plus
**displacement-unbounded** — and rule 4's set is the derived warning-suppression set; [§12.13](#sec-statics-facts-vocabulary).)

Also recorded: the DI answer. A container's singleton table is a process-global mutable map — the same state made
less visible, resolved at runtime, reflection-shaped (and Bezel has no member-access reflection, §18). Wiring is
constructor parameters from a composition root; when state must genuinely be ambient-and-mutable, a synchronized
static `Locked<T>` is the visible, compiler-checked spelling of what a DI singleton hides. Guard-across-`await` is a
lint, not a ban (a "no-suspend type" concept would be disproportionate); lock ordering stays out of scope (the actor
idiom is the recommendation when ordering gets complicated).

**No poisoning, and the detector that replaces it (v0.263).** A `Locked<T>` whose guard holder throws releases the lock
by unwind and does **not** mark the lock poisoned. Rust's flag exists because a panicking holder can leave an interior
that nothing later re-examines; Bezel's interior is *"reachable only through a scoped guard"* (spec §30.12) and must be
a sendable class, so a half-updated interior is structurally unaliasable and observable only through the next guard —
and the next guard's public entry re-evaluates the **class invariant**, which spec §22.16 evaluates at construction
exit and at the public entry and exit points. That is a detector with content, where a poison flag carries only
"someone threw somewhere". Two limits are recorded rather than assumed: invariants are never evaluated on throw paths,
and checked-contracts mode is not the release default — so in a default build the detector is off, and a torn interior
is caught on the next checked run rather than in production, an asymmetry a program relying on invariant detection must
know it has. Memory safety is untouched either way: what poisoning would add over the invariant is a coarser signal
plus a second failure mode on every acquisition.

<a id="sec-data-parallelism"></a>
### 12.9 Data parallelism: MOVE, FREEZE, and the recorded fork-join design (v0.186)

Bezel had *parallelism* (domains, §12.5–§12.6) but no *data parallelism*: "run this function over this array on all
cores" measured ~60 lines of ceremony (worker channels, a context class per worker, `FireAndForget` supervision
loss). A user-side library cannot close the gap — function values are never sendable, and the capture-free call-site
check is compiler-known machinery — so the combinators are compiler-known, in the position `Domain.Spawn` already
occupies. The v0.186 surface engages §12.5's Send/Sync decline without reopening it: no constraint vocabulary, no
declared properties — only boundary checks at call sites, the accepted §12.6 pattern.

Two strategies shipped: **MOVE** (`Parallel.Map`/`Reduce`/`ForEach` — chunk copies or ownership partition; zero new
checking rules, one table extension of the spawn-position category) and **FREEZE** (`FrozenArray<T>` — the `string`
immutable-crossing precedent generalized to bulk data; `Shared<FrozenArray<T>>` sendable via a recorded §28.13
membership ruling, the set's first deep-immutability member). **Freeze-time aliasing is closed conditionally, not
absolutely** (recorded at graduation): the consume-site freeze — with the v0.186 statement that span locals count as
live borrows of their carried sources — and §9.7's span-escape bullets close every body-local route to a surviving
writer; borrows held in fields, collection slots, or pending async frames are invisible to any body-local rule, so
the v0.180 domain clause is **extended** to consumption into domain-reachable constructions (`FrozenArray.From`, the
`Shared`/`Locked`/`RwLocked` constructors, admitted static stores) — the same recorded-obligation architecture as
every §30.14 residual, and the deliberate alternative to inventing a whole-program alias analysis for one
constructor. Seed semantics for `Reduce` are per-worker and stated plainly (a non-identity seed is incorporated once
per worker) — the alternative, threading one seed through, serializes the reduction; hiding the semantics is how
PLINQ earned its documentation warts. Declined, with reasons that stay binding: a **`parallel for` statement** (its
safety needs per-index write-disjointness dependence analysis the spec could neither state simply nor check totally;
a runtime check hides per-iteration cost, UB is not in the vocabulary, and a "trust me" independence annotation is
exactly what §3.6 rule 1 forbids — and the library-first posture already governs synchronization, §30.12);
**move-capturing spawn bodies** (§12.6's one-capture-mode decline stands; explicit `owned` arguments are semantically
identical and visibly costed); **user-generic parallel libraries** (the first step onto §12.5's declined road; the
compiler-known table can widen later without breaking); **a runtime send-check** (declined in §12.6, undisturbed).

**The recorded fork-join extension (designed, not shipped).** In-place divide-and-conquer — quicksort over one
buffer, `Parallel.Join(lambda() => QSort(lo), lambda() => QSort(hi))` — needs the two banned capabilities at once: shared reads
of frame data without copies, and disjoint mutable writes into one allocation. The design is recorded here so its
review cycle starts from settled rulings; **none of it is normative in v0.186** (no grammar, no §3.6 row, no
invariant change):

1. **Blocked-primary `Join`** (ruled): the parent *blocks*, which is both the soundness mechanism (a blocked
   continuation stalls its whole domain — §30.13 run-to-completion — so nothing domain-reachable mutates during the
   extent) and the liveness mechanism (the blocked parent lends its thread to the team; rayon's recruitment). The
   awaiting variant is recorded as weaker *and more novel*: it forfeits work-stealing and needs a frame-wide
   reachability admission with no existing shape to reuse (the TaskScope-sibling attack refutes the §28.14-reuse
   claim). The §30.3 dispensation it needs rests on an *enforced* pure-compute property: task/channel waits already
   fault inside sync closures (v0.162), and a **fork-extent runtime guard** makes every §30.12 blocking acquisition
   raise `InvalidOperationException` inside a fork extent — the guard's enumeration must cover the whole blocking
   family as of the landing revision, **including `Locked<T>`/`RwLocked<T>`** (§12.8), reachable through statics by
   name where no capture check sees them.
2. **A `concurrently-readable` derived type property** (§3.6 form, checked only at fork positions): sendable value
   types and `string`; `FrozenArray<T>` and `Shared<FrozenArray<T>>`; admitted read-only views whose source is
   admitted. Locks, channels, and the task family are deliberately excluded — capturing a lock the blocked parent
   holds is a deadlock vector, not a sharing vector (Bezel's Sync-analog is narrower than Rust's, on purpose).
3. **Fork-position closure admission** (`par` marker — a §28.14-family contextual modifier, compiler-known
   declarations only): captures remain §28.9 borrows (no second capture mode — §12.6's decline holds); fork-closure
   bodies are **write-restricted** (stores through captured borrows rejected; only captured lend-span writes and
   closure-local state); captured sources are frozen for the extent (§28.14's freeze, trivially enforced by
   quiescence in the blocked form).
4. **Disjoint span lending**: `SplitAt` witnesses with **provenance-linear** admission (per-binding linearity is
   vacuous for copyable records — the admission traces captures back to a disjointness witness, each witness feeding
   at most one closure) and **opaque lending by type** (ruled): a distinct lend-span family with no `Buffer`
   projection and `Slice`-only narrowing, so buffer recovery is closed by ordinary name resolution — a helper over
   `Span<T>` cannot receive one. The interprocedural body-rule alternative was declined: it requires a
   fork-reachability classification of functions, a whole-program analysis class with no precedent here. One
   inherited residual is named, not absorbed: §22.12 rule-4 warned statics (and, v0.221, rule 2's form-(iii) warned statics; form (iv) was retired at v0.334) are reachable from fork closures exactly
   as from any two domains today — sharpened at graduation: fork closures are lexically the *confining* domain's
   code, so a program honoring rule 4's one-domain confinement obligation would **newly** race if its own fork
   closures touch the static from team domains; the future landing must extend the confinement obligation (or the
   fork admission) to fork extents explicitly.
5. **The invariant amendment, ratified in principle** and pinned for the landing revision: §30.14's flagship sentence
   becomes location-granular — *"no mutable memory location is ever accessible from two running domains, except that
   a `Join` extent may lend provably disjoint slot ranges of one allocation to distinct fork closures, under the
   fork-admission rules"* — because object-granular reachability is the wrong predicate for the construct
   (its flagship case lends one buffer's disjoint ranges to two running domains). It is deliberately **not** applied
   in v0.186: nothing normative in this revision violates the current sentence, and flagship prose should never be
   weaker than the shipped language requires. A **memory-model clause** (fork-extent fork/join happens-before edges)
   lands with it.

The `.AsParallel()` endnote stays recorded: the §29.11 affine-query ruling removes PLINQ's two classic hazards
structurally (re-enumeration is already an error; consumption transfers the whole plan), but operator callbacks are
storing signatures and chunking needs an indexable source — high-cost machinery for wins the explicit combinators
already deliver. Evidence first.

*(FREEZE's recorded pressure, v0.260.)* FREEZE reaches exactly one shape — the buffer. The likely first user
pressure on that boundary is the **frozen class tree** (a build-once object graph shared read-only across domains:
configurations, parsed documents, routing tables, compiled schemas); the pressure record, and the two design
questions a proposal must start from, live at stdlib
[§4.19](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-frozen-array) (proposal 0102 §6).

<a id="sec-calls-mint-storage-lends"></a>
### 12.10 Task ownership at the call site: calls mint, storage lends (v0.190)

[§12.1](#sec-pattern-a-vs-pattern-b-is-type-directed) and §12.2 presume something the ownership chapter did not actually say until v0.190: that the caller of `FetchConfigAsync()` **owns** the returned task. The producers table classified a non-`owned` return from an instance method as a borrow of the receiver, with no exception for the `Task` family — so by the letter, every unmarked instance, interface, or function-value task call minted a state-machine frame that nobody owned, while §30's entire disposal story (`using var task := client.GetAsync(url);` — the then-hypothetical `HttpClient`, removed from the spec in v0.269; both decision trees above) required the opposite. Nor could any rule patch this by keying on `async`: it is an implementation-side modifier that no signature or interface exposes (spec [§28.12](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-asynchronous-disposal)). v0.190 resolves the contradiction with a classification keyed on the construct rather than on a marker (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-producers), #1786): a **call** whose declared — pre-substitution — return type is a `Task`-family type produces an owned task, with no marker and no factory warning, whatever the callee kind; a read of **storage** — a property, field, indexer, or local — remains a borrow of that storage. Calls mint, storage lends: a call is the moment a frame comes into being (or is forwarded by someone who owned it), and storage is the place a task someone else already owns is shared from. (Since v0.261 the per-callee-kind matrix this doctrine motivates — declared return kind × callee kind, every cell cited — is spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-announcement-table)'s announcement table.)

The declined alternative was the marker: require `owned` on every fresh-task-returning signature. The blast radius alone was disqualifying — every async signature in every program, every task-returning function type, both decision trees above — but the deeper defect is what the *unmarked* spelling would then mean, because a marker whose omission compiles must leave the unmarked form meaning something. As a borrow it is a standing leak-by-omission trap, worst through interfaces, where a caller cannot see whether the implementation behind a signature is async; as a hard error it forces the marker onto every task signature — [§5.5](#sec-local-variable-ownership-is-inferred-not-annotated)'s "redundant noise" verdict, transplanted to the language's most common async surface. The declared return type already carries exactly the bit the marker would add; this is the announcement-by-declared-type doctrine the iterator convention had established, applied to the type family whose calls produce frames by construction. Also declined: post-substitution keying (`First<T>()` at `T = Task<int>` would mint a disposal obligation over a collection-owned element — the decline v0.194's bare-type-parameter ruling later reaffirmed, [§5.7](#sec-factory-method-ownership)), and extending calls-mint uniformly to all deferred-activation kinds — unsound for iterables, where the stored-collection view idiom (`method Items(): IEnumerable<Item> => _items;`) is legitimate and common. The task/iterable asymmetry is principled, and is recorded here so the iterable analogue is not later "fixed" by copying this ruling blindly.

**The launder-family fences and the iterable completion (v0.223).** Three 2026-07-24 rulings closed the seams this asymmetry left open, all by *announcement coherence* — no value may be viewed through a slot whose type-announced classification contradicts its own. A `Task`-family value's calls mint, so it may not inhabit a bare-type-parameter or concrete function slot whose invocations are classified as lends: #1802 excludes calls-mint substitutions from bare-parameter slots (per-instantiation) and #1820 fences the retyping/formation and lambda-body routes (a property reference over a `Task`-family property is a formation error; a minting lambda under a concrete slot is rejected; return-slot bridging respects the family boundary of §27.1.1). A static iterable factory's calls also mint (the iterator convention), so the same fence bars bridging its reference to a non-iterable slot; the one route that stays open — a bare-parameter or retyped slot at an iterable substitution — is the residual #1787 inherits. And #1787 completes the iterable analogue this asymmetry deliberately left for later: instance and extension iterable-returning callees announce the fresh-frame-vs-stored-view distinction with the `owned` marker (the static face needs none — it has no stored view to disambiguate), and the owning heads take no unmarked instance spelling at all (the #1660 declaration-site reject, preserved). Copying calls-mint to iterables was declined again; the marker is the information-bearing bit here, exactly as it was *not* for tasks. Post-substitution keying and the capture-set-transitive-source alternative were both re-declined (wrong axis — the channel is container ownership, not carried sources).

**The residual closes: the iterable mirror (v0.256, [#3246](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3246); ruled under the owner's 2026-08-18 delegation).** The one route v0.223 left open — a static iterable factory's formed value reaching a lend-classified slot through a bare-parameter substitution or a retyped intermediate — was self-admitted in the normative text as "a leak until that issue classifies or fences it", the closure options pre-enumerated: classify the slot, fence the substitution, or extend the family. The 2026-08-18 back-of-book elegance review (owner-commissioned) surfaced it as its highest-value finding, and the ruling **fences the substitution**: the calls-mint exclusion is mirrored onto the iterable-interface family at bare-parameter function slots (spec §28.3), and iterable-interface returns gain the same announcement-bearing assignability boundary `Task`-family returns carry (spec §27.1.1 — covariance within the family only), closing the retyping route. Classifying the slot is impossible in either direction — a mint classification double-disposes stored views, a lend classification leaks minted frames; that impossibility is why A2 exists — and the ruling does *not* extend the family: A2 is untouched, iterable calls still classify under the ordinary rules, and the fence is admissibility, never classification — the "not later fixed by copying this ruling blindly" caution above, honored. The honest cost is the `Task` fence's conservatism, one step wider: iterable mints are callee-kind-keyed where task mints are family-keyed, and a function slot erases the callee kind, so the fence rejects the sound instance-callee inhabitants along with the minting factories, with the identical named workarounds (a family-shaped slot, a concrete function type, statement-form consumption). Test-side cost was verified near zero at the ruling (no test exercises the leak route as accepted behavior; the #1802/#1820 fence corpus exists). Whether the A2 triple-posture for frame-producing returns should be revisited wholesale — the structural unification retiring the split — had already been accepted as proposal 0096 when this fence landed; the unification graduated one revision later (v0.258, the reversal record below) and subsumed the fence. The fence was the minimal soundness closure and was right under either outcome, which is exactly why it landed first.

The rule is the §30.11 precedent made general. v0.184 had already decided, for one type, that a `TaskCompletionSource` owns its task and consumers reach it through the `.Task` **property** ([§12.7](#sec-memory-model-edges) above; spec [§30.11](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-taskcompletionsourcet-and-ownedtaskcompletionsourcet)) — the source owns, storage lends. v0.190 draws that line everywhere, callee side included: an unmarked `Task`-family return type is itself container-ownership-transferring, so a non-`async` body must supply an owned task on every return path, and a *stored* task is re-exposed through a property, field, or collection slot — never through a non-`owned` method return. Costs, recorded: the stored-task *method* accessor became inexpressible (the storage-accessor shape belongs to properties — the same property-face resolution as the static idioms of §13.6); and the pre-existing `owned Task` markers on `Domain.Spawn` and the `Parallel` combinators became emphasis rather than contrast — recorded deliberately against the v0.186 note that the two spellings were "deliberate, not to be silently harmonized": this is the non-silent harmonization that note demanded.

**The reversal: calls mint for the iterable family too, and A2 retires (v0.258, proposal 0096; ruled under the owner's 2026-08-18 delegation).** The v0.190 decline above — "unsound for iterables, where the stored-collection view idiom is legitimate and common" — was a **migration-cost** argument about one idiom, not a soundness argument, and its own closing sentence set the reopening bar: the iterable analogue was "not later 'fixed' by copying this ruling blindly". What reopened it is not a better argument about `Items()`; it is the **fence-accretion ledger** that warning implicitly demanded a future reviser produce — the itemized record of what the split cost after v0.190: the three v0.223 fence rulings (#1787/#1802/#1820), §27.1.1's announcement-bearing family boundary, §27.6's formation bar with its normatively-recorded open question, two self-admitted leak residuals ("a leak until that issue classifies or fences it"), and #3246 as the **fourth fence for one seam** — at v0.256 roughly 1,900 words of new fence text across five carriers, every entry announcement-coherence machinery whose whole content is *"this family announces, that one does not"*, none of it needed by the `Task` family, which mints. The v0.190 record weighed the stored-view migration; it could not have weighed the fences, because they did not exist yet. **The closure record's central sentence is confronted, not deleted:** "classifying the slot is impossible in either direction — a mint classification double-disposes stored views, a lend classification leaks minted frames" was true, but the impossibility is **conditional on the stored-view spelling**: a mint classification double-disposes only while a lend-face *method* inhabitant exists, and the property respell removes that inhabitant — `Items(): IEnumerable<Item> => _items;` becomes `public property Items: IEnumerable<Item> => _items;`, the identical remedy v0.190 applied to the stored task, at the cost v0.190's record already called acceptable. After the respell every iterable-returning *method* face mints, every stored view lives on the accessor face whose lend-only guarantee is axiom A4's, and the classification §12.10 recorded as impossible is simply the rule; A2's row is a register tombstone. The unification also retires the v0.223 owning-head declaration-site rejection and its `Drain()`/`ReceiveAll()` §29.11 carve — the carve's ground never fit its occupant (`Drain()` is no deferred LINQ operator; §28.3 filed it under two different grounds two paragraphs apart; §29.11's closed consumption list could not even parse the spec's own `return _items.Drain();`; and the prescribed `owned`-return remedy was unsound at the one member the carve protected, §14.4's `OwningCache` returning the receiver's owned field) — the two members becoming ordinary minting members, at one real, stated cost: `Drain()` must return a genuinely fresh drain-view frame rather than the receiver at the owning static type (stdlib §4.5's receiver-identity optimization retired — the `for` desugaring's `using var __source` disposes the minted frame at loop end, and the frame must not *be* the collection). The v0.256 fence and its conservatism — which had reached the standard library's own query surface, rejecting six §5 members at iterable-head substitutions — are subsumed and refunded: the mint-laundering inhabitants are rejected inhabitant-side (§27.1.1/§27.6 read against declared, pre-substitution slots; a minting lambda at its return path), and the sequence-of-sequences projection over the property face is legal again. Re-declined, each with its ground: the **marker** (v0.190's own declined alternative transplanted — it would churn every static iterable declaration and stdlib operator to buy an announcement the declared type already carries, and reinstate the leak wherever omitted); the **narrow owning-head repair** (it needed a drain-through-borrow predicate v0.223 correctly refused to invent); and **stopping at the fence** (the option the ledger indicts — a fifth fence would have followed the fourth). Kernel accounting: the unified rule is a pure K6.2 + K6.3 derivation; no axiom replaces A2.

**An unsettled task's disposal attaches the frame's discharge; a faulted task retains the task whose fault it carries (v0.334, proposal 0168).** Two roots of the disposal matrix's first run shared one gap at the task's teardown. Spec §30.1 let a frame run on after its task was disposed, but only `OwningTask<T>` had a stated discharger for what the frame delivered afterwards — §30.5's result-disposing continuation; a `Task<C>` disposed under the named `using var` drop that §30.10 itself names as legal left its kind-1 result, its late-chained source and its fault instance with a teardown that had already run ([#5788](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5788)). And §30.9 made a faulted task "own its fault exception" while deferring an awaited operand's disposal "until the exception's handling completes" — an event that never comes inside a frame whose body lets the instance escape, so `await Inner()` over a faulting `Inner` had two owners of one instance and no disposal moment for the temporary ([#5787](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5787)). The decision takes the two mechanisms the text already had and makes each the rule: every `Task`-family disposal before settlement attaches `OwningTask<T>`'s continuation, which performs at the terminal state every disposal the teardown would have performed; and a task faulted by an instance another task owns retains that task, as the owning `WhenAll` aggregate retains its first non-successful input, the capture ending the frame's handling. Declined: forbidding disposal of an unsettled task (it rejects the unsupervised drop: seven rule-corpus cases, three Guide 04 examples and about fifteen test programs); stating the discharge frame-side (two statements of one fact, and nothing for a late chain's source); moving ownership to the faulting task (two awaiters of `Inner` would lose their instance); re-raising a fresh cause-chained instance (exception identity would change across an `await`).

<a id="sec-determinism-stated-hashes-assigned"></a>
### 12.11 Determinism is stated, and identity hashes are assigned (v0.204)

Spec §30.13's *Determinism* subsection adds no rule — every clause of it was already a consequence of serial-per-domain execution, the five memory-model edges, sendability, and the no-object-address invariant. It is stated anyway, for two reasons. First, as an audit obligation on the implementation: once "a domain's execution is a deterministic function of what its edges deliver" is normative, any within-domain nondeterminism given fixed edges — an uninitialized read, an unpinned iteration order, an address leak into an observable value — is a runtime *bug by definition*, not implementation latitude; the paragraph converts a diffuse design intention into a checkable property.
