Second, as the foundation record/replay tooling needs: a recorded execution is replayable exactly because everything a domain observes arrives through an enumerable set of runtime-mediated edges (proposal 0045 is the tooling design; the spec paragraph is what makes it *possible* rather than heroic). The exclusions are exactly two — elapsed real time, and foreign memory beyond declared writebacks — because those are the two places the runtime genuinely does not mediate.

Two supporting rulings landed with it. **Identity hashes are assigned, not derived** (spec §8.3): the .NET lazy-syncblock design with the entropy source replaced by a per-domain deterministic sequence. Without it the determinism statement is false for any program that hashes a class by identity — an address-derived hash is a per-run accident — and the address itself would leak into observable values, which §32.12's no-object-address invariant exists to prevent. Objects never hashed pay nothing; the storage is the runtime's per-object metadata word or a side table (implementation detail); programs are promised only within-run stability and address-independence, so no existing program's meaning changes. **`OwningDictionary<K, V>` iteration is deterministic-given-history, not order-pinned** (stdlib §4.9): pinning insertion order outright would force order metadata into the drain-and-rebuild representation whose economics §4.9 already chose; determinism given the operation-and-hash history is everything replay needs and is what the reference implementation already does. The borrowing containers (`Dictionary`, `Set`) were already insertion-order-pinned, so no frame-by-frame journaling of container iteration is ever needed.


<a id="sec-choosing-the-concurrency-shape"></a>
### 12.12 Choosing the concurrency shape: structured by default (v0.211)

The mechanisms are settled individually — `TaskScope` (stdlib §6.10), `Task.WhenAll` (borrowing and owning overloads, §6.7), `WhenAny`, `Task.FireAndForget`, bare task binds, and `Domain.Spawn` (§30.14) — but the map between them was reconstructible only from scattered passages. The doctrine, in one place:

**Structured is the default.** Spec §30.10 states the posture: concurrent fan-out reaches for the nursery — `await using var scope := new TaskScope();` — and the two discard idioms are the named escape hatches, not peers. The nursery's guarantee is lifetime-shaped: children cannot outlive the scope, and the scope's disposal is the join.

**The fault fork is the real chooser.** `WhenAll` *collects*: it awaits every task and re-raises the first non-success — it never cancels or abandons the laggards (§30.10's aggregation contract). `TaskScope` *supervises*: a child's fault cancels the siblings through the scope token, and `Join` surfaces the failure after quiescence. So: results-of-independent-work → `WhenAll` (all work is wanted regardless of failures); a unit of work whose parts stand or fall together → `TaskScope`. `WhenAny` is a selector, not a supervisor — the losers keep running and remain the caller's to settle. `FireAndForget` is the recorded abandon-with-supervision spelling for work whose result nobody will read (§17.13 routes the `Task` discard here precisely so abandonment is always a visible decision). `Domain.Spawn` is not a concurrency combinator at all — it is an isolation boundary (§12.5), chosen for state separation, with sendability doing the checking.

*v0.328 addendum (proposal 0145).* The nursery's `await using`-only rule was stated by binding form, and a binding form is checkable only at the static type `TaskScope`: a scope reaching any other owning position — an `owned` field or parameter, an owning element, a `Shared<T>` payload, an owning position of `Object` or an interface through an upcast — discharged on §28.12's implicit synchronous path and skipped the drain with children running, voiding the quiescence §28.14 states normatively for the type. The rule is now stated by holder: a `TaskScope` is minted only as an `await using var` initializer at its own type and owned by that binding alone, with borrows free — no implicit discharge runs on a borrow and §28.6 rejects `Dispose()` through one, so no borrow reaches a synchronous disposal path. *A bare conversion ban* (`TaskScope` admitting no §5.1 reference conversion) was declined: it reaches only the upcast face, leaves the own-type holders open, and rejects harmless borrows such as a `Helper(d: IAsyncDisposable)` call. *A per-instantiation check at type arguments* (admitting `List<TaskScope>`, rejecting only owning substitutions) was declined: it would re-run the holder rule through every generic body after substitution, including a new reject-at-substitution clause for `owned T` parameters and returns, which §28.1 erases rather than rejects at substitutions it does not admit, and a check on `new T()`, which `where T: new()` admits at `TaskScope`; the whole-argument ban is §14.2's existing shape (a dispatched generic method admits no move-only value type as a type argument, explicit or inferred), and no borrowing use of a `TaskScope` type argument exists to admit. *The degraded synchronous destructor* (cancel plus detached drain, the #1422 shape) stays where stdlib §6.10 left it — the fallback only if the static rule proves too restrictive, which the holder rule, with zero rejected sites measured across the tests, the documents and the rule corpus, did not.

Why this is a rationale entry rather than new normative text: every sentence above restates a rule the spec already carries; what was missing was the adjacency. (The streaming as-completed combinator is `Task.WhenEach` — stdlib §6.7, graduated from proposal 0023 at v0.212: it *reports* completions in order without supervising or settling anything, the observer complement to `TaskScope`.)

<a id="sec-statics-facts-vocabulary"></a>
### 12.13 The statics-and-sharing taxonomy: facts, not lists (v0.245)

Recorded v0.245 (proposal 0087, [#3053](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3053); the 2026-08-11 elegance review, finding 6). Through v0.244, three curated type lists governed statics and cross-domain sharing — spec §22.12 rule 2's form-(i) set, rule 4's suppression list, and §28.13's `Shared`-payload set — maintained in parallel and related by prose set arithmetic ("this set is form-(i) *minus* … *minus* … *plus* the three members"). Each *membership* was a recorded ruling; the three-list structure itself was defended nowhere, and a normative document stating set differences between its own allowlists is a taxonomy that has outgrown its presentation. v0.245 replaces the presentation: four orthogonal per-type facts — **synchronized**, **immutable**, **self-distributing**, **displacement-unbounded** — declared once at each stdlib type (stdlib §1), from which all three sets are derived by one-line formulas; the "grown only by recorded ruling" discipline attaches to the facts, and the lists become computed views. The reproduction was proved exact before landing — all thirty memberships (ten types × three sets) match the prior text — so the change is doc-structural with zero behavior delta. The review's conjecture that *three* facts would suffice was refuted by construction: `Locked<T>`/`RwLocked<T>` are internally synchronized yet excluded from mutable statics for a recorded fourth reason (rule 2's overwrite-never-disposes semantics would strand a payload-scaled displaced value), so the fourth fact is what keeps the derivation exact — without it the data-owning locks would silently join form (i), a behavior change. Declined alternatives: the status quo (checker taxonomy leaking into the user's mental model — one `static` field could meet a hard error, two co-firing warnings, or silence depending on which of three lists its type sat in, with nothing at the type saying which); deriving only §28.13 (most of the cost, half the benefit); a user-declarable trait (the facts are compiler-known *trust grants* about specific implementations — the axiom-A10 family's shape — not self-certifiable structural properties; opening them to user types is a real language design, named so it is not smuggled in as notation); and keeping the lists normative with facts in tooling only (two normative statements of one truth is exactly the drift class `check_docs.sh` exists to catch — instead the facts are the single normative carrier, and the graduation added a mechanical check recomputing the spec's informative membership parentheticals from the fact lines). The governance clause transformed rather than survived verbatim: one fact ruling may now flow into two sets — that is the feature — so the record requirement moved with it (a fact ruling states which facts it grants and confronts each derived-set consequence), and the old vice-versa protection is carried structurally by the two facts that ever made the sets diverge.

**v0.323 addendum: sendability takes the same home** (proposal 0136 S2-8, owner-accepted). Spec §30.14 had kept a per-type prose list of the compiler-known types' sendability beside the stdlib's own statements, the same two-carrier shape this section retired for the statics sets. The per-type classifications now live only in each type's stdlib **Sendability** statement (stdlib §1), and §30.14 states the four forms a statement takes. As for the statics sets, the move reproduces the prior classifications with no verdict change. Where the two documents disagreed — `OwningBuffer<T>` at a value element type, which stdlib §4 called sendable against §30.14's "a sendable class type", the classification the stdlib itself named as normative — the stdlib statement carries §30.14's verdict, not sendable, and whether it should be sendable is left to a filed question. Sendability did not become a fifth fact: it is not a boolean property of the type (three of the forms are conditional on an element or payload type), and it is independent of the four facts (`CancellationTokenSource` is synchronized and not sendable; `Channel<T>` declares no fact and is sendable), so no formula over the facts reproduces it. Declined: keeping §30.14's list normative with the stdlib statements pointing to it (two normative statements of one truth, the drift class this section names), and a fifth fact (it would need a conditional fact, and would misstate `Channel<T>`'s recorded fact-less line).

**v0.324 addendum: the unstated default** ([#5593](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5593)). A compiler-known class type whose section states no sendability is not sendable (stdlib §1). This is the facts' own closure ("a type declaring no facts is in none of the derived sets") applied to sendability. A statement is a claim about a type's internal structure, which only the type's section can make, so a type nobody has classified does not cross. Declined: reading spec §30.14's transfer-closed rules over a compiler-known class. Those rules read instance fields, and the stdlib is not normative for a type's internal data structures, so the reading would classify by text that is not the contract: a reference implementation's fields where a section shows one, and nothing where it does not. Today it would decide one type only, `Type`, the one unstated `sealed` library class, and it would make `Type` sendable because no fields are shown, not because anyone had checked its state. Whether `Type` should be sendable is a per-type question for its own section. Also declined: a sendable default, which would let a type cross domains before anyone had checked its structure. A type whose structure argues for sending is given a statement in its own section (`Counter<K>` at sendable-value or `string` keys, the first).

**v0.325 addendum: three types held at the default** ([#5606](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5606)). `StringBuilder`, `Random` and `Stopwatch` carry no sendability statement and are not sendable (stdlib §1). Their structure argues for sending, since each holds only copy state, but two things stand between that structure and a statement. First, no **sendable** form of spec §30.14 names the ground: the form's grounds are internal synchronization, deep immutability and a channel endpoint, and none fits a mutable, unsynchronized holder of copy state. A statement would therefore first add a ground to the forms, which changes a settled rule and widens what may cross; that is a design change, the same shape as the question whether `OwningBuffer<T>` at a value element type should be sendable ([#5578](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5578)). Second, the three listings do not declare the classes `sealed`. §12.6 makes a sendable declared class `sealed` so that an owned value of a sendable static type cannot hold a subclass instance that carries origin-domain borrows. Stdlib §1 keeps the transfer-closed rules, rule 1 included, off compiler-known classes, and no rule bars user code from deriving from a library class whose section does not declare it extensible. The library classes already stated sendable share that seam (`Array<T>`, `Mutex`, `Channel<T>`, `Counter<K>`); it is one question for all of them ([#5613](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5613)), and stating three more open-listed classes sendable while it is open would widen it. Deferred: a statement for the three on a copy-state ground ([#5614](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5614)), until the seam is ruled. `Type` is listed `sealed`, so the seam does not reach it, but `owned Type` is inadmissible (spec §28.9), so `Type` cannot cross as the owned transfer every sendable form names; whether it crosses as a shared reference, as `string` does, is a separate question ([#5615](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5615)).

**v0.327 addendum: the copy-state ground, `OwningBuffer<T>` at value elements, `Type`, and the comparer-held clause** ([proposal 0138](proposals/0138-compiler-known-sendability.md); [#5614](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5614), [#5578](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5578), [#5615](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5615), [#5604](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5604)). With the subclass seam closed (§12.6, *v0.327 addendum*), the deferral above is lifted. The **sendable** form gains a fourth ground, *a holder of copy state only*: every member reads and writes the state by value, retains no borrow past its call, and exposes no address of the storage — the issue's wording, kept whole. It is stated by member behaviour, not by storage layout, because stdlib §1 is not normative for internal data structures, and it is what already makes `Array<char>` sendable under the conditional form. `StringBuilder`, `Random` and `Stopwatch` take statements on it. `OwningBuffer<T>` at a sendable value element is the same shape — wholly owned copy state that crosses by move, as `Array<double>` already does — and becomes sendable; the one thing it has that the three do not, an interior address from `DangerousPointer()`, is an FFI-band value that no borrow tracks whether or not the buffer moves, so spec §32.10's cross-domain bullet now covers an address of Bezel-owned storage whose owner has crossed, beside the crossed `Ptr<T>` it already covered. `OwningBuffer<T>` is classified by the conditional form, not by the ground, so the ground keeps its no-address condition and no statement made on it ever reaches §32.10. Declined: dropping that condition from the ground (it would let a future copy-state statement export an untracked address with only §32.10's trust behind it, for no type that needs it); keeping `OwningBuffer<T>` at value elements not sendable (the stdlib called it sendable through v0.322, and no structural ground distinguishes it from `Array<double>`). **`Type`** is sendable, crossing as a shared reference exactly as `string` does: it is the one §28.9 runtime-managed type the `string` bullet of §30.14 did not name, its one interned instance is immutable and already reachable from every domain through `typeof`, and `owned Type` is inadmissible, which is why no statement form of the compiler-known classes could carry it — spec §30.14 classifies it directly, as it classifies `string` and the value types, and stdlib §2.13 restates the verdict; the borrowing collections' element condition reads `sendable-value, string or Type` wherever it read `sendable-value or string`. Declined: keeping `Type` not sendable (a domain re-deriving a type token with `typeof` is a workaround for a value that is already shared), and a fifth statement form for shared-reference crossing (the forms classify the stdlib's own classes; the runtime-managed types are the spec's). Not widened: `FrozenArray<T>`'s element restriction, which is its own and has no use at `Type`. **The comparer.** A hash collection built with an `IEqualityComparer<K>` retains a borrow of it (rationale §17.14: injection relocates the container's identity whole), and its sendability statement classifies the type however it was constructed, so a comparer-built `Dictionary<string, int>` could carry an origin-domain borrow across. The statement stays, and the construction carries spec §28.3's domain clause as an individually recorded trust clause, the comparer-held clause (K4.3's *recorded trust, or nothing*), named by the trust-admission report at the constructor call. Declined: a statement that holds only for the comparerless constructors (construction is not in the type, so it is not a type-level rule); requiring a capture-free or stateless comparer (a stateless comparer is still an object its origin domain disposes, so the cross-domain borrow dangles whatever its state). Deferred: a comparer type parameter (`Dictionary<K, V, C>`, or a constructor constrained `where C: IEqualityComparer<K>, sendable`) as a static bound — an API redesign across four types, taken only when a program needs a comparer-built collection to cross; an `owned IEqualityComparer<K>` parameter is not that bound, since an interface-typed field is never transfer-compatible.

**v0.328 addendum: `Type` carries the immutable and self-distributing facts** (0138 §13's filed question; delegated ruling 2026-10-08, changelog v0.328). The facts, not a listing beside `string` in spec §22.12 rule 3, because the fact line is the one carrier of a library class's statics treatment and a facts ruling has to confront each derived set, which a rule 3 listing would skip while rule 4 still read `Type` as "class type". **Immutable** is the ground the sendability statement already rests on (one interned, never-written instance per type); **self-distributing** records that `typeof` is the type's own distribution channel, which keeps it out of §28.13's `Shared`-payload set — where it could not go in any case, `owned Type` being inadmissible. **Displacement-unbounded** is not granted, and **synchronized** is not claimed: a mutable `static` of `Type` stays rejected by rule 2's closing sentence, as every class type outside form (i) is. It is not given an admitted form of its own. Such a form would be sound — a pointer-word store of a never-reclaimed instance has none of §12.7's grounds against it, neither the reclamation race nor the leak nor a torn read — but no program needs one: `typeof(X)` is written wherever the slot would be read, a `static readonly` `Type` is now warning-free, and a slot that must be rebound after initialization is a field of a class held in the `static owned readonly` `Locked<T>` registry idiom (rule 4(a)). A new mutable form would need store semantics of its own under rule 2 for a slot no program has asked for; it is declined (`DECLINED.md`), reopenable by a program none of those spellings serves. The one verdict that moves is rule 4's: a `static readonly` `Type` field or auto-property is warning-free, where it took the class-type row's warning. Zero such declarations exist in the tree, and `typeof(X)` can be written wherever such a static would be read, so the ruling is small; it is recorded because the derived sets are machine-checked and an unrecorded member is a gate failure, not a judgement call.

<a id="sec-recovery-unit"></a>
### 12.14 The recovery unit: the error kernel, and process supervision (v0.263)

Recorded v0.263 from proposal 0106 §3.3/§7.5.3. Bezel has strong fault **containment** and, by design, no fault **recovery** machinery; this entry states what the language is therefore promising and what it is not, because the absence had never been written down and a reader could only infer it from the shape of what exists.

**The error-kernel doctrine.** Push fallible work outward; keep irreplaceable state in a unit that must not fail. The sendable rules already push programs this way — a domain's state is unreachable from outside it (§12.5–§12.6), an entry is capture-free (spec §30.14), and everything crossing is moved or immutable — so the doctrine names a shape the rules already enforce rather than adding a mechanism. Two consequences worth stating: a unit holding state that cannot be rebuilt should do as little fallible work as possible, and the fallible work belongs in a unit whose loss costs only its own progress. Read against §12.12's map, the actor idiom's serialized domain is a placement decision, not a default: state entangled enough to want serializing is often exactly the state whose loss is unrecoverable, and putting the risky work in the same unit is the arrangement the doctrine warns about.

**The recovery-unit fork, and where Bezel stands.** Recovery admits two answers, and a language must say which it is building. **(1) In-process supervision** — a supervisor owns child specifications, observes each child's exit, and restarts from the specification, as OTP does. Bezel has the hard precondition for it already and for free (spec §30.14's capture-free entry rule means a restarted child provably inherits nothing from the dead one), and lacks every other part: nothing multiplexes child exits, no restart policy vocabulary exists, and a supervisor parameterised over a *stored* entry value is not writable in user Bezel, the entry being a call-site syntactic category (the precedent is stated for `Parallel`, stdlib §6.12). **(2) The OS process as the recovery unit** — the supervisor is outside the program, and the program's job is to fail fast, cleanly, and legibly. **Bezel's posture is (2), and the fail-fast band is built for it**: allocation failure and stack exhaustion are fatal and uncatchable (spec §31.4), an uncaught exception exits with code 70 after printing the exception (spec §33.3), and the spec already tells programs whose correctness depends on shutdown effects to arrange an external mechanism — *"a separate process holding the lock with a TTL, a write-ahead log, a watchdog"* (spec §22.13). A Bezel service is expected to be run under something that restarts it.

**What this settles and what it leaves open.** It settles the claim: the language does not promise in-process restart, and a program that needs a component rebuilt after a fault gets that from the process supervisor, not from a language mechanism. It does not decline (1) — a supervisor built on stdlib pieces stays a legitimate future proposal, and its cost drops once the pieces behind it exist (a multiplexable completion set; a cross-domain stop signal, which the v0.263 `Shared<CancellationTokenSource>` ruling supplies). What the posture changes is the target: with process supervision named as the recovery unit, an in-process supervisor is asked to cover only what an OS supervisor covers badly — sub-process-granularity restarts of work whose loss the process should survive — rather than to reproduce OTP. Forcible teardown is not one of the missing pieces; it is declined at [§12.4](#sec-cooperative-cancellation).

<a id="sec-statics-verdict-grounds"></a>
### 12.15 Static members: the grounds behind §22.12's verdicts (v0.291)

Recorded v0.291 (proposal 0122 L14, [#3845](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/3845)). Spec §22.12 states a static field or auto-property's verdict — *ok*, *warn*, or *error*, by mutability against the declared type's kind — in two tables, with the admitted mutable forms (i)–(iii) and the bare-generic-parameter rejection (iv), the store-semantics triple, and rule 4's notes (a)–(i) beneath them. The *grounds* for those verdicts are recorded here rather than inline in the section, keyed by the section's own labels, so that the section states what each rule is and this record states why. Three neighbouring records already carry part of the story and are cited, not repeated: [§12.7](#sec-memory-model-edges)'s *Warned value statics (v0.221, #1393)* paragraph (why forms (iii) and (iv) are admitted warned rather than rejected, why `string` and record statics were declined, and why neither warned form takes a memory-model publication edge), [§12.13](#sec-statics-facts-vocabulary) (why membership in the form-(i) set and the warning-suppression set is derived from per-type facts rather than curated), and [§12.8](#sec-locked-rwlocked) (why the data-owning locks and `FrozenArray<T>` sit in rule 4's set and not in form (i)). Nothing here is normative: the verdict is the spec's, and a ground that stops holding is reopened by ruling, not by re-reading this record.

**Form (i) excludes `Channel<T>`.** The channel object is the *receiving* end (spec §30.15), single-consumer by ownership, and a process-global receiving end would readmit the multi-consumer receive that stdlib §6.9 explicitly defers. The spelling the spec steers to — a domain-confined `Channel<T>` with a `ChannelSender<T>` static published in its place — keeps the single consumer where the type puts it.

**Form (ii) excludes `OwningTask<T>?`.** The admitted shape is §22.13 pattern (b)'s `null`-initialized, once-set cache of an asynchronous computation; `OwningTask<T>?` is single-await (spec §30.5), useless as a shared cache, and a displaced owned task would strand its result.

**Form (iii) is warned, not rejected, and its category is closed.** Why the form is warned rather than rejected is [§12.7](#sec-memory-model-edges)'s *Warned value statics* paragraph: the category is the violation-tolerant one — no reference content, so ignoring the warning risks stale or torn values and never memory unsafety, a strictly milder violation class than rule 4's undefined behavior. The multi-word forms that paragraph names without enumerating are `decimal`, the nullable forms' flag-and-value pairs, and per-slot mixes of an inline array's elements. The category — primitives, enums, and plain heap-free inline arrays — grows only by recorded ruling, and the shapes outside it were **declined, not deferred**: mutable statics of `string` (and `string?`), record, choice, and compiler-known value-builtin types (`DateTime`, `Guid`, …), plain inline arrays of any other element type, and inline-array-typed auto-properties. The `string` and record grounds — the overwrite dilemma between a reclamation race and an unbounded leak, and the torn read that fabricates an invariant-violating value — and the stdlib's synchronized spellings as the remedies are §12.7's too; the citations behind them are the **displacement-unbounded** fact (stdlib §1) for the leak's payload-scaled cost, spec §22.16 for the record invariant, and stdlib §6.4 (record-typed atomics want a `Mutex`; today, `Locked<T>` over a sealed cell class, spec §30.12) for the remedies. A plain inline array of any other element type takes the same grounds component-wise: a `string`- or record-element array imports its element category's dilemma, and a class- or interface-element plain array carries *borrows* (spec §15.1.2) whose cross-domain dangling is memory-unsafe.

**The mutable owning-inline-array static is retired (v0.334, [proposal 0162](proposals/0162-explicit-disposal-marks-the-holder-frees.md), [#5785](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5785), [#5782](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5782)).** A mutable static field of owning-inline-array type is rejected under rule 2's closing sentence, with every other mutable static that would hold an owned referent. Through v0.333 it was admitted warned as form (iv), its slot stores, `move arr[j]` and per-slot restoration "following §15.1.1 unchanged" — which K9.4's ground (static state is observable across method boundaries, so no per-method window can cover it) rejects for a slot exactly as for a whole field, a tension four lanes of the disposal matrix found independently; a slot store into it had no stated verdict between §15.1.1's per-slot drop and §22.13's static no-drop posture; and a destructor run by the exit pass could store into a slot the pass had already disposed, leaving an object no drain re-enrolled and no flag faulted. Its only uses were the form's own tests (two gtest programs, two lit programs, three rule cases). The `static readonly` form, note (h), stays as the one static owning inline array: disposed at exit, written once, its slots fixed — restoration is per-slot assignment ([§12.7](#sec-memory-model-edges)'s ground for why the retired form had to be mutable), so a `readonly` array admits no slot store and therefore no `move`. A replaceable process-global pool is a `static owned readonly OwningArray<T>`, whose indexer store disposes the displaced element (spec §28.11), or the `Locked<T>` registry §22.12 names. Declined: keeping the form with `move arr[j]` barred (closes neither the slot-store nor the re-population question for the same reach); keeping it with a stated per-slot drop and an exit drain over re-populated slots (a third overwrite regime and a second drain rule for a form with no use outside its tests); and retiring note (h) with it (it holds only what it was initialized with, and is used).

**Overwrite never disposes.** A racing first use may still hold and await the displaced task, so overwriting an admitted mutable static cannot take spec §28.7's drop-on-overwrite; the displaced referent stays alive until process exit instead — an accepted bounded cost of the same kind as spec §22.13's ownership-discharge exemption, bounded because displacement can occur only during first-use races. The same ground holds at the owning-inline-array form: a racing use in another domain may still hold a displaced slot's value, so statics never take the per-slot drop-on-overwrite that owning-inline-array locals and instance fields take.

**Rule 4(a): the displacement-unbounded four are not mutable statics.** Rule 2's overwrite-never-disposes store semantics would strand a displaced value of unbounded payload until process exit — the payload-scaled cost each type's fact line names, and the cost [§12.7](#sec-memory-model-edges) records as declined for the v0.186 suppression-list types. §12.8 records the recast that placed the locks and `FrozenArray<T>` in rule 4's set rather than form (i).

**Rule 4(e): a function-typed static warns at form (iii)'s hazard class, and does not discriminate captures.** The captured-variable cell is reachable and writable from every domain through the field, with no other declaration at which to name the obligation. The hazard class is form (iii)'s rather than rule 4's because a capture whose source is a local scope cannot reach a static at all — spec §28.9's and §28.3's borrow-escape rules reject a function value that outlives a captured borrow's source — so only runtime-managed and value captures survive there, and what a violated obligation risks is therefore a wrong or torn value, never memory unsafety. Capture-free function values are not distinguished from capturing ones because the initializer's value may come from a call whose captures the declaration cannot see, and no rule of the specification consults another declaration's body to check a declaration.

**Rule 4(g): an owning-classified choice is rejected outright.** An `owned` payload is process-lived with no disposal site even when every borrow-carrying component is `null`, which is why note (f)'s null-source admission cannot reach it; admitted, the form would be a process-lived owned value with no disposal site and no diagnostic, since the program-exit pass enrols only `static owned` fields and owning-inline-array-typed static fields and the `owned` field modifier is inadmissible on a choice-typed field. Rejecting the `readonly` form makes it agree with rule 2's closing sentence, which already rejects the mutable one.

**Rule 5: the guarantees are carried by the type.** The capture-free admission at every subscribe position declared on `ISharedEvent<T>`/`SharedEventSource<T>` is the enforceable statement of a guarantee whose member-kinded form was unenforceable through an `IEvent<T>` alias — resolved by construction; [§13.6](#sec-retiring-the-event-member-kind) records the retirement that made the type the carrier.

**`static owned` storage is disposed only at exit (v0.334, [proposal 0162](proposals/0162-explicit-disposal-marks-the-holder-frees.md), [#5760](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5760)).** Through v0.333 spec §28.6 listed a `static owned` field read by its declaring type among the owned receivers of an explicit `Dispose()`, by the same declaring-type privacy symmetry that admits an instance field — a consistency argument, not a use case. Every other static rule assumed the opposite: K2 classifies static storage as the one always-live source, disposed only at exit; the producers' static rows and §28.3's *Static stores* bullet admit static-storage-sourced borrows into any field or static with no residual because "the always-live holder holds only what is always live" (the v0.331 record below); K9.4 bars `move` of the same field because static state is observable across method boundaries. An early disposal falsified all three, and across domains the flag is no backstop (K4.3's third clause), so the borrows stored under the always-live claim carried no clause — while rule 4 named "the checked-disposal backstop of §28.2" for exactly that race, and for the warning-suppression set the program was warning-free. v0.334 removes the receiver: static owned storage admits no `move`, no slot store and no explicit disposal, K9.4's ground stated once for all three. Measured reach: zero explicit disposals of a `static owned` name in every corpus. Early shutdown of a global is spelled by a `Close()`/`Flush()` method on its type, a `Locked<T>` registry whose guarded value is replaced, or a `Shared<T>` handle. Declined: keeping the receiver and recording the race (a trust clause needs a backstop, and across domains there is none); admitting it per type for types with a defined cross-domain post-disposal fault (a per-type carve for zero sites; §30.12's detection covers live guards, not a later acquisition).

**A borrowed Pattern B operand is spent, not disposed; no static is declared at `OwningTask<T>` (v0.335, [proposal 0171](proposals/0171-borrowed-owning-task-award-spends.md), [#5834](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5834)).** Through v0.334 spec §30.5 had the await of a *borrowed* `OwningTask<T>` operand — a `using var`, a plain parameter, a field read, a slot, static storage — "still dispose the task implicitly", a K4.2 site-announced kill through a borrow that neither proposal 0162 nor 0168 revisited. It collided twice: §30.9's retention paragraph promised that a fault escaping the awaiting body stays owned by the operand task, retained by borrow and re-raised to every awaiter as "the same live instance", while the await's "terminal act, after handling completes" disposed that operand and its instance at the capture; and K9.4 and §28.6 made static owned storage disposable by the exit pass alone, while the await disposed a `static owned readonly OwningTask<T>` in place. The kill was never needed: Pattern B's single award is the **spent** state §30.4 already names, a property of the result buffer, not of the task's life. v0.335 makes the borrowed-operand award spend and dispose nothing — the holder disposes the task as it disposes any task it holds, finding no result and disposing the fault instance then — which is also what the compiler had done all along for a bound operand (it marks the task spent and leaves it to its binder). It retires the v0.190 reading of #1785 that a `using var` operand's scope-end disposal is the idempotent no-op after the await's, and its twin in §30.11's source-kind accounting, where the `OwningTaskCompletionSource<T>`'s own disposal of its task becomes the task's single disposal. The consume table's K3.5 row, the owned-operand kill, is unchanged. With the kill gone the static face has no soundness defect left, and it is narrowed anyway, on the owner's standing preference for narrowing a feature of negligible reach over carrying a rule: a `static readonly OwningTask<T>` is awarded once in the whole process, by whichever domain's await arrives first, every other awaiter raising — form (ii)'s own ground for excluding the nullable mutable form, now applied to the `readonly` column as note (j), with the bare-parameter substitution rejected at the instantiation as (g)'s is. Measured reach: no static of `OwningTask<T>` type in `tests/`, the documents or the rule corpus; no program's verdict moves. Declined: deferring the borrowed-operand await's in-place disposal to the faulted task's teardown (keeps a kill through a borrow and patches one of its two collisions); rejecting a borrowed Pattern B await in an `async` body unless every fault is handled; a compile error at an await whose operand is static owned storage; and recording the await as K9.4's one in-place disposal of static storage (single-domain under rule 4).

**Static stores admit no borrow but static storage's (v0.331, [proposal 0151](proposals/0151-static-stores-admit-only-static-sources.md), [#5676](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5676)).** Until v0.330 the property-store bullet sent a static store to the field-store bullet, whose residual admits a parameter- or receiver-sourced borrow under K4.1's backstop. That backstop is a disposed flag read by a later use in the same domain; a static is reachable from every domain, so the source's owner disposes in one domain while another reads the static, and neither the flag nor the domain clause — which names a transfer or a consume of the source, and here there is none — orders the race. The admission also contradicted the always-live claim every static read rests on: spec §28.3's *Static field access* producers row and its callee-side list's item 2 say every admissible static form keeps its referents alive to process exit, which a stored borrow falsifies. The rule is now what that claim needs — a fresh value (discharged), `null`, or static storage. Declined: the status quo; a recorded K4.3 static-held clause (a trust clause needs a backstop, and across domains there is none — K4.3's own third clause); rejecting the static-sourced borrow too (it cannot dangle: process-lived storage never disposes a displaced referent, spec §22.13); and a warning (the hazard is a cross-domain use-after-free with no deterministic fault — rule 4's violation class, not form (iii)'s). Reach: no program in `tests/`, the documents or the rule corpus stores a non-static-sourced borrow into a static.

<a id="sec-events"></a>
## 13. Events

<a id="sec-raise-instead-of-invoke"></a>
### 13.1 `Raise` instead of `?.Invoke`

The C# idiom `event?.Invoke(this, args)` carries two operators of boilerplate. The `?.` exists because an empty event is a null delegate. The `Invoke` exists because delegates need explicit invocation. Forgetting the `?.` raises `NullReferenceException` for an empty event.

Bezel uses `event.Raise(payload)` (sync) and `await event.RaiseAsync(payload)` (async). Three properties:

- **No null check.** Zero subscribers is just zero subscribers; `Raise` is a no-op. Whether the implementation backs the event with a null sentinel is invisible at the call site.
- **No `Invoke` indirection.** The verb `Raise` means "call all subscribed handlers." That replaces both `?.` and `Invoke`.
- **Symmetric with `Subscribe`.** The public face — the pattern's property — has type `IEvent<T>` (only `Subscribe`); the producer face — the private field — is `EventSource<T>` (both `Subscribe` and `Raise`). The asymmetry — outside callers can't `Raise`, inside callers can — is enforced by the static types of two declarations, not by a special access rule.

Direct invocation `event(args)` was considered. It's cleaner at the call site but conflicts with the asymmetric inside/outside types — "why is `event(args)` legal here but not there?" is harder to explain than "the type changes."

<a id="sec-payload-types-not-handler-types"></a>
### 13.2 Payload types, not handler types

v0.53 declared events with a handler function type: `event Clicked: (Button, ClickEventArgs) -> ()`. The generic parameter `TArgs` in `EventSource<TArgs>` was defined as "the handler function type." This created a type-system incoherence: `Subscribe(handler: TArgs)` was correct (handler *is* a function), but `Raise(args: TArgs)` would mean `Raise` takes a function, not the event's data. The call site worked around this with pseudo-tuple syntax that had no foundation in the type system.

v0.54 fixes this by making events name a **payload type**: `event Clicked: ClickedEvent`. The generic parameter `T` in `EventSource<T>` is the payload, and `Raise(payload: T)` is a well-typed generic method that means exactly what it says. The handler type `(T) -> ()` is derived mechanically from the payload type and the sync/async modifier.

Three options were considered:

1. **Compiler-synthesized `Raise`.** Keep the function-type declaration; the compiler generates a per-event `Raise` whose parameters mirror the handler's. This would be the first compiler-generated method not expressible in the generic type — a crack in the "no hidden magic" principle.
2. **Payload types (chosen).** Events name a payload record. `Raise(payload: T)` is a normal generic method. The handler type is derived.
3. **Tuple types.** Add language-level tuples to make the handler's parameter list a first-class type. Tuples are a large feature commitment for a single use case.

Option 2 wins because it requires no new language concepts, keeps `EventSource<T>` fully expressible as a generic class, and aligns with the existing recommendation (stdlib [§6.5](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-events-ievent-eventsource), "Payload records") that event arguments be records. The cost — one record declaration per event — is modest and produces better-documented APIs than multi-parameter handlers.

The subscribe-side trade-off is that handlers take a single payload parameter instead of multiple: `lambda(e) => e.X` instead of `lambda(btn, args) => args.X`. This could be partially recovered by lambda-parameter destructuring (`lambda(ClickedEvent(_, x, y, _)) => ...`) in a future revision, but the single-parameter form is clear enough on its own.

**No async payload that holds borrows (v0.332, proposal [0154](proposals/0154-no-async-event-payload-that-holds-borrows.md); [#5711](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5711)).** `AsyncEventSource<T>.RaiseAsync` holds its payload in the raise frame until the returned task settles — the one standard-library position with an `async` method's retention and no `async` marker (spec §30.1). v0.274 had tried to restrict the payload by deriving a rule from §30.1's conditional one and was refuted at the gate; the restriction was deferred until a rule stated it. v0.331's `Task<T>` row (proposal 0150) is that shape: a type-level rejection of a value type that holds borrows, `CancellationToken` excepted, grounded on the frame outliving the synchronous call (K2.1 + K6.1) and on spec §9.7's posture that a value type that holds borrows is stored past its bound at no position — a stipulation of regime uniformity, not a missing backstop, so the rule holds with proposal 0153's flag-checked span reads. The same rule now stands at `AsyncEventSource<T>` (stdlib §6.5). It costs `await _ev.RaiseAsync(span)` awaited in the raising scope, which is sound but which no measured program writes and which buys nothing: a handler cannot pass such a payload to an `async` callee, store it or retain it, so it works synchronously anyway, and a class payload — the buffer object, or a class holding it with an offset and a length — expresses the same program under the settlement clause with its flag. Declined: the status quo (a recorded residual, with no backstop for a span at v0.331); a flow rule at the abandonment shapes only (a new provenance class for one member's result, admitting a type no handler can use asynchronously); a K4.3 trust clause resting on the flag-checked span read (0150's declined option B at a second member, kept declined on the same posture); and the same restriction at `IAsyncEvent<T>` and `EventSource<T>`, which retain nothing. The move-only leg of the v0.274 deferral stays deferred, and retiring this rule with the other storage bans on a value that holds borrows rides proposal 0153's deferred option E.

<a id="sec-method-reference-subscriptions"></a>
### 13.3 Method-reference subscriptions

Once payload records are the convention, method-reference subscription becomes the natural idiom for handlers that have a name worth keeping:

```bezel
public method UpdateStatus(e: ClickedEvent)
  // ...
end UpdateStatus;

_clicked += UpdateStatus;           // method reference at the pattern's source field; no lambda wrapping needed
```

The method's signature `(ClickedEvent) -> ()` matches the handler type derived from the event's payload. The compiler resolves the `+=` form to `_clicked.Add(UpdateStatus)` via the desugaring described in spec [§17.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-notes-on-operators). Lambdas remain available for ad-hoc handlers; named methods are preferred when the handler has a stable identity that supports later `-=` removal.

<a id="sec-events-on-interfaces"></a>
### 13.4 Events on interfaces

v0.61 prohibited event declarations in interfaces, deferring the feature pending design work on the interaction between interface-level events and the `IEvent<T>`/`EventSource<T>` type split. v0.62 lifts the restriction.

The resolution is straightforward given the type split that already existed. From outside the declaring class, every event has type `IEvent<T>` (sync) or `IAsyncEvent<T>` (async). That's exactly what an interface event member should expose: the public, subscribe-only surface of an event. The implementing class declares the event normally; its compiler-generated `EventSource<T>` backing satisfies the interface contract because `EventSource<T>` implements `IEvent<T>`. No new mechanism is required — the existing types compose to give interface events for free.

The motivating use cases are conventional: `INotifyPropertyChanged` and analogous observable contracts, UI component abstractions where multiple concrete types share an event-bearing interface, and plugin extension points where the host subscribes to events declared by the plugin contract. Without interface events, each of these patterns required either an abstract base class (which consumes the single-inheritance slot) or an awkward `GetEventX(): IEvent<X>` method that worked around the missing language feature. The cost of supporting the feature is one new grammar production (`IfaceEvent` in Appendix A.4) and a paragraph of semantics; the benefit is that observable contracts become idiomatic.

C# has supported interface events since C# 1.0 (2002); the precedent is well-established. The deferral in v0.61 was conservative; v0.62's resolution does not require any changes to the existing event system.

*(v0.187: interface events retired with the member kind — proposal 0035, §13.6 below. The contract shape is now the get-only `IEvent<T>` property, which removes precisely the awkwardness this section recorded in the `GetEventX(): IEvent<X>` workaround: no parens, declaratively read-only, and satisfied by the very property the pattern already declares. The use cases recorded here are served unchanged.)*

<a id="sec-handler-exception-policy-first-error-wins-not-aggregate"></a>
### 13.5 Handler exception policy: first error wins, not aggregate

When a synchronous event handler throws, subsequent handlers in the subscription list are not invoked, and the exception propagates out of `Raise` to the raiser. The same rule applies to async event handlers: if one handler's task faults or is cancelled, subsequent handlers are not invoked and the exception propagates from the awaited `RaiseAsync`. This is "first error wins": the publisher sees one exception and that's the end of the dispatch.

The alternative — running every handler regardless of failures and aggregating the exceptions — was considered. C# went this route for `Task.WhenAll` (which produces an `AggregateException`) and some event-like patterns. Bezel chose first-wins for three reasons.

The first is that aggregation introduces a special exception type that callers must handle differently. `AggregateException` in C# has a complex API (`Flatten`, `InnerExceptions`, `Handle`) and exists primarily because async-with-many-tasks produces multi-failure shapes that ordinary stack-walking exceptions cannot represent. Events are a different shape: a publisher fires once, multiple handlers respond, and the publisher usually does not care about all of them — it cares that *something* failed. Promoting "something failed" into a multi-exception object asks every event consumer to know about an aggregation type.

The second is that handlers that need to run independently can opt in trivially:

```bezel
using var sub := button.Clicked.Subscribe(async lambda(e) => do
  try
    await ProcessAsync(e);
  catch
    var ex: Exception => Log(ex)        // swallow per-handler failures
  end;
end);
```

The handler catches its own exceptions, returns a successful task, and `Raise`/`RaiseAsync` continues to the next subscriber. Each handler decides whether its failure should poison the rest of the dispatch. This puts the policy where the failure semantics are known — in the handler, which knows whether its work is best-effort or critical — rather than in the publisher, which usually doesn't.

The third is that aggregation across handler boundaries can mask bugs. If a handler throws because it has a real defect (a null-deref, a state-machine violation), the publisher silently aggregating and continuing means the bug surfaces later, in a different code path, with the original stack trace already obscured by the aggregation wrapper. First-wins keeps the original exception visible at the point it was thrown.

The cost is real: a publisher that wants every handler to run regardless of failures must implement that policy itself, typically by catching inside each handler at subscribe time. That's the trade we're making — a small amount of subscriber-side ceremony for a simpler default and clearer failure semantics.

The published policy is normative (stdlib [§6.5](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-events-ievent-eventsource), "Handler exceptions" — the normative home since the member kind's retirement, v0.187). Future revisions could add an opt-in aggregating raise method (e.g. `RaiseAggregated(payload: T)`) without breaking existing code, but the default would not change.

<a id="sec-retiring-the-event-member-kind"></a>
### 13.6 Retiring the `event` member kind (v0.187, proposal 0035)

Through v0.185, `public event Clicked: ClickedEvent;` was a member kind: a one-line declaration that generated a hidden `private owned EventSource<ClickedEvent>` backing field, with the language's only access-site-dependent member-type rule (former spec §24.3 — `IEvent<T>` outside the declaring class, `EventSource<T>` inside). v0.187 removes the member kind (proposal 0035, accepted 2026-07-12; #1550). An event is now the two-declaration pattern of stdlib §6.5 — `private owned readonly _clicked: EventSource<ClickedEvent> := new();` plus `public property Clicked: IEvent<ClickedEvent> => _clicked;` — and stdlib §6.5 is the normative home of event semantics. Subscriber-side call sites are unchanged.

**Why.** Everything observable about events was already library semantics on the stdlib §6.5 types; the member kind bought one saved line and one saved name, and paid with a carve-out family: two grammar productions (`EventDecl`, `IfaceEvent`), a placement rule (classes/interfaces only), the two-faced typing rule, spec §28.14 form 4's honorary-field clause for the generated backing member, spec §22.12 rule 5's member-kind keying (whose guarantee was unenforceable through an `IEvent<T>` alias — #1647), and its own modifier table. The two-line pattern is the floor for a public event under Bezel's own commitments — declaration-fixed member types, the type fence, no runtime type tests on the producer path — so no in-language shorthand could beat it by more than the one line.

**Alternatives declined** (0035 §5.1.2/§6): expansion sugar (option (a) — retains the member kind and a minted visible name to close a one-line gap) and its bare-name variant (rejected in advance); C3 field-type inference (a new inference form); C4 fused field+property declaration (a new declaration form); C5 primary-constructor capture (wrong lifetime story); C6 public source field (loses the fence); C7 a raiser/source split for instance events (two names, no gain); C8 a `Publisher` base class (consumes the single-inheritance slot); C9 rename-only; C10 an interface-typed field with downcast raise (a runtime type test on the producer path). Deleting the `+=`/`-=` desugaring was separately declined: it would close #1618 by deletion (record-receiver method-reference equality) but churn every producer-side subscription site; the desugaring survives, re-homed to spec §17.2 and keyed on the three source types, with its `readonly` interaction stated for the first time (the desugaring is recognized before `readonly`-assignability checking — previously unstatable, since events rejected `readonly` and the generated field had no name). #1618 stays open as a real bug.

**Statics (S1).** `static event` is replaced by the sealed pair `ISharedEvent<out T>`/`SharedEventSource<T>` — internally synchronized, capture-free at every subscribe position. The capture-freedom is a stdlib-stated entry in spec §28.14's capture-free register, keyed on the resolved callee, made alias-proof by two companion rules: the pair implements neither `IEvent<T>` (an upcast would launder capture admission through `IEvent<T>.Subscribe`'s capture-admitting `scoped` signature — the severed composition is a recorded cost, bridged by a channel relay, stdlib §6.5), and method-reference formation over any capture-free-register position is rejected (the formed plain function type would erase the admission; the bar covers `Domain.Spawn` too, closing the same latent formation hole there). This is the first enforceable statement of the v0.163/v0.180 rule-5 guarantee; #1647 closes by construction. User classes cannot be cross-domain publication points in v1 (a user `Subscribe` is retention-incapable under the call-bounded regime; the lift path is §28.14's deferred unbounded tier, #1427). S2 (regressing the guarantee) and S3 (silently synchronizing instance events — reversing v0.163's recorded instance-events-unsynchronized choice) were declined. The shared async twin is deferred until demand appears; until it ships a cross-domain async publication point is not spellable (`static async event`, grammatical through v0.185 and unused in every document, has no direct replacement — the migration note says so honestly; the channel bridge is the alternative).

**Small rules that land with the retirement.** (1) A bare read of a `static` `owned` field returned through a non-`owned` return is a borrow of process-lived static storage (spec §28.3/§28.6, v0.187) — the callee-side rule the static idiom's public property needed; previously only the caller-side producers-table row existed. (v0.196 narrows the admission to exactly that property face — #1804: the static-*method* arm v0.187 admitted alongside it never had a caller-side row, so a concrete static method call returning storage was factory-classified at every call site, pairing two owners over one obligation; the arm is retired, the pattern's property face plus the caller-side `T.P` row being the complete sound surface.) (2) Spec §2.3's always-available list gains all six event types (Q7, spelling (i)) — a recorded reversal of the first draft's "declinable knob" posture: the keyword's import exemption died with the keyword, and requiring `import System.Threading` to declare any event would fight the declaration-cleanliness the retirement exists to serve; relocating the types to `System` (the `Subscription`/v0.76 precedent) stays recorded as the equally-principled alternative. (3) `EventSource<T>`/`AsyncEventSource<T>` gain public parameterless constructors (#1646 tracks the compiler; sequenced first in the implementation roadmap — the pattern is the graduation's front door). (4) `event` moves from Appendix B.1 to B.3 as the language's one reserved-for-future identifier: pre-retirement code fails with a reserved-word diagnostic rather than parsing `event` as an identifier, and a one-line declaration form stays re-introducible without a breaking change (un-reserving is a separately declinable follow-up).

**Recorded costs and drops.** One extra line and one extra name per public event; producer sites read `_clicked` rather than `Clicked`; interface auto-satisfaction is gone (the implementer writes the property — see the §13.4 postscript above); a §28.14 form-4 narrowing for off-idiom discharges (the pattern's property is a getter hop, so an external capture-bearing subscription discharged neither by `using var` nor into an `owned` field of `this` is now rejected — correct-by-default, never unsound; the *transparent-getter* lift restoring form-4 parity for the idiom's property shape is recorded as liftable). Dropped without relocation, each recorded: former §24.1's placement rule (now just §9.7's owned-field rejection in records/enums); §24.1's and §24.7's modifier prose (each declaration's own grammar governs); §24.7's static-events-on-interfaces exclusion (dissolves — a static interface property is unspellable under §11.8, and a static method returning `ISharedEvent<T>` is an ordinary contract); the four-field `ClickedEvent` variant of former §24.2 (a duplicate declaration once the pattern example declares the three-field form). One wording correction rode along, recorded: former §24.5 called snapshot-raise "the same posture as" §29.12's loud-mutation discipline; stdlib §6.5 now states the true relation — both make mutation-during-traversal defined and loud, but the responses differ (snapshot vs fault), because a self-unsubscribing one-shot listener is a legitimate idiom while mid-iteration collection mutation is a bug.

<a id="sec-operators"></a>
## 14. Operators

<a id="sec-++-and-are-statements"></a>
### 14.1 `++` and `--` are statements

Statement-only, no expression form. `i++` vs `++i`, `arr[i++]`, sequence-point confusion, undefined behavior — none of those exist in Bezel because `++` produces no value. The cases where the old or new value is genuinely needed are clearer when written explicitly.

**And not overloadable (v0.289, proposal 0122 L4).** Through v0.288 a record, enum, choice or class could declare `operator ++`/`operator --`, and `x++` on such an operand meant `x := T.operator++(x)`. That was a second desugaring beside the built-in `x := x + 1`, with its own ownership corner — a class-typed `++` producing a fresh owned value into an owned field or an owning slot, rejected into a borrowing slot — that arose nowhere else in the language; spec §17.6 spent three paragraphs on it. This section had only ever defended statement-hood. The overload is gone: `++`/`--` are built-in statements over exactly the operand types §6.6's compound form admits — integer, floating-point, `decimal`, `char` — and a type advances by `x := x + 1` under its own `operator +` or by a named method — the posture §25.1 already takes for `not`, and the shape the standard library's own `ISteppable<T>.Step` takes. This was the re-measure `DECLINED.md`'s bitwise-overloadability row invites each subtraction pass, and its grounds were tested one by one: the shared `OpToken` cost is real and was paid; the compound-assignment ground does not reach `++` (it is not a compound assignment); and no rationale ground becomes incoherent (§14.4's `~` argument concerns the binary bitwise set, whose keep stands). Reopening bar: a real program whose advance cannot be spelled as `x := x + 1` or as a named method.

<a id="sec-mandatory-parentheses-on-mixed-precedence"></a>
### 14.2 Mandatory parentheses on mixed precedence

Mixing bitwise (`&`, `|`, `^`) with equality, relational, or logical operators in the same expression is a syntax error. `a & 1 = 0` doesn't compile; write `(a & 1) = 0`.

The rule costs nothing on programs that mean what they say and catches the most common precedence pitfalls inherited from C. Shift mixed with arithmetic is the same: `a << 2 + 1` is rejected; parenthesize.

**Range sits in the bitwise band (level 6).** High precedence (just below Primary) would force explicit parentheses around any arithmetic in range bounds; the lowest binary level would break `score in 60..100` and similar `in`-with-range idioms. Level 6 lets both arithmetic-in-bounds (`0..n+1`) and `in`-with-range (`x in a..b`) parse without parentheses, and a bitwise-with-range mix falls to the mandatory-parenthesization rule above (spec §17.2).

<a id="sec-comparison-operators-must-be-defined-together"></a>
### 14.3 Comparison operators must be defined together

`<`, `<=`, `>`, `>=` together. `=` and `<>` together. Languages that derive `>=` from `not (a < b)` get the NaN cases wrong. Forcing each to be defined explicitly keeps the semantics explicit.

<a id="sec-not-is-not-user-overloadable"></a>
### 14.4 `not` is not user-overloadable

`not` is the boolean negation operator. It is not user-overloadable. v0.61 listed `not` among the user-overloadable operators ([§25.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-overloadable-operators)) and contradicted the [§6.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-bitwise-operations) statement that `not` is "valid only on `bool`." v0.62 resolves this by prohibiting `not` overloading entirely.

The reasoning: `not` reads as a boolean operation. A reader seeing `not x` expects `x` to be (or to behave like) a `bool`. Overloading `not` on arbitrary types would invite confusion — three-valued logic, set-complement semantics, "negate" as in "produce additive inverse" all have superficial appeal, but each interpretation departs from the boolean reading in ways that are easy to misread. Domain-specific inversion is better expressed by a named method (e.g. `.Negate()`, `.Invert()`, `.Complement()`), which makes intent explicit at every call site.

The bitwise complement operator `~` remains user-overloadable. Types that have a meaningful bit-pattern inversion or set-complement operation can overload `~`; types with logical-style negation should provide a named method.

**The binary bitwise operators (`&`, `|`, `^`, `<<`, `>>`) stay user-overloadable, recorded here since v0.284 (the 2026-08 subtraction review).** No stdlib type overloads them and no client exists yet, but the keep is structural three ways: `~`'s own ground names the *set-complement* role, which is incoherent without union and intersection beside it; removal would touch the shared grammar productions (Appendix A's `OpToken`/`IfaceStaticOperator`) and permanently strand the compound-assignment desugar for `&=`/`|=`/`^=`/`<<=`/`>>=`; and proposal 0078's checked-enum investigation found, in its words, that "**no flags-enum idiom exists** in the language or library" — its recorded flags idiom is const integer masks, which need no overloadability — so the keep is structural rather than client-anticipating; one lit guard (#2162) pins the enum-operator composition 0078 left compile-time-unaffected. Re-measured each subtraction pass.

<a id="sec-no-implicit-conversion-operators"></a>
### 14.5 No implicit conversion operators

Implicit conversions make overload resolution unpredictable, hide allocation costs, and let inappropriate values silently flow into APIs. Bezel has no `implicit` keyword — and no `explicit` one either: spec §25.4 admits no user-defined conversion operators at all, implicit or explicit. A conversion between user types is written as a named factory or projection method.

**Why named methods (spec §25.4).** The conversion is visible at the call site (`dto.ToUser()` rather than a cast), and its name can communicate intent (`FromDecimal`, `ToReadOnly`) where a cast cannot. Overload resolution does not have to consider user-defined conversions: the conversion is a regular method call under ordinary resolution. And the pattern composes with the rest of the language (extension methods, fluent chaining) with no conversion-specific machinery. C# supports both `implicit` and `explicit` user-defined conversion operators. The implicit form is widely considered a misfeature in retrospect, even within the C# community: it makes overload resolution opaque and lets unintended conversions slip into expressions silently. The explicit form is less harmful but offers no advantage over a named method with the same compile-time check. Both are omitted, consistent with spec §5.1's rule that an implicit conversion exists only where no programmer-visible information is lost, and with §6.2's prohibition on implicit `decimal`/binary-float mixing.

**Overload resolution tie-breaking (v0.128).** v0.127's §22.6 said overloads are distinguishable by number, type, and `ref`/`out` modifiers, but was silent on how to choose when two applicable overloads are each reachable through a different built-in implicit conversion (§5.1). §5.1 catalogs the implicit conversions; it did not say how they rank during overload resolution. v0.128 closes the gap conservatively: an exact match is preferred over any implicit conversion, but built-in implicit conversions are not ranked against one another, so a call that reaches two overloads only through (equally-ranked) conversions is ambiguous and is a compile error citing the candidates. The programmer disambiguates with an explicit cast.

We considered importing C#'s finer "better conversion target" ordering (narrower target preferred, so `int→long` would beat `int→float`). We declined it: §5.1 deliberately admits an implicit conversion only when no programmer-visible information is lost (or, for wide-integer-to-float, when an explicit cast would be unjustified noise), and it places every numeric widening in one undifferentiated row — including the precision-losing `int→float`. Ranking those conversions would be net-new semantics layered on top of §5.1, not a clarification of it. The conservative rule keeps faith with Bezel's "explicit over implicit" stance (the same principle that omits user-defined conversion operators, §25.4) and with the existing treatment of overloaded method references (§27.6), which already makes the absence of a unique winner a compile error citing the candidates. The cost is that a few calls that a C#-style rule would silently resolve are instead flagged for an explicit cast; that is the intended trade.

<a id="sec-compound-assignment-narrows-back-checked"></a>
### 14.6 Compound assignment narrows back, checked (v0.184)

`b += 10` on a `byte` is the one place every C-family language needs an exception to its no-implicit-narrowing rule, because §6.2-style promotion makes the natural desugaring `b := b + 10` ill-typed. C#'s answer is a hidden cast that silently truncates in unchecked contexts, guarded by an RHS-convertibility test to bound the damage. Bezel's answer (§6.6) is the same hidden cast made *checked*: the write-back raises `OverflowException` out of range, so the only loss is range and range loss is loud — which is why Bezel does not need C#'s guard and admits a runtime `int` RHS (`acc += n`), the same posture as §6.1's checked `a + b`. The line is drawn at the integer family (plus `char`): a binary-float or `decimal` result against a narrower LHS is rejected outright, because §5.2's float narrowings round or truncate *silently* — `b += 1.5` adding-then-flooring is exactly the invisible loss §5.1 exists to exclude, and the explicit `b := byte(b + 1.5)` spelling is the visible version. The carve-out is scoped to the compound form; plain assignment keeps the strict rule everywhere.

<a id="sec-shift-counts-checked"></a>
### 14.7 Shift counts are checked, not masked (v0.242)

Adopted v0.242 (proposal 0079; owner-ruled 2026-08-11, from the elegance review). Through v0.241, spec §6.5 reduced the shift count modulo the left operand's bit width — `1 << 33` on `int` silently computed `2`, a count of `-1` masked to `31` — defended by "a shift never raises (it is a bit operation)." That sentence conflates two different things. *Bits shifted out of the left operand are discarded*: that **is** the operation, exactly as `&` discards bits — correct, silent, and kept. *A count outside `0..width−1` silently reduced modulo the width*: that is not the operation — the count is a *quantity* selecting an operation, not a bit pattern being operated on, and quantities out of domain are exactly what the checked posture faults on. `a + b` overflowing raises (§6.1); `a / 0` raises; `byte(300)` raises; `1 << 33` returned `2`. The masking was inherited from C-family hardware behavior (the v0.154/#1094 record settled only *which* operand's width masks; negative counts were discussed nowhere), and the honest content of a defending rationale entry would have been "C# did it." §17.14 compounded it: constant evaluation *knew* the count was out of domain and computed the masked value anyway (`const Flags: long := 1 << 64;` folded to `1`).

The rule (spec §6.5): the count's domain is `0..width−1` of the left operand's type; outside it, a constant count is a compile error and a runtime count raises `OverflowException` — the same choice as 0078's enum-cast fault and 0086 item 1's range-step fault, one ruling at three sites. Deliberate modular counts are spelled visibly: `v << (k & 31)` — one `&`, in-domain by construction, and the reader sees the masking instead of having to know it. The precedent survey found the post-C trend uniformly away from silent masking — Rust's checked semantics panic on over-shift (masking only in the unchecked-release concession; Bezel's always-checked §6.1 takes the checked branch of that fork unconditionally), Go faults on negative counts and never masks, Zig types the count at `log2(width)` bits, Swift reserved masking for a separately-spelled `&<<` — and no surveyed language chose silent masking on the merits rather than inheriting it. Codegen cost is honest: LLVM shifts are poison at counts ≥ width, so the backend already spent an unconditional AND on every non-constant shift; the checked rule replaces it with one perfectly-predicted compare+branch, and in-domain constant counts now compile to the bare shift with zero checks. **Declined:** Zig-style typed counts (needs `u5`/`u6` widths the type system should not grow for one operator); Go/Swift-style total non-masking semantics (silently value-producing for what is almost always a bug); faulting on negative counts only (halves the inconsistency, no principled line); a warning instead of an error for constant counts (§17.14's posture: constant-evaluation faults are never deferred). Deliberate modular *bit motion* has named homes in the accepted `BitOperations` toolkit (proposal 0008's rotate/wrapping family) — the same operator-strict/method-explicit split as §6.1 arithmetic.

<a id="sec-decimal-from-binary-float"></a>
### 14.8 `decimal(x)` reads the shortest round-trip form (v0.311)

A binary float has three candidate decimal values: its exact binary expansion, a fixed-precision rounding of it, and its shortest round-trip form, the digits its no-format `ToString()` prints (stdlib §2.18). Spec §5.2 takes the third, then rounds as §4.4 rounds a literal: `decimal(x)` is the value the `decimal` literal written with `x.ToString()`'s digits denotes. The conversion therefore agrees with what a program already shows for `x`, and `decimal(0.1)` is `0.1`.

The survey (the owner's, 2026-09; sources not re-fetched for this revision). Java's `BigDecimal.valueOf(double)` converts through `Double.toString`'s canonical form, which its documentation calls generally the preferred way; `new BigDecimal(double)` gives the exact expansion, with a documented warning. Kotlin's `Double.toBigDecimal()` and `Float.toBigDecimal()` convert through `toString`. Go's shopspring `decimal.NewFromFloat` uses the shortest round-trip digits. Python's `Decimal(float)` gives the exact expansion (`Decimal(0.1)` is `0.1000000000000000055511151231257827…`), and its documentation flags the surprise. .NET's `(decimal)double` rounds to 15 significant digits (7 for `float`), which collapses distinct doubles (`(decimal)(0.1 + 0.2)` is `0.3`) and differs from .NET's own shortest-round-trip `double.ToString()`.

**Declined:** the exact expansion, which puts 28 digits of binary noise into every non-dyadic value; a fixed significant-digit rounding, which needs a precision constant beside the one `ToString()` already defines and maps distinct doubles to one decimal. The shortest form is injective on doubles, so distinct doubles of magnitude at least 10^−12 give distinct decimals. Below that, §4.4's scale bound of 28 merges values, as it must for any rule. The rounding there reads the shortest form, not the binary expansion, so `decimal(2.5e-28)` is a tie that goes to the even `2e-28`, as the literal `2.5e-28m` does.

<a id="sec-binary-float-parse-range"></a>
### 14.9 Binary-float `Parse` faults where the literal does (v0.313)

`double.Parse` and `float.Parse` read a numeral at run time, and stdlib §9.2 gives them the literal's reading: the value rounded once to the type as spec §4.3 rounds a literal, with `OverflowException` exactly where that rounding is not finite. So `double.Parse("1e999")` faults where the literal `1e999` is a compile error, `TryParse` answers `false` there, and `Convert.ToDouble` follows by stdlib §2.3's mirror sentence. Three grounds carry it. One digit string gets one verdict, in source and in text. The `Parse` families fault alike: the integers (stdlib §9.1) and `decimal` (stdlib §9.3) already raise `OverflowException` where the number spelled does not fit the type. And input that spells a number the type cannot hold is a data problem, which the stdlib's strict-fault model (stdlib §3.2) reports rather than substitutes. Spec §5.2's per-target-family split, under which `float(d)` overflows to `±Infinity`, governs converting a value already in a binary type, as §6.3 governs arithmetic on one. `Parse` converts no value: it reads a numeral, and §4.3 declines to let a numeral denote an infinity. Underflow is in range, as for a literal: a numeral that rounds to zero or to a subnormal denotes that value.

The survey (the owner's, 2026-09; sources not fetched for this revision). .NET's `double.Parse` returns `±Infinity` on overflow since .NET Core 3.0, and raised `OverflowException` before it. Java's `Double.parseDouble` and Python's `float` return the infinity. Go's `strconv.ParseFloat` returns `±Inf` together with an error whose `Err` is `ErrRange`, and C's `strtod` returns `±HUGE_VAL` and sets `errno` to `ERANGE`. Bezel sides with the earlier .NET contract, and in substance with Go and C, which report the range error rather than hide it. Having no out-of-band error slot, it reports through the fault, and `TryParse` is the branch-shaped tier.

**Declined:** `±Infinity`, the majority's answer, which makes one digit string a compile error as a literal and an infinity as text, and needs a binary-float carve-out in stdlib §2.3; and the infinity with a range error beside it (Go's shape), which needs an error slot the `Parse` family does not have and would be a second mechanism for one fault condition (stdlib §1's no-dual rule).

**The input grammar (v0.314).** `Parse` reads a `double` literal's shape with a sign before it: digits on both sides of any point, an optional signed exponent, and no whitespace, digit separator or hexadecimal form (stdlib §9.2). It is the integer grammar (stdlib §9.1) carried to a fraction and an exponent, as strict as the integer and `decimal` grammars (stdlib §9.1, §9.3) on whitespace and separators, and every rendering the no-format `ToString()` produces for a finite value lies inside it, so stdlib §2.18's round trip needs nothing wider. The narrow grammar is the compatible starting point: admitting `".5"` or `"0x1p3"` later widens what parses, while retracting either would break programs. An input outside the grammar spells no number, so its `FormatException` comes before any range question. `decimal.Parse` reads the same shape without the exponent (stdlib §9.3), so no numeric `Parse` admits a bare point.

**JSON (v0.314).** `ReadFloat` takes `Parse`'s reading and range, raising `SerializationException`, the deserializer's fault, where `Parse` raises `OverflowException`: a document never reads back a non-finite value `WriteFloat` refuses to write (stdlib §2.15). A `float` component narrows `ReadFloat`'s `double` as spec §5.2's `float(x)` does, and faults where a finite number does not fit (spec §9.8). That rounds twice. A single rounding of the text to `float` would need a second float read in the deserializer vocabulary (stdlib §2.14), which every `IDeserializer` implementation would then have to supply, or a JSON-private path that synthesis cannot name for other formats. The two readings differ only for a number whose `double` rounding lands exactly on the midpoint between two adjacent `float` values (or between `float.MaxValue` and the overflow threshold), where the second rounding's tie goes to even and the first rounding's direction is lost. The difference is one `float` step, in range or at the edge: `1.0000000596046447753906251` is `1.0000001` to `float.Parse` and `1` in a `float` component, and `3.4028235677973366e38` is `float.MaxValue` to `float.Parse` and a fault in a `float` component. A number `WriteFloat` writes for a `float` value never meets the case, since its digits read back to exactly that value, which the second rounding leaves unchanged; only a number written for some other value can.

**Declined:** `strtod`'s wider input (hexadecimal significands, a bare leading or trailing point), which no literal, rendering or JSON number uses and which would tie the contract to the C library; `±Infinity` from `ReadFloat` and from a `float` component, which would let a document decode to a value `WriteFloat` rejects; and one rounding of the JSON text to `float`, for the vocabulary cost above.

**Non-finite spellings (v0.316).** Every rendering of a non-finite `double` or `float` — the no-format `ToString()`, an interpolation hole, every floating specifier — spells it `NaN`, `Infinity` or `-Infinity`, and `Parse` reads back exactly those three (stdlib §2.18, §9.2). They are the names of the constants a program already writes (`double.NaN`, `PositiveInfinity`, `NegativeInfinity`) and the spelling spec §4.3 uses for the values, so the text a program prints is the text its author reads in source and documentation; .NET's invariant culture renders the same three tokens. A specifier lays out a number's digits, and a non-finite value has none, so `F2`, `E3` and `P` add no zeros, exponent or `%` to it: one value has one spelling whatever format a hole names.
