A NaN renders `NaN` whatever its sign and payload: those bits are not part of a number's value, `=` treats every NaN alike (spec §4.3.1), and `BitConverter` (stdlib §2.12) carries them where they matter. `Parse` matches the three exactly: the round trip needs only the rendered spellings, and admitting another later would widen what parses without breaking a program. The finite grammar's one case-insensitive point, the exponent's `e` or `E`, is the literal's own (spec Appendix A's `FloatLiteral` admits both), while no source spelling of `NaN` or `PositiveInfinity` varies in case. `Boolean.Parse` (stdlib §9.4) accepts `true` and `false` in any case and is not the model here: case-insensitive matching would admit spellings no rendering produces, and it remains available later as a widening that breaks no program.

**Declined:** the C library's lowercase `nan`, `inf` and `-inf`, which `%g` produces and no Bezel source spells; `strtod`'s spellings in `Parse` (`infinity`, `nan(…)`, any case), which would tie the contract to the C library, as the grammar above declines for hexadecimal; case-insensitive matching of the three tokens, `Boolean.Parse`'s posture, for the reason above; and `+Infinity` beside `-Infinity`, which no rendering produces — the sign §9.2 admits before a numeral belongs to the numeral grammar, and the non-finite tokens stand outside it.

<a id="sec-strings"></a>
## 15. Strings

Strings appear in nearly every program. The choice of representation affects memory footprint and indexing performance, and the obvious choices each have a real cost.

Three options were on the table:

- **UTF-32, fixed width.** Simplest implementation, O(1) indexing trivially, but pays 4× memory for ASCII text.
- **UTF-8 without O(1) code-point indexing.** What Rust, Go, and Swift do. Smallest memory, efficient sequential operations, but indexing the i-th code point becomes O(i), and the API needs cursor types and view-based abstractions to stay usable.
- **Adaptive width (PEP 393).** Per-string storage of 1, 2, or 4 bytes per code point, chosen at construction. O(1) indexing always; ASCII pays one byte; the representation is invisible to programmers.

Bezel picks PEP 393.

The Swift route was attractive on technical merit but requires a substantial design package — substring views, cursor types, multiple abstraction levels — that Bezel doesn't have time to do well in this revision. Without that package, plain UTF-8 with O(i) indexing puts too much friction on basic string code.

A two-types route ("normal `string` is UTF-32; `Utf8String` is the memory-efficient form") fragments the ecosystem. Every API author chooses a side and every consumer pays for conversions across the boundary.

PEP 393 hides the choice in the runtime. The user model is "string is a sequence of code points indexed in O(1)." The implementation picks 1, 2, or 4 bytes per string. ASCII text pays one byte; mixed-BMP text pays two; only strings with code points above the BMP pay four. Indexing is one branch on the kind, reliably predicted. CPython has thirteen years of experience showing this works.

`char` remains 32 bits — the right type for a code point. `s.IsAscii` gives FFI and IO a runtime predicate to skip encoding work for the dominant case. The char-level non-allocating substring view landed in v0.104 as `ReadOnlyStringSpan` (stdlib [§3.3](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-readonlystringspan)) — a record carrying a borrow of the string. A byte-level view from ASCII strings (`ReadOnlyAsciiByteSpan`, v0.106) was retired in v0.188 — a borrow-bearing record cannot cross the FFI boundary (spec §32.5 admits only the §32.13 C-compatible records), and v0.165's span-destination encoding (`Encoding.GetBytes(s, destination: Span<byte>)`, stdlib [§3.2](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-encoding)) serves the allocation-conscious byte path for every string, not only ASCII ones. Byte access from strings routes through `Encoding.UTF8.GetBytes` in either its allocating one-shot or its buffer-reusing span-destination form. See rationale [§5.15](#sec-owned-buffers-and-spans) for the full design story.

<a id="sec-convert-is-a-familiarity-alias"></a>
### 15.x `Convert` is a familiarity alias (ruled 2026-07, #1356)

`Convert`'s string↔primitive members exactly duplicate the §9 primitive `Parse`/`TryParse`/`ToString` members. Ruled: **kept, explicitly as a C#-familiarity alias** — the duplication is documented rather than deleted (deleting would break arriving muscle memory for zero space savings), and `Convert` gains no cross-primitive conversion role: value conversion is the §5.2 checked cast, bit reinterpretation is `BitConverter.Reinterpret` (the recorded split), and `Convert` stays a thin naming veneer over Parse/ToString.

(v0.320, [#5500](https://github.com/s8lap/bezel-lang/issues/5500)) **`Convert.ToString` takes a `float`.** Without a `float` overload, `Convert.ToString(0.1f)` resolves to `ToString(double)`, `float → double` being its one applicable conversion (spec §22.6), and renders `"0.10000000149011612"` where `0.1f.ToString()` is `"0.1"`: a veneer whose stated contract is the argument's own `ToString()` text (stdlib §2.3) would give a different string for one primitive, and give it silently. `ToString(value: float)` makes the call an exact match, so the equation holds. It is a new member, and §15.y's line admits it on this section's own ground: .NET declares `Convert.ToString(Single)`, and the reader arriving with it expects `"0.1"`. Two alternatives are declined (owner decision). *Keeping the five overloads* leaves the silent divergence. *The rest of .NET's set*, `char` and the narrow integers (`sbyte`, `byte`, `short`, `ushort`, `uint`, `ulong`), has the same arrival ground and is declined on its cost: each such call is a §22.6 ambiguity today, a compile error rather than a wrong string, whose remedy, the value's own `ToString()`, is one call, so the seven overloads would be duplicates that repair nothing. The `float` overload is admitted for the silent divergence it removes, not for arrival alone.

**`Path` is kept on this section's arrival ground.** Stdlib §7.4's six `Path` members (`Combine`, `GetFileName`, `GetExtension`, `GetFileNameWithoutExtension`, `GetDirectoryName`, `GetFullPath`) are the string-level path operations a file-touching service needs, carrying .NET's names and meanings so arriving code reads as written. The set is kept as it stands; a further member is added on a recorded need, not for parity with .NET's wider class.

<a id="sec-stdlib-regularization-v0290"></a>
### 15.y Inherited duplicates are tolerated, new ones are not — and the four retired at v0.290 (proposal 0123)

§15.x's ground is *arrival*: `Convert` stays because deleting it would break muscle memory that .NET readers bring with them, and that ground extends no license to a duplicate that serves no arriving habit (proposal 0074 §7 drew the line, and measured it — 38 in-tree occurrences kept `Guid.NewGuid`). The 2026-09 regularization applied the line with counts. **Retired (v0.290):** `Type.Implements(iface)` — the reflective conformance test has one spelling, `iface.IsAssignableFrom(t)` (0 call sites anywhere; the lattice was unimplemented); `Stopwatch.Restart()` — `Reset(); Start();` (1 test call site); `String.IsNullOrEmpty(value: string?)` — `value = null or value.IsEmpty`, `IsEmpty` having landed at v0.231 (3 test call sites); and the static `String.Compare(a, b[, ignoreCase])`, whose only capability the instance surface lacked — case-folded ordering — is now the instance `CompareTo(other, ignoreCase)` beside `Equals(other, ignoreCase)` (12 test lines migrate). **Kept, with the count that keeps each:** `Stopwatch.ElapsedMilliseconds` (19 test / 6 doc / 7 src occurrences — a client), `CancellationTokenSource.IsCancellationRequested` (the reason stands in stdlib §6.x beside it), and `Decimal.Zero`/`Decimal.One` (21 in `tests/`, above the bar that separates them from `ElapsedMilliseconds`; the `int.Zero` asymmetry is real and is an alias pending a re-measure, not a decline).

Two regularizations ride the same principle from the other side. *Overload pairs for an optional trailing argument* were a second idiom for a shape the document already spelled with §22.5 defaults (`Increment(key, delta := 1)`, `token: CancellationToken := CancellationToken.None` at seventeen sites); nine such pairs — thirteen rows — are one defaulted signature each, and stdlib §1 states the rule so it does not regrow. The shapes that keep a pair are an interface-required member beside its widened twin (`CompareTo`/`Equals` with `ignoreCase`), because §11.2's exact-match conformance fixes the shorter signature, and an omitted argument with no nameable default — the to-the-end window forms, and the JSON facade's `JsonOptions`. *`Counter<K>` spelled key membership `ContainsKey` alone* — a name minted at v0.215 with no .NET antecedent, on the one map-like type on which `key in tally` therefore did not compile; it is `Contains(key)`, and stdlib §1 records that `ContainsKey` is the dictionaries' inherited alias and is not minted on new types.

*`Math.Sign` is one generic.* `Sign<T>(x: T): int where T: INumber<T>` replaces four per-type overloads by the principle that retired `Sum`'s (stdlib §5): the per-type forms predated §2.19's abstraction, and `Sign(decimal)` was added at v0.228 as a symmetry completion fifty-two revisions after `INumber<T>` shipped. The return type is `int` at every substitution, so no call changes meaning and `DECLINED.md`'s result-type block on `Average` does not reach it; the two costs are stated — `pure` is lost until `INumber<T>`'s members are `pure`-declared (recorded as the follow-on, beside `Min`/`Max`/`Clamp`), and a `char` argument, which converts co-equally to all four per-type parameter types and so was a §22.6 ambiguity under them, is rejected by the `INumber<T>` bound instead (0 sites). **`Abs` stays generic-free** for the reason the same trace exposes: `Abs<T>` returns `T`, so `Math.Abs(floatVal)` — `double` under the per-type overloads, `float → double` being its one applicable §5.1 conversion — would infer `T = float` and change its result type at every `var` and every follow-on (a `short` or `sbyte` argument, co-equal across all four and so a §22.6 ambiguity, would instead newly compile); that is the very mapping the `Average` row records as blocked, and a per-width return-type change with the `pure` loss is not what this evidence earns.

<a id="sec-instance-member-shadows-extension"></a>
### 15.z `OwningSet<T>` pops rather than takes: an instance member shadows a same-named extension

`OwningSet<T>`'s transfer members are `Pop`/`TryPop`, not `Take`/`TryTake`, and the ground is measured shadowing rather than taste. Under language spec §26.5 rule 1 an instance member blocks a same-named extension **by name**, so an instance `Take(query: T)` on the set made LINQ's `Take(count: int)` unreachable on an `OwningSet<T>` receiver — permanently, for every caller. `Pop`/`TryPop` un-shadow it (`set.Take(2)` resolves to the lazy operator) and put the member in the transfer family the documents already teach: `Get`/`Peek` borrow, `Pop`/`Drain` transfer, which `OwningStack<T>` and `OwningQueue<T>` already spell.

`OwningDictionary<K, V>` keeps `PopValue`/`TryPopValue` with its own reason recorded at stdlib §4.9. `OwningSet<T>` has no such ambiguity — its element and its query are the same type and the same thing — which is why its member can lose the qualifier.

**`CountWhere` is declined, evidence-gated** ([proposal 0098 §6](proposals/0098-stdlib-surface-program.md)). The same block-by-name rule (stdlib §1, *Block-by-name*) makes LINQ's predicate `Count(pred)` unreachable on every receiver that declares or inherits a `Count` member, as it does the `Contains`, `First`, `Last`, `Reverse` and `ToArray` operators on receivers declaring or inheriting those names. A per-name workaround tier — a `CountWhere` beside the blocked `Count(pred)` — fixes one name of six while leaving `FirstWhere`/`LastWhere` undeclared; the one-call composition `coll.Where(pred).Count()` is already idiomatic; no corpus use or filed issue evidences the friction; and the recorded general remedy is the extension-call-disambiguation deferral ([§18](#sec-things-deferred)). The reopening bar: field evidence that the collision costs real users real time on the `Count`/`Contains` names specifically — at which point the question is the `*Where` tier or the disambiguation syntax, not `CountWhere` alone.

<a id="sec-ffi"></a>
## 16. FFI

<a id="sec-hand-written-declarations-not-bindgen"></a>
### 16.1 Hand-written declarations, not bindgen

Auto-generated bindings (Rust's `bindgen`, Python's `ctypesgen`, SWIG) parse C headers and produce language-side declarations automatically. Bezel's FFI is manual: the programmer writes the Bezel-side signature, and the compiler trusts it — the same hand-declared model as Python's `ctypes`.

Auto-generated bindings tend to expose every quirk of the C API; hand-written declarations let the programmer expose only what's needed, with the names they want. The cost is some typing per imported function. A bindgen tool could generate `extern` block source code in the future; nothing precludes that.

<a id="sec-distinct-types-for-cstring-wstring-rawbytes"></a>
### 16.2 Distinct types for `CString`, `WString`, `RawBytes`

C strings, UTF-16 strings, and arbitrary byte buffers have different ownership stories, different encoding assumptions, and different valid operations. Conflating them — as `void*` does — pushes the burden of remembering which is which onto the programmer at every call site. Distinct types let the compiler refuse calls that mix them up.

<a id="sec-typed-pointers-not-marshal"></a>
### 16.3 Typed pointers, not an untyped `Marshal` surface

Foreign memory access (spec §32.12, v0.186) had two candidate shapes: a Marshal-style static class (`Marshal.ReadInt32(p)` / `WriteInt32(p, v)` over bare `IntPtr` and byte offsets) and typed pointer wrappers. `Ptr<T>` won on §16.2's own argument: an untyped read surface is `void*` with method-name suffixes — `Marshal.ReadDouble(p)` compiles whether `p` came from `sqlite3_column_blob` or `__errno_location`, while `Ptr<double>` vs `Ptr<int>` moves that mistake to compile time. Element-scaled `LoadAt`/`Add` also delete the classic FFI bug class of hand-computed `i * sizeof(T)` with a platform-dependent `sizeof` — the compiler that knows the target stride computes it. The cost was weighed honestly: a generic intrinsic record family with a per-instantiation gate is more machinery than fourteen static methods; §16.2's recorded value judgment outweighs the surface count.

Also declined, with reasons. **Operator pointer arithmetic** (`p + 1`, `-`, `<`): named methods keep the element-scaling in the method's one-line contract, and C itself makes ordering over unrelated allocations undefined; nothing in the binding corpus (sqlite3, curl, BLAS, POSIX) needs them. **`Ptr<Ptr<T>>`**: every `T**` shape in the corpus is expressible as `Ptr<IntPtr>` plus one visible `FromIntPtr` re-typing per indirection level; nested pointee gating buys generality nothing needs. (A record wrapping `Ptr<T>` is itself a valid pointee via the single-field rule — a sanctioned reconstruction of typed double indirection where a binding genuinely wants it, not a loophole: it carries a declared type at every level.) **Panama-style checked foreign memory** (bounds/lifetime-carrying handles): the checking would be exactly as good as the trusted declarations it derives from; `RawBytes.FromForeign(p, length)` *is* the length-carrying form, opt-in where a length exists, and a general checked tier would promise verification §32's trust posture cannot deliver.

<a id="sec-instance-state-and-foreign-callbacks"></a>
### 16.4 Instance state and foreign callbacks: three tiers

The v0.163 callback rule (static reference / non-capturing lambda only) answered "how does a bound method cross a C function-pointer boundary?" with "it cannot" — correct for a bare pointer, but it left C's ubiquitous user-data idiom without a typed home, and the owner's question ("marshal an object method that can update the class object it comes from, the way C# does") deserved a designed answer. v0.186's answer is three tiers keyed to **when native may invoke**:

- **Pull** (call-shaped APIs): an ordinary instance method makes the extern call and writes results into `this` from returns, boundary `out`/`ref` (spec §32.5), and `Ptr<T>` reads (spec §32.12). Most of the question dissolves here; nothing about §32 ever prevented it — what prevented it in practice was the missing expressiveness this revision lands.
- **Call-bounded push** (`qsort_r`, `sqlite3_exec`): every invocation is bracketed by one synchronous extern call, so the callback's environment is structurally alive — its captures live on the calling frame, and extern bodies cannot suspend. §28.14's call-bounded caller rule ("any closure … every captured source trivially outlives the call") transfers across the boundary with one substitution: the callee obligations, compiler-verified in-language, become §32.10 documented trust. The `env` clause (spec §32.8) declares the two ABI facts no analysis can infer — which parameter is the `void*`, and which callback position receives it — and the compiler synthesizes the trampoline.
- **Retained push** (curl's split registration, buses, signal handlers): native holds the pointer past the registering call; no frame bounds the environment, so no lifetime-checked admission can be sound. The registry idiom (spec §32.8: static callback + integer token + `Locked<OwningDictionary<…>>`) makes the safety structural — synchronized state, token indirection (a stale token is a graceful miss, not a use-after-free), wrapper-ordered teardown with the blocking-acquisition discipline stated.

**Why `env` and not `scoped`** — the natural-looking reuse, declined on three grounds: (i) it would give one keyword opposite meanings — in-language `scoped` marks the *storage-bounded* tier (the callee may retain, bounded by an object), while the extern clause marks the *call-bounded* tier (the callee must **not** retain); (ii) v0.175's "`scoped` is illegal on extern" reasoning — storage obligations and quiescence unverifiable across the boundary — remains true and is kept, not reversed; (iii) the marker's grammatical job is ABI slot designation, not a lifetime tier — the paired parameter's lifetime tier is the plain call-bounded default it already has. `ctx` (what C calls the slot) lost to `env` (what Bezel puts there — the closure environment). Slot *inference* (no marker at all) is impossible: `sqlite3_exec` has two pointer-shaped candidate slots, and the callback-side position genuinely varies (first for `sqlite3_exec`'s callback, last for `qsort_r`'s comparator) — both associations must be declared.

**Why the retained tier is a documented idiom, not machinery**: every candidate mechanization (a `registry`-typed extern clause, auto-minted tokens, a compiler-managed handle table) re-derives exactly `Locked<OwningDictionary<…>>` with less visibility, adds grammar for what ten lines of ordinary code state, and forecloses per-binding policy (token reuse, multi-registration, what a "miss" means). It lands as normative guidance text so it is teachable, citable, and testable — the same status as §32.6's ownership patterns.

**The C# `GetFunctionPointerForDelegate` shape** — minting a retained (thunk, environment) pair as an `IntPtr` — is declined: it is precisely a retained environment with no bounding frame, i.e. the storage-bounded tier the boundary cannot verify. C# affords it because the GC roots the delegate via `GCHandle` and the *programmer* remembers to free it — the remembered-convention shape §28 exists to replace. The address-minting surface (`Interop.CodeAddress`) stays static-only so no retained pair is mintable.

**The checked-mode thread guard** rides §28.2's deterministic-backstop posture: foreign-thread invocation of an `env`-paired callback is undefined behavior, and checked mode converts the thread-axis violation into a deterministic abort via a per-activation record in the environment block (correct across concurrent calls and reentrancy; alive exactly while sanctioned invocations are). The **temporal axis** — invocation after the call returned — deliberately gets no promised backstop: the record the guard would read is itself dead at that point; an implementation may poison the environment block at call exit as a quality-of-implementation strengthening. A recorded asymmetry, not a silent one.

<a id="sec-c-compatible-layout-promise"></a>
### 16.5 The C-compatible layout promise

Struct marshalling (spec §32.13, v0.186) enters through §3.6's derived-property mechanism because "passable to C" is exactly a structural predicate; §3.6 itself mandates that form. The layout promise is the language's **first** normative representation guarantee, so its scope is the design's load-bearing feature: classified records get the target C ABI's layout; every other record keeps unspecified layout, preserving future representation freedom.

§3.6's reservation clause ("dispatch of behavior on a derived type property is reserved to compiler-known standard-library types") was engaged head-on rather than lawyered around. A static representation guarantee attaching to *user* records purely by structure is more than admissibility, even though nothing executes differently by classification. Two readings existed: (a) representation is not "behavior", clause untouched — defensible on the letter but eroding the clause's evident purpose (keeping derived properties from silently acquiring semantic weight on user types); (b) the clause needs a scoped, visible exception. Reading (b) was taken and ratified together with the promise; neither stands without the other.

What the promise deliberately excludes: **platform-independence** (sizes and layouts vary as C's do; *which* C struct matches which platform is the programmer's trusted declaration — glibc vs macOS `struct addrinfo` is the canonical divergence, and each platform gets its own declaration); **unions and bitfields** (no structural spelling survives §3.6's computed-never-declared discipline, and bitfield layout is ABI-quirky; the workarounds are recorded in the spec text); **by-value passing** (Stage D: frontend ABI classification — eightbyte splitting, INTEGER/SSE classing, hidden `sret` — is genuinely large, per-target, miscompile-prone work, and the by-address subset unblocks the whole binding corpus except `div_t`-style small-struct returns).

Two recorded deviations-with-reasons ride this area. First, the **inline-array element set** was extended to the full FFI-compatible scalar set *uniformly* — direct extern parameters and record fields alike — one deliberate step past accepted proposal 0033 §4.1's letter, which committed only the field position: pointer-sized scalars pack exactly as primitives do, and the asymmetry ("`[16]ulong` legal as a `SigAction` field, `[2]IntPtr` illegal as a parameter") would have been a rule with no rationale. (`[N]R` arrays of C-compatible records stay excluded as a recorded liftable bound, spec §32.14.) Second, a plain inline-array extern argument passes **by address with no call-site marker**, deviating from the v0.169 marked-mutation doctrine (§22.3) — deliberately: by-address is the *only* passing mode an inline array has at the boundary (C's array-to-pointer convention), so there is no unmarked-by-value alternative for a marker to distinguish, and a mandatory marker would mark nothing.

<a id="sec-variadic-imports-per-arity-aliases"></a>
### 16.6 C-variadic imports as per-arity aliases

§17.9's `params` decline is engaged, not contradicted, by spec §32.11's `variadic(N)` clause: no Bezel-side method becomes variadic, no allocation is hidden, and every call site has fixed, declared arity — what changes is only the emitted *calling sequence* for a symbol that is variadic on the C side. The clause exists to close a real trap: binding a variadic C symbol with a plain fixed-arity extern is undefined behavior on ABIs whose variadic callees consult a caller-set register (SysV x86-64's `%al`), and it often works until it doesn't — the worst failure shape. The compiler cannot detect the mismatch (the symbol's C-side arity is invisible), so the spec documents the trap loudly and provides the sound spelling.

Promotions are a compile error, not an implicit conversion: C promotes `float`→`double` and small integers→`int` in variadic slots; Bezel requires the *declaration* to carry the promoted type, so the promotion is visible where the contract is written. One recorded extension past accepted 0033's promoted-type list: single-integer/pointer-field records (spec §32.13's typed handles) are admitted in variadic positions — such a record marshals as its promoted scalar field, so the slot accounting is unchanged, and a typed handle in a `curl_easy_setopt` slot is the natural exemplar. **True call-site varargs** are declined for v1: they need a new call-arity rule and per-call-site promotion checking, and the measured corpus (curl_easy_setopt, open, fcntl, ioctl — all fixed small arities behind one symbol) is fully served by per-arity aliases; recorded as the v2 candidate if real bindings show alias proliferation.

<a id="sec-deferred-ffi-surface-reasons"></a>
### 16.7 Deferred FFI surface, with reasons

Spec §32.14 lists what stays out of v0.186's FFI; this section records the reasoning that must survive the deferral.

**Export-to-native**: three blockers — symbol-export/C-ABI entry generation belongs to the module/build layer (#1365, proposal 0034); a foreign process entering a Bezel `.so` on an unregistered thread needs a runtime-initialization story §32.10's foreign-thread obligations presuppose rather than provide; and the binding corpus (0007/0011/0014 and the review exemplars) contains no consumer. **`FnPtr<F>`** (calling pointers received from native) is the single most dangerous primitive in this space — it executes, not merely corrupts — and `Interop.CodeAddress` is one-way precisely so this stays independently deferred behind its own evidence bar. **Stage D by-value structs** wait for a dedicated review cycle with per-target ABI-classification tests (§16.5).

**`ForeignHandle<T>`** (the `GCHandle` analog: object↔`IntPtr`) was evaluated and deferred on four grounds: (1) the pinning half of `GCHandle`'s job does not exist here — Bezel heap objects never relocate, so the handle would provide only address minting plus liveness discipline; (2) the liveness discipline it offers is *weaker* than the registry idiom's — a stale handle resolve is silent undefined behavior, a stale registry token is a graceful miss under a lock, so for retained callbacks (the only tier that wants it) the idiom strictly dominates on safety at the cost of one dictionary lookup; (3) it would break the **no-object-address invariant** v0.186 otherwise establishes (spec §32.12): after this revision the only addresses in the language are buffer interiors, foreign values, and code — object identity stays outside the address space, load-bearing for §30.14 and for the unforgeability of §28's dispose-through-borrow rules; (4) the reopening condition is recorded — a measured per-invocation registry cost that matters, or a native API that requires the user pointer to be the object itself — and reopening takes its own small proposal. **The recorded direction for handing a Bezel object to native code**, should the question recur (v0.260, proposal 0102 §5): for the **callback** tier — the only tier with a live use case — the shipped mechanism is the `env` user-data marker (spec [§32.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-callbacks)), which makes the callback parameter capture-admitting and lets the runtime recover the function value from the opaque `env` slot without the programmer ever seeing, minting, or storing an object address; for the **retained-object** tier, the direction is the **registry token** — an integer key into a Bezel-side registry consulted under a lock, whose stale resolve is a graceful miss rather than undefined behavior. Both preserve the no-object-address invariant of spec [§32.12](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-foreign-memory), which is ground (3) above; a proposal that wants the handle must first say what it does with that invariant.

**Declaration-bound destructors** (`extern owned handle Sqlite3Db closes sqlite3_close;`-style) are declined with 0033 §7.2's three reasons: the pattern it automates already works today in ten lines (probe `p9_wrapper`, exit 0); real bindings grow a wrapper class anyway (the destructor is three of its lines); and foreign destructors have *policies* a bound function name cannot express (`sqlite3_close` returns `SQLITE_BUSY`; `fclose` can fail and lose data) — a destructor body expresses the chosen policy, a `closes fn` clause silently discards every return code.

**Startup symbol resolution** (spec §32.11) is pinned eager — every failure before `Main` — with the cost recorded: absent conditional compilation (0034) and runtime loading, a module declaring any platform-absent symbol makes every program linking it unrunnable on that platform even if the call is never reached. Fail-fast was chosen because extern declarations are per-platform artifacts already and a probe-for-availability binding wants conditional compilation, not late failure; the item reopens alongside 0034.

<a id="sec-things-explicitly-rejected"></a>
## 17. Things explicitly rejected

<a id="sec-truthiness-in-conditions"></a>
### 17.1 Truthiness in conditions

`if (user)` is forbidden. Conditions must be `bool`. Truthiness collapses zero, empty string, empty collection, and null.

<a id="sec-string-concatenation-with-non-strings"></a>
### 17.2 String concatenation with non-strings

`"x = " + 42` doesn't compile. Use interpolation: `$"x = {42}"`. Implicit `.ToString()` calls hide allocation behavior.

<a id="sec-goto"></a>
### 17.3 `goto` — declined; labeled `break`/`continue` adopted instead (v0.201)

`goto` remains declined. Multi-level loop exit is **labeled `break`/`continue`** (spec §21.5, v0.201; proposal 0050): a loop may carry a `name :` label, and `break name;`/`continue name;` target it. Labeled break keeps the structured subset of goto's power — forward-only, cannot enter a scope, cannot form a loop, target named on a construct the jump is inside — so every scope-exit obligation stays statically enumerable and the disposal story needed zero new rules (the jump reuses the multi-scope unwind edge `return`/`throw` already defined). The closers ride v0.170's named-construct rule (`end scan;`); a labeled loop's keyword closer (`end for;`) is rejected per that rule's own "otherwise". Declined alongside goto: numeric break levels (positional targets are fragile under edit) and labeling arbitrary statements (dead weight — only loops are targets). Extraction into a method remains good style where the inner logic deserves a name; it is no longer the only spelling.

<a id="sec-default-interface-methods"></a>
### 17.4 Default interface methods

Interfaces have only signatures. Default interface methods (C# 8+) blur the line between interfaces and abstract classes and create surprising behavior around explicit implementation. For shared logic, use an abstract class.

<a id="sec-member-hiding"></a>
### 17.5 Member hiding

See [§8.1](#sec-member-hiding-is-forbidden).

<a id="sec-inline-fixed-size-value-type-array-fields"></a>
### 17.6 Inline fixed-size value-type array fields

*Previously rejected; provided in v0.98.* Inline arrays `[N]T` are now a first-class type. They can appear as locals, as class fields (including with owned slots via `[N owned]T`), and as record fields when the slots are not `owned` (records hold only copyable value types, so move-only `[N owned]…` inline arrays are not admitted as record fields). A class can have an inline-array field whose storage is part of the class's layout, equivalent to C#'s `fixed` or Rust's `[T; N]`. See language spec [§15.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-inline-arrays) for the type form and [§15.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-move-only-value-types) for the move-only category that supports inline arrays with owned slots. The v0.40-v0.97 rejection rationale was that the use case is rare; experience and the v0.97 verification rounds suggested that inline arrays' value extends beyond fixed-size buffers into FFI marshalling, packed records, and as the foundational storage primitive over which owning collections compose.

<a id="sec-combined-visibility-modifiers-protected-internal"></a>
### 17.7 Combined visibility modifiers (`protected internal`)

C#'s `protected internal` (union: visible to derived classes *or* same-module code) and `private protected` (intersection: visible to derived classes *only within* the same module) are not provided. Most C# developers cannot state the semantics of either without looking it up. Bezel's four levels — `public`, `internal`, `protected`, `private` — are individually clear and cover the majority of needs. The intersection case (framework-internal override points) is a real gap, but the workaround is acceptable: use `internal` and rely on code review for the "only subclasses should call this" convention. If implementation experience shows the pain is worse than expected, `protected internal` meaning the intersection can be added as a purely additive change in a future revision.

<a id="sec-readonly-local-bindings-let-val-readonly-var"></a>
### 17.8 Readonly local bindings (`let`, `val`, `readonly var`)

Many languages provide a way to declare a local variable that cannot be reassigned after initialization: `let` (Swift, Rust, JavaScript), `val` (Kotlin, Scala), `final` (Java). Bezel deliberately does not provide this in the current version.

`var` is always reassignable. `const` requires a compile-time constant. `using var` is non-reassignable and carries disposal semantics — with the honest footnote (2026-07, review finding #1360) that per spec §28.10 a `using var` whose RHS is value-typed, runtime-managed, or a borrow has a no-op disposal, making it an *admitted* (if unidiomatic) readonly-local spelling. That admission is tolerated for generic and desugaring uniformity, not endorsed as the readonly-local idiom; a runtime-`const` (see the deferred list, §18) remains the candidate if demand materializes. There is no `let`, `val`, or `readonly var` for runtime-computed but non-reassignable locals.

The omission is a cost-benefit judgment. The bug class prevented by readonly locals — accidental reassignment of a local that should have been stable — is real but small relative to the complexity budget. Most locals in well-written code are single-assignment regardless of enforcement, and the ownership system ([§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows)) already tracks local-variable state far more aggressively than mutability alone. Adding a fourth local-declaration form (`let`, `readonly var`, or similar) would increase the decision burden on every local declaration without a proportionate safety payoff.

C# has lived without readonly locals for 25 years. Java added `final` locals but few teams enforce their use. The practical evidence is that this is a nice-to-have, not a must-have.

*(v0.201 — delivered; proposal 0049 train A.)* The named candidate landed: local `const` accepts any initializer, classified by form (spec §21.1) — a §17.14 constant expression of primitive/`string` type keeps compile-time-constant semantics; everything else is a **runtime const**, a single-assignment binding excluded from compile-time positions. The central ruling is ownership: a runtime `const` **rejects** owned-producing initializers, keeping the clean division — `using var` is the owned, scope-pinned binding with declared disposal; `const` is the non-owned one whose bindings never change state (not by assignment, consumption, or disposal). The permissive owned-`const` alternative was declined as an undeclared-disposal duplicate of `using var`. The checkable payoff is §7.4's amendment: a `const` local's narrowing is never invalidated — the invalidation forms are all compile errors on a `const`, so the guarantee costs no new analysis. The no-op-disposal `using var` footnote above stops being needed: `const` is now the endorsed readonly-local idiom.

<a id="sec-variadic-parameters-params"></a>
### 17.9 Variadic parameters (`params`)

There is no `params` keyword. A method that accepts a variable number of arguments uses `Array<T>` and the caller writes a collection literal:

```bezel
public method Log(messages: Array<string>) ...
Log(["hello", "world"]);
```

The brackets at the call site are syntactic friction but they make the allocation visible. A `params` mechanism that silently constructs an array from bare arguments hides allocation behavior — exactly the kind of implicit cost that Bezel's design philosophy aims to surface. The brackets also make the call site unambiguous when reading: `Log(["a", "b"])` is clearly one argument of type `Array<string>`, while `Log("a", "b")` under a `params` rule could be a two-argument overload or a params call, depending on what's declared.

<a id="sec-auto-ranked-extension-specificity"></a>
### 17.10 Auto-ranked extension specificity (the deleted §26.5.1 rules 2 and 3)

Through v0.185, extension-member resolution ranked equally-applicable candidates by three specificity relations (spec §26.5.1): subtype ordering on receiver-substituted heads (rule 1), a recursive more-concrete ordering on declared generic heads (rule 2 — `extension IEnumerable<int>` auto-beating `extension IEnumerable<T>` for an `Array<int>` receiver), and constraint-implication ordering (rule 3 — a `where`-constrained block beating a less-constrained same-head block when every constraint of the loser is "implied by" the winner's). Rules 2 and 3 are deleted (proposal 0039, accepted 2026-07-12): rule 1 alone remains, evaluated on receiver-substituted heads over applicable candidates, and every collision the deleted rules would have ordered is the §26.5 rule-4 ambiguity error.

The case against them was cost without a user. Rule 3's "implied by" was an entailment judgment whose semantics the spec never defined (is `where T: Dog` implied by `where T: Animal`? does implication chain through `where T: U` parameter chains, or through interface inheritance?) — the only constraint-entailment reasoning anywhere in the language; constraint *satisfaction* (spec §14.2) checks concrete arguments at instantiation, a different and decidable question. Rule 2 required a second ordering relation besides subtyping — a recursive concreteness comparison — plus the v0.183 clarification that rule 1 compares receiver-substituted heads while rule 2 compares declared heads: a two-views subtlety that existed only to keep the two rules from contradicting each other. Neither rule was ever implemented — the reference compiler applied first-declared-wins (#1307), so the spec's own worked example silently returned a declaration-order-dependent answer — and neither was ever exercised: the complete stdlib inventory (proposal 0039 §5, independently re-derived by its critique pass) shows every same-name pair across overlapping stdlib extension blocks differs in arity, so applicability alone decides every stdlib call and zero call sites resolve through rules 2–3. Deleting them also shrank #1307 from "implement a recursive ordering plus an entailment judgment" to "implement subtype ranking plus the rule-4 diagnostic".

What is lost is auto-ranked specialization: a generic block plus a same-head specialized (rule 2) or extra-constrained (rule 3) block sharing one member name and signature, the special form selected automatically. The remedies are the ones the language already records: keep one block and dispatch internally; split the blocks across modules so clients import one; or export the specialization under a **distinct name** — spec §22.6's posture (v0.181) that when same-name declarations cannot be soundly auto-ranked, the answer is distinct names, the convention the standard library uses everywhere. The terminal state — a rare, loud, remediable ambiguity error — is the terminal state §18's extension-call-disambiguation entry already records as acceptable for extension collisions.

The deletion is the cheap-to-reverse direction, and the re-addition condition is recorded: if demonstrated demand for auto-ranked specialization ever materializes, **rule 2 alone** may return as a compatible widening — the rules applied in order, so a restored rule 2 would only convert former ambiguity errors into resolutions and could not re-route any program that resolves under rule 1 alone. No re-addition condition is offered for rule 3: it would return with its unspecified entailment judgment, the worst item on the ledger.

<a id="sec-defer-errdefer"></a>
### 17.11 `defer` / `errdefer` statements (v0.259)

A statement registering an action to run at scope exit (`defer`), or only on the failure edge (`errdefer` / `scope(failure)`). Declined on three grounds, one per half of the design (proposal 0099, charter ruling C5).

**(1) The value half is redundant.** Bezel is affine: every owned value's disposal is already scheduled by the binding form that introduced it ([§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows), [§28.10](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-using-var)), and the tighter scope a `defer` would emulate is `do … end` ([§21.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-do-end-blocks)). Languages that need `defer` for cleanup need it because their cleanup is not attached to values; ours is. Adding `defer` would give every resource two spellings for one obligation — the both-ways surface the marker discipline is built to avoid.

**(2) The ordering half is already provided, on one stack.** `finally` composes with `using var` and with owned-local discharge on a **single establishment-ordered cleanup stack**: a `finally` is *"established at entry to the protected block … on the single establishment-ordered cleanup stack it ranks before anything the block itself establishes"* ([§31.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-try-catch-finally)). A `defer` would be a second registration mechanism ranking on the same stack — new syntax for an existing position.

**(3) The failure half needs a primitive the language deliberately lacks.** `errdefer` / `scope(failure)` must ask, at scope exit, *"are we unwinding?"* — D's `scope(failure)` is defined in terms of it and C++'s `std::uncaught_exceptions()` is its canonical spelling. Bezel exposes no such predicate, and adding one would make a program's meaning depend on the ambient unwind state at a point where nothing names an exception — the opposite of the exception model's design, in which the only ways to observe an in-flight exception are `catch` (which names its type) and `finally` (which does not, and therefore cannot branch on it). The **commit-flag guard is the sanctioned spelling**, and the standard library packages it as `ScopeGuard` (stdlib [§2.21](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-scopeguard)): the flag is a field, the "are we unwinding?" question is replaced by "was `Dismiss()` reached?", and the answer is a fact about the program's own control flow rather than about the runtime's.

**Reopening condition:** field evidence from external users that the `ScopeGuard` spelling is systematically forgotten or mis-placed in a way a statement form would prevent — together with a proposal for the uncaught-exception predicate that names its interaction with `finally`, nested unwinds, and async continuations. Absent that predicate the failure half is not designable, whatever its ergonomics.

<a id="sec-contract-carrying-type-aliases"></a>

### 17.12 Contract-carrying type aliases (v0.263)

An alias declaration carrying a predicate — `type Port = int requires(value in 0..65535)` in sketch — so that a constrained scalar can be spelled once and reused, the Ada constrained-subtype shape. Declined as spelled (proposal 0106 §6.2(a)), on three grounds, any one of which is fatal.

**(1) An alias sees through, so the constraint would buy the wrong half.** A type alias is *"a pure synonym — it does not create a new type"* (spec §27.8): identity, equality, dispatch, and overload resolution all read the aliased type. A constrained alias would therefore deliver range safety and **not** nominal distinctness — and distinctness is the half a services language actually needs, since the bug a wrapper prevents is passing a user id where an order id was meant, not passing 70000 where a port was meant. Bezel already prescribes the mechanism for the needed half: a single-field record (§27.8).

**(2) There is no boundary to check at.** Because the alias is transparent, no conversion site exists — spec §25.4 admits no user conversions at all, so the language has no position at which "entering the constrained type" happens. A check would have to be attached to assignment, argument, and return positions by a rule invented for this feature: precisely the parallel classification mechanism spec §3.6's closing rule forbids for anything that classifies types.

**(3) It could not appear in a public signature.** Aliases are module-internal, and exportable aliases (`public type`) are their own deferral (§18) inside the module-identity family. A constrained alias would be a validity guarantee that stops at the module boundary — the boundary where validation matters most.

Its useful content survives elsewhere: a validity predicate belongs to the *type* that carries the value, which is the record-invariant design spec §22.16 records as out of scope for this revision and proposal 0106 §6.2(d′) works out. **Reopening bar:** not new syntax for the alias, but new evidence against ground (1) — a demonstration that a transparent constrained type is what the language wants, which would first have to survive §27.8's see-through rule and §25.4's no-conversions posture.

<a id="sec-ambient-authority"></a>
### 17.13 Ambient authority: no object-capability discipline (v0.263)

Owner-ratified decline-with-reasons, 2026-08-20 (proposal 0106 §5bis, Q6). Bezel's I/O entry points are **ambient**: `File.ReadAllText(path)` is a static method on a static class (stdlib §7.3) and needs no capability object; any code that can name the type can reach the filesystem. Languages in the object-capability line (E, Monte, and Pony's authority side) make the opposite choice — no code holds authority it was not handed, so reachable authority is exactly what a caller passed in. The question had never been asked in any of the registers, and it is settled here rather than left silently open.

**Why declined.** Object-capability discipline is not a feature that can be added to a stdlib; it is a property of *every* entry point in it, and of the language's rules about how a reference is obtained. Taking it seriously would mean no static I/O surface anywhere, authority parameters threaded through every layer that touches the outside world, and a story for how a linked artifact obtains its initial authority — the blast-radius shape that declined user-facing allocators (§18) and a mandatory task-return marker (§12.10), here at a larger radius. What it buys — confinement of untrusted *code within one process* — is a guarantee Bezel has not undertaken to give: the compile-time guarantees are about memory and ownership, and §16's FFI posture already states that a program linking foreign code trusts it, since a single `extern` declaration voids the exactly-once guarantee for whatever it touches (spec §32.10). A capability discipline that stops at the FFI boundary would be a confinement claim the language cannot honour.

**What Bezel does promise about reach, stated because the supply-chain era makes the absence worth naming.** A linked artifact can reach every ambient entry point its imports name — that is the whole of the promise, and it is a small one. What legibility exists is at the *module* level, not the value level — and it is thinner than v0.263 stated (corrected v0.265, spec §2.3): the *typed* foreign-memory surface (`Ptr<T>`, `Interop`) sits behind an import that is deliberately not auto-imported, but that import governs **naming**, not access. Use without import (spec §2.8.3 rule 2) and the auto-imported `System`-resident FFI-boundary types (`IntPtr`, `CString`, `WString`, `RawBytes`) each reach foreign memory with no such line, so the set of files performing raw access is **not** enumerable from the import graph. Anyone reasoning about what a dependency can do should audit its **resolved callees**, and should read neither the absence of a capability parameter nor the absence of an `import System.Interop;` line as a restriction.

**Reopening bar — narrow and named.** Not a general reconsideration of ocap, which would be a different language. What could reopen is a **capability-scoped standard-library profile**: a bounded, opt-in surface for the multi-tenant and plugin-host cases, where untrusted code runs in-process and the host must bound what it reaches, justified by real demand from such a host rather than by the general principle. Such a profile would still have to answer the FFI boundary before it could claim confinement.

<a id="sec-comparer-injection"></a>
### 17.14 Ordering comparers on sorted collections — and why equality-comparer constructors are not the same case (v0.282)

The sorted collections take no per-instance ordering comparer (declined at v0.136: the single-order rule — equality, ordering, and hashing live in one place, on the type; the workaround is a wrapper record with the desired `CompareTo`). The hash collections *do* take `constructor(comparer: IEqualityComparer<K>)`. Until v0.282 the two postures never cited each other, and the single-order rule, read alone, indicts the constructors. The ruling that reconciles them: **they are not the same operation.** An `IEqualityComparer<K>` carries *both* facets a hash container consults — `Equals` and `GetHashCode` — as one object, so injection relocates the container's identity **whole**: the two functions that must agree — the facets the container consults — travel together, and the type's `CompareTo` is never consulted. (K's own `Equals` remains observable elsewhere, e.g. `DictionaryEntry<K, V>` record equality — the claim is about the container's lookups, not every use of K.) An injected *ordering* comparer would do the opposite: the container would find by the instance's order while the element's own equality stayed the type's — and since a sorted container's identity *is* `CompareTo`-equality (stdlib §4.18), the split would be directly observable in `Contains`/`TryAdd`. The single-order rule's real content is *identity must not fork*; the equality-comparer constructors satisfy it, the ordering injection would not. Both postures stand, and stdlib §4.8/§4.17 now cite this record.

<a id="sec-things-deferred"></a>
## 18. Things deferred

Not in this version; the design is open to them in future revisions. Several of these were priced in study proposals rather than here — proposal 0061 (`docs/proposals/0061-expression-form-studies.md`) and the later `*-study.md` proposals — so a deferral scan of this list alone under-reads what has been examined; read the studies beside it.

- **Sum types / discriminated unions.** *Shipped in v0.200 (proposal 0041, spec §35)* — closed nominal sums as the `choice` kind, with the three-way payload-derived ownership classification and static exhaustiveness by case name; the reasons and declined alternatives are [§9.6](#sec-choices-why-a-new-kind). What remains open is what the proposal deliberately excluded: structural/anonymous unions (declined — §9.6), GADTs, multi-level exhaustiveness counting, and `Result` combinators over **owned payloads** (each recorded liftable in proposal 0041; the value-payload combinators and declared-result construction target-typing shipped in v0.233 — proposal 0075 stages 1–2, spec §35.3, stdlib §2.20 — while the owning-payload form waits on ownership-polymorphic function parameters, recorded as 0075's stage 3(a)).
- **Static interface members.** *Shipped in v0.176 (#807, spec §11.8)* — abstract-only, recurrence-constrained, with compiler-provided primitive conformances. Of the follow-ups it unblocks, the `FromCollectionLiteral` promotion *shipped* (stdlib §2.19's target pair; proposal 0125 Part A) and the `IDeserializable` promotion is recorded and deferred (proposal 0125 Part B, #1507); the `Range<T>` de-intrinsicking once listed beside them was **ruled out as a follow-up in v0.292** (#1508): the admitted element set is a strict subset of the `ISteppable<T>` conformers, so the constraint cannot replace the intrinsic rule without widening it, and the widening is a deferred design change (`DECLINED.md`).
- **Trait/typeclass-style polymorphism.** Interfaces handle most of what traits do; the gap is associated types.
- **Const generics.** Generic parameters that are values, not types.
- **Units of measure at the type-level erased pole.** F#-style unit exponents carried in the type, checked statically, erased at runtime — `float<m/s>` distinct from `float<m>`, with the product of a `<m>` and a `<1/s>` inferred as `<m/s>`. **Not in this version, and recorded here rather than in §17 because what stands in the way is unbuilt machinery, not a rejected idea** (the placement rule the allocator entry below states). The design space has three poles and the entry reaches exactly one of them. *(i) Type-level erased units* — this entry: its two prerequisites are themselves deferrals on this list, associated types (the trait/typeclass entry above) and const generics (the entry above), since a unit algebra needs type-level exponents that can be added and compared; the standard library records the same wall from the other side, `Average` blocked because no constraint expresses its result-type mapping (stdlib §5). Spec [§14.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-recursive-instantiation) closes the route that would have avoided both: a *normalizing* encoding — one that reduces `Mul<Meter, Div<One, Second>>`, or proves `Mul<A, B> = Mul<B, A>` — is the expanding in-component instantiation shape §14.6 rejects at the declaration, and a non-recursive phantom encoding escapes that rule only by degenerating, since without normalization `Meter * Second` and `Second * Meter` are distinct types and every dimensional identity must be hand-declared. The consequences past the type system are the second half of the price: [§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis) synthesis would see only the underlying `double`, [§32.13](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-c-compatible-records)'s typed-handle admission does not cover `double` single-field records, and an artifact interface would carry a unit-expression type argument whose textual identity is fragile ([§2.8.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-artifact-interface)). *(ii) Units carried by the value* — the dimension travels in the value, arithmetic checks at evaluation, conversion is an explicit boundary operation — needs **no language change at all**: a value record carrying its dimension, [§25](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-operator-overloading) operator overloads doing the check, and named conversion members, which is what §25.4's no-implicit-conversions posture asks for rather than a fight with it. Its single-dimension case is **already shipped**: `TimeSpan` (stdlib §2.5) names the unit in every producer (`FromSeconds` and kin) and every readout (`TotalSeconds` and kin), and declares its operators on the dimensionally correct split — `+`/`-` closed under `TimeSpan`, `*` by a scalar. *(iii) Nothing* — the status quo for every other dimension, and the honest description of most services code, whose unit bugs are duration and byte-size confusions at parse boundaries rather than dimensional algebra. **Reopening bar:** the two prerequisite deferrals landing first, together with a use case whose dimensions are not adequately served by pole (ii) — scientific or embedded computation rather than services work, which is the domain this entry's decline is scoped to. *(v0.263, proposal 0106 §1.2/§5.4/§6.2(c).)*
- **Reflection beyond `typeof`/`GetType`.** This splits into three pieces with different dispositions. (1) *Type-lattice metadata* — kind predicates, `BaseType`, `IsAssignableFrom`, enum introspection — **delivered in v0.113** on the `Type` token (stdlib [§2.13](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-the-type-class)). (2) *Member-access reflection* — field/method enumeration, by-name member access, dynamic instantiation — is **declined, not deferred**: it contradicts the ownership model (a reflective `owned`-member access has no static site to carry its contract) and is the maximal violation of the no-hidden-magic principle; see rationale [§5.18](#sec-type-identity-not-reflection). It is recorded as declined so the question is settled rather than perennially reopened. (3) *Generic-instantiation decomposition* (`GenericArguments`/`GenericDefinition`) remains genuinely **deferred** — it is useful but cannot be expressed without also adding an unbound-generic `typeof` (`typeof(Array<>)`), which is a language change; it is to be decided together with that syntax, not bundled into a metadata revision. Attribute-only reflection, if reflection pressure ever materializes, is the realistic future direction and is itself a separately-scoped question (the attribute-system entry below).
- **Macros / metaprogramming.** Source generators, compile-time evaluation, syntax-tree macros.
- **Serialization: complete (object-graph tier fully delivered).** The serialization stack landed in nine planned non-breaking stages: the format-abstraction vocabulary (v0.114, stdlib [§2.14](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-serialization-vocabulary) — `ISerializer`/`IDeserializer` agents, `ISerializable`/`IDeserializable` capabilities); the concrete JSON and binary format implementations (v0.115, `System.Serialization`, stdlib [§2.15](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-concrete-serialization-formats)); compiler-synthesized conformance for records and enums (v0.116, language spec [§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis) — modeled on the existing auto-equality synthesis, a contained dedicated compiler feature, deliberately not the open-ended C# source-generator model and not gated on the macro system above); field-name customization (v0.117, the `serialized` clause of language spec [§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis) — a closed compiler-recognized modifier with three effects (rename, exclude, tolerate; rename and tolerate combinable), deliberately *not* an attribute system and improving on Swift's `CodingKeys` by being per-member and total); operation-scoped unknown-field tolerance (v0.118 — `IgnoreUnknownFields` on the deserializer, *not* a type annotation, because tolerance is a property of a particular read rather than of the type's wire contract); tree-shaped class synthesis (v0.119, the `serialized tree` class-header clause of language spec [§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis) — synthesis for classes whose serialized graph is a tree, the assertion enforced dynamically so a non-tree graph is a *detected error*, never silent duplication); the deferred-borrow-binding vs definite-assignment working-out (v0.120, rationale only, no spec text — determining the tension had a sound resolution before any syntax); shared-reference DAG synthesis (v0.121, the `serialized graph` class-header clause of language spec [§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis) — identity-preserving deserialization of aliasing acyclic graphs, the owning tree reconstructed owner-before-borrower and every back-edge bound *through the constructor* so [§7.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-definite-assignment-for-non-nullable-fields) holds unmodified); and polymorphic dispatch (v0.122, the `polymorphic` registry of language spec [§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis) — a closed, explicitly-registered discriminator→derived-type map, no reflective subtype discovery, the discriminator consumed before component binding; explicit-registration-never-reflection per [§5.18](#sec-type-identity-not-reflection), the model the mainstream reached only after reflective `$type` became a remote-code-execution vector; rationale [§5.19](#sec-serialization-without-reflection)). One piece remained through v0.123 — *cyclic* class synthesis — and it is **delivered in v0.124**: language spec [§9.8](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-serialization-synthesis) specifies the `serialized cyclic` clause, the statically-checked self-contained-constructor eligibility rule, and the two-phase construction whose intermediate state is provably unobservable, with [§7.9](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-definite-assignment-for-non-nullable-fields) preserved unmodified — the v0.123 working-out's discharge made normative. **The serialization stack is now complete**: every class object graph (tree, DAG, cyclic) is synthesizable, polymorphic edges resolve through an explicit closed registry, and the only remaining reason to hand-write `Serialize`/`Deserialize` is a class whose constructor cannot be made self-contained — a recorded, compile-time-detected, principled exclusion, not a deferred feature. *(v0.141: the keyword model described here was retired for **type-driven** `Shared<T>`/`Weak<T>` serialization — the serialized shape is now read from field types, and a cycle closes through a nullable `Weak<T>?` back-edge on a `Shared<T>` spine, with no two-phase construction and no self-contained-constructor rule; the working-out is [§5.22](#sec-type-driven-serialization).)* The staging was deliberate throughout: the vocabulary (v0.114) is the one piece that cannot be retrofitted, and each later stage was an independent non-breaking addition forward-compatible with the next, every hard question (the deferred-binding tension, v0.120; the cyclic unobservability proof, v0.123) resolved reasoning-first before its spec text.
- **Custom collection-literal protocols.** User-defined classes opt into *sequence*-literal construction by conforming to `ICollectionLiteralTarget<TSelf, T>` (borrowing, `ReadOnlyArraySpan<T>` parameter) or `IOwningCollectionLiteralTarget<TSelf, T>` (owning, `owned OwningArray<T>` parameter) — stdlib §2.19, language spec [§15.4.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-the-user-defined-sequence-literal-protocol). What remains deferred: a user-extensible *dictionary*-literal protocol — the `[k: v, ...]` paired form for user-defined map-like types. The built-in `Dictionary`/`OwningDictionary` keep the dictionary-literal form; user map-like types use ordinary constructors until the paired-factory protocol is designed. See rationale [§5.16](#sec-collection-literals-for-complex-collections).
- **Synchronization contexts for async.** Needed if Bezel targets desktop UI frameworks.
- **Module-private types.** A `module` modifier restricting visibility to a sub-module rather than the whole assembly.
- **Ref-returning indexers.** Would let `dict[key]++` be one operation instead of two. The language currently has no ref returns ([§22.14](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-no-params-no-ref-returns) of the spec); adding them is deferred.

  *(Joint note with axiom [A4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-axiom-register), added under the 2026-08-18 review programme; v0.260, proposal 0102 §4.)* A4 keeps accessor read faces lend-only, on the recorded ground that an accessor read has **no marker position available** — and A4 is **kept**: the accessor model is projections in all but name (a property or indexer read *projects* a view of the receiver's state with the mode fixed to "borrow", the write face spelled separately as `set(owned)`), and what A4 forbids is not projection but a *minting* accessor. What would reopen it is recorded here because it is the same design this entry needs: a **projection form** — a `subscript`-style member that yields a projection whose mode is declared at the yield, as in Hylo — manufactures exactly the missing marker position, and a mutable in-place projection *is* a ref-returning indexer while a transferring projection *is* the minting accessor A4 forbids. **The two therefore reopen together or not at all**: a proposal for either must address both, because a design that delivers one delivers the other's premise for free, and adopting one while declining the other would leave the language with a projection mechanism it refuses to use at half its positions. A third party rides on the same hinge: the accessor face's guaranteed-lend property is the load-bearing complement of the calls-mint/storage-lends doctrine ([§12.10](#sec-calls-mint-storage-lends)), which the v0.258 iterable unification (proposal 0096) completed by making the stored view's property face — A4's guarantee — the respell target, so a projection proposal must also confront the storage-lends half of that doctrine, not only A4's letter. Neither is proposed; the shape is recorded so a future proposal starts from the joint question rather than from the narrower one.

- **User-facing allocators and arena allocation.** A mechanism by which a caller supplies the allocator a callee's allocations draw from — an arena or bump allocator freed as a unit, a pool, a tracking allocator — as Zig's `std.mem.Allocator` parameter convention, C++'s allocator template parameters, or Rust's `allocator_api` provide. **Not in this version, and the reason is that Bezel's memory model already collects most of the payoff without the mechanism.** Allocation strategy is **implementation-defined**: nothing in this specification's companion says where a `new` expression's storage comes from, and nothing observes it. Because ownership is tracked at compile time and every owned value's disposal point is *statically known* (spec [§28.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-local-variables-ownership-and-borrows), [§28.7](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-reassigning-owned-fields)), an implementation is free to region-allocate a set of values whose lifetimes it can prove coincide and free them as a unit — arena and batch-free become an **implementation optimization**, invisible in the language, rather than an API the programmer routes by hand. What a *user-facing* allocator would cost is the reason it is not here: an allocator parameter infects every signature that allocates, transitively, so it reaches most of the standard library and most user code — the same blast-radius argument that declined a mandatory `owned` marker on task returns ([§12.10](#sec-calls-mint-storage-lends)) — and it crosses `owned`-transfer boundaries, where a value allocated from one arena is transferred to a holder whose lifetime the arena does not bound, which is a second, *dynamic* lifetime discipline running beside the static one and answerable only by the programmer. **Reopening condition:** a real embedded or hard-realtime adopter whose target forbids a general allocator, or measured allocation-pressure evidence from a running Bezel service that implementation-side region allocation cannot address. Absent one of those, the mechanism buys placement control the language deliberately does not otherwise expose. *(v0.260, proposal 0102 §3, ruled under the owner's 2026-08-18 delegation; recorded here in §18 — not §17 — because the reopening condition describes an open design, not a rejected one.)*
- **Asymmetric record auto-equality customization.** *Shipped in v0.244 (proposal 0084, spec [§9.2](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-equality-skip))* — the `equality skip` clause: per-member exclusion from auto-equality mirroring `serialized skip`'s grammar, placement, and eligibility interaction; the decision record is [§7.5](#sec-equality-skip). The entry's recorded need — exclude one field (cached hash, debug timestamp) from auto-equality without the five-member road — is exactly what shipped; what remains open is only the *logic* slice (projection equality), which stays on §25.2's declared-operator route by design.
- **Stack-allocated runtime-sized buffers (`stackalloc` analog).** v0.99 introduced `Span<T>` and `ReadOnlySpan<T>` as records carrying borrows of an `OwningBuffer<T>`; v0.100 added the write capability to `Span<T>`; v0.101 thickened the API surface with iteration, search, equality, and bulk-mutation methods; v0.102 generalized to admit value-typed T; v0.103 added byte-buffer accessors on `Span<byte>` and `ReadOnlySpan<byte>`. What remains deferred: a stack-allocated runtime-sized buffer (C# `stackalloc` analog) that would let spans view into stack memory rather than only heap memory. The use case is performance — avoiding heap allocation in hot paths like parsers. Bezel's existing inline arrays (`[N]T`, language spec [§15.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-inline-arrays)) cover the fixed-size case; a runtime-sized stack form would extend the same lifetime story to runtime-N. The design must address how stack-bound lifetimes interact with closures, exception unwinding, and recursive calls. Low priority — the heap-backed `OwningBuffer<T>` is sufficient for almost all use cases; the gap is real but narrow.
- **Spans over additional view sources.** v0.99-v0.103 spans (`Span<T>` and `ReadOnlySpan<T>`) view only into `OwningBuffer<T>`. v0.104 added `ReadOnlyStringSpan` (stdlib [§3.3](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-readonlystringspan)) for the string-source case. v0.106 added `ReadOnlyAsciiByteSpan` for the byte view over ASCII strings (retired v0.188 — the allocation-conscious byte path is `Encoding.GetBytes` with a span destination, v0.165). v0.107 added `ArraySpan<T>` and `ReadOnlyArraySpan<T>` (stdlib [§4.15](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-arrayspant-readonlyarrayspant)) for sub-range views over `Array<T>`. v0.108 added the `IReadOnlySpan<TElement>` interface (stdlib [§4.16](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-ireadonlyspan)) unifying the read-only surface across all concrete span types; v0.336 retired it ([proposal 0175](proposals/0175-retire-the-read-only-span-interface.md)), breadth across backings being one overload per span type. What remains deferred: `Span<T>` over an inline array `[N]T` — would need a new borrow-source kind that ties the span's lifetime to a stack frame, with rules for closure capture, exception unwinding, and recursive calls. Substantial design work for a narrow performance gain over the existing `OwningBuffer<T>`-backed form. Deferred — the five concrete span types (OwningBuffer-backed pair, ReadOnlyStringSpan, and ArraySpan-pair) plus the read-only interface cover the dominant use cases.
- **Move-only value types in `OwningBuffer<T>` / `Span<T>` / `ReadOnlySpan<T>`.** v0.102 admits value-typed T but excludes the move-only value types — both kinds of language spec [§15.3](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-move-only-value-types): the owning inline arrays `[N owned]T` and `[N owned]T?`, and the owning-classified choice (language spec [§35.4](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-choice-classification), move-only since v0.200). A buffer of move-only values would itself have move-only slot semantics — admissible in principle but requires a third category in the slot-semantics dispatch (class T, non-move-only value T, move-only value T) without an obvious motivating use case. Users who need a runtime-sized container of fixed-size owning arrays today wrap them in a class. Deferred until a motivating use case emerges.
- **Lambda-parameter destructuring.** *Shipped in v0.183 (#1408, spec §27.4)* — irrefutable positional-record destructuring in lambda parameter position, `lambda(ClickedEvent(_, x, y, _)) => ...`; the head names the parameter's exact record type, bindings are ordinary positional-field reads (borrows for class-typed fields, §28.3), and refutable pattern elements remain excluded — a parameter list is not a `case`.
- **Tuples and tuple destructuring.** A built-in anonymous-product type — `(int, string)` as a type, `(42, "hi")` as a value — with positional access and `for var (k, v) in dict do ...` destructuring in patterns and loops. This is an **ergonomic convenience, not a capability gap**: every use case is already expressible. Bezel's model uses positional records (language spec [§9.1](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-positional-records)) for value-only products, and *owning classes with multiple `owned` fields* for products that carry owned resources (a class may own its fields per [§28.5](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-owned-fields); a method may return `owned C`; one `using var` discharges the whole aggregate, cascading to every owned field). The `OwningDictionary<K, V>.Drain()` case — once thought to need tuples — is handled by exactly this pattern via the `DrainedEntry<K, V>` owning class (stdlib [§4.9](Bezel_Standard_Library.md#sec-owneddictionaryk-v)), with no language change. What tuples would add is purely notational: positional `(a, b)` destructuring sugar and one-shot anonymity, versus the named, documented shape that records and classes provide. Bezel's design philosophy already argues (records-vs-tuples) that a name and a documented shape is a feature, not a limitation; the same argument applies to the owned case. Class deconstruction (a `Deconstruct`-style positional surface for classes) was evaluated and declined for the same reason — see [§5.17](#sec-why-no-class-deconstruction). Tuples remain available as a low-priority future convenience; the structural-vs-nominal identity question and the named-vs-positional-fields question would need resolving, but neither blocks any program from being written today.
- **Runtime-evaluated `const` for locals.** *Shipped in v0.201 (proposal 0049 train A, spec §21.1)* — the classification split (compile-time vs runtime const), the never-owned ownership rule, and the §7.4 never-invalidated narrowing guarantee; reasons and declined alternatives in [§17.8](#sec-readonly-local-bindings-let-val-readonly-var).
- **Post-condition loops (`do-while` / `repeat-until`).** A loop that executes the body at least once before checking its termination condition. The current workaround — `while true do ...; if not cond then break; end; end;` — is straightforward but heavier than the dedicated form would be. The open design questions are syntactic: a literal `do ... while (cond);` form clashes with [§21.6](Bezel_Language_Spec.md#sec-do-end-blocks)'s `do ... end` blocks, so the addition would either need to disambiguate by the presence of `while`/`until` (which parses but reads ambiguously) or introduce a new keyword (`repeat ... until cond;` is the Pascal/Modula-2 form and would fit Bezel's overall vocabulary). The construct stays deferred as a decision, not a promise (v0.183): the payoff is the removal of one `if not cond then break; end;` line from an uncommon loop shape, and every candidate spelling has a real cost — a literal `do … while` collides with the block form, and `repeat … until` spends a new reserved word on that payoff. With the #745 block-form unification RFC declined (DECLINED.md D-0003), no syntax window is open for the form to ride. If it is ever adopted, `repeat StatementList until Expr;` is the presumptive spelling; until a stronger need is demonstrated, the workaround is the decision.
- **Multi-consumer channels / MPMC work queues.** Deferred at v0.133 pending `Shared<T>` (since delivered) and re-affirmed at v0.174 "pending pipeline evidence." That evidence now exists (2026-07 review probe: a 3-worker pool needs one channel per worker, a hand-written sendable carrier class per argument tuple, and blind `i mod N` round-robin — a slow item pins its worker while the others idle; ~90 lines for a trivial pipeline), so the deferral now stands on its remaining reason alone: receiver cloning is shared ownership of the *consume* capability, and §28.13's domain-locality questions for the synchronized core set are unresolved. The honest design options are `Channel<T>` joining the §28.13 synchronized set with cloneable receivers, or a dedicated MPMC `WorkQueue<T>`. Until one is designed, the N-single-consumer-channels + round-robin pattern is the sanctioned pool idiom, its load imbalance acknowledged rather than hidden.
- **Channel iteration (`ReceiveAll`).** The consume-until-closed loop is currently exception-terminated: `Receive` raises `ChannelClosedException` on drained-and-closed, so normal termination flows through a `catch` (stdlib §6.9). The language already has the right consumption shape — `await for` over `IOwningAsyncEnumerable<T>` with owned iteration variables — and the recorded future direction is `Channel<T>.ReceiveAll(): IOwningAsyncEnumerable<T>`: single-consumption (affine, like a lazy query), yields each item owned, ends the enumeration normally at drained-and-closed, and propagates disposal to a pending receive — the `ChannelReader.ReadAllAsync` shape. *Shipped in v0.186 — spec §30.15, stdlib §6.9; landed as sketched here (affine per §29.11, normal completion at drained-and-closed, abandonment deregisters). The entry was annotated late (v0.201, proposal 0049 train C), which misled two planning documents in the interim — the same-revision annotation rule now on the spec-change checklist exists because of this entry.*
- **`sendable` as a `where` constraint.** *Shipped in v0.201 (proposal 0049 train B, spec §14.2 "Derived-property constraints")* — the general rule decided once (derived-property constraints admitted individually by recorded ruling; nothing propagates unless written; no negated form), `sendable` admitted first, and `Domain.Spawn`/`Channel<T>` now state the requirement in their signatures (stdlib §6.8/§6.9). §12.5's decline of inference-driven Send/Sync typing stands untouched: the constraint is opt-in per declaration and appears only where a signature already traffics in domain crossing.
- **Exportable type aliases.** A module-level `type` alias is always module-internal (spec §27.8, v0.183). Making aliases exportable (`public type`) is deferred to the module-identity work (proposal 0034's family): it touches artifact metadata, §2.7 resolution order, and the collision rules, while the workaround — the importing module declares its own alias — is one line.
- **Extension-call disambiguation syntax.** When two equally-specific extensions collide (spec §26.5 rule 4), the remedies are import surgery or a static-class helper method; there is no call-site form naming the providing module. A first-class spelling is deferred: extension blocks are nameless, so any design must qualify by *module*, dragging §2.7 chain-resolution changes with it — and the collision is rare, loud, and totally remediable today.
- **`owned` as a generic argument on user-defined types.** v0.75 through v0.96 admitted `owned T` as a generic-argument modifier (`Array<owned T>`, `Task<owned T>`, etc.) via a hard-coded opt-in list. v0.97 retired this mechanism in favor of separate named classes (`OwningArray<T>`, `OwningTask<T>`, `OwningDictionary<K, V>`, etc.). What remains deferred is whether and how user-defined generic types should be able to express owning-element semantics: a mechanism by which a user-written `Pool<T>` could declare an "owning T" variant and define the corresponding per-operation semantics (which methods accept owned arguments, which dispose on removal, which transfer ownership out on drain). v0.98's inline-array primitive (`[N owned]T?`) gives user-defined owning containers a foundation to compose over (see rationale [§5.14](#sec-inline-arrays-and-array-kind-split)), but expressing this with full generic-argument-level support is deferred.
